# Paper 1 – Basic Sciences in Urology: All Answers by Section

**Compiled from:** 2015–2025 actual Paper 1 (12 papers), Practice Sets 1–14 Paper 1 and Practice Set 18–22 SAQ sheets — 238 answers.
**Repeats merged:** questions asked more than once are printed once, with every wording and source listed under "Asked as"; their answers are combined so that no fact from any version is lost. 45 merged topics replace 109 original answers; 174 topics in all.
**Layout:** the 17 revision-book sections; in each, long answer questions (LAQ) first, then short answer questions (SAQ), then a Rapid Revision Sheet of that section's key points.

**Contents**

**Section 1 · Basic Principles — Long answer questions (LAQ)**

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Iodinated and other contrast agents (use in urology) and their complications
**Asked as:**
- Discuss Iodinated contrast radiological agents and their complications. — *Summer 2016 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Complications of intravenous contrast media. — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Use of contrast agents in urology and their complications — *Summer 2022 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q1 and 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)

### Introduction
- Iodine (atomic number 53, K-edge 33 keV) absorbs X-rays strongly. All agents are built on a **tri-iodinated benzene ring** (tri-iodinated benzene rings).
- They are filtered by the glomerulus, not secreted or reabsorbed, so they concentrate in urine. Half-life is about 2 hours with normal function; biliary excretion rises in renal failure.
- Modern practice uses **non-ionic low-osmolar** (iohexol, about 600–850 mOsm/kg) or **iso-osmolar** (iodixanol, 290) agents. High-osmolar ionic agents caused far more reactions.
- Uses: CT (computed tomography) urography, IVU (intravenous urography), pyelography, cystourethrography, angiography.

### Classification
| Class | Example | Osmolality (mOsm/kg) | Iodine : particles |
|---|---|---|---|
| High-osmolar ionic monomer | Diatrizoate | ~1500–2000 (5–8 × plasma) | 3 : 2 |
| Low-osmolar non-ionic monomer | Iohexol, iopamidol | ~600–850 | 3 : 1 |
| Low-osmolar ionic dimer | Ioxaglate | ~600 | 6 : 2 |
| Iso-osmolar non-ionic dimer | Iodixanol | ~290 | 6 : 1 |

- **Why it matters:** a higher ratio gives more opacity per particle. Fewer particles mean less fluid shift, pain and reactions. So non-ionic low- or iso-osmolar agents are standard intravascularly.

### Types and uses (contrast agents in urology)

| Agent | Principle | Urological use |
|---|---|---|
| Iodinated: ionic high-osmolar (diatrizoate); non-ionic low-osmolar (iohexol); iso-osmolar dimer (iodixanol, 290 mOsm/kg) | Iodine absorbs X-rays; glomerular filtration | CT (computed tomography) urography, angiography; retrograde pyelogram, urethrogram, cystogram |
| Gadolinium (GBCA, gadolinium-based contrast agent) | Shortens T1 relaxation; chelated | MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) of renal mass, prostate, MR urography |
| Microbubbles | Gas in shell, intravascular | CEUS (contrast-enhanced ultrasound): cysts; voiding urosonography |
| Carbon dioxide | Negative contrast | Angiography in CKD (chronic kidney disease) |

### Complications
**A. Hypersensitivity (allergic-like) reactions**
- Mostly non-IgE (immunoglobulin E) direct mast-cell release: idiosyncratic, not dose-related. Severe reactions: 0.22% with ionic vs 0.04% with non-ionic agents (Katayama, 1990).

| Type | Timing | Features |
|---|---|---|
| Immediate | Within about 1 hour (most within minutes) | Urticaria, angio-oedema, bronchospasm, hypotension; anaphylaxis if severe |
| Non-immediate | Hours to days | Maculopapular rash; T-cell mediated, milder |

- Risk factors: previous reaction (strongest), asthma, atopy, severe cardiac disease, drug allergy, female sex. **Shellfish ("iodine") allergy is not a cross-reaction.**
- **Physiological reactions:** warmth, metallic taste, nausea, vasovagal hypotension with bradycardia, arrhythmia, seizures.

**B. CA-AKI (contrast-associated acute kidney injury)**
- **CA-AKI** = AKI within 48 hours of contrast, whatever the cause. **CI-AKI (contrast-induced AKI)** = the subset caused by contrast.
- KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) AKI: creatinine rise ≥0.3 mg/dL in 48 hours, ≥1.5 × baseline in 7 days, or urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for ≥6 hours.
- **Mechanism:** afferent vasoconstriction causes medullary hypoxia (ischaemic ATN (acute tubular necrosis)), plus direct tubular toxicity.
- **Risk factors:** CKD (chronic kidney disease), diabetes, age, anaemia, contrast volume, intra-arterial route.
- ACR (American College of Radiology)/NKF (National Kidney Foundation) 2020: risk was overstated; it is rare but real, mainly at eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <30 mL/min/1.73 m².

**C. Others**
- **Metformin:** lactic acidosis only if AKI develops. ACR: eGFR ≥30 and no AKI, continue. eGFR <30 or AKI, withhold 48 hours and restart when function is stable.
- **Thyroid:** iodine load can trigger hyperthyroidism/thyrotoxicosis (Jod-Basedow) in Graves' disease or nodular goitre. It blocks radio-iodine scans or therapy for several weeks.
- **Extravasation:** usually minor, but large power-injected volumes can rarely cause compartment syndrome or skin necrosis. Elevate the limb, apply compresses and observe. Get a surgical review if swelling increases, blisters form, or perfusion or sensation changes.
- **Also know:** iodide "mumps"; myasthenia flare; pregnancy (crosses placenta, not teratogenic); breastfeeding can continue; phaeochromocytoma safe with IV (intravenous) non-ionic agents; dialysis patients need no urgent dialysis.
- 4. **Also:** physiological (warmth, vasovagal), thyrotoxicosis, extravasation. Retrograde studies are safe in allergy.

### Complications of gadolinium
- **NSF (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis)** at GFR <30; group II agents carry almost no risk. Brain deposition with linear agents.

### Prevention, treatment and management
**Premedication for high-risk patients / prior reaction (ACR 2023)**
| Regimen | Drugs |
|---|---|
| 1 | Prednisone 50 mg orally 13, 7 and 1 hour before + diphenhydramine 50 mg 1 hour before |
| 2 | Methylprednisolone 32 mg orally 12 and 2 hours before + diphenhydramine 50 mg 1 hour before |
- First consider ultrasound, MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) or non-contrast CT. Breakthrough reactions still occur, so keep resuscitation drugs ready. Retrograde pyelography is safe even after anaphylaxis, because little contrast is absorbed.

**Treating reactions (treatment of reactions)**
```
Stop injection → airway, oxygen, call for help
  Urticaria → observe ± H1-antihistamine (may progress)
  Bronchospasm → salbutamol inhaler 2 puffs (90 µg each), up to 3 times
  Anaphylaxis → intramuscular (IM) adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg (1 mg/mL) every 5–15 min (repeat),
                max 1 mg; IV fluids
  Vasovagal → legs up, IV fluids, IV atropine
Observe ≥30 min (minutes); later allergy testing; record the reaction
```

**Preventing CA-AKI**
- Lowest dose of a low- or iso-osmolar agent; weight-based dosing; a low tube voltage (80 kVp, kilovolt peak) needs less contrast. Avoid repeat doses within 48 hours; stop NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs).
- **IV isotonic saline** for eGFR <30 not on dialysis: the only proven measure.
- **PRESERVE (2018):** bicarbonate and N-acetylcysteine (NAC) gave no benefit. **AMACING (2017):** no prophylaxis was non-inferior to hydration at eGFR 30–59. Removing contrast by dialysis does not reduce risk.

**Diagram to draw:** tri-iodinated benzene ring, monomer versus dimer, on an osmolality scale.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ratio 1.5 (ionic), 3 (non-ionic monomer), 6 (iodixanol, 290 mOsm/kg).
> - Non-ionic agents: severe reactions 0.04% vs 0.22% ionic.
> - Anaphylaxis: IM adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg, max 1 mg; shellfish allergy is not a risk.
> - Premedication: prednisone 50 mg at 13-7-1 h or methylprednisolone 32 mg at 12-2 h, plus diphenhydramine.
> - CA-AKI: saline if eGFR <30; bicarbonate and NAC useless; hold metformin only if eGFR <30 or AKI.
> - NSF risk at GFR <30; group II agents almost none.

## Laparoscopic access (Veress, Hasson cannula, extraperitoneal space), gases, and open vs laparoscopic vs robotic radical prostatectomy
**Asked as:**
- Discuss types of access, gases used in Laparoscopic urology and describe comparative analysis of open, Laparoscopic and Robotic Radical prostatectomy. — *Summer 2018 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Hassan's canula. — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Describe instrumentation for development of extraperitoneal space in laparoscopy? — *Summer 2020 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Access in laparoscopy
**A. Transperitoneal: closed (Veress)**
- Veress needle: 14-gauge, spring-loaded blunt stylet. Check flow and tip recoil first. Two "pops": fascia, then peritoneum.
- **Sites:** umbilicus (thinnest wall; aim towards pelvis in non-obese); lower quadrants; **Palmer point** (left subcostal, midclavicular line) with suspected adhesions; avoid scars.
- **Confirmation tests:** aspiration–injection–aspiration of saline; **drop test**; advancement test (1 cm free). Then insufflate: opening pressure <8–10 mmHg, set limit 15 mmHg.
- First trocar inserted blind, or under vision with an **optical trocar** (0° lens inside a clear tip).

**B. Open (Hasson)**
- 2-cm infra-umbilical incision; fascia and peritoneum opened under vision; stay sutures; blunt cannula.
- Safer with adhesions or previous surgery and early in training; more gas leak.

#### Hasson cannula (Hassan's canula)
*Assumed to mean the Hasson cannula, used for open laparoscopic access.*

**Introduction**
- Described by **Harrith Hasson (1971)** for open ("direct vision") laparoscopy. The first port is placed under vision instead of a blind Veress needle and trocar.
- **Parts:** outer sleeve with a cone-shaped (olive) adapter, **blunt obturator**, struts to anchor fascial stay sutures, and a gas inlet with tap.

**Indications**
- **Previous abdominal surgery or expected adhesions** (main indication).
- Organomegaly, pregnancy, aortic aneurysm, children, thin patients.
- Retroperitoneoscopy and extraperitoneal access (open start, then balloon dilatation).

**Technique (transperitoneal)**
1. 2-cm semicircular infra-umbilical incision.
2. Open fascia and then peritoneum separately, under vision, finger-sized.
3. Confirm entry by sight and finger sweep (free adhesions).
4. Place two 0 silk fascial stay sutures.
5. Insert the cannula with its **blunt obturator**.
6. Seat the cone in the defect; wrap stay sutures round the struts to seal.
7. Remove obturator; insufflate at high flow (to 12–15 mmHg).
8. At closure, tie the stay sutures to close the fascia.
- **Retroperitoneal:** 2–2.5 cm incision below the 12th rib tip, finger dissection onto psoas, balloon dilatation, then port.

**Advantages over Veress**
- No blind puncture: fewer major vessel and bowel injuries; no preperitoneal insufflation; no gas embolism from intravascular insufflation.
- Fast insufflation; easy fascial closure (less port-site hernia).

**Disadvantages**
- Larger incision; **gas leak** around the port; slightly longer in obese patients.
- Early retroperitoneal use caused subcutaneous emphysema and hypercarbia, now solved by balloon-cuffed blunt ports.

**Complications**
- Bowel injury if adherent under the wound (repair through the same incision); wound infection; port-site hernia; vascular injury is still possible.

**Evidence**
- Reviews show no single entry method is clearly safest for major injury; open entry has fewer failed entries.

**C. Retroperitoneal**
- 2-cm incision below the tip of the 12th rib; finger dissection behind Gerota's fascia, in front of psoas → **balloon dilatation** (commercial or **Gaur** glove-on-catheter balloon) → balloon-tip port to prevent gas leak and emphysema.

**D. Extraperitoneal (pelvic):** infra-umbilical, behind rectus into the Retzius space with a balloon (used for extraperitoneal prostatectomy).

#### Instrumentation for development of the extraperitoneal space
- Extraperitoneal laparoscopy works outside the peritoneum: the **flank retroperitoneum** (kidney, adrenal, ureter) or the **pelvic prevesical (Retzius) space** (extraperitoneal prostatectomy, lymphadenectomy).
- There is no natural cavity, so a working space must be **dissected before insufflation**.

| Instrument | Description and use |
|---|---|
| Open (Hasson) access set | Scalpel, S-retractors, artery forceps |
| **Index finger** | Blunt first pocket; feels psoas behind, Gerota's fascia in front |
| **Commercial balloon dissector** | Transparent silicone balloon on a stiff hollow shaft; inflated with room air by bulb (maximum 800 mL, 40 pumps). Oblong for the retroperitoneum, round for the pelvis. Laparoscope inside confirms position |
| **Gaur balloon (1992)** | Size 7 glove (or glove fingers, condom) tied with 0 silk on an 8-Fr red rubber catheter; filled with saline (600–800 mL). Cheap, but no rigid shaft or view; latex can rupture into fragments |
| **Optical (visual) obturator trocar** with 0° scope | Enters under vision; yellow retroperitoneal fat confirms the plane; scope sweeps the space |
| **Blunt-tip balloon trocar** | Internal balloon plus external foam cuff gives an airtight seal; prevents gas leak, emphysema and hypercarbia |

**Technique: Retroperitoneal**
1. Full flank, table flexed; 1.5–2.5-cm incision below the 12th rib tip.
2. Split muscles; open the lumbodorsal fascia; finger dissection between psoas and Gerota's fascia.
3. Balloon inflated cephalad then caudad, in front of psoas, behind Gerota's fascia.
4. Blunt-tip balloon trocar; insufflate; secondary ports under vision.

**Technique: Pelvic extraperitoneal**
- Infraumbilical incision → open anterior rectus sheath, separate recti → finger tunnels behind the rectus to the pubis → pierce transversalis fascia → balloon midline, then each side.

**Pitfalls**
- Balloon in muscle → flank hernia. Too anterior → peritoneal tear or colon injury; too posterior → psoas or quadratus bleeding.
- Peritoneal tear shrinks the space; convert if needed. Extraperitoneal CO₂ needs more ventilation.

**E. Others:** hand-assisted (6.5–7.5 cm port); single-port/LESS (laparoendoscopic single-site); direct trocar entry without pneumoperitoneum.

- **Trocars:** **blunt dilating trocars** have replaced bladed ones (about fivefold less epigastric injury, fewer hernias). Close fascia of midline ports >10 mm or stretched sites (all ≥5 mm in children).
- **Access complications:** preperitoneal insufflation, vessel or bowel puncture, gas embolism, subcutaneous emphysema.

### Gases used
| Gas | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|
| **CO₂ (carbon dioxide; standard)** | Non-combustible, cheap, **highly blood-soluble** (embolism less lethal), quickly absorbed | Absorbed → hypercarbia, acidosis, arrhythmia; dangerous in COPD (chronic obstructive pulmonary disease) |
| **Nitrous oxide** | Less peritoneal pain, fewer acid–base effects | **Supports combustion**: no electrosurgery |
| **Helium** | Inert, no hypercarbia; use in lung disease | Poorly soluble → embolism; costly |
| **Argon** | Inert | Embolism risk |
| Air, oxygen | – | Embolism, combustion: abandoned |

- **Physiology:** keep pressure 10–15 mmHg (12 mmHg more physiological). Pressure reduces venous return, raises afterload, causes **oliguria** (renal vein and parenchymal compression) and splints the diaphragm.
- CO₂ absorption plateaus at 30–60 minutes; higher with extraperitoneal insufflation. Hypercarbia peaks after extubation.
- **Gas embolism:** sudden collapse, millwheel murmur, end-tidal CO₂ rise then fall. Treat: stop insufflation, **left lateral, head-down**, 100% oxygen, aspirate via central line.

### Comparison of open (RRP), laparoscopic (LRP) and robotic (RALP) radical prostatectomy
- **Background:** anatomical nerve-sparing open RRP (Walsh, 1982) was the standard. First LRP (Schuessler, 1997) was too slow; French centres standardised it in 2000, but laparoscopic suturing of the anastomosis remained a barrier. The robot (da Vinci, 2000) removed that barrier, and RALP is now dominant in the USA.

| Parameter | Open RRP | LRP | RALP |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vision | Direct, loupes | 2D, unstable | **3D, 10× magnified**, stable |
| Operating time | Shortest | Longest early | Longer than open; falls with experience |
| Instruments | Hands, haptic feedback | Rigid, fulcrum effect | **Wristed** (7 degrees of freedom); no haptics |
| Learning curve | Moderate | **Steepest** (suturing) | Shorter |
| Blood loss | 300–1000 mL | Less (pneumoperitoneum tamponade) | Least; **transfusion 1–2%** |
| Hospital stay, catheter | Longer; catheter ~2 weeks | Shorter | Usually 1 day; catheter ~1 week |
| Pain, return to work | More | Less | Less; normal activity 3–4 weeks |
| Positive margins | Similar | Similar | Similar (~15%; ~9% if pT2) |
| Continence (1 year) | ~93% (expert) | Similar | Similar; possibly earlier |
| Potency | Surgeon-dependent | Similar | Similar |
| Bladder neck stricture | Up to 16% (older series) | Lower | **<2%** (mucosa-to-mucosa) |
| Unique risks | Wound infection, hernia | Conversion, CO₂ effects | Steep Trendelenburg (eye, nerve), robot failure (<0.5%) |
| Cost | Lowest | Moderate | **Highest** |

### Evidence
- **Yaxley 2016 (randomised, one open vs one robotic surgeon):** equal continence, potency and early margins at 3 months; less blood loss and shorter stay with robot.
- **Coughlin 2018:** no difference in urinary or sexual function at 24 months.
- **LAP-01 (robotic vs laparoscopic):** earlier continence with robot.
- **Concept:** the **surgeon, not the platform**, decides outcome; margins depend mainly on tumour (Gleason ≥7, stage) and surgeon volume. The robot's advantages are recovery and wider reach (obese patients, prior surgery).
- **Also know:** transperitoneal vs extraperitoneal approach (no consistent difference); Retzius-sparing RALP (earlier continence, more margins); single-port (SP) robot; new robotic platforms; telesurgery.

**Diagram to draw:** Veress needle sites (umbilicus, Palmer point) and trocar layout for RALP; Hasson cannula with cone, stay sutures and blunt obturator in the abdominal wall; flank section with balloon between psoas and Gerota's fascia.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Veress tests: aspiration, drop, advancement; opening pressure <10 mmHg.
> - CO₂ standard (soluble, non-flammable); helium in COPD; N₂O supports combustion.
> - Gas embolism: left lateral head-down, oxygen, aspirate.
> - Yaxley 2016: equal function and margins; robot less blood loss and stay.
> - Hasson 1971: open entry under vision.
> - Main indication: previous surgery or adhesions.
> - Avoids blind Veress injuries; drawback is gas leak.
> - Space is created first: finger, then balloon.
> - Gaur balloon: glove on red rubber catheter.
> - Balloon trocar seals and prevents emphysema.
> - Balloon in muscle causes hernia.

## Pneumoperitoneum physiology, complications of retroperitoneoscopy and laparoscopic donor nephrectomy
**Asked as:**
- Describe physiological and hemodynamic changes due to creation of pneumoperitoneum during laparoscopy. Describe complications of Retroperitoneoscopy. — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe hemodynamic & physiologic changes d/t pneumoperitoneum during lap. — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered, with Q2, in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q2)
- Complications of retroperitoneoscopy. — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)* (also answered, with Q1, in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q2)
- Physiological and hemodynamic effects of laparoscopy. Write in detail about lap donor nephrectomy — *Practice Set 10 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)* (also answered in Practice Set 3 Paper 1, Q1 and Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3a)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

*"d/t" is read as "due to" and "lap" as laparoscopy.*

### Part A: Physiological and haemodynamic effects of pneumoperitoneum (laparoscopy)

#### Introduction
- **Pneumoperitoneum** is insufflation of gas, usually CO₂ (carbon dioxide), into the peritoneal cavity at 10–15 mmHg to create a working space.
- Its effects have **two drivers**: **pressure** (compression of the cava, renal veins, kidneys and diaphragm) and **absorbed CO₂** (hypercarbia, acidosis, sympathetic drive). Patient position and volume status modify both (position modifies both).
- **CO₂** is standard: non-combustible, cheap, highly blood-soluble, so embolism is less lethal (less dangerous). Helium or argon in severe lung disease; N₂O (nitrous oxide) supports combustion.

#### Choice of insufflant
| Gas | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|
| **CO₂ (standard)** | Non-combustible; highly blood-soluble, so embolism is less lethal; cheap | Hypercarbia, acidosis, arrhythmia; COPD (chronic obstructive pulmonary disease) patients cannot clear it |
| Helium, argon | Inert; no hypercarbia | Poorly soluble, so gas embolism risk; costly. Helium used in lung disease or to salvage hypercarbia |
| N₂O (nitrous oxide) | Less peritoneal irritation | Supports combustion; no electrosurgery |
| Room air, oxygen | — | Embolism, combustion; abandoned |

#### Effects by pressure (supine, Campbell Table 18.1)
| Effect | 5 mmHg | 10 mmHg | 20 mmHg | 40 mmHg |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Heart rate (HR) | ↑ | ↑ | ↑ | ↓ |
| MAP (mean arterial pressure), SVR (systemic vascular resistance) | ↑ | ↑ | ↑ | ↑ |
| Cardiac output | ↔/↓ | ↔/↑ | ↔/↓ | ↓ |
| Venous return, cardiac output (Summer 2019 source) | — | Variable | Variable | ↓ sharply |
| GFR (glomerular filtration rate), urine output | ↔ | ↓ | ↓↓ | ↓↓ |
| Arterial PCO₂ | ↔ | ↑ | ↑ | ↑ |
| Arterial pH | ↔ | ↔/↓ (another source: ↔) | ↓ | ↓ |

- Renal function is the first casualty (from 10 mmHg). Afterload rises at every pressure. Output collapses at 40 mmHg. Acidosis builds from 20 mmHg.

#### Cardiovascular (haemodynamic) changes
- **Afterload rises** at every pressure: aortic and splanchnic compression plus catecholamine and **vasopressin** release raise MAP and SVR.
- **Preload depends on volume status.** In normovolaemia or hypovolaemia, pressure up to 20 mmHg compresses the IVC (inferior vena cava; the cava collapses) and venous return falls (IVC compression lowers venous return). In hypervolaemia the cava resists and venous return may rise. At ≥40 mmHg venous return falls sharply in everyone; above 40 mmHg output collapses.
- **Arrhythmias**: hypercapnia causes tachycardia and ventricular ectopics; peritoneal stretch causes **vagal bradycardia** or asystole. A new arrhythmia may warn of (herald) pneumothorax, hypoxia or gas embolism.
- **CVP (central venous pressure) reads falsely high** because intrathoracic pressure rises; do not guide fluids by it.
- Leg venous return falls (leg venous stasis): theoretical DVT (deep vein thrombosis) risk.

#### Respiratory changes
- **Diaphragm splinted** (splinted diaphragm lowers FRC and compliance, raises airway pressure): FRC (functional residual capacity) and compliance fall, peak airway pressure rises; atelectasis and V/Q (ventilation–perfusion) mismatch. Dead space is unchanged. PEEP (positive end-expiratory pressure) helps in lung disease.
- **Head-down** (steep Trendelenburg) adds a further fall in vital capacity, endobronchial tube slip and facial oedema.
- **Barotrauma** (pressure >15 mmHg for long; at high pressure): pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum.

#### Acid–base changes (CO₂ absorption)
- CO₂ is absorbed across (through) the peritoneum and, extraperitoneally, through fat and disrupted lymphatics. Absorption rises over 30–60 minutes, then plateaus.
- **Hypercarbia and respiratory acidosis** and rising end-tidal CO₂. Treat (managed) by raising minute ventilation and lowering pressure.
- **COPD** patients cannot blow it off: arterial gases if insufflation exceeds 1 hour (>1 hour), continued in recovery; consider helium. **Hypercarbia peaks after extubation**, as stored CO₂ is released.

#### Renal changes
- **Oliguria** comes from **renal vein and parenchymal compression**, not ureteric compression or hormones. Cortical flow falls; medullary flow rises up to 20 mmHg, then falls. It reverses on desufflation (reversible).
- Keep pressure ≤10 mmHg (another source: ≤10–12 mmHg); avoid fluid overload. If needed give furosemide, mannitol 12.5–25 g or dopamine 2 µg/kg/min to keep urine >100 mL/hour, rather than fluid boluses (overload, oedema). Important in live donor nephrectomy.

#### Other systems (others)
- **Splanchnic**: hepatic and mesenteric flow fall (rare mesenteric thrombosis); less ileus than open surgery; no rise in reflux.
- **ICP (intracranial pressure) and IOP (intraocular pressure)** rise with hypercarbia and head-down tilt: caution in head injury, shunts and glaucoma.
- **Hormonal:** catecholamines, cortisol and **vasopressin** rise; renin–angiotensin activation adds to afterload.
- **Stress and immune response**: cortisol, catecholamines and prolactin rise as in any surgery, but hyperglycaemia, insulin resistance, catabolism and IL-6 (interleukin-6) rise less than after open surgery (smaller stress and immune response), which may explain faster recovery; oncological benefit unproven.
- **Also know:** hypothermia from cold dry gas; shoulder-tip pain from diaphragmatic CO₂ irritation.

#### Effects of position and approach
| Position | Heart rate | MAP | Cardiac output | Other effects |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Head-up | ↑ | ↓ | ↓ | Output ↓, hypotension |
| Head-down (Trendelenburg) | ↓ | ↑ | ↑ (but lungs worse) | Venous return and output ↑; lung volumes ↓, ICP and IOP ↑ |
| Lateral flank, kidney rest | — | — | — | Lung V/Q mismatch; rhabdomyolysis risk with kidney rest |

- **Summary:** head-down raises MAP and output but worsens lungs; head-up lowers output.
- **Retroperitoneal** insufflation causes smaller swings in output and venous pressures (smaller haemodynamic swings) than transperitoneal; pelvic extraperitoneal insufflation raised arterial CO₂ more.

#### Safe practice
- Preoperative cardiopulmonary assessment; correct arrhythmias; optimise volume.
- **Practical rule: work at 10–12 mmHg** (12 mmHg keeps stroke volume unchanged); 20 mmHg only briefly (first trocar, venous bleeding); never exceed 20 mmHg for long.
- Monitor end-tidal CO₂, saturation, airway pressure and urine output.
- **Recent advances:** low-pressure (8 mmHg) surgery with valveless insufflators (AirSeal); helium (or argon) for lung disease (poorly soluble, so embolism risk).

**Diagram to draw:** abdomen under pressure with arrows to diaphragm (lungs), IVC (preload), aorta (afterload) and renal vein (oliguria), plus CO₂ absorption to blood.

### Part B: Complications of retroperitoneoscopy

#### Introduction
- **Retroperitoneoscopy** is laparoscopic or robotic surgery in the retroperitoneum without entering the peritoneum. It gives direct hilar access and avoids bowel, but the space must be **created**.
- **Why different (why its complications differ):** small space with no natural cavity; space made by finger and **balloon dissection** (commercial or Gaur glove balloon) behind Gerota's fascia and in front of the psoas; fewer landmarks; dissection close to the lens; gas tracks along tissue planes; CO₂ (carbon dioxide) is absorbed through fat and disrupted lymphatics, so hypercarbia appears early if the port leaks.
- Minimally invasive renal surgery: about 15% complications, mostly postoperative. Learning curve about 20 cases.

#### Classification (by phase of surgery)
| Phase | Complications |
|---|---|
| Access and space creation | Wrong plane, balloon rupture, peritoneal tear, muscle bleeding |
| Insufflation | Subcutaneous emphysema, hypercarbia, pneumothorax, gas embolism |
| Dissection | Vascular, visceral, nerve, thermal injury |
| Exit and recovery | Port bleeding, hernia, rhabdomyolysis, urinoma, lymphocele |

#### 1. Access and balloon-related
- **Balloon in muscle** (wrong plane, intramuscular) → postoperative **flank hernia** or bulge.
- **Too anterior** → peritoneal breach or colon injury; **too posterior** → bleeding from quadratus lumborum or psoas.
- **Balloon rupture**: latex glove balloon breaks into many fragments (silicone leaves one) that must be retrieved.
- **Peritoneal tear**: CO₂ enters the peritoneum and collapses the small working space. Manage (fix) by venting with a Veress needle or an extra port (to vent), enlarging the tear, or converting to transperitoneal.
- Disorientation: keep the **psoas horizontal** as the landmark.

#### 2. Gas-related
- **Subcutaneous emphysema** (commonest; commoner than transperitoneal): crepitus over flank, chest and neck; pneumoscrotum. Cause: leak around the port. Prevent with a **balloon-tip (blunt-tip) trocar (port)**, tight incisions and 12 rather than 15 mmHg.
- **Hypercarbia**, especially with emphysema; a sealed port removes the difference from transperitoneal surgery.
- **Pneumothorax (1.6–4%, commoner retroperitoneally)**: pleura is near the upper pole and 12th rib. Signs: neck and chest emphysema; tension gives hypotension, absent breath sounds and high airway pressure. Treat with needle decompression (16-G needle in the second or third intercostal space, midclavicular line), then chest drain. Pneumomediastinum and pneumopericardium (about 0.8%) also occur.
- **Gas embolism** (gas into an opened renal vein [renal vein injury], IVC [inferior vena cava] or lumbar vein): sudden collapse; end-tidal CO₂ rises abruptly, saturation falls, then end-tidal CO₂ falls; "millwheel" (mill-wheel) murmur. Stop insufflation (gas), **left lateral, head down**, 100% oxygen, aspirate through a central line.

#### 3. Vascular injury
- Commonest complication of minimally invasive urology; major injury <1 in 1000 but fatal in 8–17%. Upper-tract (retroperitoneal-organ) work carries about five times the pelvic vascular risk (commoner in upper-tract work).
- Sites: renal artery and vein, **lumbar and gonadal veins** (gonadal vessels), adrenal vein, IVC, aorta. In the small posterior space the IVC or aorta can be mistaken for hilar vessels.
- **Management of venous bleeding**: raise (increase) pressure temporarily to 25 mmHg, suction to see the site, compress / tamponade with a sponge, haemostatic sealant, extra 5-mm port for suturing or a Satinsky clamp, hand-assist, add ports, suture or clip, or convert if uncontrolled. Arterial: clip or staple; aortic injury needs conversion. Never clip blindly.

#### 4. Visceral injury
- **Peritoneum and colon**; duodenum (right); spleen, pancreas (left); liver; pleura; ureter; adrenal; usually by thermal spread.
- **Thermal bowel injury** by four mechanisms: direct activation, direct coupling, capacitive coupling, insulation failure. It may present up to 18 days later with low-grade fever and pain; diagnose by CT (computed tomography) with oral contrast.

#### 5. Positioning and nerve injury
- **Rhabdomyolysis** (about 1% retroperitoneal; 0.4% overall): men, BMI (body mass index) ≥25, kidney rest, full flexion, long cases. Brown urine, hip pain, CK (creatine kinase) >5,000 units. Prevent: limit kidney rest to <1 hour, cases <5 hours, avoid hypotension.
- **Nerves** (neuropraxia): brachial plexus (axillary roll), sciatic; in the field, **genitofemoral and lateral femoral cutaneous** on the psoas, ilioinguinal and iliohypogastric (thigh numbness, flank bulge).

#### 6. Postoperative
- Bleeding or haematoma; port-site hernia or haematoma; wound infection.
- **Urinoma** (partial nephrectomy, pyeloplasty); **lymphocele** or chylous leak after hilar or node dissection.
- Delayed bowel injury; DVT (deep vein thrombosis).
- **Conversion** to transperitoneal or open surgery for bleeding, peritoneal tear or lost orientation.

#### Retroperitoneal versus transperitoneal
| Retroperitoneal more | Retroperitoneal less |
|---|---|
| Emphysema, pneumothorax, hypercarbia, disorientation | Bowel injury, ileus, adhesions; peritoneum spared |

#### Prevention
- Open (Hasson) access below the 12th rib tip; balloon wholly in the retroperitoneum; balloon-tip port; 12 mmHg; trocars under vision; respect the 12th rib and pleura; check haemostasis at low pressure.

**Diagram to draw:** flank cross-section showing balloon between psoas and Gerota's fascia, with peritoneum pushed medially and port positions (flank port positions for retroperitoneoscopy).

### Part C: Laparoscopic donor nephrectomy

#### Introduction
- First described by **Ratner (1995)**; now the preferred approach (open under 2% in the United States). **Donor safety comes first** at every stage.

#### Donor evaluation
- Questionnaire → physical and psychosocial review, blood group, HLA (human leukocyte antigen) typing → **GFR by at least two methods, one measured** → metabolic, serology, cancer screening → **CT (computed tomography) angiography** for vessels → cardiac review if needed.
- Exclusions: kidney disease, malignancy, active infection, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, significant obesity; bilateral stones generally exclude.

#### Side selection
- **Left preferred:** longer renal vein, simpler dissection. **Right** if the left has multiple arteries or complex veins, or the right kidney is smaller: **the donor keeps the better kidney.**
- **Anatomy matters:** arterial variation in 25–40%; early (prehilar) branching and accessory arteries must be seen on CT, as they may contraindicate the laparoscopic route.

#### Techniques
| Technique | Features |
|---|---|
| Pure transperitoneal | 3–4 ports; colon reflected; Pfannenstiel extraction |
| **Hand-assisted** (about two-thirds) | Hand port doubles as extraction site; tactile feedback, quick haemostasis |
| Retroperitoneoscopic | No bowel mobilisation; direct hilum; less room |
| Robotic; LESS (laparoendoscopic single-site surgery) | Wristed instruments; single incision, less pain |

#### Operative steps (left)
1. Reflect colon; divide **gonadal, adrenal and lumbar veins**.
2. Preserve periureteric tissue and the **gonadal–ureter triangle** (ureteric blood supply).
3. Keep pressure ≤10 mmHg; hydration and mannitol or furosemide to keep urine >100 mL/hour (counters pneumoperitoneum oliguria).
4. Divide ureter, then **artery first, then vein**. Vein stapled lateral to the adrenal vein; Endo-TA (non-cutting) stapler gives extra vein length.
5. Extract quickly; cold flush. Warm ischaemia a few minutes.
- **Hem-o-lok:** manufacturer contraindicates it on the donor renal artery after stump bleeds.
- **Open donor nephrectomy** is kept for complex anatomy or previous surgery, more often on the right.

#### Advantages versus open
- Less pain, shorter stay, faster return to work, better cosmesis; **more people volunteer to donate**; equal graft function.

#### Disadvantages and complications
- Longer operation, learning curve, shorter vessels (right vein), slightly longer warm ischaemia.
- Complications about **16%** (mostly gastrointestinal); Clavien ≥IV 2.5%; **mortality 3 per 10,000**; conversion 0.26–3.34%, mainly for vascular injury.

#### Long-term donor counselling
- **Also know:** kidney paired donation for ABO-incompatible or crossmatch-positive pairs; living-donor grafts survive longer than deceased-donor grafts.
- Function 60–80% of pre-donation; lifetime ESRD (end-stage renal disease) **90 per 10,000** versus 14 in matched non-donors (Muzaale, 2014); more gestational hypertension.

**Diagram to draw:** left renal hilum with gonadal, adrenal and lumbar veins, stapled artery and vein, and the gonadal–ureter triangle.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Two drivers: pressure and absorbed CO₂; work at 10–12 mmHg.
> - Afterload rises at all pressures; output collapses at 40 mmHg.
> - Oliguria from renal vein compression; reversible.
> - Hypercarbia peaks after extubation; COPD needs arterial gases.
> - Gas embolism: left lateral, head down, oxygen, aspirate.
> - Retroperitoneoscopy: space is created, not natural: wrong plane gives hernia, peritoneal tear or bleeding.
> - Emphysema commonest; balloon-tip port and 12 mmHg prevent it (prevents leak).
> - Pneumothorax 1.6–4%, commoner retroperitoneally.
> - Rhabdomyolysis about 1%: limit kidney rest to <1 hour.
> - Ratner 1995; left kidney preferred; hand-assisted in about two-thirds.
> - Mortality 3 per 10,000; conversion 0.26–3.34%.
> - Donor ESRD 90 versus 14 per 10,000 (matched non-donors).

## Describe IVU & its applications in Urology.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q2

### Definition and principle
- IVU (intravenous urography; also IVP, intravenous pyelography or excretory urography) is a series of timed abdominal radiographs after intravenous iodinated contrast.
- Contrast is **freely filtered by the glomerulus and not reabsorbed**. As water is reabsorbed it is concentrated in the tubules (**nephrogram**) and then outlines the calyces, pelvis, ureters and bladder (**pyelogram**).
- So IVU shows **anatomy plus a rough, qualitative idea of function and drainage** of each kidney.
- **Current status:** largely replaced by CTU (CT [computed tomography] urography). It is still used where CT is unavailable (common in India) or when dynamic excretion and drainage must be seen.

### Contrast media
| Type | Example | Feature |
|---|---|---|
| High-osmolar ionic | Diatrizoate | More reactions; obsolete for IV use |
| **Low-osmolar non-ionic** (standard) | Iohexol, iopamidol | Fewer reactions |
| Iso-osmolar non-ionic | Iodixanol | High-risk renal patients |

### Preparation
- Check creatinine or eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate), allergy, asthma, pregnancy, diabetes on metformin, myeloma.
- Laxative the night before (bowel gas hides the kidneys); short fast but **no dehydration**.
- Premedicate high-risk patients, e.g. prednisone 50 mg at 13, 7 and 1 h plus diphenhydramine 50 mg at 1 h.

### Technique: film sequence
| Film | Timing | Shows |
|---|---|---|
| **Scout (control) KUB** (kidney–ureter–bladder) | Before contrast | Calcification, bones, gas, technique |
| Contrast | Low-osmolar, about 1 mL/kg (usually 50–100 mL) IV | — |
| **Nephrogram / nephrotomogram** | About 1 min (60–90 s) | Renal size, outline, parenchyma |
| Pyelogram | 5 min | Calyces; then apply **ureteric compression** |
| Compression film | 10 min | Well-distended calyces and pelvis |
| **Release (full-length) film** | About 15 min | Whole ureters |
| Bladder and **post-void** films | 20–30 min | Bladder outline, residue, lower ureters |
| Delayed films | Up to 24 h | Level of obstruction; **no excretion at 24 h = non-functioning kidney** |
- **Also know:** films in expiration; prone film fills the ureters; oblique views; erect film for nephroptosis.
- Compression is avoided in suspected obstruction or colic, aortic aneurysm, recent abdominal surgery and trauma.

### Contraindications
- Previous severe contrast reaction; significant renal impairment; pregnancy (relative); dehydration, myeloma.

### Nephrogram patterns
| Pattern | Causes |
|---|---|
| Absent | Renal artery occlusion, agenesis, avulsion |
| **Delayed, increasingly dense** | **Acute obstruction**, renal artery stenosis |
| Striated | Acute pyelonephritis, obstruction |
| **Rim (cortical rim) sign** | Renal infarction (capsular collaterals) |
| Bilateral persistent dense | Hypotension, ATN (acute tubular necrosis) |

### Applications in urology
| Condition | IVU features |
|---|---|
| **Calculi** | Confirms an opacity is in the tract; **radiolucent stone = filling defect**; site, obstruction, calyceal anatomy before SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) or PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) |
| **Acute obstruction** | Delayed dense nephrogram, delayed excretion, dilatation down to the block, **standing column** of contrast, forniceal rupture |
| **PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction** | Ballooned pelvis, delayed drainage, non-dilated ureter; crossing-vessel indentation |
| **Duplex system** | Upper moiety obstructed or non-functioning: lower moiety displaced, "**drooping lily**" |
| **Ureterocele** | "**Cobra head**" (spring onion) at the bladder |
| **Horseshoe kidney** | Low, medially rotated; lower calyces point medially |
| **Retrocaval ureter** | "Reverse J" or **fish-hook** at L3 |
| **Medullary sponge kidney** | "**Paintbrush**" papillary striations |
| **Papillary necrosis** | **Ring sign**, "lobster claw" calyces |
| **Genitourinary TB** (tuberculosis) | **Moth-eaten** calyx, **phantom calyx**, Kerr's kink, **pipestem** or beaded ureter, contracted bladder, autonephrectomy |
| **Urothelial tumour** | Irregular filling defect, non-visualised calyx |
| Retroperitoneal fibrosis | Medial deviation and tapering of ureters with hydronephrosis |
| Pelvic lipomatosis | Pear-shaped bladder |
| Schistosomiasis | Ureteric striations, calcified bladder |
- **Trauma: one-shot intraoperative IVP:** 2 mL/kg IV push, single film at 10 min. It confirms a functioning contralateral kidney before exploration in the unstable patient.
- **Also know:** nephroptosis (descent >5 cm or two vertebral bodies supine to erect); urinary fistulae (ureterovaginal); renal masses (distortion); post-operative assessment; diverticula.

### Complications
- **Contrast reactions:** immediate (urticaria to anaphylaxis) or delayed (rash). Severe reaction: stop contrast, **IM (intramuscular) adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg** every 5–15 min (maximum 1 mg), oxygen, β2-agonist. Observe 30 min.
- **CA-AKI (contrast-associated acute kidney injury):** prevent with hydration, low or iso-osmolar contrast, lowest dose.
- Radiation; metformin-related lactic acidosis in renal impairment.

### Limitations and current role
- Two-dimensional; bowel gas; poor parenchymal detail; misses small masses; poor in renal failure.
- CTU is more sensitive for stones, masses and urothelial lesions, but gives a higher radiation dose than IVU. IVU remains useful in resource-limited settings.

### Recent advances
- Split-bolus CTU (fewer phases); dual-energy CT (virtual unenhanced images); MR urography without radiation.

**Diagram to draw:** normal IVU with minor and major calyces, pelvis, ureter and the three narrowings; beside it a "drooping lily".

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Contrast is filtered, not reabsorbed: nephrogram then pyelogram.
> - Delayed, increasingly dense nephrogram = acute obstruction; no excretion at 24 h = non-functioning.
> - Classic signs: drooping lily, cobra head, fish-hook, paintbrush, ring sign, phantom calyx.
> - Severe reaction: IM adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg; CTU has replaced IVU.

## Principles of tissue engineering and its use in urology
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q1
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q2; this answer stresses the principles)

### Definition and rationale
- **Tissue engineering** applies engineering and life sciences to build biological substitutes that restore, maintain or improve tissue function (Langer and Vacanti, 1993).
- **Why in urology:** bowel in the urinary tract causes mucus, stones, metabolic acidosis, infection and cancer; grafts for long urethral defects are scarce. The aim is to replace "wrong tissue" with "right tissue".

### Principles: the triad plus bioreactor and blood supply
```
Biopsy -> cells expanded in culture -> seeded on scaffold (+ signals)
       -> conditioned in bioreactor -> implanted -> host vascularises and remodels
```

**1. Cells**
| Source | Comment |
|---|---|
| Autologous differentiated cells (urothelial and smooth muscle cells from a bladder biopsy) | No rejection; expand well; not usable if the organ is malignant |
| Adult stem cells: bone marrow or adipose mesenchymal, muscle-derived, urine-derived | Easy harvest; act largely by paracrine (growth factor) effects |
| Embryonic stem cells | Pluripotent; ethical issues, teratoma risk |
| iPSCs (induced pluripotent stem cells) | Adult cells reprogrammed; patient-specific; tumour risk |

**2. Scaffolds (biomaterials)**
- **Requirements:** biocompatible; degrade at the rate new tissue forms; porous for cell entry and vessel ingrowth; mechanically suited (a compliant bladder, a non-collapsing tube).

| Type | Examples | Feature |
|---|---|---|
| Natural polymers | Collagen, alginate, silk | Good cell recognition; weak |
| Acellular tissue matrices | Bladder acellular matrix, SIS (small intestinal submucosa) | Native architecture and stored growth factors |
| Synthetic polymers | PGA (polyglycolic acid), PLA (polylactic acid), PLGA (their copolymer) | Controlled shape and degradation; no bioactivity |
| Newer | Electrospun nanofibres, hydrogels, bio-ink | Mimic extracellular matrix |

- **Seeding:** urothelial cells on the luminal side and smooth muscle cells on the outer side, copying native layers.
- **Unseeded** matrices rely on host cells migrating in, so they work only for short onlay defects (about 0.5 cm for tubes in animal work). Longer or tubular gaps need **seeded** scaffolds, or they fibrose and contract.

**3. Signals**
- Growth factors (for example VEGF, vascular endothelial growth factor, for vessels) released from the scaffold, extracellular-matrix cues and mechanical stimuli direct cell growth and differentiation.

**4. Bioreactors**
- Culture chambers that perfuse nutrients and oxygen through thick constructs and apply **physiological conditioning**, such as cyclic stretch and filling for bladder smooth muscle, so the tissue is functional before implantation.
- Types: static culture, spinner flask, rotating wall, perfusion and mechanical-stretch bioreactors. They also allow monitoring and sterile, standardised production.

**5. Vascularisation and innervation**
- Oxygen diffuses only about 100–200 µm, so thick constructs die centrally without vessels. This is the main barrier.
- Strategies: **omental wrap** (used by Atala), angiogenic factors, co-seeding endothelial cells, built-in channels by bioprinting.
- Innervation and coordinated contraction cannot yet be recreated.

### Uses in urology
| Organ | Status |
|---|---|
| **Bladder** | **Atala et al. (2006):** seeded collagen–PGA constructs with omental wrap in 7 myelomeningocele patients; compliance improved. Industry phase II trial: **no significant gain in compliance or capacity**. Experimental (Chapter 48). |
| **Urinary conduit** | Neo-Urinary Conduit trial (NCT01087687): survived about 250 days on average; **half developed stenosis**; stiff material, no blood supply (Chapter 135) |
| **Urethra** | Acellular matrix onlay for short strictures and hypospadias; **Raya-Rivera et al. (2011):** seeded tubular PGA–PLGA urethras in **5 boys**, functional up to 6 years; tissue-engineered oral mucosa |
| **Sphincter** | Autologous muscle-derived cells for SUI (stress urinary incontinence); biodegradable scaffold slings |
| **Vagina** | Engineered vaginas in MRKH (Mayer–Rokitansky–Küster–Hauser) syndrome (Raya-Rivera, 2014) |
| **Penis** | Corporal tissue and tunica grafts (stem cell-seeded SIS in Peyronie disease): animal or experimental; stem cells for ED (erectile dysfunction) investigational |
| **Kidney** | Decellularised–recellularised scaffolds (animal), kidney organoids, renal assist devices |
| **Testis** | Spermatogonial stem cell banking in prepubertal cancer patients |

### Recent advances
- **3D bioprinting:** cells in hydrogel "bio-ink" laid layer by layer from CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) data; vascular channels built in. Printed (non-living) models already aid planning.
- **Organoids:** kidney organoids from iPSCs; bladder and prostate tumour organoids for drug testing.
- Robotic vascularised composite bladder allograft (Chapter 135): a transplant route that avoids bowel but needs immunosuppression.

### Challenges
- Vascularity, innervation, fibrosis and contracture; scale-up, cost, culture time, regulation; stem cell tumour risk; promising pilots followed by disappointing controlled trials.
- **Also know:** gene-modified cells; growth factor-releasing "smart" scaffolds; cell sheet engineering.

**Diagram to draw:** biopsy → culture → seeding on scaffold → bioreactor → implant with omental wrap.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Triad: cells, scaffold, signals; plus bioreactor and blood supply.
> - Diffusion limit about 100–200 µm: vascularity is the main barrier.
> - Atala 2006 promising; phase II trial showed no gain; conduit half stenosed.
> - Raya-Rivera 2011: seeded urethras in 5 boys.

## Radionuclide (isotope) scans in urology: principles, renal scans, MAG3 and applications
**Asked as:**
- Radionuclide scans and its applications in uro — *Practice Set 13 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Isotope studies of urology — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*
- Renal isotope scans — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- MAG 3 — *Practice Set 7 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 13: also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3d–Q3f. Practice Set 2 Q3d: renal scans in depth: see Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3e; diuretic renography: see Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3f. Practice Set 2 Q3e: also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3f and 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3e; diuretic protocol: see Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3f)

*"Uro" is read as urology.*

*MAG 3 — assumed meaning: Tc-99m MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renal scan: principle, technique, interpretation and uses.*

### Principles
- Nuclear medicine shows **function, not structure**. A tracer, given in a tiny (subpharmacological) dose, follows a physiological or molecular process; the patient is the radiation source.
- **Gamma camera** (sodium iodide crystal, photomultipliers, collimator): photon, collimator (admits photons from one direction), sodium iodide crystal (light), photomultiplier tubes, image. Planar (about 20 mm resolution) or **SPECT (single-photon emission computed tomography)** with CT (about 10 mm).
- **PET (positron emission tomography):** the positron annihilates with an electron (positron annihilation), giving two 511 keV photons 180° apart, detected in coincidence. This rejects noise (less noise) and allows true quantitation (quantitative SUV, standardised uptake value); sharper (about 5 mm).

| | Planar | SPECT (single-photon emission computed tomography) | PET |
|---|---|---|---|
| Image | 2D | 3D, with CT (computed tomography) | 3D, with CT or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) |
| Resolution | About 20 mm | About 10 mm | About 5 mm |
| Quantitation | Semiquantitative | Semiquantitative | SUV |
| Main use | Renal function | Problem-solving | Cancer |

- **⁹⁹ᵐTc (technetium-99m):** 6-hour half-life, single 140 keV photon: the workhorse. **¹⁸F:** about 110 minutes, lowest positron energy, so the sharpest PET image. ⁶⁸Ga comes from a generator; ¹¹C (20 minutes) needs an on-site cyclotron.

### Applications (overview)

| Area | Tracer | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Kidney** | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid), -MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine), -DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) | GFR (glomerular filtration rate), split function, obstruction, scars, transplant |
| **Bone** | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-MDP (methylene diphosphonate) | High-risk prostate cancer staging; low specificity |
| **Prostate** | **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET**; choline; fluciclovine | PSMA preferred (NCCN [National Comprehensive Cancer Network]) for staging and recurrence; specificity >90% |
| **Therapy** | ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617; radium-223 | mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer); VISION trial (2021) |
| **FDG (fluorodeoxyglucose) PET** | Glucose analogue trapped in cells | **Residual seminoma >3 cm**; penile cancer with palpable nodes; distant urothelial disease. Poor in prostate and clear cell RCC (renal cell carcinoma) |
| **Renal mass** | Sestamibi SPECT; girentuximab PET | Oncocytoma "hot" on sestamibi; clear cell RCC "hot" on girentuximab |
| **Adrenal** | ¹²³I-MIBG (metaiodobenzylguanidine), ⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE, FDG | Metastatic or extra-adrenal phaeochromocytoma; MIBG best in MEN-2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2) |
| **Sentinel node** | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-nanocolloid plus blue dye | Penile cancer, impalpable nodes |
| **Scrotum** | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-pertechnetate | Torsion cold, epididymitis hot |
| **Reflux** | Direct radionuclide cystography | VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) follow-up: more sensitive, lower dose than VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) |
| **Infection** | ¹¹¹In-labelled leucocytes; ⁶⁷Ga | Occult abscess; not in transplant |

### Renal tracers
- Each tracer images the part of the nephron that handles it. Plasma clearance is about 20% filtration and 80% tubular secretion.

| Feature | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid) | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) | ⁹⁹ᵐTc-DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Handling | Glomerular filtration only | Protein-bound; tubular secretion | Filtered, then retained by (in) proximal tubules (megalin, cubilin); tubular cortical binding |
| Study | Dynamic | Dynamic | **Static cortical**, at 2 hours (2 h) |
| Main use | **GFR (glomerular filtration rate)**; captopril study; renovascular | **Split function, obstruction (first choice)**, drainage; transplant | **Pyelonephritis and scars** |
| Renal failure | Poor images in renal failure | Good images in renal failure | Delay imaging |
| Dose | 5–10 mCi | 5–10 mCi | 5 mCi adult; 50 µCi/kg child |

### MAG3: principle
- **⁹⁹ᵐTc-MAG3:** protein-bound tracer cleared mainly by **proximal tubular secretion**. Extraction is nearly **60%** per pass, versus about 20% for DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid).
- So it gives **good images even with poor GFR (glomerular filtration rate)**, lower background and lower cost. **Agent of choice for split function and obstruction.**

### Dynamic renography (MAG3): method and interpretation
- **Preparation:** hydrate (**hydration**); (**bladder**) catheter for outlet obstruction or neurogenic bladder, and in children; cap nephrostomy. Dose 185–370 MBq (5–10 mCi) IV (intravenous) bolus (intravenously) in adults.
- **Perfusion phase** (first minute): 1–2 second frames (frames every 1–2 seconds); the kidney appears within seconds of the aorta (renal curve follows aortic upstroke).
- **Function (uptake) phase:** regions of interest with background subtraction. **Split function (DRF, differential renal function) = area under each curve at 1–3 minutes**; normal up to 45:55 (**normal 45–55%**). Normal (MAG3) peak at 3–5 minutes (MAG3 peaks at 3–5 minutes); bladder by 10–15 minutes.
- **Drainage phase:** a rising curve needs furosemide (see Q3f). Upright or post-void images test gravity drainage.
- **GFR:** DTPA camera method (Gates) or plasma-sample clearance.
- **Diuretic renography ("well-tempered" diuretic renogram):** dilatation is not obstruction. Furosemide 0.5 mg/kg (adult maximum 40 mg) in adults; 1 mg/kg in children, given when the system is full; separates a patulous system from obstruction. Timing varies (F+20, F+15, F0, F−15) and must be stated, so the report must state it.

| T½ (half-clearance time) after furosemide | Meaning |
|---|---|
| <10 minutes (min) | Not obstructed |
| 10–20 minutes (min) | Indeterminate |
| >20 minutes (min) | Obstructed |

- **Curve types (O'Reilly):** I normal; II rising (obstructed); IIIa dilated, washes out with diuretic; IIIb equivocal.

### MAG3 uses
- PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction and megaureter: **surgery if DRF <40% with poor drainage or falling DRF**; follow-up after pyeloplasty.
- Transplant: acute rejection (perfusion falls) versus delayed graft function (perfusion kept); urine leak.
- **Also know:** indirect cystography for VUR (vesicoureteric reflux); split function before nephrectomy.

### MAG3 pitfalls
- Dehydration, full bladder, wrong ROI (region of interest), mistimed diuretic, massive dilatation and neonatal immaturity mimic obstruction. **Trend on serial scans** matters.

### Other renal applications (special studies)
- **Captopril renography (DTPA):** the stenotic kidney keeps GFR by angiotensin II efferent constriction. Captopril removes this (it), so uptake falls. Repeat off ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors: improvement **≥10%** off ACE inhibitors means renovascular hypertension is likely. Largely replaced by CT (computed tomography) angiography.
- **Transplant (MAG3):** acute rejection: perfusion falls and worsens; delayed graft function: perfusion kept, improves. Detects (shows) urine leak without iodinated contrast.
- **DMSA (cortical scan):** pyelonephritis gives an ill-defined defect that resolves by about 6 months; a scar is sharp, with cortical thinning, and persists.

| | Acute pyelonephritis | Scar |
|---|---|---|
| Defect | Ill-defined | Sharp, cortical thinning |
| Kidney | Normal size | Often small |
| At 6 months | Resolves | Persists |

- **Radionuclide cystography:** direct (tracer instilled into the bladder) or indirect (after MAG3). More sensitive and lower dose than VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram), but little anatomy. So VCUG first; nuclear cystography for follow-up.

### Infection imaging
- **¹¹¹In-oxine leucocytes** (image at 24 hours): best for occult abdominal or genitourinary infection. ⁹⁹ᵐTc-HMPAO (hexamethylpropyleneamine oxime) leucocytes give better images, but urinary excretion obscures the tract. Neither in a transplant (leucocytes home to the graft).
- **Also know:** ⁶⁷Ga-citrate (non-specific, slow).

### Oncology
- **FDG (¹⁸F-fluorodeoxyglucose):** a glucose analogue phosphorylated and trapped (Warburg effect). Urinary excretion limits it to distant disease.
  - **Seminoma residual mass >3 cm** after chemotherapy (EAU [European Association of Urology]). Not for NSGCT (non-seminomatous germ cell tumour), because teratoma is not FDG-avid.
  - Penile cancer with palpable nodes; equivocal bladder or upper-tract metastases.
  - Poor in prostate cancer and clear cell RCC (renal cell carcinoma).
- **Prostate:** **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET** is preferred (NCCN [National Comprehensive Cancer Network]) for high-risk staging and biochemical recurrence. Specificity >90%; nodal sensitivity only 30–40%. Calais trial (2019): detection 56% (PSMA) versus 26% (fluciclovine). Bone scan (⁹⁹ᵐTc-MDP, methylene diphosphonate) remains conventional.
- **Renal mass:** **sestamibi SPECT** "hot" in oncocytoma; **girentuximab** (anti-CAIX, carbonic anhydrase IX) PET "hot" in clear cell RCC (ZIRCON trial).
- **Also know:** ¹²³I-MIBG (metaiodobenzylguanidine) and ⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE for phaeochromocytoma; ⁹⁹ᵐTc-nanocolloid sentinel node biopsy in penile cancer; ⁹⁹ᵐTc-pertechnetate scrotal scan (torsion cold, epididymitis hot) when ultrasound is equivocal.

### Therapy (theranostics)
- **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617:** beta emitter for mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer) after ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) and taxane: **VISION (2021)**. PSMA PET confirms the target first.
- **Radium-223:** alpha emitter for bone-only mCRPC.

### Advantages and limitations
- Quantitative function, low radiation, safe in contrast allergy and pregnancy (MAG3 fetal dose <5 mGy).
- Poor anatomy; PET is costly; results depend on technique.

**Diagram to draw:** MAG3 renogram: perfusion spike, peak at 3–5 minutes, washout, 1–3 minute split window; obstructed curve rising; renogram curves types I, II, IIIa and IIIb. Gamma camera chain: patient, collimator, crystal, photomultiplier, image.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ⁹⁹ᵐTc: 6 hours, 140 keV; PET: two 511 keV photons.
> - DTPA filtered = GFR; MAG3 secreted = obstruction and transplant; DMSA cortical = scars.
> - MAG3: tubular secretion, about 60% extraction; best for split function and drainage.
> - Split function (DRF) from 1–3 minute counts; normal 45–55%.
> - T½ >20 minutes (min) obstructed, <10 min not.
> - Hydration, catheter, correct diuretic timing: the well-tempered renogram.
> - Captopril renogram: ≥10% improvement off ACE inhibitors.
> - Scar persists at 6 months; pyelonephritis resolves.
> - PSMA PET preferred; FDG for residual seminoma >3 cm.
> - MIBG best in MEN-2; sentinel node with ⁹⁹ᵐTc-nanocolloid.
> - Sestamibi hot = oncocytoma; ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 (VISION).

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Newer generation CT scan
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3a

### Introduction
- Computed tomography (CT) is the reference imaging for stones, masses and trauma. Attenuation is expressed in Hounsfield units (HU): water 0, air −1000.

### Evolution
| Generation | Feature |
|---|---|
| Helical (~1989) | Continuous table movement, volumetric data |
| Multidetector (1998 → 320-row) | Sub-millimetre slices, 3D reconstruction |
| Dual-source/dual-energy (2005) | Two energies: material analysis |
| Photon-counting (US approval 2021) | Energy-sorts each photon: less noise, dose and metal artefact |

### Dual-energy CT (DECT)
- Two energies (e.g. 80 and 140 kVp) compared pixel by pixel: **uric acid vs non-uric-acid stones**, **virtual unenhanced** images (one phase fewer) and **iodine maps** (hyperdense cyst vs enhancing mass).

### Low-dose CT
| Protocol | Dose |
|---|---|
| Conventional abdomen/pelvis | 10–20 mSv |
| Low-dose | <3–4 mSv |
| Ultra-low-dose | 1–2 mSv |
- Stone sensitivity stays ~95%; use standard dose if body mass index >30. EAU (European Association of Urology): ultrasound first; AUA (American Urological Association): CT first.

### Reconstruction
- Filtered back projection is noisy; iterative reconstruction halves dose; **deep learning reconstruction** gives good images at ~1 mSv.

### Contrast phases and protocols
| Phase | Timing | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Arterial | 20–30 s | Renal arteries, donor anatomy, active bleeding |
| Corticomedullary | 30–70 s | Hypervascular (clear cell) tumours, renal vein |
| Nephrographic | 90–120 s | Most sensitive for renal masses |
| Excretory | 7–10 min | Collecting system, ureter, urine leak |
- **CT urography:** non-contrast, nephrographic and excretory phases (~20–30 mSv). **Split-bolus** merges phases, lowering dose but may miss subtle urothelial lesions.
- **Renal mass:** enhancement ≥20 HU = solid, 10–20 equivocal, <10 cyst; Bosniak 2019 for cysts.
- **Adrenal:** <10 HU unenhanced = lipid-rich adenoma; otherwise 15-minute washout (absolute >60% or relative >40% = adenoma).
- **Also know:** stone HU (uric acid 200–400, calcium oxalate 600–1200; indinavir invisible); bone window for fragments; partial volume causes pseudoenhancement of small cysts; ALARA (as low as reasonably achievable) dosing, since radiation risk has no threshold.

**Diagram to draw:** Timeline: axial → helical → multidetector → dual-source → photon-counting.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Doses: conventional 10–20, low-dose <3–4, ultra-low 1–2 mSv.
> - DECT: uric acid stones, virtual unenhanced, iodine maps.
> - Enhancement ≥20 HU = solid; deep learning enables ~1 mSv CT.

## History of Laparoscopy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- Laparoscopy is endoscopic viewing of the peritoneal cavity through a pneumoperitoneum; it grew from optics to robotic surgery.

### Optics and access
| Year | Pioneer | Milestone |
|---|---|---|
| **1806** | **Bozzini** | **Lichtleiter**, candle-lit light conductor |
| 1877 | Nitze | Distal electric light and lenses |
| **1901** | **Kelling** | Insufflated abdomen of a **dog** |
| **1910** | **Jacobaeus** | First human series; "laparoscopy" |
| **1938** | **Veress** | Spring-loaded needle |
| **1966** | **Hopkins** | **Rod-lens system** |
| 1970s–80s | **Semm** | Automatic insufflator; operative laparoscopy |
| 1970s | Hasson | Open access |
| 1987 | Mouret | Video cholecystectomy |
- **Also know:** Desormeaux 1853, first therapeutic endoscopy; fibreoptics (Tyndall's principle) for flexible scopes; CO₂ became the standard insufflant.

### Urological milestones
| Year | Pioneer | Milestone |
|---|---|---|
| **1976** | **Cortesi** | Impalpable testis, first urological use |
| **1990–91** | **Clayman** | **First laparoscopic nephrectomy**; nephroureterectomy 1991 |
| **1992** | **Gaur (India)** | **Balloon retroperitoneal access** |
| **1995** | **Ratner** | Live-donor nephrectomy |
| 1997 | Schuessler | Laparoscopic radical prostatectomy (slow, not adopted) |
| 2000 | Guillonneau & Vallancien | Standardised prostatectomy |
| **2000** | **da Vinci**; **Binder** | Robot cleared in US; first robotic prostatectomy |
| 2000s | **Menon** | Vattikuti Institute Prostatectomy |
| 2007 | Raman; Rane | Single-port nephrectomy |

### Why the robot spread
- Laparoscopic prostatectomy stalled because intracorporeal suturing was hard. The robot gave wristed instruments and a 3D magnified view.
- da Vinci generations: S 2006 (fourth arm), Si 2009 (dual console), Xi 2014 (flexible docking), SP 2018 (single port).

### Indian contribution and recent advances
- Gaur's balloon made retroperitoneoscopy practical; indigenous robot SSI Mantra with telesurgery.
- New robots (Versius, Hugo, Hinotori), telesurgery, haptic feedback.

**Diagram to draw:** Timeline 1806 → 1901 → 1910 → 1938 → 1990 → 2000.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bozzini 1806; Kelling 1901 (dog); Jacobaeus 1910 (human); Veress 1938; Hopkins rod-lens.
> - Urology: Cortesi 1976; Clayman 1990; Gaur 1992; Ratner 1995.
> - da Vinci 2000; Binder first robotic prostatectomy; robot solved suturing barrier.

## Foley's (urethral) catheters, guidewires and DJ stents with their complications
**Asked as:**
- Foley's catheter. — *Summer 2016 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Urethral catheters, guidewires and DJ stents — *Practice Set 18 SAQ Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in part in 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3e and 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q1)
- Complications if DJS — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)* — *Taken as complications of a DJ (double-J) ureteric stent.*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Foley's / urethral catheters
#### History
- **Frederic Foley**, a Boston urologist, developed the modern balloon catheter (Campbell: 1929; published in the 1930s) for **haemostasis after prostatectomy**. Reybard (1855) had made the first self-retaining one.

#### Design
- **Size: Fr (French) = 3 × diameter in mm.** Adults (adult) usually 14–16 Fr; clot retention 20–24 Fr three-way. Newborn 6 Fr.
- Normal calibre: (male) meatus 24 Fr (narrowest point), bladder neck 28 Fr, prostatic urethra 32 Fr, female urethra 22 Fr.
- **Balloon:** usually 10 mL; larger (30 mL) for traction. Use (inflate balloon with) **sterile water**, only after urine flows at the hub; volume is printed on the port.
- **Lumens:** one (intermittent, specimen); two-way (drainage + balloon) is routine; three-way adds irrigation for haematuria (stiff wall resists collapse). Each lumen reduces (shrinks) drainage size.
- **Tips:** straight; **coudé** (curved, for prostatic enlargement, tip up); **Councill** (open tip, over a wire).
- **Materials:** latex (cheap, allergy); silicone (long term, less urethritis and encrustation; better urethral tolerance); PVC (polyvinyl chloride); coatings (silver, antibiotic) reduce bacteriuria but not clinical infection.
- **Special:** Malecot (winged, no balloon); Lapides diagnostic (rings 1 cm apart); urodynamic.

#### Indications and contraindications
- **Indications:** retention, clot retention, output monitoring, long surgery, immobility, after urological surgery.
- **Absolute contraindication:** suspected urethral injury (blood at meatus, pelvic fracture). Do an RUG (retrograde urethrogram) first (**suspected urethral injury → RUG first**).
- **Relative:** AUS (artificial urinary sphincter): deactivate, then 12–14 Fr; stricture.

#### Technique
- Aseptic; 10–15 mL lignocaine gel. In men, advance **to the hub** and see urine before inflating. Replace the foreskin.
- **Difficult:** 18 Fr coudé → guidewire → cystoscopy, Councill over wire → suprapubic catheter.

#### Complications
- **CAUTI (catheter-associated urinary tract infection):** 75% of hospital UTIs; bacteriuria 5–10% per catheter-day; **biofilm** forms within minutes.
- Trauma (about 0.3% of inpatients): false passage, balloon inflated in the urethra. Also stricture, traumatic hypospadias, encrustation, paraphimosis, non-deflating balloon (puncture with a wire).

#### CAUTI prevention
- Review daily and remove early; closed dependent system; clean meatus with water; culture only if symptomatic. Treat only symptomatic infection and **change the catheter**.

### Guidewires
| Feature | Details |
|---|---|
| Uses | Access, safety wire, straightening ureter, guiding dilation and stents |
| Construction | Stainless-steel core with outer wrap; **nitinol** core is kink-resistant |
| Coating | PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene): standard safety wire. **Hydrophilic** "glide": passes impacted stones, but too slippery to be a safety wire. Hybrid: hydrophilic tip, PTFE shaft |
| Size | 0.018–0.038 inch (commonly 0.035/0.038); 80–260 cm, 145 cm most useful; floppy tip 1–3 cm |
| Tip and stiffness | Straight, angled, J; standard to super-stiff (straightens tortuous ureter) |

### DJ (double-J) stents: introduction
- The **double-J (double-pigtail)** stent (Finney 1978) is a self-retaining polymer tube with coils in the pelvis and bladder; adult usually 6 Fr, length by height. Consent should cover its symptoms and complications.
- **Materials:** polyurethane, silicone (hydrocoated silicone less painful); **metallic (Resonance)** for extrinsic malignant compression.
- **Indications:** obstruction (infected obstructed kidney, malignancy, pregnancy), after URS (ureteroscopy) with injury, pyeloplasty, transplant, leak.
- **Placement:** retrograde pyelogram → wire → stent over wire under fluoroscopy or vision.
- **Problems:** stent symptoms (bladder irritation and reflux) eased by α-blockers and antimuscarinics; migration; encrustation (forgotten stent); failure with extrinsic compression.

#### Classification of DJ stent complications
| Timing | Complications |
|---|---|
| **At insertion** | **Misplacement**: proximal end below the obstruction; **submucosal** passage or perforation (suspect when force is needed) |
| **Early** | **Stent-related symptoms**; haematuria; infection and urosepsis; **vesicoureteric reflux** up the stent (flank pain on voiding) |
| **Late** | **Migration** (proximal or distal; short stent); **encrustation**; **stent failure** (obstruction); biofilm and colonisation; **fracture**; **forgotten stent**; rarely **ureteroarterial fistula** |

#### Stent-related symptoms
- About **4 in 5 patients**: urgency, frequency, dysuria, haematuria, flank and groin pain; sexual and work problems.
- **Mechanism:** the **intravesical coil irritates the trigone**, and **reflux up the stent** distends the kidney on voiding.
- Measured by **USSQ** (Ureteral Stent Symptom Questionnaire).
- **Reduced by:** **α-blockers and antimuscarinics**; intraureteral placement; hydrocoated silicone; shortest dwell. Diameter does not matter.

#### Encrustation and forgotten stent
- Minerals and biofilm deposit on the stent. Risk rises with **dwell time**, infection, stone-forming urine and pregnancy. Leads to obstruction, sepsis, fracture, **ureteric avulsion on removal**, renal loss.
- **FECal grading (Forgotten, Encrusted, Calcified):** I minimal linear; II circular; III partial on one or both coils; IV complete on one coil; V both coils.
- **Management:** non-contrast CT (computed tomography); never pull by force; clear **bladder coil** (cystolithotripsy), then **ureter** (ureteroscopy), then **renal coil** (flexible ureteroscopy or PCNL [percutaneous nephrolithotomy]).

#### Stent failure
- Extrinsic compression (malignancy, radiation), debris, encrustation. Obstructive symptoms → image; change stent, **PCN** (percutaneous nephrostomy) or **metallic stent**.

#### Prevention
- Stent only when indicated; correct length; **stent register**; long-term stents changed every 3–6 months (local practice); **pregnancy: about every 4 weeks**.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section of a three-way Foley; DJ stent in situ with bladder coil on the trigone and arrows showing reflux.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Foley balloon catheter: designed for post-prostatectomy haemostasis.
> - Fr = 3 × diameter (mm); inflate with sterile water; coudé for prostatic enlargement, Councill over a wire.
> - Suspected urethral injury → RUG first; AUS → deactivate, 12–14 Fr.
> - CAUTI 5–10% per day; remove early; change the catheter when treating.
> - Hydrophilic wire to pass, PTFE wire to stay (safety).
> - DJ symptoms in about 80%: trigonal irritation and reflux; α-blockers and antimuscarinics help.
> - Long-dwelling stents: monitor for encrustation; encrustation rises with dwell time; FECal I–V.
> - Encrusted stent: bladder, then ureter, then kidney; keep a stent register.

## Andrew Novick.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3f

*Assumed meaning: Andrew C. Novick and the urological work linked to his name, mainly nephron-sparing surgery.*

### Who he was
- **Andrew C. Novick** was a **Cleveland Clinic** urologist and long-time chairman of its urology department. He was a world authority on renal surgery, an editor of *Campbell-Walsh Urology* (9th edition), and died in 2008.

### Contributions
1. **Nephron-sparing surgery (NSS) / partial nephrectomy (PN):** he established its technique, indications and long-term cancer control. His group showed tumours ≤4 cm did as well with NSS as with radical nephrectomy, supporting the T1a/T1b split.
2. **Renovascular surgery:** aortorenal, splenorenal and hepatorenal bypass; **bench (ex vivo) surgery with autotransplantation** for complex lesions.
3. **Renal transplantation** surgery.

### Indications for PN
| Category | Situations |
|---|---|
| **Imperative** | Bilateral RCC (renal cell carcinoma); solitary functioning kidney |
| **Relative** | Contralateral kidney threatened (stones, stenosis, diabetes, hypertension); hereditary RCC |
| **Elective** | cT1a (≤4 cm) with a normal other kidney; now larger selected tumours |
- **Relative contraindications:** <20% of nephron mass left; pedicle encasement; venous thrombus.

### Principles of NSS
1. Three-dimensional CT (computed tomography) maps tumour, vessels and collecting system.
2. **Early hilar control.** Keep warm ischaemia under about 25–30 minutes; if longer, use **ice-slush hypothermia** and mannitol.
3. Excise with a **negative margin** (width immaterial); intraoperative ultrasound for endophytic tumours.
4. **Watertight collecting-system closure**, vessel ligation, capsular renorrhaphy.
5. Drain for urine leak.
- Function afterwards depends on parenchymal **quality and quantity preserved**; ischaemia matters less. At least 20–30% of one kidney is needed to avoid dialysis.

### Legacy
- PN is now the AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology) standard for cT1a tumours.

**Diagram to draw:** PN steps: clamp, ice slush, excision, closure, renorrhaphy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Novick (Cleveland Clinic): nephron-sparing, renovascular and bench surgery.
> - PN indications: imperative, relative, elective (cT1a ≤4 cm).
> - Warm ischaemia under about 25–30 minutes; ice slush if longer.
> - Negative margin is enough; keep ≥20–30% of one kidney.

## Proteinuria
**Asked as:**
- Proteinuria — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*
- Proteinuria — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q3f)

### Definition
- Normal protein excretion is **<150 mg/day** (another source: **80–150 mg/day**) (albumin <30 mg/day). The filter (glomerulus) blocks (holds back) albumin and the proximal tubule reabsorbs filtered small proteins.
- Normal mix: about 30% albumin, 30% globulins, 40% tissue proteins (Tamm–Horsfall).
- **Nephrotic range:** >3.5 g/day, with hypoalbuminaemia, oedema and hyperlipidaemia.

### Detection and quantification (KDIGO, Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes, categories by ACR)

| Category | ACR (albumin-to-creatinine ratio) | PCR (protein-to-creatinine ratio) | 24-h protein |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 normal–mild | <30 mg/g (<3 mg/mmol) | <150 mg/g (<15 mg/mmol) | <150 mg |
| A2 moderate | 30–300 mg/g | 150–500 mg/g | 150–500 mg |
| A3 severe | >300 mg/g (>30 mg/mmol) | >500 mg/g | >500 mg |
- **Spot early-morning ACR is first line**; PCR if non-albumin protein suspected; 24-h urine with creatinine check. Alternatives: PCR, 24-h urine.
- **Dipstick** (tetrabromophenol blue) detects albumin only (mainly albumin), from 20–30 mg/dL; misses dilute urine and **light chains**. **Sulfosalicylic acid** detects (precipitates) all proteins; dipstick negative but sulfosalicylic acid positive → suspect myeloma.

### Types

| Type | Mechanism | Amount | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Glomerular** (commonest) | Leaky filter | Often >2 g; albumin ≈70% (about 70%) | Diabetes, glomerulonephritis, FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis), amyloid |
| **Tubular** | Failed reabsorption of small proteins | <2 g (seldom >2–3 g); mainly globulins | Fanconi syndrome, heavy metals, drugs |
| **Overflow** | Excess plasma protein | Variable (0.3–2 g) | **Myeloma** (Bence Jones), myoglobin, haemoglobin |

| By timing | Features |
|---|---|
| **Transient** | Fever, exercise, heart failure; commonest in children |
| **Orthostatic** | Young men, upright only, <1 g; benign (normal first-morning sample). Test with a split (supine overnight vs upright day) collection |
| **Persistent** | Mostly glomerular; needs full work-up |

### Evaluation
```
Positive dipstick → repeat; exclude UTI (urinary tract infection), fever, exercise
→ persistent → quantify (ACR, PCR or 24-h protein)
→ creatinine/eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate), sediment (dysmorphic red cells / RBCs, casts), ultrasound
→ glomerular pattern (>2 g, albumin) → nephrology ± renal biopsy; C3, C4, ANA if systemic
→ globulins → electrophoresis, immunofixation (myeloma)
```
- UTI: urinary tract infection; eGFR: estimated glomerular filtration rate; RBC: red blood cell; ANA: antinuclear antibody.

### Urological relevance
- **Haematuria work-up:** proteinuria, casts or dysmorphic cells suggest (suggests) medical renal disease; urological bleeding rarely raises protein much (adds little protein).
- **Reflux nephropathy:** proteinuria marks progression to renal failure.
- **After nephrectomy or partial nephrectomy:** >50% loss of mass causes hyperfiltration, proteinuria and FSGS. AUA (American Urological Association) 2021 counts proteinuria as a reason to prefer (favour) nephron sparing and refer to nephrology.
- **Living donors:** significant proteinuria excludes donation. **Also know:** mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors cause it.

### Management
- Treat the cause; blood pressure control; **ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor or ARB (angiotensin receptor blocker)**; SGLT2 (sodium–glucose cotransporter-2) inhibitors.

**Diagram to draw:** glomerulus and proximal tubule marking glomerular, tubular and overflow sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Normal <150 mg/day; nephrotic >3.5 g/day.
> - ACR A2 30–300, A3 >300 mg/g.
> - Glomerular, tubular, overflow; orthostatic proteinuria is benign.
> - Dipstick misses light chains; use sulfosalicylic acid or immunofixation.

## Acetylcysteine and Contrast Induced Nephropathy (CIN)
**Asked as:**
- Acetylcysteine. — *Summer 2018 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*
- Acetylcysteine — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Contrast Induced Nephropathy (CIN) — *Winter 2017 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Answered in 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3b; core answer repeated here.* (Summer 2019 Paper I Q3e)

### Acetylcysteine

#### Introduction
- **NAC (N-acetylcysteine):** acetylated form of the amino acid cysteine (acetylated cysteine); a **thiol (sulfhydryl, –SH) compound**. Given orally, intravenously or by nebuliser (oral, intravenous or nebulised).

#### Mechanisms of action
- **Glutathione precursor:** supplies cysteine, the rate-limiting substrate for **glutathione** synthesis.
- **Antioxidant:** the free –SH group scavenges reactive oxygen species.
