- **Mucolytic:** –SH breaks **disulfide bonds** in mucoproteins, lowering mucus viscosity.
- **Thiol–disulfide exchange:** theoretically (in theory) forms a soluble mixed disulfide with cystine.

#### Clinical uses
**1. Paracetamol (acetaminophen) overdose: main indication**
- Overdose saturates normal pathways; the toxic metabolite **NAPQI** (N-acetyl-p-benzoquinone imine) accumulates (builds up) once glutathione is used up (exhausted), causing hepatic (liver) necrosis.
- NAC restores glutathione. It is most effective **within 8 hours**, but still worth giving later (still given later).
- **IV (intravenous) 21-hour regimen:** 150 mg/kg, then 50 mg/kg over 4 h, then 100 mg/kg over 16 h. Shorter two-bag regimens are now used in some countries.

**2. Mucolytic:** COPD (chronic obstructive pulmonary disease), cystic fibrosis.

#### Urological relevance
- **Contrast-induced AKI (acute kidney injury):** once widely used (early small trials, KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2012 suggestion). **PRESERVE (2018):** NAC gave no benefit over saline. It is **no longer recommended**; isotonic saline hydration is the only proven measure.
- **Cystinuria:** thiol action is theoretical; standard thiols are **tiopronin** and D-penicillamine, not NAC.
- **Cyclophosphamide cystitis:** **mesna** (also a thiol; another thiol) is the agent used.
- **Mucus after enterocystoplasty or neobladder:** sometimes used orally or in irrigation; evidence weak.
- **Male infertility:** antioxidant for oxidative stress (high ROS (reactive oxygen species), sperm DNA fragmentation); limited evidence.
- **Ischaemia–reperfusion** (renal surgery, transplant): antioxidant protection studied; not established.

#### Adverse effects
- **Anaphylactoid reactions** with IV loading (flushing, rash, bronchospasm): slow the infusion, give antihistamine.
- Nausea, vomiting; unpleasant sulphur smell; bronchospasm when nebulised in asthma.
- Safe in pregnancy and renal impairment for paracetamol poisoning; benefit outweighs risk.

**Diagram to draw:** Paracetamol → NAPQI → glutathione conjugation, with NAC replenishing glutathione.

### Contrast Induced Nephropathy (CIN)

#### Definitions
- **CIN (contrast-induced nephropathy)** (older term): creatinine rise ≥25% or ≥0.5 mg/dL within 48–72 hours of iodinated contrast, with no other cause.
- Current terms (ACR (American College of Radiology)):
  - **CA-AKI (contrast-associated acute kidney injury):** AKI within 48 hours of contrast, whatever the cause.
  - **CI-AKI (contrast-induced AKI):** the subset actually caused by contrast.
- **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) AKI:** creatinine rise ≥0.3 mg/dL in 48 hours, ≥1.5 × baseline in 7 days, or urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 hours.

#### Pathophysiology
- **Renal vasoconstriction** causes **medullary hypoxia** and ischaemic tubular injury, plus **direct tubular toxicity**. Usually transient; dialysis rarely needed.

#### Risk factors
- **Patient:** CKD (chronic kidney disease, strongest), diabetes, age, dehydration, heart failure, anaemia, NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs).
- **Procedure:** high volume, repeated doses within 48–72 hours, intra-arterial route, high-osmolar agents.
- **eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) thresholds:** real risk mainly at **eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²** (IV (intravenous) contrast); ESUR (European Society of Urogenital Radiology) uses **<45** for intra-arterial first-pass exposure.
- Older uncontrolled studies overstated the risk; ACR: rare but real.

#### Prevention
- Consider ultrasound or non-contrast CT (computed tomography) instead.
- Lowest dose of **low- or iso-osmolar** agent; low kVp (80); avoid repeat doses within 48 hours; stop NSAIDs.
- **IV isotonic saline** (e.g. 1 mL/kg/h before and after) for high-risk patients (eGFR <30 not on dialysis): the only proven measure.
- **PRESERVE (2018):** sodium bicarbonate and **NAC (N-acetylcysteine)** gave no benefit over saline.
- **AMACING (2017):** no prophylaxis was non-inferior to hydration at eGFR 30–59.
- Prophylactic dialysis does not help.

#### Metformin
- Risk is lactic acidosis if AKI develops. **eGFR ≥30 and no AKI: continue.** eGFR <30 or AKI: **withhold for 48 hours**, restart when creatinine is stable.

**Diagram to draw:** contrast → vasoconstriction + tubular toxicity → medullary hypoxia → AKI.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Thiol; glutathione precursor, antioxidant, mucolytic.
> - Antidote for paracetamol overdose; best within 8 h.
> - PRESERVE 2018: no benefit in contrast AKI.
> - Cystinuria uses tiopronin, not NAC.
> - CIN = rise ≥25% or 0.5 mg/dL in 48–72 h; now CA-AKI vs CI-AKI.
> - Main risk: eGFR <30 (IV); <45 intra-arterial.
> - Isotonic saline only proven; PRESERVE: bicarbonate and NAC useless.
> - Metformin: withhold 48 h only if eGFR <30 or AKI.

## Non iodinated contrast materials
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3a

### Introduction
- Used when iodinated contrast is risky (allergy, renal impairment) or with MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) and ultrasound.

### 1. Gadolinium-based contrast agents (GBCAs)
- **Mechanism:** paramagnetic gadolinium **shortens T1 relaxation** of nearby water → bright enhancement. Free gadolinium is toxic, so it is **chelated** (linear or macrocyclic; macrocyclic is more stable). Usual dose 0.1 mmol/kg; renal excretion.
- **NSF (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis):** skin and organ fibrosis, joint contractures, no proven treatment. Risk: eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <30 mL/min/1.73 m², dialysis, AKI (acute kidney injury).

| ACR (American College of Radiology) group | Agents | NSF risk |
|---|---|---|
| I (linear) | Gadodiamide, gadopentetate, gadoversetamide | Highest |
| **II** | Gadobenate, gadoteridol, gadoterate, gadobutrol | **Little or none, even in ESRD (end-stage renal disease)** |
| III | Gadoxetate (liver-specific agent) | Few NSF data; recent ACR editions have moved some agents between groups, so check the current manual |

- **Gadolinium deposition:** traces retained in brain (dentate nucleus) and bone, mainly with linear agents; no proven harm. Europe suspended most linear agents (2017).
- **Other:** allergic reactions rarer than with iodinated agents; not nephrotoxic at usual doses; avoid in pregnancy unless essential.
- **Uses:** renal mass, prostate mpMRI (multiparametric MRI), MR urography and angiography.

### 2. Ultrasound contrast (microbubbles)
- Gas core (perfluorocarbon or sulfur hexafluoride) in a lipid or albumin shell; strong harmonic echoes.
- Purely **intravascular**; gas exhaled by lungs. **No nephrotoxicity or radiation.** Rare cardiopulmonary reactions.
- **CEUS (contrast-enhanced ultrasound):** solid vs cystic renal mass (Bosniak), ablation monitoring, surveillance in CKD (chronic kidney disease). Intravesical use: **voiding urosonography for VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)**; ureteric patency after PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy).

### 3. Other agents
- **CO₂ angiography:** negative contrast; not allergenic or nephrotoxic. Useful in CKD; **never above the diaphragm** (cerebral embolism).
- **Ferumoxytol** (iron oxide): MRI agent in renal failure. Lymphotropic iron nanoparticles help nodal staging (nodal metastases fail to darken).
- **Barium:** GI (gastrointestinal) studies only; contraindicated if perforation suspected.
- **Also know:** air as negative contrast (historical); radiotracers (⁹⁹ᵐTc, PET (positron emission tomography)) are not contrast agents; MRI safety (pacemakers, metal).

**Diagram to draw:** Chelated gadolinium (macrocyclic vs linear ring).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - GBCAs shorten T1; NSF risk eGFR <30; group II agents almost no risk.
> - Microbubbles: intravascular, no nephrotoxicity; ceVUS for reflux.
> - CO₂ angiography: never above diaphragm.

## Health-related quality of life (HRQOL) instruments in urology, including after urinary diversion
**Asked as:**
- Health related quality of life instruments (HRQOL) in urology. — *Summer 2018 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*
- Assessment of quality of life after urinary diversion — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- **HRQOL (HRQoL, health-related quality of life):** the parts of life affected by health: physical, emotional and social function and the patient's bother; the patient's view of how disease and treatment affect physical, emotional and social function, plus **bother**. It differs from a **symptom score**, which only counts symptoms.
- Must be **patient-reported**; doctors underestimate dysfunction (urinary, sexual and bowel dysfunction).

### Types of instruments
| Type | Measures | Examples | Limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Generic** | Domains common to all diseases; allow comparison across diseases | **SF-36** (Short Form-36), SF-12, **EQ-5D** (EuroQol five-dimension; 5 items + VAS (visual analogue scale); gives utilities for QALYs (quality-adjusted life-years)), Sickness Impact Profile | Insensitive to diversion-specific issues |
| **Cancer-specific** | Cancer effects | **FACT-G** (Functional Assessment of Cancer Therapy–General), **EORTC QLQ-C30** (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer core questionnaire) | General cancer domains |
| **Disease-specific** | Domains of one disease; more responsive | See below | |
| **Bladder-cancer-specific** | | **FACT-Bl** (40 items), **EORTC QLQ-BLM30** (muscle-invasive module, 30 items), **BCI (Bladder Cancer Index**, 34 items: urinary, bowel, sexual domains), FACT-VCI (Vanderbilt Cystectomy Index) | Weight symptoms of one diversion, so biased in cross-diversion comparisons |
| **Neobladder-specific** | | IONB-PRO (23 items) | Not usable across diversions |

### Urology-specific instruments
- **Prostate cancer:** **EPIC (Expanded Prostate Cancer Index Composite)**, EPIC-26, UCLA (University of California, Los Angeles) Prostate Cancer Index, FACT-P.
- **Bladder cancer:** FACT-Bl, **Bladder Cancer Index**, EORTC QLQ-BLM30 and NMIBC-24, FACT-Vanderbilt Cystectomy Index.
- **Kidney cancer:** FKSI (FACT–Kidney Symptom Index).
- **LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms):** **IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score)** 7 items + 1 quality-of-life item; BPH Impact Index.
- **Incontinence and OAB (overactive bladder):** **ICIQ** (International Consultation on Incontinence Questionnaire), **OAB-q**, King's Health Questionnaire, I-QOL.
- **Sexual function:** **IIEF** (International Index of Erectile Function; 15 items), SHIM (5 items; no bother, so not true HRQOL), FSFI (Female Sexual Function Index).
- **Others:** NIH-CPSI (chronic prostatitis), O'Leary–Sant (interstitial cystitis), Wisconsin Stone QOL, USSQ (ureteric stent).
- **PROMIS (Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System):** free item banks, **computer-adaptive testing**, common T-score.

### Psychometric properties (choosing a tool)
- A good tool is **reliable** (Cronbach α >0.7), **valid** in that population and **responsive** to change.

| Property | Meaning | Threshold |
|---|---|---|
| Internal consistency | Items agree | Cronbach α >0.7 (>0.9 = redundant) |
| Test–retest reliability | Stable over time | r >0.7 |
| Content (face) validity | Right items present | Expert judgement |
| Criterion validity | Agrees with gold standard | r ≥0.7 |
| Construct validity | Convergent and divergent | Correlations |
| **Responsiveness** | Detects real change | **MCID (minimal clinically important difference)**: AUA (American Urological Association) Symptom Index 3.1 points; otherwise ≥½ SD |

- Validity is population-specific; translations need revalidation.
- Development: define domains → generate items → pilot → psychometric testing (Likert scales).

### Quality of life after urinary diversion
#### Domains to assess
- Urinary: continence, catheterisation, stoma problems.
- **Body image**, sexual function, bowel function, social and emotional function, global HRQoL.

#### Evidence: ileal conduit versus neobladder
- **No diversion proven superior.** Studies are mostly cross-sectional and retrospective, without baseline data or randomisation.
- Verdict depends on instrument: generic tools mostly show **no difference** (largest group of studies); EORTC tools tend to **favour neobladder**; the urinary-focused BCI **penalises neobladder** for leakage.
- **Consistent signal:** body image favours neobladder; urinary function favours conduit.
- Large prospective cohort: global HRQoL exceeded baseline in all diversions by 6 months.
- Neobladder: daytime continence improves over 6–12 months; **night-time incontinence** (up to 50%) is the main bother.
- **Also know:** informed patients have less decision regret; most adapt to their diversion; PROMIS (Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System).

#### Practical approach
- Baseline and serial validated questionnaires (3, 6, 12 months), a disease-specific plus a generic tool, and realistic counselling.

**Diagram to draw:** Pyramid: symptoms → function → bother → overall HRQOL. Bar sketch: body image (neobladder better) versus urinary function (conduit better).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Generic (SF-36, EQ-5D) vs disease-specific (EPIC, IPSS, ICIQ, IIEF) plus bladder-specific (FACT-Bl, BLM30, BCI) tools.
> - SHIM lacks bother; I-QOL lacks symptoms.
> - Reliable, valid, responsive; Cronbach α >0.7; IPSS MCID ~3 points.
> - No diversion proven superior; studies flawed.
> - Neobladder better body image; conduit better urinary function.

## PET scans in urology.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3e

### Principle
- **PET (positron emission tomography):** tracer emits a positron → annihilation with an electron → **two 511-keV photons at 180°** → coincidence detection. Fused with CT (computed tomography) or MRI.
- Quantitative (**SUV, standardised uptake value**); costly. Half-lives: ¹⁸F 110 min, ⁶⁸Ga 68 min, ¹¹C 20 min (on-site cyclotron).

### Tracers
| Tracer | Mechanism | Main use |
|---|---|---|
| **¹⁸F-FDG (fluorodeoxyglucose)** | Glucose analogue trapped after phosphorylation (Warburg effect) | Bladder, UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma), penile, seminoma |
| **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen)** ligands (⁶⁸Ga-PSMA-11, ¹⁸F-DCFPyL, ¹⁸F-PSMA-1007, rhPSMA-7.3) | Bind cell-surface PSMA | Prostate staging and recurrence |
| ¹¹C/¹⁸F-choline | Cell membrane synthesis | Prostate recurrence (older) |
| ¹⁸F-fluciclovine | Amino acid transport | Prostate recurrence |
| ¹⁸F-NaF (sodium fluoride) | Bone turnover | Bone metastases |

### Uses by organ
**Prostate cancer**
- FDG is poor (low glucose metabolism). PSMA has specificity >90%.
- **Primary staging (high risk):** **proPSMA (2020)**: PSMA PET/CT more accurate than CT plus bone scan (92% vs 65%). Nodal sensitivity only 30–40%, so a negative scan does not exclude micrometastases.
- **BCR (biochemical recurrence):** detection rises with PSA (prostate-specific antigen); about half positive below 0.5 ng/mL. PSMA beats fluciclovine (56% vs 26%). CONDOR: management changed in 63.9%.
- **Theranostics:** PSMA-positive mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer) → **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 (VISION 2021)**.
- NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): PSMA PET preferred; outcome benefit still unproven.

**Testis**
- **Seminoma residual mass >3 cm:** FDG-PET **≥6 weeks after chemotherapy**. Negative scan reliable (observe); positive → consider resection.
- **Not for NSGCT (non-seminomatous germ cell tumour):** teratoma is not FDG-avid.

**Bladder and UTUC**
- FDG for nodal and distant staging in MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer): modest sensitivity, high specificity; urinary excretion hides the primary. Use when CT is equivocal.

**Kidney**
- FDG not routine (clear cell RCC (renal cell carcinoma) poorly avid). **⁸⁹Zr-girentuximab** (anti-CAIX, carbonic anhydrase IX) identifies clear cell RCC (ZIRCON).

**Penis**
- FDG confirms metastases in **palpable inguinal nodes**; poor for impalpable nodes.

- **Also know:** EMPIRE-1 (fluciclovine-guided salvage radiotherapy); false positives from inflammation; neuroendocrine cancer loses PSMA.

**Diagram to draw:** Positron annihilation with two 511-keV photons hitting a detector ring.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - proPSMA: PSMA PET 92% vs 65% accuracy.
> - Seminoma residual >3 cm: FDG-PET at ≥6 weeks; not NSGCT.
> - FDG poor in prostate and clear cell RCC.
> - VISION: ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 in mCRPC.

## Bacteriuria in pregnancy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3b
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3b)

### Definition
- **ASB (asymptomatic bacteriuria):** ≥10⁵ CFU (colony-forming units)/mL of one uropathogen in a midstream sample without urinary symptoms (≥10² CFU/mL on a catheter sample).

### Epidemiology
- Prevalence is **not raised** by pregnancy (about 2–10%); higher with low socioeconomic status, multiparity, sickle cell trait. Organisms as in non-pregnant women (mainly *E. coli*).

### Why it matters (pathophysiology)
- **Progesterone** relaxes ureteric smooth muscle; the **uterus compresses the ureters at the pelvic brim** (right more). Dilatation from about 15 weeks; normal by 6 weeks postpartum.
- Result: **stasis and hydroureteronephrosis**; bacteriuria persists and ascends.
- GFR (glomerular filtration rate) rises 30–50%: creatinine and drug levels fall.
- **Untreated ASB → pyelonephritis in 15–45%** (historical), 60–75% in the third trimester. Treatment cuts this to **1–4%**.
- Pyelonephritis causes **preterm birth, low birth weight** and maternal sepsis. **Cochrane 2019:** treating ASB reduces all three.

### Screening
- **Urine culture at 12–16 weeks** or the first visit (USPSTF [US Preventive Services Task Force], IDSA [Infectious Diseases Society of America]). Dipstick misses many cases.

### Treatment
| Safe | Restricted or avoided |
|---|---|
| **β-lactams** (amoxicillin, cephalexin): throughout | **Nitrofurantoin:** stop from 35 weeks (neonatal haemolysis, G6PD [glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase] deficiency); not for pyelonephritis |
| Fosfomycin: lower tract only | **Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole:** avoid 1st trimester (folate, neural tube); caution near term (kernicterus) |
| Gentamicin: pyelonephritis | **Fluoroquinolones**, **tetracyclines**, chloramphenicol: avoid |
- ASB and cystitis: culture-directed, **4–7 days** (cures more than a single dose); **repeat culture** after treatment.

### Pyelonephritis
- **Admit**; IV (intravenous) β-lactam (± gentamicin) until afebrile, then oral to complete ≥14 days. No improvement by **72 h → ultrasound** (stone, obstruction, abscess).

### Prophylaxis
- After one pyelonephritis or two cystitis episodes: nightly nitrofurantoin or cephalexin until delivery, or close culture surveillance (Cochrane 2015).
- **Also know:** urinary diversion or augmentation, SCI (spinal cord injury): most treat ASB in pregnancy.

**Diagram to draw:** right hydroureter compressed at the pelvic brim.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prevalence unchanged; risk of pyelonephritis raised (15–45% untreated).
> - Screen by culture at 12–16 weeks; treat 4–7 days.
> - β-lactams safe; no fluoroquinolones or tetracyclines; nitrofurantoin not after 35 weeks.

## Recist Criteria.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition and evolution
- **RECIST (Response Evaluation Criteria In Solid Tumours):** standard imaging rules for tumour response and progression.
- **WHO (World Health Organization) 1981** (bidimensional) → **RECIST 2000** (unidimensional) → **RECIST 1.1 (2009)**, now standard. Used in trials for metastatic RCC (renal cell carcinoma), urothelial and prostate cancer.

### Lesions at baseline (RECIST 1.1)
| Category | Definition |
|---|---|
| Measurable lesion | Longest diameter **≥10 mm on CT (computed tomography)**, ≥20 mm on chest X-ray, ≥10 mm with callipers |
| Measurable node | **≥15 mm short axis**; 10–15 mm pathological but non-measurable; <10 mm normal |
| Non-measurable | Small lesions, blastic bone metastases, effusions, ascites |
| Target lesions | Up to **5 (maximum 2 per organ)**; largest, reproducible; **sum of diameters** recorded |
| Non-target | All other lesions, recorded as present or absent |

### Response categories
| Response | Target lesions |
|---|---|
| **CR** (complete response) | All disappear; nodes <10 mm |
| **PR** (partial response) | **≥30% fall** in sum versus **baseline** |
| **PD** (progressive disease) | **≥20% rise** versus **nadir** and **≥5 mm absolute**, or any **new lesion** |
| **SD** (stable disease) | Neither PR nor PD |

- **Overall response** combines target, non-target and new lesions; confirm response at ≥4 weeks in single-arm trials.
- **End points:** ORR (objective response rate = CR + PR), PFS (progression-free survival), duration of response.

### Limitations
- Size only, ignoring necrosis (TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors)); bone poorly assessed; observer variability.

### Modifications
| Criteria | Use |
|---|---|
| **iRECIST (2017)** | Immunotherapy: first progression is "unconfirmed", confirmed only on the next scan, allowing for **pseudoprogression** |
| **Choi** | Density fall counts as response |
| **PCWG3 (Prostate Cancer Working Group 3)** | Bone: 12-week scan is the new baseline (flare), then **≥2 new lesions** = progression; PSA (prostate-specific antigen) progression ≥25% and ≥2 ng/mL above nadir; soft tissue by RECIST |
| PERCIST | PET (positron emission tomography) metabolic response |

**Diagram to draw:** number line of change in sum: −30% (PR), +20% (PD).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RECIST 1.1: ≥10 mm lesion, node ≥15 mm short axis; 5 targets, 2 per organ.
> - PR ≥30% fall from baseline; PD ≥20% and ≥5 mm rise from nadir, or new lesion.
> - iRECIST for immunotherapy; PCWG3 for prostate.

## Outline algorithm for difficult catheter placement.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- **Normal calibre:** meatus 24 Fr (narrowest), bladder neck 28 Fr, prostatic urethra 32 Fr.
- **Causes:** BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia, commonest), urethral stricture, false passage, meatal stenosis, bladder neck contracture, urethral trauma, abnormal anatomy.
- **Golden rule:** escalate **blind → wire → vision → suprapubic**; never repeat forceful blind passage.

### Before starting
- History (surgery, radiotherapy, AUS (artificial urinary sphincter), reconstruction), DRE (digital rectal examination).
- **Absolute contraindication:** suspected urethral injury (pelvic fracture, blood at meatus, high-riding prostate) → **RUG (retrograde urethrogram)**; pelvic fracture urethral injury → suprapubic tube.
- **Technique:** 10–15 mL lubricant (lidocaine gel), penis stretched, steady pressure; inflate only after urine flows at the hub.

### Algorithm (Campbell Fig. 15.6)
```
First attempt fails
 ├─ Prostatic obstruction suspected
 │     → 18-Fr coudé, tip (marker) pointing up
 │     → fails → flexible cystoscopy
 └─ Not suspected (stricture likely)
       → smaller catheter
       → fails → hydrophilic glidewire
             → fails → flexible cystoscopy
At cystoscopy / with wire in bladder:
 ├─ Stricture → wire across → 5-Fr catheter, aspirate urine to confirm
 │      → super-stiff wire → serial dilatation (1 Fr above catheter size)
 │      → Councill (open-tip) catheter over wire
 └─ False passage → pass under vision (scope anterior)
        → wire → Councill over wire
Wire cannot be passed / dense stricture / urethral disruption
 → suprapubic catheter (ultrasound-guided percutaneous, or open)
```
- **Why 18-Fr coudé:** stiffer, larger tip area, curved tip follows the bulbar-prostatic angle; 14–16 Fr usually buckle.
- **Blood on the tip** suggests false passage: stop blind attempts.

### Special situations
| Situation | Approach |
|---|---|
| AUS | Deactivate; smallest catheter 12–14 Fr; remove early |
| Obese or female | Exposure: Trendelenburg, retract pannus, two fingers in vagina to guide |
| Buried penis | Infrapubic pressure; flexible cystoscopy |
| Neobladder, continent pouch | Never force; scope + wire; suprapubic only with imaging guidance |
| PUV (posterior urethral valves) neonate | 6–8 Fr, or 5-Fr open-ended over glidewire; else suprapubic or vesicostomy |

**Also know:** filiforms, balloon dilators; complications: false passage, haematuria, stricture.

**Diagram to draw:** coudé catheter following the bulbar-prostatic curve with tip up.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blind → wire → vision → suprapubic.
> - BPH: 18-Fr coudé, tip up.
> - Councill catheter over wire after dilatation.
> - Suspected urethral injury: RUG first, no blind catheter.

## Indications for outpatient cystourethroscopy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3e

### Introduction
- **Flexible cystourethroscopy** under local anaesthesia is the **commonest office procedure** in urology; no lithotomy needed.
- **Prerequisites:** consent; treat any UTI (urinary tract infection) first; lidocaine gel with **≥5 minutes dwell** (ideally 10–20); no antibiotic prophylaxis unless risk factors (age, diabetes, immunosuppression, catheters, prior UTI).

### Diagnostic indications
| Indication | Comment |
|---|---|
| **Haematuria** | All **gross haematuria** (with CT (computed tomography) urography, cytology). Microhaematuria by AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) risk: intermediate and high risk; shared decision in low risk |
| **Bladder cancer surveillance** | NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer): first check at 3 months; low risk then yearly; high risk 3–4-monthly for 2 years; also upper-tract follow-up of UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) |
| Recurrent UTI | Look for stone, diverticulum, stricture, fistula, foreign body |
| LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) | Not routine; before invasive BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) treatment (median lobe before urethral lift, bladder-neck contracture, stricture) |
| Pain syndromes | IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome): **Hunner lesion**; chronic pelvic pain with bladder pain or low flow |
| Women | Suspected urethral diverticulum, vesicovaginal fistula, mesh or suture erosion after sling, recurrent UTI after surgery |
| Others | Urethral stricture assessment and follow-up; abnormal urine cytology; radiation cystitis; foreign body |

### Therapeutic indications
- **Removal or exchange of ureteric stents** (exchange with fluoroscopy).
- **Biopsy and fulguration** of small (≤1 cm) low-grade recurrences; office laser ablation.
- **Intradetrusor botulinum toxin** injection (OAB (overactive bladder), neurogenic detrusor overactivity).
- Urethral bulking agents; stricture dilatation; small stones or foreign bodies; blue-light cystoscopy where available.

### When not indicated or done in theatre
- Not needed for the index stress incontinence patient, initial OAB or uncomplicated LUTS.
- **No surveillance cystoscopy** in chronic indwelling catheters (AUA/SUFU 2021).
- Theatre if TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) needed, poor tolerance, children, or complex diversions.

**Diagram to draw:** haematuria risk-group flowchart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Haematuria and NMIBC surveillance are the two commonest indications.
> - Flexible scope, lidocaine dwell ≥5 minutes, no routine prophylaxis.
> - Therapeutic: stent removal, botulinum toxin, fulguration of small recurrences.

## MR urography
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- MRU (magnetic resonance urography): magnetic resonance imaging of kidneys, ureters and bladder giving anatomy and function **without ionising radiation**.

### Techniques

| | Static MRU | Excretory MRU |
|---|---|---|
| Sequence | Heavily T2-weighted; urine is bright | Gadolinium-enhanced T1 |
| Contrast | None | GBCA (gadolinium-based contrast agent) |
| Depends on function | No | Yes; low-dose furosemide improves it |
| Best for | Dilated or obstructed system | Non-dilated system, excretion |

- **Functional MRU (children):** hydration, bladder catheter, furosemide 1 mg/kg (maximum 20 mg), T2 images, then dynamic T1 after slow contrast injection. Patlak analysis gives differential function, GFR (glomerular filtration rate) and drainage.

### Appearances
- Stones and calcification give **signal voids**; tumour, clot, gas and sloughed papillae are low-signal defects in bright urine.

### Indications
- CT (computed tomography) urography contraindicated: contrast allergy, renal insufficiency, **pregnancy** (separates physiological from calculous dilatation; small stones missed).
- **Children:** complex anomalies, ectopic ureter in the incontinent girl, duplex systems, megaureter, crossing vessels in UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) obstruction, ectopic kidneys.
- Hydronephrosis: alternative to MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renography; equivalence not yet proven.
- Upper tract tumours and renal masses.

### Advantages and limitations

| Advantages | Limitations |
|---|---|
| No radiation; best anatomical detail | Cost, availability, long study (up to 1 hour) |
| Anatomy, function and drainage in one test | Sedation often needed under 6 years |
| As accurate as CTU (CT urography) for obstruction | Poor for stones; complex post-processing |
| Safe in iodinated contrast allergy | Pacemakers, metal implants, claustrophobia |

### Gadolinium safety
- **NSF (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis)** was the concern at GFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m². Group II agents (gadobenate, gadoteridol, gadoterate, gadobutrol) carry little or no risk even in end-stage disease.
- **Pregnancy:** gadolinium only if it clearly helps.
- **EAU (European Association of Urology):** CTU is preferred to MRU for upper tract urothelial cancer.

**Diagram to draw:** coronal static T2 MRU of a hydronephrotic kidney with a dark stone in the ureter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Static T2 (no contrast, function-independent) versus excretory gadolinium T1.
> - Stones are signal voids; CTU preferred for urothelial cancer.
> - Functional MRU: furosemide 1 mg/kg, Patlak analysis for split function.
> - NSF risk minimal with group II agents.

## Urine cytology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3b

### Definition and principle
- Microscopy of shed urothelial cells (Papanicolaou, 1945).
- **Principle:** high-grade cells are poorly cohesive, shed readily and look atypical; low-grade cells look almost normal. So cytology detects **high-grade tumours and CIS (carcinoma in situ)** but misses low grade.

### Specimens
- Voided urine (not the first morning sample, whose cells have degenerated), at least 30 mL; bladder washing; upper tract washings.

### Performance
- Sensitivity 31–62%, specificity 94–100%; strongly grade-dependent.
- **False positives:** instrumentation, stones, infection, radiotherapy, intravesical BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin), polyomavirus ("decoy" cells), ileal conduit.
- **False negatives:** low grade, low cellularity, degeneration.

### The Paris System
- Published 2016; **2nd edition 2022**. Aim: detect **HGUC (high-grade urothelial carcinoma)**. Key criterion: **N:C (nuclear-to-cytoplasmic) ratio**, plus hyperchromasia, coarse chromatin and irregular nuclear membrane.

| Category | Criteria |
|---|---|
| Non-diagnostic | Inadequate |
| Negative for HGUC | N:C <0.5, or atypia explained by a benign cause |
| AUC (atypical urothelial cells) | N:C ≥0.5 and one feature |
| Suspicious for HGUC | N:C ≥0.7, at least two features, few cells (<5–10) |
| HGUC | N:C ≥0.7, at least two features, many cells |
| LGUN (low-grade urothelial neoplasm) | Fibrovascular cores; rarely diagnosable |
| Other malignancy | Squamous, adenocarcinoma, secondary |

- It reduces equivocal "atypia" reports; cancer risk rises with each category.

### Clinical uses
- **Not** for screening or initial microhaematuria evaluation (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association / Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction] 2020).
- Gross haematuria; NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) surveillance (mandatory in high risk); suspected CIS; upper tract tumours.
- **Positive cytology, normal cystoscopy:** image upper tracts, selective washings ± ureteroscopy, random bladder and prostatic urethral biopsies.
- **Also know:** FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation; chromosomes 3, 7, 17, 9p21), NMP22 (nuclear matrix protein 22), BTA (bladder tumour antigen), CxBladder: more sensitive, less specific; none replaces cystoscopy.

**Diagram to draw:** normal urothelial cell beside an HGUC cell with high N:C ratio.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - High specificity, low sensitivity; good for high grade and CIS.
> - Paris System: N:C ≥0.7 for suspicious and HGUC; AUC N:C ≥0.5.
> - Positive cytology, normal cystoscopy: upper tracts, random biopsies.
> - Not for screening or initial microhaematuria.

## Patient positioning in lap urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3a
(pneumoperitoneum physiology also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q2)

### Principles
- Positioning uses **gravity** to move bowel away and gives port or robot access while keeping ventilation safe.
- Injury comes from **stretch, compression or ischaemia**, so position and duration act together; nerve injury occurs in about 2.8%. Shared team responsibility (ASA [American Society of Anesthesiologists] advisory, 2000).
- Secure IV (intravenous) access, oximetry and BP (blood pressure) cuff before final positioning: tucked arms become inaccessible.

### Positions

| Position | Used for | Set-up | Hazards |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Steep Trendelenburg**, low lithotomy or split legs | RARP (robot-assisted radical prostatectomy), cystectomy | Arms tucked and padded; chest strap over egg-crate; **no shoulder braces**; tilt about 22–30° | Brachial plexus; femoral nerve (excess hip flexion); facial and laryngeal oedema; corneal abrasion; tube into right bronchus |
| **Modified flank** (30–45°) | Transperitoneal nephrectomy, pyeloplasty | Back on gel rolls; upper arm across chest; wide tape; tilt to test | Pressure points, slippage |
| **Full lateral (90°)**, table flexed | Retroperitoneoscopy, adrenalectomy | **Axillary roll** 3–4 fingerbreadths below axilla; lower leg flexed 45°, upper straight on pillows | **Rhabdomyolysis**; sciatic stretch |
| **Prone** | Posterior adrenalectomy | Pad torso, elbows, hips | Airway, face and eyes |

### Rhabdomyolysis
- About 0.4% (1% retroperitoneal). Risk: men, BMI (body mass index) ≥25, **kidney rest**, full flexion, long cases. Downside hip pain, brown urine, CK (creatine kinase) >5,000 units. Prevent: limit kidney rest (Campbell gives 20–30 minutes in one place, under 1 hour in another), avoid hypotension, keep cases under 5 hours.

### Prevention and physiology
- Abduction ≤90°; pad ulnar groove and fibular head; recheck after every table move.
- **Head-down** raises cardiac output but splints the diaphragm; restrict fluids.
- Nerve injury on day 1: neurology review, conduction studies, physiotherapy.
- **Also know:** forearm compartment syndrome from infiltrated IV lines; Xi side-docking avoids stirrups.

**Diagram to draw:** full flank position with axillary roll and table break.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Nerve injury about 2.8%; brachial plexus is commonest at risk.
> - Never shoulder braces in steep Trendelenburg.
> - Axillary roll below, not in, the axilla.
> - Rhabdomyolysis: men, BMI ≥25, kidney rest; CK >5,000.

## Clinical trial methodology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- A **clinical trial** is a prospective study that assigns people to an intervention to test its effect on health outcomes. The **RCT (randomised controlled trial)** is the gold standard because randomisation balances known and unknown confounders.

### Phases
| Phase | Purpose | Subjects |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Microdose pharmacokinetics | Very few |
| I | Safety, dose, toxicity | 20–80, often volunteers |
| II | Activity, efficacy signal | Patients, about 100–300 |
| III | Comparative efficacy versus standard | Hundreds to thousands; basis of approval |
| IV | Post-marketing surveillance | General population |

### Design
- **Parallel**, **crossover** (own control; stable chronic disease), **factorial** (two questions at once), **cluster** or **adaptive**.
- Hypothesis: **superiority**, **non-inferiority** (pre-set margin) or equivalence.
- Surgical innovation follows the **IDEAL** framework (Idea, Development, Exploration, Assessment, Long-term study).

### Randomisation and allocation
- Simple, **block** (equal numbers), **stratified** (by prognostic factors) or **minimisation**.
- **Allocation concealment** (central or web-based) stops selection bias.

### Blinding
- Single (patient), double (plus assessor) or triple (plus analyst). Surgery: sham procedures or blinded assessors.

### Endpoints
- One **primary endpoint** sets sample size; secondary endpoints are exploratory.
- **Hard** (overall survival) versus **surrogate** endpoints, valid only if they meet the **Prentice criteria**; in prostate cancer only MFS (metastasis-free survival) does.
- **PROs (patient-reported outcomes)** with validated tools; complications graded by **Clavien–Dindo** or CTCAE (Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events).

### Sample size and analysis
- Set α (type I error, usually 0.05), power (80–90%) and expected effect.
- **ITT (intention-to-treat)** preserves randomisation; per-protocol supports non-inferiority claims.
- Kaplan–Meier curves for time-to-event data.

### Ethics and reporting
- Declaration of Helsinki, ICH-GCP (International Council for Harmonisation–Good Clinical Practice), informed consent, ethics committee, DSMB (data safety monitoring board).
- **Registration** before enrolment (CTRI [Clinical Trials Registry–India]).
- **CONSORT** (Consolidated Standards of Reporting Trials): checklist plus **participant flow diagram** (25 items in the 2010 version; updated 2025).
- **Also know:** lead-time bias; Will Rogers phenomenon.

**Diagram to draw:** CONSORT flow diagram: enrolment → randomisation → allocation → follow-up → analysis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Phase III RCT with concealed allocation and blinding is the gold standard.
> - Primary endpoint drives sample size; surrogates need the Prentice criteria.
> - ITT analysis; CONSORT flow diagram for reporting.

## Evidence-based medicine and critical appraisal of research papers
**Asked as:**
- Describe evidence based medicine — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*
- Criteria for evaluation of quaLliy of medical research papers — *Practice Set 6 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(evidence-based medicine also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3f)

*Assumed meaning: "quaLliy" = quality, i.e. critical appraisal.*

### Definition
- **EBM (evidence-based medicine)**: "the conscientious, explicit and judicious use of current best evidence in making decisions about the care of individual patients" (**Sackett, 1996**).
- It integrates best research evidence, **clinical expertise** and **patient values**.
- **Critical appraisal:** systematic assessment of a paper's **validity, results and applicability**; the "Appraise" step of EBM.

### Steps ("5 As")
1. **Ask** a focused question in **PICO** form (Population, Intervention, Comparison, Outcome).
2. **Acquire**: PubMed, Cochrane Library, guidelines.
3. **Appraise**: validity (randomisation, blinding), effect size (ARR [absolute risk reduction], **NNT [number needed to treat] = 1/ARR**), precision (95% CI [confidence interval]), applicability.
4. **Apply** with expertise and patient preference.
5. **Assess** (audit) the outcome.

### Critical appraisal: three core questions
**1. Is it valid? (internal validity)**
- **PICO question** (Population, Intervention, Comparison, Outcome); suitable design.
- **RCTs (randomised controlled trials):** true randomisation, **allocation concealment**, comparable groups at baseline, **blinding**, complete follow-up (>80%), **ITT (intention-to-treat) analysis**.
- **Bias** checked:

| Bias | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Selection | Groups differ at entry |
| Confounding | Third factor (comorbidity) linked to exposure and outcome |
| Performance, detection, attrition | Unequal care, outcome assessment or dropouts |
| Lead-time, length-time; stage migration (Will Rogers) | Screening or better tests make survival look longer |
| Publication | Negative studies unpublished |

**2. What are the results?**
- Effect: RR (relative risk), **ARR (absolute risk reduction), NNT (number needed to treat) = 1/ARR**.
- Precision: **95% CI (confidence interval)**; p value; **sample size and power** (usually 80%; α 0.05).
- **Surrogate endpoints** (end points) must meet the **Prentice criteria**; validated outcome tools (validated PROs [patient-reported outcomes]); Clavien–Dindo grading.

**3. Does it apply? (external validity)**
- Similar patients and setting; benefit outweighs harm.

### Levels of evidence (Oxford CEBM [Centre for Evidence-Based Medicine], as adapted/used by EAU [European Association of Urology])
| Level | Type of evidence / design |
|---|---|
| **1a** | Systematic review or meta-analysis of RCTs (randomised controlled trials) |
| **1b** | At least one RCT (single RCT) |
| 2a | Well-designed controlled study without randomisation (controlled non-randomised) |
| 2b | Quasi-experimental (cohort) study |
| 3 | Non-experimental: case-control, correlation, case reports, case series |
| 4 | Expert committee reports or opinion |

- **Grades**: A (consistent level 1), B (level 2–3), C (level 4 or consensus).
- **GRADE** (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation): certainty high to very low; RCTs start high, observational studies low; **downgrade** for bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision, publication bias; **upgrade** for large effect or dose–response. Recommendations **strong or weak** (used by EAU). AUA (American Urological Association): evidence strength A/B/C; strong, moderate or conditional recommendations.

### Pitfalls in appraisal
- **Bias**: selection, confounding, **lead-time**, **length-time**, stage migration (Will Rogers).
- **Surrogate endpoints** must meet the **Prentice criteria**.

### Reporting checklists
| Design | Checklist |
|---|---|
| RCT | **CONSORT** (Consolidated Standards of Reporting Trials) |
| Systematic review / meta-analysis | **PRISMA** (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic reviews and Meta-Analyses) |
| Observational | **STROBE** (Strengthening the Reporting of Observational studies in Epidemiology) |
| Diagnostic accuracy | **STARD** (Standards for Reporting Diagnostic accuracy studies) |
| Surgical innovation | **IDEAL** framework (Idea, Development, Exploration, Assessment, Long-term study) |
- **Also know:** CONSORT, PRISMA, STROBE reporting checklists; validated PROs (patient-reported outcomes); Cochrane RoB 2 (risk-of-bias) tool; trial registration; conflicts of interest.

### Limitations
- Few surgical RCTs; trial patients differ from real ones; publication bias; "cookbook medicine" if patient values are ignored.

**Diagram to draw:** evidence pyramid and the three overlapping circles of EBM.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Sackett 1996: best evidence + clinical expertise + patient values.
> - 5 As: Ask (PICO), Acquire, Appraise, Apply, Assess.
> - Level 1a = systematic review of RCTs; GRADE: strong or weak.
> - NNT = 1/ARR; beware lead-time, length-time and confounding bias.
> - Three questions: valid, results, applicable.
> - Randomisation, concealment, blinding, ITT, follow-up >80%.
> - CONSORT (RCT), PRISMA (reviews), STROBE (observational), STARD (diagnostic).

## Doppler in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q3b

### Principle
- **Doppler shift:** sound reflected from moving red cells changes frequency in proportion to velocity and the cosine of the insonation angle: Δf = 2f₀v cosθ / c. Keep the angle **below 60°**.
- **RI (resistive index) = (PSV − EDV) / PSV** (peak systolic and end-diastolic velocity); reflects downstream resistance.

### Modes
| Mode | Shows | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Colour** | Velocity and direction (red towards, blue away by convention) | Flow in kidney, testis, ureteric jets |
| **Spectral (duplex)** | Waveform at one point; velocities, RI | Penile and renal arteries |
| **Power** | Amplitude only; 3–5 times more sensitive, less angle-dependent | Low flow: torsion, prostate lesions |

### Applications
| Organ | Findings |
|---|---|
| **Scrotum** | **Torsion:** absent intratesticular flow (compare with the other side); epididymitis: increased flow; **varicocele:** venous reflux on Valsalva; intratesticular RI >0.6 suggests poor spermatogenesis |
| **Penis (after intracavernosal injection)** | **PSV <25 cm/s = arterial ED (erectile dysfunction)**, 25–35 equivocal, >35 normal; **EDV >5 cm/s and RI <0.75 = veno-occlusive dysfunction**; Peyronie plaque assessment |
| **Priapism** | Ischaemic: absent cavernosal flow; non-ischaemic: high flow (PSV >50 cm/s), locates the fistula |
| **Kidney** | Normal RI 0.47–0.70; **raised RI in acute obstruction** (useful in pregnancy, where change in RI is more accurate); renal artery stenosis: high velocity and parvus-tardus waveform; arteriovenous fistula after biopsy or PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) |
| **Transplant** | Arterial thrombosis: absent signal; **venous thrombosis: reversed diastolic flow**; stenosis: high velocity; rejection: loss of diastolic flow, high RI |
| **Bladder** | Ureteric jets (absent on the obstructed side) |
| **Tumours** | Vascularity of renal and testicular masses; tumour thrombus; hypervascular prostate cancer foci |

- **Also know:** intraoperative micro-Doppler in microsurgical varicocelectomy; contrast-enhanced ultrasound and elastography in multiparametric ultrasound.

### Limitations
- Operator- and angle-dependent; poor in obesity and deep vessels; early torsion may show collateral flow, so **Doppler cannot exclude torsion**.

**Diagram to draw:** spectral waveform marking PSV and EDV with the RI formula.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RI = (PSV − EDV)/PSV; normal renal 0.47–0.70.
> - Penile PSV <25 cm/s arterial; EDV >5 cm/s veno-occlusive.
> - Absent intratesticular flow = torsion, but explore on clinical grounds.

## Christian Chaussey
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q3c

*Taken as Christian Chaussy, the Munich urologist who pioneered extracorporeal shock wave lithotripsy; answered as the history and principles of SWL.*

### Contribution and history
- Engineers at **Dornier** found by chance that shock waves from raindrop and micrometeorite research affected tissue.
- Chaussy, with Brendel and Schmiedt in **Munich**, developed the technique in animals and performed the **first human SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) on 7 February 1980 on the Dornier HM1**. They reported it in the *Lancet* in 1980.
- **HM3** (water bath, electrohydraulic spark, biplanar fluoroscopy): first installations Stuttgart 1983 and Indianapolis 1984. It remains the reference for efficacy.
- SWL made most open stone surgery obsolete.

### Principles
- A shock wave is a short acoustic pulse: **peak positive 30–100 MPa, then a negative tail of about −10 MPa**.
- Generated at **F1**, focused onto the stone at **F2** through water or gel coupling (water and tissue have similar acoustic impedance); targeting by fluoroscopy or ultrasound.

| Energy source | Feature |
|---|---|
| Electrohydraulic (HM1, HM3) | Underwater spark, ellipsoid reflector; most effective; electrode wear |
| Piezoelectric | Ceramic elements; long life, little pain; small focal zone, low energy |
| **Electromagnetic** (modern) | Coil and membrane with lens; consistent; dominant today |

### Mechanisms of fragmentation
- **Spallation:** reflected wave at the back surface creates tension.
- **Shear and tear** inside the stone.
- **Quasistatic squeezing** and **dynamic squeezing** (strongest force in initial breakage).
- **Cavitation:** collapsing bubbles erode the surface; excess bubbles shield the stone and injure tissue.

### Practical points
- Better results at **60–90 shocks/min**, with power **ramping** and air-free coupling.
- Poor results: stone >1000 HU (Hounsfield units), skin-to-stone distance >10 cm, lower pole >1 cm.
- **Contraindications:** pregnancy, uncorrected coagulopathy, untreated UTI (urinary tract infection), distal obstruction, aortic aneurysm >4 cm.
- **Also know:** burst wave lithotripsy and ultrasonic propulsion are newer descendants.

**Diagram to draw:** ellipsoid reflector with spark at F1 and stone at F2.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Chaussy, Munich: first human SWL 7 February 1980, Dornier HM1.
> - HM3 (1983) is still the efficacy benchmark.
> - Spallation, squeezing, shear, cavitation; electromagnetic sources today.

## Flow cytometry
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 1, Q3d

### Definition
- **Flow cytometry** measures physical and fluorescent properties of **single cells in suspension** flowing one at a time through a laser beam, thousands per second.

### Principle
1. **Sample:** fresh cells, urine, bladder washings, or nuclei from paraffin blocks.
2. **Staining:** fluorochrome-labelled antibodies to CD (cluster of differentiation) antigens, or **DNA-binding dyes** (propidium iodide, DAPI [4′,6-diamidino-2-phenylindole]).
3. **Hydrodynamic focusing** lines the cells up in single file.
4. **Laser interrogation:**
   - **Forward scatter** = cell size.
   - **Side scatter** = internal complexity (granularity).
   - **Fluorescence** = amount of bound antibody or DNA.
5. Photomultiplier detectors → histograms and dot plots; **gating** selects populations.
- **FACS (fluorescence-activated cell sorting)** physically separates selected cells.

### DNA content analysis
- **DNA index** = tumour G0/G1 peak ÷ normal diploid peak. DI 1.0 = diploid; about 2.0 = tetraploid; any other = **aneuploid**.
- **S-phase fraction** estimates proliferation.
- Aneuploidy and high S-phase generally mean higher grade and worse prognosis.

### Applications in urology
| Area | Use |
|---|---|
| **Bladder cancer** | Ploidy of bladder washings: aneuploidy with high grade, CIS (carcinoma in situ), recurrence; replaced by **UroVysion FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation)** |
| **Upper-tract urothelial cancer** | Aneuploidy → poor long-term survival |
| **Prostate cancer** | Aneuploid tumours progress more after treatment than diploid |
| **Neuroblastoma** | **Hyperdiploid favourable**; diploid or tetraploid adverse; part of COG (Children's Oncology Group) risk |
| **Urinalysis** | Automated urine analysers count RBC (red blood cells), WBC (white blood cells) and bacteria |
| **Transplantation** | **Flow cytometric crossmatch**: more sensitive than CDC (complement-dependent cytotoxicity) crossmatch for donor-specific antibody; Luminex single-antigen beads; CD3 count to guide antithymocyte globulin |
| **Male infertility** | Sperm DNA fragmentation (SCSA [sperm chromatin structure assay]); haploid cells in testicular aspirates |
| **Lymphoma** | Immunophenotyping of testicular or renal lymphoma |

### Advantages and limitations
- **Advantages:** rapid, objective, quantitative, many parameters per cell.
- **Limitations:** architecture lost; near-diploid aneuploidy missed; normal cells dilute tumour peaks; ploidy now adds little independent value.

**Diagram to draw:** DNA histogram with diploid G0/G1 peak, S-phase and aneuploid peak.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Forward scatter = size; side scatter = granularity; fluorescence = marker or DNA.
> - DNA index 1.0 diploid; aneuploidy = worse prognosis.
> - Flow crossmatch is more sensitive than CDC crossmatch.

## VCUG
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram; MCU, micturating cystourethrogram):** fluoroscopic study of bladder filling and voiding after catheter instillation of contrast. It shows the bladder, **posterior urethra** and UVJ (ureterovesical junction) competence.
- **Gold standard for VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)** and PUV (posterior urethral valves).

### Indications
- **Children:** febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) with abnormal ultrasound or recurrent infection (EAU [European Association of Urology]: also age <12 months); bilateral hydroureteronephrosis (PUV); dilated ureter; ureterocele; neurogenic bladder.
- **Adults:** stricture (with RGU [retrograde urethrogram]), diverticula, fistula, post-operative leak, voiding dysfunction.

### Technique
- **Sterile urine** first. After UTI, wait until urine is sterile and the child is improving (about a week); the old 4–6-week delay is unnecessary.
- Scout film → catheter: **5 Fr feeding tube (infants), 6–8 Fr (children), 14–16 Fr (adults)** → confirm tip position.
- **Gravity fill** (≤70 cm H₂O) to expected capacity: <2 years weight (kg) × 7; 2–14 years (age × 30) + 30 mL; >14 years 300–500 mL. (Koff formula: (age + 2) × 30 mL.)
- **Oblique views** (UVJ, prostatic urethra) → remove catheter → **void under fluoroscopy** → **post-void film**.
- **Cyclic VCUG** (2–3 fill–void cycles) detects 12–20% more VUR.

### Findings
- **VUR grade (International Reflux Study, 1981):**

| Grade | Appearance |
|---|---|
| I | Ureter only, not dilated |
| II | Pelvis and calyces, no dilatation |
| III | Mild–moderate dilatation, minimal blunting |
| IV | Moderate dilatation and tortuosity |
| V | Gross dilatation; papillary impressions lost |

- Filling-phase reflux has a poorer resolution prognosis than voiding-only reflux.
- **PUV:** dilated, elongated posterior urethra, prominent bladder neck, trabeculation.
- Ureterocele, diverticula, residual urine; post-void pelvic retention suggests PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction.

### Limitations and complications
- Invasive, radiation, UTI, catheter trauma; snapshot (occult VUR); interobserver variability; autonomic dysreflexia (injury above T6).

### Alternatives
- **Direct radionuclide cystography:** more sensitive, lower dose, poor anatomy.
- **CeVUS (contrast-enhanced voiding urosonography):** no radiation, accuracy close to VCUG.
- **PIC (positional instillation of contrast) cystography:** occult VUR with negative VCUG.

**Diagram to draw:** VUR grades I–V.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Gold standard for VUR and PUV; sterile urine first.
> - Capacity (age × 30) + 30 mL (2–14 years); cyclic study detects 12–20% more.
> - Grades I–II non-dilating, III–V dilating.

## Pioneers of urology: Hopkins, Huggins, Calne, Gaur and Asopa
**Asked as:**
- Describe contribution of a. Prof. Herald Hopkins b. Dr Charles Huggins c. Prof. Roy Caine d. Dr D D Gaue — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Hari Asopa and Charles Huggins — *Practice Set 7 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Names read as Prof. Harold Hopkins, Dr Charles Huggins, Prof. Sir Roy Calne and Dr D. D. Gaur.*
*Assumed meaning: contributions of each to urology.*

### a. Prof. Harold Hopkins (British physicist)
- **Fibreoptics (1954, with Kapany):** coherent bundles of glass fibres transmit an image by total internal reflection (Tyndall's principle). This made flexible endoscopes possible.
- **Rod-lens system (1966):** the old scope used thin glass lenses separated by long air spaces. Hopkins replaced them with **long glass rods separated by short air gaps**. Result: more light transmission, a brighter and wider image, and a **smaller calibre**. Developed commercially with Karl Storz.
- It underpins all modern rigid endoscopes and enabled the first rigid ureteroscope (1979).

### b. Dr Charles B. Huggins (1901–1997) (urologist, University of Chicago)
- Canadian-born American urologist, University of Chicago.
- **1941, with (Clarence) Hodges:** showed that prostate cancer is **androgen-dependent** (hormone dependent). Castration (or oestrogen) relieved far-advanced disease and caused **regression of metastatic prostate cancer**, with falling serum acid phosphatase, pain relief and weight gain; injected androgens aggravated it (made it worse). This proved that **prostate cancer is hormone (androgen) dependent**.
- Used serum **acid phosphatase** to monitor response, an early tumour marker (the first tumour marker).
- **1945:** bilateral adrenalectomy for castration-resistant disease (relapse after castration, suspecting adrenal androgens) (modest, brief responses; morbidity high).
- **Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine, 1966** (shared with Peyton Rous) for hormonal treatment of prostate cancer: the basis of ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) and of all hormonal cancer therapy.
- **Also know:** canine prostate physiology; adrenalectomy for breast cancer.
- **Legacy:** basis of all ADT (androgen deprivation therapy): orchidectomy, oestrogens, LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonists and antagonists, and later abiraterone (blocking adrenal androgens).

### c. Prof. Sir Roy Calne (Cambridge transplant surgeon)
- Showed in dogs that **6-mercaptopurine and then azathioprine** prolong renal allograft survival; azathioprine with steroids became standard immunosuppression.
- Performed the **first liver transplant in the UK (1968)**.
- **First clinical use of ciclosporin** in kidney transplantation (late 1970s), the calcineurin-inhibitor era; acute rejection fell markedly.

### d. Dr D. D. Gaur (Mumbai, India)
- **1992: balloon dissection of the retroperitoneum.** A size 7 surgeon's glove tied on an 8-Fr red rubber catheter, inflated inside the retroperitoneum, creates a working space.
- This made **retroperitoneoscopy** practical and cheap; used for ureterolithotomy, nephrectomy and other renal surgery.

### e. Hari S. Asopa (Indian urologist)
**1. Asopa dorsal inlay urethroplasty (2001)**
- Single-stage graft urethroplasty for anterior urethral stricture by a **ventral sagittal urethrotomy** approach.
- **Steps:** expose the urethra ventrally without circumferential mobilisation; open the urethra ventrally through the stricture; incise the **dorsal urethral plate** in the midline down to the corpora; **inlay** a free graft (buccal mucosa or penile/preputial skin) and quilt it to the tunica albuginea; close the urethra over a catheter.
- **Rationale:** the graft lies on the well-vascularised corporal bed; no mobilisation preserves spongiosal blood supply; suits bulbar and penile strictures.
- Ranks with **Barbagli dorsal onlay** and **Kulkarni one-sided** repair among dorsal graft techniques.

**2. Asopa hypospadias repair (1970s)**
- One-stage repair using a **vascularised inner preputial island flap** still attached to the outer preputial skin, transferred ventrally to form the neourethra; a forerunner of the Duckett transverse preputial island flap.

**Diagram to draw:** conventional lens scope versus Hopkins rod-lens scope (glass rods with short air gaps); dorsal inlay: urethra opened ventrally, dorsal plate incised, graft on corpora (cross-section).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hopkins: rod-lens system 1966; fibreoptics 1954.
> - Huggins and Hodges 1941: androgen dependence; castration regresses prostate cancer; Nobel 1966.
> - Huggins 1945: adrenalectomy for castration-resistant disease.
> - Calne: azathioprine, ciclosporin, first UK liver transplant 1968.
> - Gaur 1992: glove-balloon retroperitoneoscopy.
> - Asopa: ventral sagittal urethrotomy, dorsal plate incised, free graft inlaid (2001).

## Interventional radiology in urology and therapeutic angioembolisation
**Asked as:**
- Therapeutic angioembolization. — *Practice Set 7 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Interventional urological treatment in urology — *Practice Set 8 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning: interventional radiology (image-guided percutaneous and endovascular) treatment in urology.*

### Principles
- Guidance by ultrasound, fluoroscopy or CT (computed tomography), usually under local anaesthesia. **Seldinger technique:** needle, guidewire, then devices over the wire.
- **Beforehand:** correct coagulopathy, manage anticoagulants, urine culture, antibiotic prophylaxis.
- **Requirements:** experienced team, resuscitation in the suite; unstable non-responders need surgery.

### Non-vascular procedures
| Procedure | Indications and points |
|---|---|
| **PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)** | Obstructed infected kidney (as effective as retrograde stent), malignant ureteric obstruction, urinary leak or fistula, access for PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy). Puncture a **posterior calyx** through the avascular Brödel plane. Transfusion 0.5–4% |
| **Antegrade stenting** | When retrograde stenting fails: malignant obstruction, ileal conduit, transplant ureter |
| Antegrade pyelography, Whitaker test | Define strictures; pressure–flow test for obstruction |
| **Percutaneous drainage** | Renal or perinephric abscess >3 cm, urinoma, lymphocele |
| Image-guided biopsy | Renal mass biopsy |

### Vascular (endovascular) procedures: therapeutic angioembolisation
#### Definition
- Deliberate **endovascular occlusion** of a target vessel, usually by transfemoral selective catheterisation under fluoroscopy, to stop bleeding, devascularise a tumour or close an abnormal vessel.
- **Superselective** embolisation of a segmental branch preserves most parenchyma.

#### Embolic agents
| Type | Examples | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Temporary | Autologous clot; gelatin sponge (2–6 weeks) | Priapism, prostate, bladder bleeding |
| Permanent particles | PVA (polyvinyl alcohol), microspheres | Tumours, PAE (prostate artery embolisation) |
| Mechanical | Microcoils, vascular plugs | Pseudoaneurysm, AVF (arteriovenous fistula), varicocele |
| Liquid | NBCA (N-butyl cyanoacrylate) glue, absolute ethanol | Tumour ablation, recurrent priapism |

#### Urological indications
| Procedure | Indications |
|---|---|
| **Renal angioembolisation** | Grade IV–V renal trauma; **pseudoaneurysm or arteriovenous fistula** after PCNL or partial nephrectomy (delayed bleed); bleeding AML (angiomyolipoma, first line); palliative embolisation of RCC (renal cell carcinoma) |
| **Internal iliac (anterior division) embolisation** | Intractable bladder haemorrhage; initial control about 90%. Spare the posterior division (gluteal ischaemia) |
| **PAE (prostate artery embolisation)** | LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) from BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); EAU (European Association of Urology) for men accepting lesser outcomes; AUA (American Urological Association) 2023 may offer |
| **Varicocele embolisation** | Coils or liquid sclerosants via femoral or jugular vein |
| **High-flow priapism** | Superselective cavernosal artery embolisation; resolution ≥75%; recurrence 30–40% |
| **PTRA (percutaneous transluminal renal angioplasty)** | Fibromuscular dysplasia; transplant renal artery stenosis |

- **Renal trauma:** active contrast extravasation, pseudoaneurysm, AVF; success high in grade IV, lower in grade V. Failure predictors: instability, grade V, urinary extravasation. Repeat embolisation often succeeds.
- **Iatrogenic bleeding** after PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) or partial nephrectomy: **treatment of choice**; superselective coils nearly 100% technically successful.
- **AML (angiomyolipoma):** **first line for acute haemorrhage**; prophylactic for >6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth or pregnancy plans. In TSC (tuberous sclerosis complex), embolise then give steroids.
- **RCC:** palliation of haematuria or pain in unfit patients; preoperative use no longer routine.
- **Intractable bladder haemorrhage** (radiation cystitis, cancer): bilateral **anterior division of internal iliac**; avoid the posterior division (gluteal pain).
- **Prostate:** PAE for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) and refractory prostatic haematuria.
- **High-flow priapism:** superselective cavernosal artery embolisation; resolution ≥75%; recurrence → repeat with a non-resorbable agent.
- **Also know:** varicocele (venous coils); lymphatic embolisation for chylous ascites; ADPKD (polycystic kidney) pain.

### Ablation
- **Cryoablation or RFA (radiofrequency ablation)**, percutaneous, for cT1a renal masses <3 cm; biopsy first. Higher local recurrence than partial nephrectomy, salvaged by repeat ablation.

### Complications
- Contrast nephropathy, access-site haematoma, coil migration.
- **Post-embolisation syndrome:** fever, flank pain, nausea, leucocytosis; up to 72 hours; self-limiting.
- **Non-target embolisation:** bowel, bladder, rectum, gluteal muscle, penile skin.
- Renal infarction or abscess; ED (erectile dysfunction) after priapism embolisation.

**Diagram to draw:** prone PCN through a posterior calyx along the Brödel line; renal angiogram showing segmental pseudoaneurysm before and after coil occlusion.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PCN equals retrograde stent for decompressing an obstructed infected kidney.
> - Superselective coils: treatment of choice for pseudoaneurysm and AVF.
> - Embolisation is first line for bleeding AML and post-PCNL pseudoaneurysm or arteriovenous fistula; prophylactic >6 cm or aneurysm >5 mm.
> - Bladder bleeding: internal iliac anterior division; spare posterior division.
> - Post-embolisation syndrome is common and self-limiting.

## Tumour markers in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 18 SAQ Sheet, Q3d

### Definition and uses
- A substance made by a tumour, measured in blood, urine or tissue.
- Uses: screening, diagnosis, staging, prognosis, response, and detecting relapse. Ideal: specific, sensitive, proportional to bulk, short half-life.

### Prostate
- **PSA (prostate-specific antigen):** a kallikrein serine protease (hK3) from luminal cells; **organ-specific, not cancer-specific** (BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia), prostatitis, instrumentation raise it). Best marker for post-treatment follow-up.
- **Refinements:** density (PSA ÷ volume; cut-offs 0.08–0.15); %free PSA (lower in cancer; for PSA 4–10 ng/mL); velocity (little value); **PHI (Prostate Health Index) = (p2PSA ÷ free PSA) × √total PSA**; 4Kscore.
- **Urine:** PCA3 (prostate cancer antigen 3) after DRE (digital rectal examination); score = PCA3 mRNA ÷ KLK3 mRNA × 1000.
- **Also know:** tissue genomic classifiers.

### Testis (germ cell tumours)
| Marker | Half-life | Source | Never raised in |
|---|---|---|---|
| AFP (α-fetoprotein) | 5–7 days | Embryonal, yolk sac | Pure seminoma, choriocarcinoma |
| β-hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin) | 24–36 hours | Choriocarcinoma, embryonal; seminoma in ~15% | Yolk sac tumour |
| LDH (lactate dehydrogenase) | 24 hours | Non-specific; reflects bulk | — |

- Measure before and after orchidectomy, on chemotherapy, in follow-up. Post-orchidectomy values give the **S stage** (S1: LDH <1.5 × normal, hCG <5000 mIU/mL, AFP <1000 ng/mL) and the **IGCCCG (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group)** risk group.
- Raised AFP with "pure seminoma" histology → treat as NSGCT (non-seminomatous germ cell tumour). False positives: liver disease, marijuana, LH (luteinising hormone) cross-reaction.
- **miR-371a-3p:** sensitive new marker; not raised by teratoma.

### Bladder and upper tract
- **Urine cytology:** sensitive and specific for high-grade cancer, poor for low grade; for surveillance, not screening.
- NMP22 (nuclear matrix protein 22), BTA (bladder tumour antigen), ImmunoCyt, UroVysion FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation): selected use only.

### Others
- **Kidney and penis:** no validated serum marker.
- **Adrenal:** metanephrines, cortisol, aldosterone–renin ratio. **Neuroblastoma:** urinary VMA (vanillylmandelic acid)/HVA (homovanillic acid) raised in 90–95%.

**Diagram to draw:** marker decline curve after orchidectomy using half-lives.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PSA: organ-specific, not cancer-specific.
> - AFP never in pure seminoma; half-lives AFP 5–7 days, hCG 24–36 hours.
> - S stage uses post-orchidectomy values.
> - Cytology: good for high grade only.

## Haemostatic agents in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 18 SAQ Sheet, Q3e

### Introduction
- Topical agents are **adjuncts** to surgical haemostasis (suture, clip, energy), used for diffuse ooze. No randomised study shows clear benefit of any single agent in partial nephrectomy.

### Classification
| Class | Examples | Mechanism / note |
|---|---|---|
| **Passive (mechanical)** | Gelatin sponge (Gelfoam), **oxidised regenerated cellulose (Surgicel)**, microfibrillar collagen, polysaccharide spheres | Matrix for platelets; needs intact clotting. Cellulose is acidic (antibacterial) |
| **Active** | Topical thrombin | Converts the patient's fibrinogen to fibrin |
| **Flowable** | Gelatin matrix + thrombin (**Floseal**) | Fills irregular beds, swells |
| **Fibrin sealants** | Fibrinogen + thrombin (Tisseel, Evicel) | Final clotting step; works despite coagulopathy; also seals leaks |
| **Synthetic sealants and glues** | PEG (polyethylene glycol) hydrogel (Coseal), cyanoacrylate, albumin–glutaraldehyde (BioGlue) | Seal; adhesive |
| **Systemic** | Tranexamic acid, aminocaproic acid, desmopressin | Antifibrinolytic; desmopressin for uraemic platelets |

### Urological uses
- **Partial nephrectomy:** renorrhaphy ± sealant or Surgicel bolster; sutureless repair in selected cases.
- **Laparoscopic venous injury:** pressure → sealant → suture.
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) tract sealing in tubeless cases; adrenal and spleen/liver ooze; prostatectomy and lymphadenectomy beds.
- **Fibrin glue:** ureteroscopic fulguration for upper-tract bleeding; fistula plugs.
- **Tranexamic acid:** lowers transfusion at radical cystectomy.

### Intravesical agents (haemorrhagic cystitis)
| Agent | Note |
|---|---|
| Alum 1% (50 g in 5 L) | First line; avoid in renal failure (aluminium toxicity) |
| Silver nitrate 0.5–1% | Chemical coagulation |
| Aminocaproic acid 200 mg/L irrigation | Thromboembolism and clot retention |
| Formalin 1–4% | Most effective; under anaesthesia; **cystogram first** to exclude reflux |

### Complications
- Swelling compresses nerves, ureter or spinal cord (gelatin, cellulose): use the least amount.
- Mimics abscess on imaging; foreign-body reaction; infection.
- Flowables entering open vessels → embolism or thrombosis.
- Bovine thrombin → antibodies and coagulopathy; human products → theoretical viral risk.
- **Also know:** energy adjuncts (bipolar, argon beam, vessel sealers); hyperbaric oxygen in radiation cystitis.

**Diagram to draw:** clotting cascade showing where passive agents, thrombin and fibrin sealant act.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Agents are adjuncts, not substitutes for sutures.
> - Fibrin sealant works despite coagulopathy; passive agents need normal clotting.
> - Surgicel: acidic, may mimic abscess.
> - Alum first line; formalin last, after cystogram.

## Sterilisation of urological instruments
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3a

### Definitions
- **Cleaning:** removes debris that shields organisms; always first.
- **Disinfection:** kills most microbes but not all spores. HLD (high-level disinfection) kills all except large numbers of spores.
- **Sterilisation:** kills all life, including spores.

### Spaulding classification
| Category | Contact | Requirement | Urological examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Critical | Sterile tissue, vessels | Sterilisation | Resectoscope, ureteroscope, nephroscope, laparoscopic and robotic instruments |
| Semi-critical | Mucosa | HLD at least (sterilise if possible) | Flexible cystoscope, TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) probe |
| Non-critical | Intact skin | Low-level disinfection | Abdominal ultrasound probe |

### Reprocessing steps
1. Bedside pre-clean; leak test for flexible scopes.
2. Manual cleaning with enzymatic detergent; brush and flush channels.
3. Rinse → disinfect or sterilise → rinse → dry channels (alcohol, air) → store hanging; record tracking.

### Methods
| Method | Conditions | Use and drawbacks |
|---|---|---|
| **Steam autoclave** | 121 °C for 15 min (15 psi) or 134 °C for 3–4 min | Cheapest, most reliable; metal instruments, autoclavable optics |
| Dry heat | 160 °C for 2 hours | Glass; rarely used |
| **Ethylene oxide** | Low temperature | Heat-sensitive scopes, cables; toxic, carcinogenic, long aeration |
| **Hydrogen peroxide gas plasma** | Low temperature, short cycle | No toxic residue; not for cellulose; long narrow lumens limited |
| **Peracetic acid (Steris)** | Automated liquid, low temperature | Sporicidal; ureteroscopes |
| **Glutaraldehyde 2%** | HLD in 20 min; sterilant after about 10 hours | Cheap; irritant, sensitiser; must rinse well |
| Gamma irradiation | Industrial | Single-use catheters, stents, sutures |

### Monitoring
- **Physical:** time, temperature, pressure records.
- **Chemical indicators:** autoclave tape; Bowie–Dick test (air removal).
- **Biological indicators:** *Geobacillus stearothermophilus* spores (steam, plasma); *Bacillus atrophaeus* (ethylene oxide, dry heat).

### Urology-specific points
- A laser-perforated ureteroscope channel lets fluid in during sterilisation, destroying the scope.
- Poor channel cleaning causes outbreaks (Pseudomonas, mycobacteria).
- **Prions** resist routine methods: single-use or special protocols.
- **Single-use scopes** avoid reprocessing (about $107 per case for reusable ureteroscopes).
- **Also know:** laser fibre re-sterilisation; limited-life robotic instruments.

**Diagram to draw:** reprocessing cycle flowchart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Spaulding: critical sterilise, semi-critical HLD, non-critical low-level.
> - Autoclave 121 °C 15 min or 134 °C 3–4 min.
> - Ureteroscopes: ethylene oxide, glutaraldehyde or peracetic acid.
> - Biological indicator: *G. stearothermophilus*.

## Ethics and informed consent in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3e

### Four principles (Beauchamp and Childress, 1979)
| Principle | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Autonomy** | Patient's deliberated self-rule; basis of consent and confidentiality |
| **Beneficence** | Act for the patient's benefit, judged by the patient's values |
| **Non-maleficence** | Avoid harm (*primum non nocere*); requires training and honest risk data |
| **Justice** | Fair distribution of resources, benefits and risks |
- Hippocratic Oath gave beneficence, non-maleficence, confidentiality; autonomy and justice are modern additions (autonomy is bounded by justice).
- **Jonsen's four-box method:** medical indications; patient preferences; quality of life; contextual features (family, cost, law).

### Informed consent: legal evolution
- From battery to negligence. **Schloendorff (1914):** right to bodily integrity. **Salgo (1957):** term "informed consent". **Canterbury v Spence (1972):** **reasonable-patient** disclosure standard.
- India: **Samira Kohli (2008)**: consent for one procedure does not cover another, except in emergency.

### Elements
1. Diagnosis, proposed treatment and alternatives, including no treatment.
2. Risks, benefits and uncertainty compared with alternatives.
3. **Check understanding** (most often missed).
4. Voluntary decision by a patient with capacity; documented.
- Material risks: no agreed cut-off (1% proposed); disclose rarer ones by severity; lay language.
- Urology examples: incontinence and erectile dysfunction after prostatectomy; retrograde ejaculation after TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate); stent symptoms; conversion to open surgery.

### Exceptions
- **Emergency** in an incapable patient (presumed consent); a conscious patient may still refuse.
- **No capacity:** surrogate using substituted judgement, then best interests.
- **Minors (<18 years):** parents consent; child gives assent.

### Improving consent
- Decision aids, repeat-back, simple written material.
- **Shared decision making:** two-way exchange of options and patient values; vital when several options are reasonable (e.g. localised prostate cancer).

### Special areas
- **End of life:** early goals-of-care talks, advance directives, DNR (do-not-resuscitate) orders.
- **Research:** written consent, ethics committee approval, extra protection for vulnerable groups.
- **Also know:** confidentiality; conflicts of interest; futility policies.

**Diagram to draw:** Jonsen four-box grid.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Four principles: autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, justice.
> - Canterbury v Spence: reasonable-patient standard; Samira Kohli in India.
> - Commonest gap: not checking understanding.
> - Exceptions: emergency, incapacity (surrogate), minors.

## Operating room set-up
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3f
(see also 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3a for operating room safety)

### Physical design
- **Zones:** protective (reception, changing) → clean (pre-op, recovery) → sterile (theatre, scrub) → disposal; one-way flow.
- **Ventilation:** positive pressure, about 20 air changes an hour, filtered air; laminar flow for implants.
- **Environment:** temperature about 20–24 °C, relative humidity about 20–60%. Patient warming: even 1–2 °C hypothermia raises SSI (surgical site infection) and blood loss (**Kurz 1996**: 3-fold wound infection).
- Power backup, piped gases, anaesthetic scavenging, radiolucent table.

### Equipment layout by procedure
| Setting | Arrangement |
|---|---|
| **Endourology** (TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), URS (ureteroscopy), PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)) | Lithotomy or prone; tower and monitor directly ahead; C-arm opposite surgeon; wires, baskets, dilators, spare scopes ready |
| **Laparoscopic, upper abdomen** | Surgeon and assistant face the abdomen; monitor opposite; scrub nurse near the foot |
| **Laparoscopic, pelvic** | 30° Trendelenburg, split legs; surgeon on preferred side, assistant opposite |
| **Robotic** | Patient cart, surgeon console, vision cart; Si docks between legs, Xi from the side |

- **Checklist before laparoscopic incision:** irrigation–suction, electrosurgical unit, full CO₂ cylinder plus spare, white-balanced camera, insufflator and Veress needle checked, **open tray ready**.
- Cables fixed; camera cable apart from diathermy; insufflator at eye level.

### Positioning safety
- Pad bony prominences; axillary roll (lateral); avoid prolonged kidney rest and full flexion (rhabdomyolysis); no shoulder braces in steep Trendelenburg (brachial plexus).

### Ergonomics and staff protection
- Monitor straight ahead, top at eye level; elbows 90–120°; table at the surgeon's pubic symphysis (laparoscopy).
- **Radiation:** ALARA (as low as reasonably achievable): time, distance, shielding; lead apron, thyroid shield, glasses; dosimeter.
- Smoke evacuation; sharps safety; vaccination.

### Safety systems
- **WHO (World Health Organization) Surgical Safety Checklist** (sign in, time out, sign out); counts; fire-risk assessment (oxidiser, fuel, ignition).

**Diagram to draw:** theatre plan: table, tower, C-arm, anaesthetist, surgeon, scrub nurse.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Zones with one-way flow; positive pressure, about 20 air changes an hour.
> - Monitor directly ahead at eye level; open tray ready for laparoscopy.
> - Hypothermia triples wound infection (Kurz).
> - ALARA: time, distance, shielding.

## Amyloidosis in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 21 SAQ Sheet, Q3c

### Definition
- Extracellular deposits of misfolded protein in **β-pleated sheet fibrils**. **Congo red stain gives apple-green birefringence** under polarised light.

### Classification

| Type | Protein | Cause |
|---|---|---|
| **AL** (amyloid light-chain; primary) | Immunoglobulin light chain | Plasma cell dyscrasia, myeloma |
