| **AA** (amyloid A; secondary) | Serum amyloid A | Chronic inflammation: TB (tuberculosis), rheumatoid arthritis, osteomyelitis, pressure sores, hidradenitis |
| **ATTR** (hereditary transthyretin) | Transthyretin | Autosomal dominant neuropathy |
| Dialysis-related | β2-microglobulin | Long-term dialysis |
| **Localised** | Usually AL | Deposits in one organ only |

### Urological sites
- **Kidney (systemic):** nephrotic proteinuria, CKD (chronic kidney disease), enlarged kidneys, renal vein thrombosis, proximal tubular defects.
- **Bladder (usually localised):** **painless gross haematuria**; yellowish plaques or nodules that **mimic carcinoma**. Secondary bladder amyloid can bleed severely; Campbell lists amyloidosis among systemic causes of haemorrhagic cystitis.
- **Ureter and renal pelvis:** stricture or filling defect, often calcified; mimics urothelial cancer; obstruction.
- **Urethra:** rare; mass, obstructive voiding, haematuria.
- **Seminal vesicles:** senile deposits in older men; can mimic cancer invasion on MRI (magnetic resonance imaging).
- **Also know:** ATTR gives neurogenic bladder (underactive detrusor, LUTS [lower urinary tract symptoms] >80%) and erectile dysfunction; adrenal insufficiency; papillary necrosis.

### Evaluation
- **Biopsy is diagnostic** (avoids unnecessary nephroureterectomy or cystectomy).
- Exclude systemic disease: serum and urine protein electrophoresis, immunofixation, free light chains, echocardiogram, renal function.

### Management
- **Localised bladder:** TUR (transurethral resection) or fulguration; intravesical DMSO (dimethyl sulfoxide) reported; recurrence is common, so follow-up cystoscopy is needed.
- **Ureter:** ureteroscopic resection or segmental excision; stent for obstruction.
- **Urethra:** observe if mild; dilatation or urethroplasty if obstructive.
- **Refractory bleeding:** irrigation, embolisation, cystectomy.
- **Systemic:** AL: chemotherapy (daratumumab with bortezomib regimen, ANDROMEDA trial) or stem cell transplant; AA: treat inflammation (colchicine in familial Mediterranean fever); ATTR: tafamidis, patisiran. AA amyloid can recur in a renal transplant.

**Diagram to draw:** Congo red stained fibrils with apple-green birefringence.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Congo red, apple-green birefringence.
> - Localised bladder amyloid mimics cancer; biopsy then TUR.
> - Always exclude systemic AL disease.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 1 · Basic Principles

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Iodinated and other contrast agents (use in urology) and their complications (LAQ)

- Ratio 1.5 (ionic), 3 (non-ionic monomer), 6 (iodixanol, 290 mOsm/kg).
- Non-ionic agents: severe reactions 0.04% vs 0.22% ionic.
- Anaphylaxis: IM adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg, max 1 mg; shellfish allergy is not a risk.
- Premedication: prednisone 50 mg at 13-7-1 h or methylprednisolone 32 mg at 12-2 h, plus diphenhydramine.
- CA-AKI: saline if eGFR <30; bicarbonate and NAC useless; hold metformin only if eGFR <30 or AKI.
- NSF risk at GFR <30; group II agents almost none.

#### Laparoscopic access (Veress, Hasson cannula, extraperitoneal space), gases, and open vs laparoscopic vs robotic radical prostatectomy (LAQ)

- Veress tests: aspiration, drop, advancement; opening pressure <10 mmHg.
- CO₂ standard (soluble, non-flammable); helium in COPD; N₂O supports combustion.
- Gas embolism: left lateral head-down, oxygen, aspirate.
- Yaxley 2016: equal function and margins; robot less blood loss and stay.
- Hasson 1971: open entry under vision.
- Main indication: previous surgery or adhesions.
- Avoids blind Veress injuries; drawback is gas leak.
- Space is created first: finger, then balloon.
- Gaur balloon: glove on red rubber catheter.
- Balloon trocar seals and prevents emphysema.
- Balloon in muscle causes hernia.

#### Pneumoperitoneum physiology, complications of retroperitoneoscopy and laparoscopic donor nephrectomy (LAQ)

- Two drivers: pressure and absorbed CO₂; work at 10–12 mmHg.
- Afterload rises at all pressures; output collapses at 40 mmHg.
- Oliguria from renal vein compression; reversible.
- Hypercarbia peaks after extubation; COPD needs arterial gases.
- Gas embolism: left lateral, head down, oxygen, aspirate.
- Retroperitoneoscopy: space is created, not natural: wrong plane gives hernia, peritoneal tear or bleeding.
- Emphysema commonest; balloon-tip port and 12 mmHg prevent it (prevents leak).
- Pneumothorax 1.6–4%, commoner retroperitoneally.
- Rhabdomyolysis about 1%: limit kidney rest to <1 hour.
- Ratner 1995; left kidney preferred; hand-assisted in about two-thirds.
- Mortality 3 per 10,000; conversion 0.26–3.34%.
- Donor ESRD 90 versus 14 per 10,000 (matched non-donors).

#### Describe IVU & its applications in Urology. (LAQ)

- Contrast is filtered, not reabsorbed: nephrogram then pyelogram.
- Delayed, increasingly dense nephrogram = acute obstruction; no excretion at 24 h = non-functioning.
- Classic signs: drooping lily, cobra head, fish-hook, paintbrush, ring sign, phantom calyx.
- Severe reaction: IM adrenaline 0.3–0.5 mg; CTU has replaced IVU.

#### Principles of tissue engineering and its use in urology (LAQ)

- Triad: cells, scaffold, signals; plus bioreactor and blood supply.
- Diffusion limit about 100–200 µm: vascularity is the main barrier.
- Atala 2006 promising; phase II trial showed no gain; conduit half stenosed.
- Raya-Rivera 2011: seeded urethras in 5 boys.

#### Radionuclide (isotope) scans in urology: principles, renal scans, MAG3 and applications (LAQ)

- ⁹⁹ᵐTc: 6 hours, 140 keV; PET: two 511 keV photons.
- DTPA filtered = GFR; MAG3 secreted = obstruction and transplant; DMSA cortical = scars.
- MAG3: tubular secretion, about 60% extraction; best for split function and drainage.
- Split function (DRF) from 1–3 minute counts; normal 45–55%.
- T½ >20 minutes (min) obstructed, <10 min not.
- Hydration, catheter, correct diuretic timing: the well-tempered renogram.
- Captopril renogram: ≥10% improvement off ACE inhibitors.
- Scar persists at 6 months; pyelonephritis resolves.
- PSMA PET preferred; FDG for residual seminoma >3 cm.
- MIBG best in MEN-2; sentinel node with ⁹⁹ᵐTc-nanocolloid.
- Sestamibi hot = oncocytoma; ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 (VISION).

#### Newer generation CT scan (SAQ)

- Doses: conventional 10–20, low-dose <3–4, ultra-low 1–2 mSv.
- DECT: uric acid stones, virtual unenhanced, iodine maps.
- Enhancement ≥20 HU = solid; deep learning enables ~1 mSv CT.

#### History of Laparoscopy (SAQ)

- Bozzini 1806; Kelling 1901 (dog); Jacobaeus 1910 (human); Veress 1938; Hopkins rod-lens.
- Urology: Cortesi 1976; Clayman 1990; Gaur 1992; Ratner 1995.
- da Vinci 2000; Binder first robotic prostatectomy; robot solved suturing barrier.

#### Foley's (urethral) catheters, guidewires and DJ stents with their complications (SAQ)

- Foley balloon catheter: designed for post-prostatectomy haemostasis.
- Fr = 3 × diameter (mm); inflate with sterile water; coudé for prostatic enlargement, Councill over a wire.
- Suspected urethral injury → RUG first; AUS → deactivate, 12–14 Fr.
- CAUTI 5–10% per day; remove early; change the catheter when treating.
- Hydrophilic wire to pass, PTFE wire to stay (safety).
- DJ symptoms in about 80%: trigonal irritation and reflux; α-blockers and antimuscarinics help.
- Long-dwelling stents: monitor for encrustation; encrustation rises with dwell time; FECal I–V.
- Encrusted stent: bladder, then ureter, then kidney; keep a stent register.

#### Andrew Novick. (SAQ)

- Novick (Cleveland Clinic): nephron-sparing, renovascular and bench surgery.
- PN indications: imperative, relative, elective (cT1a ≤4 cm).
- Warm ischaemia under about 25–30 minutes; ice slush if longer.
- Negative margin is enough; keep ≥20–30% of one kidney.

#### Proteinuria (SAQ)

- Normal <150 mg/day; nephrotic >3.5 g/day.
- ACR A2 30–300, A3 >300 mg/g.
- Glomerular, tubular, overflow; orthostatic proteinuria is benign.
- Dipstick misses light chains; use sulfosalicylic acid or immunofixation.

#### Acetylcysteine and Contrast Induced Nephropathy (CIN) (SAQ)

- Thiol; glutathione precursor, antioxidant, mucolytic.
- Antidote for paracetamol overdose; best within 8 h.
- PRESERVE 2018: no benefit in contrast AKI.
- Cystinuria uses tiopronin, not NAC.
- CIN = rise ≥25% or 0.5 mg/dL in 48–72 h; now CA-AKI vs CI-AKI.
- Main risk: eGFR <30 (IV); <45 intra-arterial.
- Isotonic saline only proven; PRESERVE: bicarbonate and NAC useless.
- Metformin: withhold 48 h only if eGFR <30 or AKI.

#### Non iodinated contrast materials (SAQ)

- GBCAs shorten T1; NSF risk eGFR <30; group II agents almost no risk.
- Microbubbles: intravascular, no nephrotoxicity; ceVUS for reflux.
- CO₂ angiography: never above diaphragm.

#### Health-related quality of life (HRQOL) instruments in urology, including after urinary diversion (SAQ)

- Generic (SF-36, EQ-5D) vs disease-specific (EPIC, IPSS, ICIQ, IIEF) plus bladder-specific (FACT-Bl, BLM30, BCI) tools.
- SHIM lacks bother; I-QOL lacks symptoms.
- Reliable, valid, responsive; Cronbach α >0.7; IPSS MCID ~3 points.
- No diversion proven superior; studies flawed.
- Neobladder better body image; conduit better urinary function.

#### PET scans in urology. (SAQ)

- proPSMA: PSMA PET 92% vs 65% accuracy.
- Seminoma residual >3 cm: FDG-PET at ≥6 weeks; not NSGCT.
- FDG poor in prostate and clear cell RCC.
- VISION: ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 in mCRPC.

#### Bacteriuria in pregnancy. (SAQ)

- Prevalence unchanged; risk of pyelonephritis raised (15–45% untreated).
- Screen by culture at 12–16 weeks; treat 4–7 days.
- β-lactams safe; no fluoroquinolones or tetracyclines; nitrofurantoin not after 35 weeks.

#### Recist Criteria. (SAQ)

- RECIST 1.1: ≥10 mm lesion, node ≥15 mm short axis; 5 targets, 2 per organ.
- PR ≥30% fall from baseline; PD ≥20% and ≥5 mm rise from nadir, or new lesion.
- iRECIST for immunotherapy; PCWG3 for prostate.

#### Outline algorithm for difficult catheter placement. (SAQ)

- Blind → wire → vision → suprapubic.
- BPH: 18-Fr coudé, tip up.
- Councill catheter over wire after dilatation.
- Suspected urethral injury: RUG first, no blind catheter.

#### Indications for outpatient cystourethroscopy. (SAQ)

- Haematuria and NMIBC surveillance are the two commonest indications.
- Flexible scope, lidocaine dwell ≥5 minutes, no routine prophylaxis.
- Therapeutic: stent removal, botulinum toxin, fulguration of small recurrences.

#### MR urography (SAQ)

- Static T2 (no contrast, function-independent) versus excretory gadolinium T1.
- Stones are signal voids; CTU preferred for urothelial cancer.
- Functional MRU: furosemide 1 mg/kg, Patlak analysis for split function.
- NSF risk minimal with group II agents.

#### Urine cytology (SAQ)

- High specificity, low sensitivity; good for high grade and CIS.
- Paris System: N:C ≥0.7 for suspicious and HGUC; AUC N:C ≥0.5.
- Positive cytology, normal cystoscopy: upper tracts, random biopsies.
- Not for screening or initial microhaematuria.

#### Patient positioning in lap urology (SAQ)

- Nerve injury about 2.8%; brachial plexus is commonest at risk.
- Never shoulder braces in steep Trendelenburg.
- Axillary roll below, not in, the axilla.
- Rhabdomyolysis: men, BMI ≥25, kidney rest; CK >5,000.

#### Clinical trial methodology (SAQ)

- Phase III RCT with concealed allocation and blinding is the gold standard.
- Primary endpoint drives sample size; surrogates need the Prentice criteria.
- ITT analysis; CONSORT flow diagram for reporting.

#### Evidence-based medicine and critical appraisal of research papers (SAQ)

- Sackett 1996: best evidence + clinical expertise + patient values.
- 5 As: Ask (PICO), Acquire, Appraise, Apply, Assess.
- Level 1a = systematic review of RCTs; GRADE: strong or weak.
- NNT = 1/ARR; beware lead-time, length-time and confounding bias.
- Three questions: valid, results, applicable.
- Randomisation, concealment, blinding, ITT, follow-up >80%.
- CONSORT (RCT), PRISMA (reviews), STROBE (observational), STARD (diagnostic).

#### Doppler in urology (SAQ)

- RI = (PSV − EDV)/PSV; normal renal 0.47–0.70.
- Penile PSV <25 cm/s arterial; EDV >5 cm/s veno-occlusive.
- Absent intratesticular flow = torsion, but explore on clinical grounds.

#### Christian Chaussey (SAQ)

- Chaussy, Munich: first human SWL 7 February 1980, Dornier HM1.
- HM3 (1983) is still the efficacy benchmark.
- Spallation, squeezing, shear, cavitation; electromagnetic sources today.

#### Flow cytometry (SAQ)

- Forward scatter = size; side scatter = granularity; fluorescence = marker or DNA.
- DNA index 1.0 diploid; aneuploidy = worse prognosis.
- Flow crossmatch is more sensitive than CDC crossmatch.

#### VCUG (SAQ)

- Gold standard for VUR and PUV; sterile urine first.
- Capacity (age × 30) + 30 mL (2–14 years); cyclic study detects 12–20% more.
- Grades I–II non-dilating, III–V dilating.

#### Pioneers of urology: Hopkins, Huggins, Calne, Gaur and Asopa (SAQ)

- Hopkins: rod-lens system 1966; fibreoptics 1954.
- Huggins and Hodges 1941: androgen dependence; castration regresses prostate cancer; Nobel 1966.
- Huggins 1945: adrenalectomy for castration-resistant disease.
- Calne: azathioprine, ciclosporin, first UK liver transplant 1968.
- Gaur 1992: glove-balloon retroperitoneoscopy.
- Asopa: ventral sagittal urethrotomy, dorsal plate incised, free graft inlaid (2001).

#### Interventional radiology in urology and therapeutic angioembolisation (SAQ)

- PCN equals retrograde stent for decompressing an obstructed infected kidney.
- Superselective coils: treatment of choice for pseudoaneurysm and AVF.
- Embolisation is first line for bleeding AML and post-PCNL pseudoaneurysm or arteriovenous fistula; prophylactic >6 cm or aneurysm >5 mm.
- Bladder bleeding: internal iliac anterior division; spare posterior division.
- Post-embolisation syndrome is common and self-limiting.

#### Tumour markers in urology (SAQ)

- PSA: organ-specific, not cancer-specific.
- AFP never in pure seminoma; half-lives AFP 5–7 days, hCG 24–36 hours.
- S stage uses post-orchidectomy values.
- Cytology: good for high grade only.

#### Haemostatic agents in urology (SAQ)

- Agents are adjuncts, not substitutes for sutures.
- Fibrin sealant works despite coagulopathy; passive agents need normal clotting.
- Surgicel: acidic, may mimic abscess.
- Alum first line; formalin last, after cystogram.

#### Sterilisation of urological instruments (SAQ)

- Spaulding: critical sterilise, semi-critical HLD, non-critical low-level.
- Autoclave 121 °C 15 min or 134 °C 3–4 min.
- Ureteroscopes: ethylene oxide, glutaraldehyde or peracetic acid.
- Biological indicator: *G. stearothermophilus*.

#### Ethics and informed consent in urology (SAQ)

- Four principles: autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, justice.
- Canterbury v Spence: reasonable-patient standard; Samira Kohli in India.
- Commonest gap: not checking understanding.
- Exceptions: emergency, incapacity (surrogate), minors.

#### Operating room set-up (SAQ)

- Zones with one-way flow; positive pressure, about 20 air changes an hour.
- Monitor directly ahead at eye level; open tray ready for laparoscopy.
- Hypothermia triples wound infection (Kurz).
- ALARA: time, distance, shielding.

#### Amyloidosis in urology (SAQ)

- Congo red, apple-green birefringence.
- Localised bladder amyloid mimics cancer; biopsy then TUR.
- Always exclude systemic AL disease.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Oncogenes
**Asked as:**
- Oncogenes — *Winter 2017 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*
- Oncogene — *Practice Set 6 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3f)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- **Proto-oncogene:** a normal gene that promotes cell growth and division (an "accelerator").
- **Oncogene:** its mutated or overactive form that drives (driving) uncontrolled proliferation. The change is **gain of function** and **dominant**: one altered allele is enough.
- The first human oncogene, **HRAS**, was isolated from (found in) the **T24 bladder cancer** cell line.

### Mechanisms of activation
| Mechanism | Effect | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Point (activating) mutation** | Constitutively active protein (protein permanently "on") | RAS (bladder); FGFR3 (fibroblast growth factor receptor 3) in low-grade papillary bladder cancer; MET germline (germline MET) in hereditary papillary RCC (renal cell carcinoma) |
| **Gene amplification** | Increased copy number (more copies) → overexpression | MYC (prostate, bladder); HER2 (human epidermal growth factor receptor 2); AR (androgen receptor) in castration-resistant prostate cancer |
| **Chromosomal translocation / fusion** | Gene placed under a strong promoter, or fusion protein | **TMPRSS2–ERG** (androgen-driven ERG in about half of prostate cancers); BCR–ABL; TFE3 fusions (translocation RCC) |
- **Also know:** promoter hypomethylation can switch oncogenes on.

### Functional classes
| Class | Examples |
|---|---|
| Growth factors | PDGF (platelet-derived growth factor), sis |
| **Receptor tyrosine kinases** | EGFR (epidermal growth factor receptor), HER2, FGFR3, MET, KIT (seminoma) |
| **Signal transducers** | **RAS** (binds GTP [guanosine triphosphate]; GTP-binding; mutation locks it "on"), BRAF, PI3K |
| **Transcription factors** | **MYC**, ERG, ETS family |
| Cell cycle / apoptosis regulators | Cyclin D1, BCL-2 |

### Oncogene versus tumour suppressor gene
| | Oncogene | Tumour suppressor |
|---|---|---|
| Normal role | Accelerator | Brake, DNA repair |
| Change | Gain of function | Loss of function |
| Alleles needed | One | Usually both (**Knudson two-hit**: retinoblastoma) |
| GU (genitourinary) examples | MYC, MET, FGFR3, ERG | RB1, TP53, VHL, PTEN |

### Urological significance
- **Bladder:** FGFR3 mutations in up to 80% of low-grade Ta tumours (10–20% of MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer)); FGFR3 and TERT promoter mutations in >60% of low-grade papillary tumours; RAS (HRAS) mutually exclusive with FGFR3; HER2 in 9–12% of MIBC; MYC overexpressed in over half.
- **Kidney:** MET activation in papillary RCC.
- **Prostate:** TMPRSS2–ERG fusion, MYC (8q gain), AR amplification after (under) hormone therapy.
- **Testis:** KIT and KRAS mutations (seminoma).

### Targeted therapy ("oncogene addiction")
- **Erdafitinib** (pan-FGFR inhibitor) for FGFR-altered (FGFR3/2-altered) advanced (metastatic) urothelial cancer; MET-active TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors) in papillary RCC; AR-targeted drugs in prostate cancer; HER2-directed drugs (trastuzumab deruxtecan received a tumour-agnostic FDA approval in 2024 for HER2-positive solid tumours; disitamab vedotin is under study in urothelial cancer).

**Diagram to draw:** growth factor → receptor → RAS → RAF → MEK → ERK → MYC pathway.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Oncogenes: gain of function, one allele; activated by mutation, amplification, translocation.
> - HRAS first found in T24 bladder cancer; FGFR3 in up to 80% low-grade Ta.
> - TMPRSS2–ERG in about half of prostate cancers; MET in papillary RCC.

## Premalignant conditions in urology (including premalignant lesions of penis)
**Asked as:**
- Premalignant conditions in urology — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Premalignant lesion of penis — *Practice Set 6 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(premalignant conditions also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)

### Definition
- Altered tissue with a raised risk of invasive cancer, often **intraepithelial neoplasia with an intact basement membrane**. Treating it prevents cancer.
- Penis: a lesion with raised risk of invasive SCC (squamous cell carcinoma). The key entity is **PeIN (penile intraepithelial neoplasia)**: dysplastic squamous epithelium with an **intact basement membrane**.

### Penis
#### Classification
**1. PeIN (HPV-associated or HPV-independent)**
- **HPV (human papillomavirus)-associated** (commonest; basaloid and warty) or differentiated (HPV-independent).
- **Mechanism (HPV):** viral E6 inactivates p53, E7 inactivates Rb (retinoblastoma protein); **p16** is the marker.
- **HPV-independent (differentiated):** arises in lichen sclerosus.
- **WHO (World Health Organization) 2022:** all PeIN is high grade; grades I–III abandoned.

| Clinical entity / lesion | Site, appearance / key points | Risk of invasion |
|---|---|---|
| **Erythroplasia of Queyrat** | Glans or prepuce; red velvety (glans) plaque | **Progresses in 10–33%** |
| **Bowen disease** | Shaft; scaly red (shaft) plaque | About 5% progress |
| Bowenoid papulosis | Shaft papules, young men; HPV 16 | Does not progress; may regress |

**2. Other lesions**
- **Lichen sclerosus (BXO [balanitis xerotica obliterans]):** SCC (squamous cell carcinoma) in **2.3–9%** (HPV-independent type).
- **Buschke–Löwenstein tumour** (giant condyloma; verrucous carcinoma): HPV 6/11; locally invasive, locally destructive, rarely metastasises; may harbour SCC.
- **Also know:** cutaneous horn; PEKMB (pseudoepitheliomatous keratotic and micaceous balanitis, controversial); condyloma acuminatum; (chronic) phimosis; genital lichen planus; Kaposi sarcoma (HHV-8 [human herpesvirus 8], check HIV [human immunodeficiency virus]).

#### Evaluation
- Biopsy first: **multiple biopsies of adequate depth** to exclude invasion; p16 staining. Examine inguinal nodes; partner screening for cervical neoplasia.

#### Treatment (organ-preserving)
| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Prepuce (preputial lesions) | Circumcision or excision with clear margin |
| Glans | **Topical 5-FU (5-fluorouracil) or 5% imiquimod**; **laser** (Nd:YAG [neodymium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet] preferred; CO₂ too superficial); **glans resurfacing** with skin graft |
| Shaft | Excision; Mohs surgery |
| Lichen sclerosus | Clobetasol 0.05% for 3 months then maintenance; circumcision; lifelong follow-up |
| Buschke–Löwenstein | Wide excision; avoid radiotherapy (anaplastic change) |

- Long-term follow-up (recurrence common after organ-preserving treatment). **Prevention:** neonatal circumcision, HPV vaccination, avoiding smoking.

### Bladder
- **CIS** (carcinoma in situ): flat high-grade lesion, umbrella cells lost; untreated, **40–83%** invade muscle. Treat with BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin).
- **Urothelial dysplasia:** 14–19% progress; 60% to CIS with prior cancer.
- **Cystitis glandularis** (intestinal type): low risk of adenocarcinoma; resect and survey.
- **Leukoplakia** (keratinising squamous metaplasia): traditionally premalignant; Campbell now says **not** (no TP53 changes). Schistosomiasis and chronic catheters predispose to SCC.

### Prostate
- **HGPIN** (high-grade prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia): precursor; isolated HGPIN no longer needs repeat biopsy. IDC-P (intraductal carcinoma of the prostate) usually means invasive cancer nearby.

### Testis
- **GCNIS** (germ cell neoplasia in situ): precursor of all postpubertal GCTs (germ cell tumours) except spermatocytic tumour; **50% invasive within 5 years**. Treat by orchidectomy or **radiotherapy ≥20 Gy**.
- **Gonadoblastoma** in dysgenetic gonads with Y material; cryptorchidism.

### Kidney and others
- **Also know:** papillary adenoma; acquired cystic kidney disease on dialysis; urethral diverticulum; bowel in augmentation.

**Diagram to draw:** normal urothelium vs dysplasia vs CIS (umbrella cells lost); penile epithelium with full-thickness atypia above an intact basement membrane.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PeIN: intact basement membrane; WHO 2022 all high grade.
> - Queyrat 10–33% vs Bowen (about) 5% progression; bowenoid papulosis does not progress.
> - Lichen sclerosus: SCC 2.3–9%.
> - Untreated bladder CIS: 40–83% invade.
> - GCNIS: 50% invasive in 5 years; radiotherapy ≥20 Gy.

## 46-XX Male
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3e

*Assumed meaning: 46,XX testicular DSD (difference of sex development), "XX male" or de la Chapelle syndrome.*

### Definition
- Testes and male genitalia in a person with a 46,XX karyotype. Old term "XX sex reversal"; renamed **46,XX testicular DSD** (2006 consensus). First described by **de la Chapelle (1964)**. About **1 in 20,000** males.

### Genetics

| Type | Mechanism | Phenotype |
|---|---|---|
| **SRY-positive** (about 80–90%) | **SRY** (sex-determining region Y) translocated from Yp to the X chromosome during paternal meiosis | Usually normal male genitalia |
| SRY-negative | Testis genes switched on without SRY: **SOX9** duplication or upregulation, SOX3, loss of RSPO1 or WNT4 | More hypospadias, undescended testes, ovotestis |

- SRY activates SOX9, so Sertoli cells form and secrete **AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone)**: no uterus. Leydig testosterone virilises.
- The **AZF (azoospermia factor) region of Yq is absent**, so spermatogenesis fails.

### Clinical features
- Normal male identity; often found at **infertility work-up** for azoospermia, or prenatally (cell-free DNA 46,XX but male ultrasound).
- Small firm testes, **azoospermia**, hypergonadotropic hypogonadism, gynaecomastia in some; progressive testicular regression.
- Height normal or **shorter than average** (no Y growth genes), unlike Klinefelter.

| Feature | 46,XX male | Klinefelter (47,XXY) |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | 1 in 20,000 | 1 in 500–1000 |
| Height | Normal or short | Often tall |
| Genital anomalies | More common | Rare |
| Sperm retrieval | Never (no AZF) | Possible by micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction) |

### Evaluation
- Semen analysis (azoospermia); FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and LH (luteinising hormone) high, testosterone low or low-normal.
- **Karyotype** 46,XX; **FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation) for SRY**; DSD gene panel if SRY-negative; scrotal ultrasound.

### Management
- **Testosterone replacement** when hypogonadal (libido, bone density, muscle).
- **Infertility:** testicular sperm extraction is futile; counsel for **donor sperm or adoption**.
- Gynaecomastia: reduction mammoplasty if persistent; hypospadias or undescended testis repaired.
- Genetic counselling and psychological support; bone density monitoring.

**Diagram to draw:** X and Y chromosomes showing Xp–Yp exchange carrying SRY onto X.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 46,XX testicular DSD: SRY on X in about 80–90%; 1 in 20,000.
> - Azoospermia always (AZF absent): no sperm retrieval.
> - Shorter than Klinefelter; FISH for SRY confirms.

## Female circumcision
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3f
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3f)

*Taken as female genital mutilation/cutting (Chapter 58 calls it "female circumcision (infibulation)").*

### Definition and epidemiology
- **FGM (female genital mutilation):** partial or total removal of, or other injury to, the external female genitalia for non-medical reasons (WHO, World Health Organization).
- About **200 million** females affected; about 3 million African girls a year. Africa, Middle East, Indonesia and Malaysia; some Indian communities.
- Usually at 4–10 years, without anaesthesia, as a rite of passage; predates Islam. Pharaonic type = infibulation.

### WHO classification
| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| I (clitoridectomy) | Partial or total removal of the clitoris and/or prepuce |
| II (excision) | Clitoris and labia minora, ± labia majora |
| III (infibulation) | Narrowing of the introitus by cutting and joining the labia, ± clitoral excision |
| IV (other) | Pricking, piercing, incising, scraping, cauterising |
- Older Toubia scheme numbers differ: infibulation is Type III in WHO.

### Complications
- **Immediate:** pain, bleeding, shock, infection, **urinary retention**, urethral or rectal injury.
- **Urological:** recurrent **UTI** (urinary tract infection), slow stream, meatal obstruction, vaginal stone from urine stasis, **fistula** after obstructed labour.
- **Gynaecological:** retained menses, dysmenorrhoea, cysts, keloid, dyspareunia, apareunia.
- **Obstetric:** obstructed labour, tears, haemorrhage.
- **Psychological:** anxiety, depression, PTSD (post-traumatic stress disorder).

### Management
- Sensitive, culturally respectful history; examine and classify; drawings or photographs for consent.
- **Deinfibulation:** simple **midline division of the scar** with subcuticular closure of each edge; exposes meatus and introitus. High success. Best before pregnancy or antenatally.
- **Never reinfibulate.**
- Excise cysts, treat stenosis, repair fistula; counselling.
- Clitoral reconstruction: less predictable, limited follow-up.

### Legal aspects
- WHO and UN (United Nations) condemn FGM, including by health workers. Illegal in many countries (UK FGM Act 2003). India has no specific law.

**Diagram to draw:** vulva with types I–III and the deinfibulation line.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO types I–IV; infibulation is type III.
> - Retention, UTI, stones, fistula.
> - Midline deinfibulation; never reinfibulate.

## Familial malignancies in urology
**Asked as:**
- Familial malignancies in Urology. — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*
- Familial malignancy in urology — *Practice Set 8 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 2: also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3f. Practice Set 8: also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3f and Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3b)

### Principles
- **Knudson two-hit hypothesis:** every cell carries one inherited mutant tumour-suppressor allele, so one more hit causes cancer: tumours are **early, bilateral and multifocal**.
- **4–6% of RCC (renal cell carcinoma)** is familial. All familial RCC syndromes are **autosomal dominant**; only HPRC (hereditary papillary renal carcinoma) involves an activated oncogene.

### Hereditary kidney cancer syndromes

| Syndrome | Gene (chromosome) | Kidney tumour | Other features |
|---|---|---|---|
| **VHL** (von Hippel–Lindau) | VHL (3p25) | Clear cell, multifocal | Retinal angioma, CNS (central nervous system) haemangioblastoma, phaeochromocytoma |
| **HPRC** | MET (7q31) | Multiple bilateral papillary (type 1) | None |
| **HLRCC** (hereditary leiomyomatosis and RCC) | FH (fumarate hydratase, 1q42–43) | **Aggressive** papillary, collecting duct | Skin and uterine leiomyomas |
| **BHD** (Birt–Hogg–Dubé) | FLCN (17p11.2) | Chromophobe, hybrid oncocytic | Fibrofolliculomas, lung cysts, pneumothorax |
| **TSC** (tuberous sclerosis complex) | TSC1 (9q34), TSC2 (16p13.3) | Multiple AMLs (angiomyolipomas); RCC 2–3% | Angiofibromas, cortical tubers |

- **Also know:** SDH (succinate dehydrogenase)-deficient RCC; BAP1 (uveal melanoma, mesothelioma); PTEN (Cowden).

### Kidney management
- **3 cm rule:** watch tumours <3 cm; nephron-sparing surgery at ≥3 cm (VHL, HPRC, BHD) to save (saving) function for repeat surgery. **Exception: HLRCC**: excise promptly, wide margins.
- TSC AML: everolimus at 3–5 cm (another source: if >3 cm); embolise or excise ≥5 cm. **Belzutifan** (HIF-2α [hypoxia-inducible factor 2α] inhibitor) approved 2021 for VHL (VHL tumours).

### Other urological cancers
- **Prostate:** BRCA2, BRCA1, ATM, CHEK2, PALB2, HOXB13. Father and brother affected: 9-fold risk. Earlier screening, cautious active surveillance, **PARP (poly ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors**.
- **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma): Lynch syndrome** (MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2): about 14-fold risk, 75-fold with MSH2. Screen with MMR (mismatch repair) immunohistochemistry, then germline testing.
- **Testis:** affected brother RR (relative risk) 8–12, father 2–4.
- **Phaeochromocytoma:** about one-third hereditary: MEN-2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2; RET), VHL, NF1 (neurofibromatosis type 1), SDHB (highest malignancy).
- **Also know:** Wilms tumour (WT1: in WAGR [Wilms, aniridia, genitourinary anomalies, retardation] and Denys–Drash; Beckwith–Wiedemann).

### Approach
- Young age, multifocality or family history: genetic counselling, germline testing, screen relatives.

**Diagram to draw:** VHL loss → HIF accumulation → VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) → angiogenesis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - All familial RCC is autosomal dominant; 3 cm rule except HLRCC.
> - VHL 3p25, MET 7q31, FH 1q42, FLCN 17p11.
> - Lynch (MSH2) → UTUC; BRCA2 → aggressive prostate cancer.

## Secondary malignancies in urology
**Asked as:**
- Secondary malignancies in Urology. — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*
- Secondary malignancies in Uro — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)* (also answered in Practice Set 3 Paper 1, Q3f)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Interpreted (read) as second cancers caused by urological or oncological treatment (radiotherapy, chemotherapy, urinary diversion, immunosuppression).*

### Definition
- A **second (treatment-related) malignancy** is a new, histologically distinct cancer caused by treatment of a first disease. It appears after a latency of years, often **in the radiation field**.

### Causes and risks
| Cause | Second cancer | Key facts |
|---|---|---|
| **Pelvic radiotherapy** | **Bladder cancer**, rectal cancer, sarcoma (in-field) | Bladder RR (relative risk) 2–4 after cervical EBRT (external beam radiotherapy), 1.5–2 after prostate EBRT, smaller after brachytherapy, little with IMRT (intensity-modulated radiotherapy). Latency 15–30 years |
| **Cyclophosphamide** | **Bladder cancer**, UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) | Only chemotherapy proven to cause bladder cancer; metabolites phosphoramide mustard and acrolein. About 4.5-fold, dose-dependent (>20 g cumulative) |
| **Urinary diversion / augmentation** | Adenocarcinoma at the uro-intestinal junction | Ureterosigmoidostomy 6–29% after 10–20 years (registry 2.6%); cystoplasty 1.6%; ileal conduit 0.02%. Risk tracks colonic mucosa exposed to urine and faeces |
| **Transplant immunosuppression** | Skin cancer, PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder), RCC (renal cell carcinoma), bladder cancer | RCC mostly in native kidneys; bladder cancer about 3-fold and aggressive |
| **Testicular GCT (germ cell tumour) treatment** | Solid tumours, leukaemia | 60–100% excess after cisplatin or radiotherapy, 200% (highest) after both; etoposide-related leukaemia |
| **Paediatric tumours** | In-field solid tumours, leukaemia | Wilms tumour 1% at 10 years |

### Mechanisms
- Radiation and alkylating (drugs) or topoisomerase-inhibitor chemotherapy: mutagenic DNA damage in normal tissue.
- Immunosuppression: loss of (lost) immune surveillance and oncogenic viruses (EBV [Epstein–Barr virus], HPV [human papillomavirus]).
- Diversion: urine–faeces contact at the anastomosis.

### Prevention and surveillance
- IMRT, smaller fields; **mesna** with cyclophosphamide (reduces cystitis).
- **Haematuria after pelvic radiotherapy or cyclophosphamide: always cystoscopy.**
- Endoscopy from 5 years after ureterosigmoidostomy; augmentation surveillance from about 10 years; excise a defunctioned ureterosigmoid anastomosis.
- Testis cancer: fewer treatment modalities; low-dose CT (computed tomography) in surveillance.
- **Also know:** pioglitazone, aristolochic acid and phenacetin as drug-related urothelial carcinogens.

**Diagram to draw:** ureterosigmoid anastomosis showing (with) adenocarcinoma at the junction.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pelvic EBRT raises bladder cancer 1.5–4-fold; IMRT little.
> - Cyclophosphamide: only chemotherapy proven to cause bladder cancer.
> - Ureterosigmoidostomy cancer 6–29% after 10–20 years.
> - Transplant recipients: bladder cancer about 3-fold.

## Genetic counselling
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 1, Q3d

*Assumed meaning: genetic counselling in urological practice.*

### Definition
- A communication process that helps patients and families understand the **medical, psychological and family implications** of a genetic condition, so they can make informed choices. It is **non-directive, voluntary and confidential**.
- **Germline testing** (blood) finds inherited variants with family implications; **somatic testing** (tumour) guides treatment.

### Steps
1. **Three-generation pedigree**.
2. **Risk assessment:** inheritance and recurrence risk (autosomal dominant 50%; autosomal recessive 25%).
3. **Pre-test counselling:** which test; possible results (pathogenic, **VUS [variant of uncertain significance]**, negative); insurance and psychological harms; consent.
4. **Post-test counselling:** interpret (do not act on a VUS); **cascade testing of relatives**; surveillance; targeted therapy.
5. **Reproductive options:** prenatal diagnosis, PGT (preimplantation genetic testing), donor gametes; psychosocial support.

### Urological applications
| Condition | Genes | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| **Prostate cancer** | BRCA2 (> BRCA1), ATM, CHEK2, PALB2, mismatch repair (Lynch), HOXB13 | NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): test metastatic, high-risk, strong family history; BRCA2: aggressive disease, PARP (poly-ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors, earlier screening |
| **Hereditary RCC (renal cell carcinoma)** | VHL, MET, FH, FLCN, TSC1/2, SDH, BAP1 | Counsel RCC at ≤46 years, bilateral or multifocal, family history |
| **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma)** | Lynch (MSH2 highest) | Screen if <60 years or Lynch-spectrum cancer history |
| **ADPKD (autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease)** | PKD1 (chromosome 16), PKD2 (chromosome 4) | Testing selects living related donors |
| **Cystinuria** | SLC3A1, SLC7A9 (recessive) | Screen siblings |
| **Male infertility** | Karyotype, AZF (azoospermia factor) deletion, CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) | AZF deletions pass to sons by ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection); absent vasa: test partner for CFTR |
| **DSD (differences of sex development)** | e.g. CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia, recessive), AIS (androgen insensitivity, X-linked) | Recurrence and gonadal tumour risk |

### Ethics and law
- Confidentiality; right not to know.
- **India:** PCPNDT Act (Pre-Conception and Pre-Natal Diagnostic Techniques Act, 1994) bans disclosure of fetal sex.

**Diagram to draw:** three-generation pedigree with standard symbols.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Non-directive; pre-test and post-test counselling; cascade testing.
> - Prostate: BRCA2 most important; test all metastatic men.
> - RCC ≤46 years, ADPKD, cystinuria, AZF/CFTR, DSD are classic urological indications.

## Congenital Penile curvature..
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 10 Paper 1, Q3d

### Definition and principle
- Curvature of the erect penis present from birth, with an **orthotopic meatus**.
- Rigidity needs **symmetric compliance** of the tunica albuginea; curvature means one aspect is less compliant.

### Embryology
- DHT (dihydrotestosterone) drives fusion of the urethral folds and maturation of spongiosum, Buck's and dartos fascia. The urethra may form normally while the mesenchyme stays deficient → dysgenetic, inelastic ventral tissue.
- Normal fetal ventral curvature resolves by 20 weeks.

### Devine and Horton classification (1973)
| Type | Abnormal tissue |
|---|---|
| I | Corpus spongiosum deficient (chordee without hypospadias) |
| II | Dysgenetic Buck's and dartos fascia; normal spongiosum |
| III | Dartos only |
| IV | Normal layers; **asymmetric corporal compliance** (true congenital curvature) |
| V | Congenitally short urethra (very rare; doubted) |

### Two clinical groups
| Feature | Chordee without hypospadias (I–III) | Congenital curvature (IV) |
|---|---|---|
| Direction | Ventral ± torsion | Ventral, lateral (often left), rarely dorsal |
| Erection size | In proportion to flaccid size | **Noticeably large** |
| Presentation | Childhood | 18–30 years, noticed at puberty |
- **Versus acquired** (Peyronie disease, fracture): congenital is painless, no plaque, no shortening.

### Evaluation
- Home photographs of erection; **artificial erection** (Gittes–McLaughlin saline test or prostaglandin) in theatre.
- Grade: mild <30°, moderate 30–45°, severe ≥45°.

### Management
- Surgery if curvature hinders intercourse or distresses.
1. Deglove; excise ventral dysgenetic tissue; mobilise spongiosum from glans to perineum.
2. Residual curve: **midline ventral septotomy** under artificial erection.
3. Still curved: **dorsal plication** contralateral to the curve: Nesbit (excise ellipses, close transversely), Yachia (Heineke–Mikulicz), 16-dot or TAP (tunica albuginea plication). **12 o'clock is nerve-free**, so midline plication is safe.
4. Severe: ventral **corporotomy with graft** (dermis, tunica vaginalis).
- **Rule:** if the meatus reaches the glans tip, do not divide the urethra; the corpora usually limit straightening.
- **Results:** single operation in almost all; recurrence 2–15%, mostly after plication.

**Diagram to draw:** penile cross-section: nerves at 11 and 1 o'clock, plication at 12.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Devine–Horton I–III = chordee without hypospadias; IV = corporal asymmetry.
> - Congenital curvature: young man, large erection, orthotopic meatus.
> - Dorsal 12 o'clock plication; never divide a urethra that reaches the glans tip.

## Circumcision undone
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 1, Q3d

*"Circumcision undone" is read as an incomplete or complicated circumcision needing revision. Foreskin restoration is mentioned briefly at the end.*

### Introduction
- Circumcision complications: **0.2–5%**, rising **10- to 20-fold after infancy**.
- Most late problems concern **skin quantity** (too little or too much) or the **exposed meatus**.

### Inadequate ("undone") circumcision
- **Too little skin removed** or asymmetrical excision: redundant prepuce covering the glans.
- Leads to **secondary (cicatricial) phimosis**, smegma retention, balanitis, adhesions and cosmetic distress.
- A large hydrocele or hernia pushes skin forward and raises this risk.

### Other complications

| Complication | Management |
|---|---|
| **Bleeding** (commonest, about 0.1%), usually frenular | Pressure, cautery, silver nitrate or suture |
| Glanular adhesions; **skin bridges** (can cause chordee, torsion) | Office lysis or excision; steroids do not help |
| **Excess skin removal**, degloving | Heals by secondary intention with ointment; severe: grafts |
| **Trapped penis** (cicatrix over glans) | Betamethasone 0.05% and retraction; else relaxing incision, flaps, Z-plasty |
| **Meatal stenosis** (almost only after circumcision) | Meatotomy or meatoplasty |
| Subcoronal fistula; glans amputation; necrosis (cautery on clamp) | Hypospadias-type repair; reattach glans within 8 hours |

### Evaluation
- Original procedure (age, device); examine skin quantity, meatus, curvature; exclude hypospadias, buried or webbed penis and BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans).

### Circumcision revision
- **Indications:** redundant or asymmetrical skin, phimosis, adhesions, cosmesis.
- **Needs general anaesthesia** in children, unlike neonatal circumcision.
- **Technique (sleeve):** mark inner and outer incisions (inner 5–10 mm from the corona), excise the excess collar, haemostasis, interrupted absorbable sutures.
- Lyse adhesions and skin bridges; correct torsion or chordee; meatotomy if needed; histology if BXO suspected.

### Prevention
- Separate prepuce fully and inspect the meatus before using a device (Gomco, Mogen, Plastibell); defer with hypospadias, curvature, buried or webbed penis; trained operator.

### Also know: foreskin restoration
- Traction tissue expansion or skin grafting, for men wanting a foreskin; results variable.

**Diagram to draw:** sleeve revision showing two circumferential incisions.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Complications 0.2–5%; bleeding commonest.
> - Too little skin: redundancy, phimosis, adhesions; too much: trapped penis, chordee.
> - Meatal stenosis almost only in circumcised boys.
> - Revision needs general anaesthesia.

## Noonan syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 22 SAQ Sheet, Q3a

### Definition and genetics
- Noonan syndrome is an **autosomal dominant RASopathy** (disorder of the RAS–MAPK [mitogen-activated protein kinase] signalling pathway) with variable expression; many cases are de novo. Incidence about 1 in 1,000–2,500.
- **Karyotype is normal** (46,XY or 46,XX). The phenotype resembles Turner syndrome, hence the old name "male Turner".
- **Mechanism:** gain-of-function variants increase RAS–MAPK signalling, which disturbs growth and organ development.

| Gene | Approximate share | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **PTPN11** (encodes SHP2 phosphatase) | About 50% | Pulmonary stenosis common |
| SOS1 | About 10% | Milder growth and learning effects |
| RAF1 | About 5–10% | Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy |
| RIT1, KRAS, others | Smaller | KRAS: more learning difficulty |

- Related RASopathies: Costello, cardiofaciocutaneous, NF-1 (neurofibromatosis type 1).

### Clinical features
- Short stature; webbed neck; hypertelorism, ptosis, low-set posteriorly rotated ears; pectus deformity.
- **Heart:** pulmonary valve stenosis (commonest), HCM (hypertrophic cardiomyopathy), septal defects.
- **Bleeding tendency:** factor XI deficiency, platelet dysfunction.
- Lymphatic dysplasia: genital (penoscrotal) lymphoedema; mild learning difficulty.

### Urological aspects
- **Cryptorchidism:** the main urological feature, in most affected boys (often bilateral).
- **Testicular dysfunction:** primary Sertoli cell dysfunction (low inhibin B, high FSH [follicle-stimulating hormone]) with poor spermatogenesis, even after orchidopexy. Leydig function is usually adequate; puberty is often delayed. Female fertility is usually normal.
- **Renal anomalies (about 10%):** mostly mild renal pelvic dilatation; also duplex system, solitary kidney, hypoplasia.
- Small excess of childhood cancers (JMML [juvenile myelomonocytic leukaemia], embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma).

### Evaluation
- Clinical diagnosis (van der Burgt criteria), confirmed by gene panel; karyotype excludes Turner in girls.
- Before surgery: **echocardiography, coagulation screen and factor XI level**, anaesthetic review.
- Renal and bladder ultrasound at diagnosis.

### Management
- **Orchidopexy by 18 months** (EAU [European Association of Urology] and AUA [American Urological Association]); laparoscopic for non-palpable testes.
- Follow-up: pubertal hormones, semen analysis, fertility counselling.
- **Also know:** growth hormone for short stature; multidisciplinary cardiac and haematology care.

**Diagram to draw:** RAS–MAPK cascade with SHP2 (PTPN11) acting upstream of RAS.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Autosomal dominant, normal karyotype; PTPN11 in about half.
> - Cryptorchidism plus Sertoli dysfunction: male subfertility.
> - Check heart and clotting (factor XI) before orchidopexy.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Oncogenes (SAQ)

- Oncogenes: gain of function, one allele; activated by mutation, amplification, translocation.
- HRAS first found in T24 bladder cancer; FGFR3 in up to 80% low-grade Ta.
- TMPRSS2–ERG in about half of prostate cancers; MET in papillary RCC.

#### Premalignant conditions in urology (including premalignant lesions of penis) (SAQ)

- PeIN: intact basement membrane; WHO 2022 all high grade.
- Queyrat 10–33% vs Bowen (about) 5% progression; bowenoid papulosis does not progress.
- Lichen sclerosus: SCC 2.3–9%.
- Untreated bladder CIS: 40–83% invade.
- GCNIS: 50% invasive in 5 years; radiotherapy ≥20 Gy.

#### 46-XX Male (SAQ)

- 46,XX testicular DSD: SRY on X in about 80–90%; 1 in 20,000.
- Azoospermia always (AZF absent): no sperm retrieval.
- Shorter than Klinefelter; FISH for SRY confirms.

#### Female circumcision (SAQ)

- WHO types I–IV; infibulation is type III.
- Retention, UTI, stones, fistula.
- Midline deinfibulation; never reinfibulate.

#### Familial malignancies in urology (SAQ)

- All familial RCC is autosomal dominant; 3 cm rule except HLRCC.
- VHL 3p25, MET 7q31, FH 1q42, FLCN 17p11.
- Lynch (MSH2) → UTUC; BRCA2 → aggressive prostate cancer.

#### Secondary malignancies in urology (SAQ)

- Pelvic EBRT raises bladder cancer 1.5–4-fold; IMRT little.
- Cyclophosphamide: only chemotherapy proven to cause bladder cancer.
- Ureterosigmoidostomy cancer 6–29% after 10–20 years.
- Transplant recipients: bladder cancer about 3-fold.

#### Genetic counselling (SAQ)

- Non-directive; pre-test and post-test counselling; cascade testing.
- Prostate: BRCA2 most important; test all metastatic men.
- RCC ≤46 years, ADPKD, cystinuria, AZF/CFTR, DSD are classic urological indications.

#### Congenital Penile curvature.. (SAQ)

- Devine–Horton I–III = chordee without hypospadias; IV = corporal asymmetry.
- Congenital curvature: young man, large erection, orthotopic meatus.
- Dorsal 12 o'clock plication; never divide a urethra that reaches the glans tip.

#### Circumcision undone (SAQ)

- Complications 0.2–5%; bleeding commonest.
- Too little skin: redundancy, phimosis, adhesions; too much: trapped penis, chordee.
- Meatal stenosis almost only in circumcised boys.
- Revision needs general anaesthesia.

#### Noonan syndrome (SAQ)

- Autosomal dominant, normal karyotype; PTPN11 in about half.
- Cryptorchidism plus Sertoli dysfunction: male subfertility.
- Check heart and clotting (factor XI) before orchidopexy.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Genitourinary infections, common organisms, bacteraemia and urosepsis: pathophysiology and management
**Asked as:**
- Discuss general principles of genitourinary infections, characteristics of common organisms and management of bacteremia and septic shock. — *Summer 2016 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe Pathophysiology of urosepsis and its management. — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe pzthophysiology of urosepsis and its management. — *Practice Set 11 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q2) — *"Pzthophysiology" is read as pathophysiology.*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

### Definitions
- **UTI (urinary tract infection):** urothelial inflammation from bacterial invasion, usually with bacteriuria and pyuria. **Sterile pyuria** needs evaluation (tuberculosis, stone, tumour).
- **Uncomplicated:** healthy non-pregnant woman, normal tract. **Complicated:** men, pregnancy, obstruction, stones, catheter, neurogenic bladder, diabetes, immunosuppression.
- **Recurrent:** ≥2 in 6 months or ≥3 in 12 months. Unresolved = never cleared; reinfection = new organism; **bacterial persistence** = same organism from a focus (stone, prostate).
- **Prophylaxis** assumes a sterile tract; **suppression** controls a focus that cannot be eradicated.
- **Urosepsis:** sepsis whose source is the urogenital tract (the urinary tract or male genital organs). It is usually Gram-negative and usually follows obstruction or instrumentation.

### Pathogenesis of UTI
- **Routes:** ascending from bowel flora (commonest); haematogenous (*S. aureus*, *Candida*); lymphatic (rare).
- **Virulence (uropathogenic *E. coli*):** **type 1 pili** (mannose-sensitive) bind bladder uroplakins and cause cystitis. **P pili** (mannose-resistant) bind renal glycolipids and are found in about 90% of pyelonephritis strains. Haemolysin damages tissue. Bacteria invade umbrella cells and form intracellular communities; quiescent reservoirs cause recurrence.
- **Principle:** a strong host is infected only by virulent strains; a compromised host (obstruction, catheter) by almost any organism.
- **Host defences:** voiding washout, lactobacilli, urine osmolality and pH, uromodulin (Tamm–Horsfall protein), urothelial exfoliation.
- **Risk factors:** **obstruction** (stasis impairs defences and turns UTI life-threatening), VUR (vesicoureteral reflux; with infection it scars the growing kidney), stones, catheters, diabetes, pregnancy, spermicides.
- **Diagnosis:** dipstick (nitrite, leucocyte esterase) rules out better than it rules in. Culture is the gold standard: ≥10⁵ CFU (colony-forming units)/mL (Kass); ≥10² in a symptomatic woman or a catheter specimen. Image (ultrasound, CT (computed tomography)) in complicated infection or fever beyond 72 hours.

### Characteristics of common organisms
| Organism | Key features |
|---|---|
| ***E. coli*** | ~85% of community and ~50% of hospital UTI; commonest cause of urosepsis; ST131 clone is ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) and quinolone resistant |
| ***Proteus*** | Urease splits urea into ammonia: alkaline urine (pH >7), **struvite stones**, persistence in stones |
| ***Klebsiella*** | Capsule; urease; ESBL and KPC (*K. pneumoniae* carbapenemase) producers |
| ***Pseudomonas*** | Nosocomial, catheter biofilm; needs ceftazidime, piperacillin, aminoglycoside, fluoroquinolone or meropenem |
| ***Enterococcus*** | **Always resistant to cephalosporins**; amoxicillin, nitrofurantoin; VRE (vancomycin-resistant) |
| ***S. saprophyticus*** | ~10% of cystitis in young sexually active women |
| ***S. aureus*** | Haematogenous renal abscess; in urine, look for bacteraemia |
| **Anaerobes, *Candida*** | Abscesses, fistulae; *Candida* with catheters and diabetes (*Candida* in catheterised diabetics) |

- **Urosepsis organisms:** *E. coli* (commonest), *Proteus*, *Klebsiella*, *Pseudomonas*, *Enterococcus*; ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) producers rising; *Candida* in catheterised diabetics.
- **Also know:** emerging *Aerococcus urinae*; *Ureaplasma* and *Mycoplasma* with negative routine cultures; *Chlamydia* and tuberculosis do not grow on routine culture (sterile pyuria).

- **Antibiotic principles (see 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3f):** urine levels matter in cystitis, tissue levels in pyelonephritis and bacteraemia. Use a narrow spectrum and short course; adjust for renal function; **drain obstruction**.

### Bacteraemia, sepsis, septic shock and urosepsis
**Sepsis-3 (2016)**
| Term (Sepsis-3, 2016) | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Sepsis** | Life-threatening organ dysfunction from a dysregulated host response to infection: acute rise in **SOFA (Sequential Organ Failure Assessment) score ≥2** |
| **qSOFA (quick SOFA)** | Respiratory rate ≥22/min, altered mentation, systolic blood pressure ≤100 mmHg; ≥2 = high risk (of death) (prompt only) |
| **Septic shock** | Vasopressor needed for MAP (mean arterial pressure) ≥65 mmHg **and** lactate >2 mmol/L despite (adequate) fluids; mortality >40% |
- SIRS (systemic inflammatory response syndrome) criteria were dropped from the definition as non-specific.

#### Causes and risk factors (urological triggers)
- **Urological triggers:** infected obstructed kidney, PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), high-pressure ureteroscopy, prostate biopsy, TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), infected stones, emphysematous pyelonephritis, abscess.
- **Obstruction with infection:** stone, PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, tumour, pregnancy, giving infected hydronephrosis or pyonephrosis. Pyonephrosis, emphysematous pyelonephritis, renal, perinephric and prostatic abscess.
- **Parenchymal infection:** emphysematous pyelonephritis, renal or perinephric abscess, prostatic abscess, Fournier gangrene.
- **Instrumentation:** PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), ureteroscopy with high irrigation pressure, TURP (transurethral resection of prostate), prostate biopsy (sepsis 0.3–3.1%), catheters.
- **Host:** diabetes, immunosuppression, old age, neurogenic bladder.

#### Pathophysiology
- **Pathophysiology (summary):** endotoxin binds TLR4 (Toll-like receptor 4). Cytokines (TNF-α (tumour necrosis factor-α), interleukins IL-1 and IL-6) and nitric oxide cause vasodilatation, capillary leak, myocardial depression and DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation), then organ failure.

**1. Entry of bacteria (and endotoxin) into blood**
- Obstruction causes stasis, impairs washout and raises pelvic pressure. Bacteria and endotoxin then enter the blood (circulation) by **pyelovenous and pyelolymphatic backflow**.
- High irrigation pressure at endoscopy does the same, and so does fragmenting infected stones, which release endotoxin (fragmenting infected stones releases endotoxin). Stone culture predicts postoperative sepsis better than bladder urine culture.
- An obstructed infected kidney is (behaves like) an **abscess with a collecting system**. Antibiotics barely reach it, and bladder urine may be sterile.

**2. Host response**
```
Endotoxin (LPS, lipopolysaccharide) / (or) Gram-positive wall products
  → TLR4 (Toll-like receptor 4) on macrophages → NF-κB (nuclear factor κB)
  → cytokines TNF-α (tumour necrosis factor α), interleukins IL-1, IL-6, IL-8
  → complement + coagulation activation + endothelial injury (activation)
```
- **Vasodilatation:** inducible nitric oxide causes (lowers) low systemic vascular resistance ("warm" shock).
- **Capillary leak:** (endothelial) glycocalyx damage leads to hypovolaemia and tissue oedema.
- **Coagulopathy:** tissue factor release causes microthrombi, platelet consumption and DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation).
- **Myocardial depression** and **mitochondrial dysfunction (failure):** cells cannot use oxygen, so lactate rises.
- **Immunoparalysis:** a later anti-inflammatory phase predisposes to (invites) secondary infection.

**3. Organ dysfunction:** AKI (acute kidney injury), ARDS (acute respiratory distress syndrome), encephalopathy, liver dysfunction (failure), DIC, multi-organ failure.

#### Clinical features
- Fever or hypothermia, rigors, flank pain, tachycardia, tachypnoea, confusion, oliguria, hypotension. Often starts (begins) within hours of an endourological procedure (endourology).

#### Investigations
- **Lactate**, blood cultures ×2 (two blood cultures), urine culture (and pelvic urine at drainage), blood count, creatinine, bilirubin, platelets, coagulation, arterial (blood) gas, glucose. Procalcitonin helps decide when to stop antibiotics.
- **Imaging:** bedside ultrasound (hydronephrosis, debris); **CT (computed tomography)** for stone, gas or abscess.

### Management (Surviving Sepsis Campaign: hour-1 bundle 2018, guidelines 2021)
**A. Resuscitation and antibiotics (SSC, Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021)**
```
Recognise (SSC: do not use qSOFA alone for screening)
→ Lactate → blood cultures ×2 + urine culture
→ IV (intravenous) broad-spectrum antibiotics within 1 hour
  (shock or probable sepsis; within 3 h if sepsis only possible)
→ 30 mL/kg (balanced) crystalloid if hypotensive or lactate ≥4 mmol/L
  (within 3 h if hypotensive or hypoperfused)
→ noradrenaline first line for MAP ≥65 (target MAP 65); add vasopressin
→ SOURCE CONTROL: stent or nephrostomy (equally effective),
  drain abscess, change catheter; stone surgery later
→ refractory shock (persistent vasopressor need): hydrocortisone 200 mg/day; ICU (intensive care unit)
→ re-measure (repeat) lactate, capillary refill
→ de-escalate by culture at 48–72 h
```
- **Empirical antibiotics:** by local resistance: third-generation cephalosporin or piperacillin–tazobactam ± aminoglycoside; carbapenem if ESBL risk. Adjust for renal function.

**B. Source control: the key urological step**
- **Drain the obstructed infected kidney urgently:** retrograde **double-J stent** or **percutaneous nephrostomy (PCN)**. Randomised trials show equal effect on sepsis (equally effective in a randomised trial (Pearle, 1998)).
  - Stent: suits coagulopathy; avoid high-pressure contrast injection.
  - PCN: better for (suits) large or impacted stones, pyonephrosis; allows (gives) pelvic urine (for) culture.
- **No definitive stone surgery during sepsis.** Treat the stone after (once) the infection clears.
- Other sources: drain renal or perinephric abscess; emphysematous pyelonephritis (percutaneous drainage, nephrectomy if diffuse gas without fluid); prostatic abscess drainage; change or remove (the) catheter; debride Fournier gangrene.

**C. Supportive care (Support)**
- Oxygen and (or) ventilation, glucose control (insulin if glucose ≥180 mg/dL), transfusion (transfuse) below haemoglobin 7 g/dL, thromboprophylaxis, stress-ulcer prophylaxis, renal replacement if needed. Watch for AKI (acute kidney injury), ARDS (acute respiratory distress syndrome) and DIC.

**D. De-escalation and duration**
- Narrow antibiotics by (to) culture at 48–72 h. Usual course (usually) 7–10 days (another source: 7–14 days); longer with an undrained focus or *S. aureus* bacteraemia; shorter courses suffice after good source control. Step down (switch) to oral therapy when (once afebrile and) stable.

### Special situations
- **Emphysematous pyelonephritis** (diabetics): fluids, glucose control, antibiotics, drain; nephrectomy for diffuse gas without fluid.
- **Pregnancy:** stent or nephrostomy; avoid fluoroquinolones.
- **Fungal urosepsis:** remove catheter, antifungal, drain fungal balls.
- **Prognosis:** urosepsis does better than sepsis from other sites if the source is drained early; delay in drainage (delayed drainage) is the main avoidable cause of death.

### Prevention
- Sterile urine before elective endourology; antibiotic prophylaxis; low irrigation pressure and access sheath; short operating time with infected stones; stage the procedure if pus is found.

**Diagram to draw:** pathway from *E. coli* adherence to bacteraemia and cytokine-driven shock; obstructed kidney leading to backflow, cytokine cascade and shock; obstructed kidney → pyelovenous backflow → cytokine cascade → shock and organ failure.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - *E. coli* ~85% community UTI; P pili cause pyelonephritis; *Proteus* causes struvite; *Enterococcus* resists cephalosporins.
> - Sepsis = SOFA rise ≥2; septic shock = vasopressor for MAP ≥65 plus lactate >2 mmol/L.
> - Obstruction plus infection is the emergency; bladder urine may be sterile.
> - Hour-1: lactate, cultures, antibiotics, 30 mL/kg, noradrenaline (SSC 2021).
> - Infected obstructed kidney: drain urgently; no definitive stone surgery in sepsis.
> - Stent and nephrostomy equally effective (stent = nephrostomy for drainage); delay definitive stone surgery.

## Describe clinical implication of AIDS on the Genito-Urinary Tract?
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q1

### Introduction
- AIDS (acquired immunodeficiency syndrome) is the late stage of HIV (human immunodeficiency virus) infection. HIV is a retrovirus that destroys CD4 T lymphocytes. It spreads by blood, semen, vaginal fluid and breast milk.

| Stage | Features |
|---|---|
| 1. Acute | 2–4 weeks after infection; flu-like illness; high viral load; antibody tests may be negative |
| 2. Clinical latency | Years; usually no symptoms |
| 3. AIDS | **CD4 <200 cells/mm³** or an AIDS-defining condition |

- **Concept:** genitourinary (GU) disease follows the falling CD4 count. Organisms become opportunistic, infections become chronic and the GU tract becomes a reservoir. With HAART (highly active antiretroviral therapy), the burden shifts to drug toxicity and diseases of ageing.

### Diagnosis (urologist's role)
- Test anyone with, or at risk of, an STD (sexually transmitted disease), and every TB (tuberculosis) patient.
- Antigen–antibody immunoassay → antibody differentiation assay; if discordant, HIV RNA (acute infection).

### Manifestations by site
| Site | Manifestations |
|---|---|
| **Genital skin and STDs** | Ulcerative STDs (herpes, syphilis, chancroid) increase HIV acquisition and transmission; HSV-2 (herpes simplex virus 2) in 50–90%. Warts (HPV (human papillomavirus) 6, 11) in about 20%, may be giant; molluscum 5–18%. Perineal cellulitis (cover MRSA (methicillin-resistant *Staphylococcus aureus*)); Fournier gangrene commoner |
| **UTI (urinary tract infection)** | About fivefold commoner. Bacteriuria about 30% at CD4 <200, 11% at 200–500, none above 500. *E. coli* and other gram-negatives; *Pseudomonas* up to 30%. *Salmonella* UTI needs lifelong prophylaxis. Culture before treatment |
| **Opportunistic UTI** | No response, or CD4 <500: look for fungi (*Candida*, *Cryptococcus*, *Histoplasma*), TB, CMV (cytomegalovirus), adenovirus |
| **Kidney** | Opportunistic pyelonephritis (*Pneumocystis*, *Candida*, mycobacteria). AKI (acute kidney injury) from sepsis, dehydration, nephrotoxins (tenofovir, indinavir, amphotericin). Obstruction by stones, lymphoma, fungal balls |
| **GU TB** | HIV raises active TB 15–20-fold. Disease is disseminated and bilateral, with **less fibrosis, fewer strictures and fewer contracted bladders**, because fibrosis needs an intact immune response. Mortality is higher. Same 6-month regimen; rifampicin lowers protease inhibitor levels |
| **Prostate** | Bacterial, TB and fungal prostatitis; prostatic abscess and urosepsis; relapse up to 70%. Cryptococcal prostatitis is a reservoir for relapsing meningitis, so give long-term antifungals |
| **Testis and epididymis** | Recurrent, chronic epididymo-orchitis (including TB). Testis harbours opportunists. Testicular atrophy and hypogonadism |
| **Voiding** | LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) worsen with disease stage. Retention may herald seroconversion. Neurogenic causes: HIV encephalopathy, toxoplasmosis, **CMV polyradiculopathy** (bladder and bowel dysfunction with sciatic pain: lumbar puncture, treat early), neuropathy |
| **Sexual function** | ED (erectile dysfunction) in about half of men under 50; protease inhibitors worsen it. **Protease inhibitors raise sildenafil levels 3–10-fold: start low.** Low libido, ejaculatory disorders |

### HIVAN (HIV-associated nephropathy)
- Nephrotic-range proteinuria and rising creatinine, often without oedema or hypertension. Typically CD4 <200 and viral load >1000 copies/mL; about 12 times commoner in Black patients.
- Biopsy: **collapsing FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis)** with tubular microcysts.
- Treatment: HAART plus ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor. Without it, dialysis follows within about 10 months.

### Stones
- **Indinavir** stones in up to 22% of treated patients. Indinavir precipitates in alkaline urine. Stones are radiolucent, and pure stones may be invisible even on CT (computed tomography).
- Stop the drug and hydrate: succeeds in up to 70%. Newer protease inhibitors rarely cause stones.

### Malignancy
| AIDS-defining | Non-AIDS-defining |
|---|---|
| **Kaposi sarcoma** (HHV-8 (human herpesvirus 8)), non-Hodgkin lymphoma, cervical cancer | Penile, anal, testicular, prostate cancers; Hodgkin lymphoma |
| Falls with HAART | Now about 70% of cancers in treated HIV |

- **Kaposi sarcoma:** genital in about 3% of AIDS; glans lesions can block the meatus. Treat with ART (antiretroviral therapy) first. Local excision, laser or intralesional vinblastine; radiotherapy 15–30 Gy; systemic chemotherapy if disseminated.
- **Penile cancer:** HIV raises HPV infection; about fourfold risk of death.

### Implications for urological practice
- **Universal precautions** for every patient: double gloves, no hand-to-hand passing of sharps.
- Occupational risk: percutaneous about 0.3%, mucous membrane about 0.09%.
- **PEP (post-exposure prophylaxis)** within 72 hours, for 28 days: tenofovir + emtricitabine plus raltegravir or dolutegravir. HIV test at 6 weeks, 3 and 6 months.
- CD4 <200: TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) *Pneumocystis* prophylaxis also lowers UTI.
- **Prevention:** male circumcision cuts heterosexual acquisition by 50–60% (three African RCTs (randomised controlled trials)); consistent condom use cuts risk about 80%; PrEP (pre-exposure prophylaxis).

**Also know:** IRIS (immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome) on starting ART; serodiscordant couples: suppressed viral load with sperm washing and assisted reproduction; transplantation is possible with controlled HIV.

**Diagram to draw:** male GU tract outline labelled with the lesion at each site.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AIDS = CD4 <200; UTI risk rises as CD4 falls.
> - HIV-TB: disseminated, less fibrosis, fewer strictures.
> - Indinavir stones: radiolucent, may be CT-invisible; stop drug and hydrate.
> - PEP within 72 hours for 28 days; circumcision cuts acquisition 50–60%.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Principles of antimicrobial therapy in UTI
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3f

### Definitions
- **Uncomplicated** urinary tract infection (UTI): healthy non-pregnant women, normal tract. **Complicated:** men, pregnancy, abnormal tract, catheter, immunosuppression.
- **Recurrent:** ≥2 in 6 months or ≥3 in 12 months.

### Principles
- **Site decides the level:** urine in cystitis, tissue elsewhere; nitrofurantoin and fosfomycin lack tissue levels: **cystitis only**.

| Killing pattern | Drugs | Dosing goal |
|---|---|---|
| Time-dependent (time above MIC [minimum inhibitory concentration]) | β-lactams, carbapenems | Frequent doses or extended infusion |
| Concentration-dependent (peak:MIC) | Aminoglycosides, fluoroquinolones (FQs) | High once-daily dose |

- **Resistance thresholds:** trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) if local resistance ≤20%; oral FQ for pyelonephritis if ≤10%.
- **Collateral damage:** FQs and cephalosporins select resistance and *C. difficile*; avoid them in cystitis.
- Culture all but simple cystitis; **drain** obstructed infected kidneys; shortest course.

### Empirical choices
| Scenario | Drug | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| Cystitis (women) | Nitrofurantoin 100 mg twice daily; fosfomycin 3 g; pivmecillinam 400 mg; TMP-SMX 160/800 mg twice daily | 5 d; single dose; 3–5 d; 3 d |
| Cystitis (men) | TMP-SMX or per culture | 7 d (= 14 d, Drekonja 2021) |
| Pyelonephritis (oral) | Ciprofloxacin 500 mg twice daily; levofloxacin 750 mg daily | 7 d; 5 d |
| Pyelonephritis (intravenous) | Ceftriaxone 1 g daily; gentamicin 5–7 mg/kg once daily | 10–14 d; oral when afebrile |
| Complicated / catheter UTI | Aminoglycoside or 3rd-generation cephalosporin per culture; change catheter | 7–14 d |
| Urosepsis | Broad-spectrum **within 1 h**, source control | – |

### Asymptomatic bacteriuria
- Treat **only in pregnancy and before mucosa-breaching urological procedures**. Not in elderly, diabetic, catheterised or spinal cord injury patients.
- **Pregnancy:** avoid FQs and TMP-SMX; cephalexin is suitable.

### Prophylaxis
- Recurrent: vaginal oestrogen, **methenamine hippurate** (non-inferior to antibiotics, ALTAR 2022), cranberry, post-coital antibiotics.
- Surgical: single dose within 1 hour; ≤24 h for percutaneous nephrolithotomy and prostheses.
- **Also know:** ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) producers; nitrofurantoin avoided if creatinine clearance <30 mL/min; gepotidacin (US 2025).

**Diagram to draw:** Concentration–time curve showing peak:MIC and time above MIC.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Recurrent = ≥2 in 6 months or ≥3 in 12 months.
> - Cystitis: nitrofurantoin 5 d, fosfomycin single, TMP-SMX 3 d (resistance ≤20%).
> - Pyelonephritis: ciprofloxacin 7 d or levofloxacin 5 d (FQ resistance ≤10%).

## Renal and perinephric abscess
**Asked as:**
- Perinephric abscess — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*
- Treatment of renal abscess — *Winter 2017 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition and types
- **Renal (cortical/corticomedullary) abscess:** pus confined to the renal parenchyma.
  - **Cortical (renal carbuncle):** haematogenous *Staphylococcus aureus* from a skin source; formerly about 80% of cases.
  - **Corticomedullary (now commonest):** ascending Gram-negative infection (*E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *Proteus*) in a damaged or obstructed kidney (stones, reflux, diabetes).
- **Perinephric abscess:** pus **outside the renal capsule but within Gerota fascia**. Rupture through Gerota fascia gives a **paranephric** abscess (paranephric beyond it).

### Aetiology (perinephric abscess)
- Rupture of a renal cortical abscess or pyonephrosis (commonest); extravasation of infected urine from obstruction (stone).
- **Haematogenous** spread in about a third (skin source, *S. aureus*).
- Infected perirenal haematoma or urinoma (trauma, surgery).
- Spread from bowel (Crohn disease, perforation), pancreas, spine.
- Risk factors: **diabetes (about a third)**, stones, obstruction, polycystic kidneys on dialysis.
- Organisms: *E. coli*, *Proteus*, *S. aureus*; anaerobes with bowel sources.

### Clinical features
- Fever, flank pain, leucocytosis.
- Perinephric abscess is **insidious:** symptoms >5 days in most; more than a third afebrile.
- Flank pain, tenderness, flank mass (about half); psoas sign (flexed, externally rotated hip).
- **Suspect it when "pyelonephritis" persists:** pyelonephritis defervesces within 4–5 days; perinephric abscess does not.

| | Pyelonephritis | Perinephric abscess |
|---|---|---|
| Symptoms before admission | ≤5 days | >5 days |
| Fever on antibiotics | Never >4 days | ≥5 days (median 7) |

### Investigations / diagnosis (needed to plan treatment)
- Leucocytosis, raised creatinine, pyuria; urine culture identifies the organism in only about a third; urine may be sterile (haematogenous or non-communicating abscess). Blood cultures positive in a quarter to a third.
- **CT (computed tomography): investigation of choice.** Renal abscess: round low-attenuation lesion with enhancing "ring sign". It shows the collection, its spread (psoas, flank), the cause (stone, obstruction) and plans drainage.
- Ultrasound: quick; guides drainage. Plain X-ray: loss of psoas shadow, scoliosis.
- AFBN (acute focal bacterial nephritis) is wedge-shaped without a wall and is treated medically.

### Treatment
**1. Antibiotics immediately (after blood and urine cultures)**
| Presumed source | Empirical therapy |
|---|---|
| Haematogenous (Gram-positive) | Penicillinase-resistant penicillin; vancomycin if allergic or MRSA (methicillin-resistant *S. aureus*) |
| Ascending (Gram-negative) | IV (intravenous) third-generation cephalosporin, antipseudomonal penicillin or aminoglycoside |
| Perinephric abscess | Aminoglycoside plus antistaphylococcal penicillin; cephalosporin or vancomycin if allergic. Adjust to culture |
- Switch to oral once afebrile; continue until clinical and imaging resolution.

**2. Size-based drainage (renal abscess)**
| Size | Management |
|---|---|
| **≤3 cm** | Antibiotics alone (stable patient) |
| **3–5 cm** | Antibiotics alone or drainage; no consistent pathway |
| **>5 cm** | **Percutaneous drainage** (CT/ultrasound-guided) first line; may need several drains |
| Any size: immunocompromised or not responding | Percutaneous drainage |

- **Perinephric abscess (if present) — lower threshold:** small (<3 cm) in an immunocompetent patient may resolve with antibiotics; drain if >3 cm. Larger or non-responding: **CT- or ultrasound-guided percutaneous drainage** (first line).
- Aspiration also distinguishes abscess from a cystic tumour; culture the pus.
- Serial ultrasound or CT until resolution. Worsening suggests perinephric spread, wrong diagnosis or resistant organism.

**3. Treat the cause**
- Relieve obstruction (stent or nephrostomy); treat stones once infection settles; control diabetes.

**4. Surgery**
- Open drainage for loculated or failed (percutaneous drainage) cases; **nephrectomy** for a non-functioning, destroyed or pyonephrotic kidney, at the same time or staged.

### Outcome
- Perinephric abscess: mortality fell from 40–50% to about 12% with CT; delay in diagnosis causes most deaths.

**Diagram to draw:** coronal kidney showing renal, perinephric and paranephric spaces / abscess sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Now mostly ascending Gram-negative abscess in a damaged kidney.
> - Perinephric: within Gerota fascia; diabetes in a third.
> - Fever beyond 4–5 days of antibiotics: get a CT.
> - CT is the investigation of choice; follow until resolution.
> - ≤3 cm antibiotics; 3–5 cm either; >5 cm percutaneous drainage.
> - Percutaneous drainage first line; nephrectomy if non-functioning.

## Ciprofloxacin
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q3d

### Class and mechanism
- Second-generation **fluoroquinolone**, derived from nalidixic acid.
- Inhibits **DNA gyrase** (topoisomerase II, main target in Gram-negatives) and **topoisomerase IV** (Gram-positives). This blocks DNA supercoiling and replication: **bactericidal**.

### Spectrum
- Enterobacteriaceae, ***Pseudomonas aeruginosa***, *Haemophilus*, *S. saprophyticus*, *S. aureus* (methicillin-sensitive); *Legionella*, some mycobacteria.
- Weak against streptococci and enterococci; **no anaerobic cover**, so bowel and vaginal flora are partly spared.

### Pharmacokinetics
- Oral bioavailability about 70%; IV (intravenous) and oral forms; half-life about 4 h.
- **Concentration-dependent killing:** AUC/MIC (area under curve to minimum inhibitory concentration) predicts effect; post-antibiotic effect.
- High urine and tissue levels; **good prostatic penetration**.
- Renal excretion: reduce dose if creatinine clearance <30 mL/min.

### Doses
- Cystitis 250 mg twice daily × 3 days; pyelonephritis 500 mg twice daily × 7 days (or 1 g extended-release daily); IV 400 mg 12-hourly.
- Chronic bacterial prostatitis: 4–6 weeks.

### Indications and restrictions
- Pyelonephritis and complicated UTI (urinary tract infection) **only where local resistance ≤10%**; prostatitis; prophylaxis for transrectal biopsy (falling, because of resistance).
- **Not first line for uncomplicated cystitis** (FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) 2016; European restrictions 2019): use only when no alternative.

### Resistance
- *gyrA*/*parC* mutations, efflux pumps, reduced porins, plasmid *qnr* genes. Linked to ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) *E. coli* (ST131). Main cause of post-biopsy sepsis.

### Adverse effects
- GI (gastrointestinal) upset (commonest), photosensitivity, *C. difficile*.
- **FDA boxed warnings:** tendinopathy and **Achilles rupture** (2008; risk higher with age, steroids, renal failure); peripheral neuropathy (2013); disabling effects (2016); psychiatric effects and dysglycaemia (2018); aortic aneurysm and dissection (2018).
- CNS (central nervous system): seizures, confusion; QT prolongation.

### Interactions
- Antacids, iron, zinc, calcium block absorption (separate by ≥2 h). Raises theophylline and tizanidine levels; potentiates warfarin.

### Pregnancy and children
- Avoid: **cartilage damage** in immature animals. Also avoid in breastfeeding.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - DNA gyrase and topoisomerase IV; concentration-dependent.
> - Use for pyelonephritis only if local resistance ≤10%.
> - Tendon rupture, neuropathy, aortic aneurysm; avoid in pregnancy and children.

## Catheter Associated Urinary Tract Infections (CAUTI)
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition
- **CAUTI (catheter-associated urinary tract infection):** symptomatic UTI (urinary tract infection) with a catheter in place >2 days (or removed within 48 hours). Distinguish from **asymptomatic bacteriuria**, which needs no treatment.

### Epidemiology and risk factors
- Commonest hospital-acquired infection (about 75–80% of hospital UTIs); commonest cause of secondary bacteraemia.
- Bacteriuria develops at about **5–10% per catheter-day**; 100% with long-term catheters. Intermittent catheterisation: 1–3% per catheterisation.
- **Risk factors:** duration (most important), female sex, older age, diabetes, breaks in the closed system, no systemic antibiotics.

### Pathogenesis
- Entry at insertion, then **extraluminal** (along the outer surface; commoner in women) or **intraluminal** (from the drainage bag).
- **Biofilm:** bacteria in a matrix on the catheter surface, formed within minutes; it shields them from antibiotics. Urease-producers (*Proteus*) cause encrustation.
- Organisms: *E. coli*, *Pseudomonas*, *Proteus*, *Enterococcus*; long-term catheters polymicrobial.

### Diagnosis
