- Fever, rigors, flank or suprapubic pain, or delirium with no other source. Pyuria does not distinguish infection.
- Culture from a **freshly replaced catheter**, not the bag. **IDSA (Infectious Diseases Society of America): ≥10³ CFU/mL** (colony-forming units) with symptoms; surveillance (CDC (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention)) definition uses ≥10⁵.

### Prevention (bundle)
- **Avoid unnecessary catheters;** review daily, remove early. Aseptic insertion.
- **Closed, dependent drainage**; bag below bladder; no routine irrigation.
- Meatal cleaning with water; no prophylactic antibiotics (delay bacteriuria only 3–4 days, select resistance).
- Alternatives: **clean intermittent catheterisation**, condom or suprapubic catheter.
- Silver-alloy catheters reduce bacteriuria in short-term use; no proven fall in symptomatic CAUTI.
- Do not treat asymptomatic bacteriuria (except pregnancy, before urological surgery).

### Treatment
- **Replace or remove the catheter** before taking the culture and starting antibiotics, if in place >2 weeks (biofilm source).
- Empirical, then culture-directed antibiotics: **7 days** if prompt response; **10–14 days** if delayed.

**Diagram to draw:** catheter in bladder showing extraluminal and intraluminal routes and biofilm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bacteriuria 5–10% per catheter-day; duration is the key risk.
> - Diagnosis: symptoms + ≥10³ CFU/mL from a fresh catheter.
> - Remove catheter early; closed drainage; treat only symptomatic CAUTI, 7 or 10–14 days.

## Complicated urinary tract infections.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3f

### Definition
- **Complicated UTI (urinary tract infection):** infection in a **structurally or functionally abnormal tract** or a **compromised host**, or with a resistant organism; more likely to fail or recur.
- **FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) 2018:** pyuria + pathogen in urine or blood + fever, chills, flank pain, with an abnormality or catheter.
- **Uncomplicated:** healthy non-pregnant woman, normal tract.

### Complicating factors
| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Obstruction | BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, stricture, stone, tumour |
| Voiding dysfunction | Neurogenic bladder, high PVR (post-void residual), VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) |
| Foreign body / iatrogenic | Catheter, stent, nephrostomy, recent instrumentation, hospital-acquired |
| Anatomical | Diverticulum, fistula, cystocele |
| Host | **Male sex**, pregnancy, diabetes, immunosuppression, renal failure, transplant, elderly |
| Other | Symptoms >7 days; recent antibiotics |

- Each causes stasis, biofilm or weak defences.

### Organisms
- ***E. coli*** under half; more *Klebsiella*, *Proteus*, *Pseudomonas*, *Enterococcus*, *Candida*. Often **MDR (multidrug-resistant)** or ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) producers.

### Evaluation
- **Urine culture mandatory before antibiotics**; blood cultures if febrile.
- Renal function, glucose, lactate.
- **Imaging:** ultrasound first; **CT (computed tomography)** if no response in 72 hours, stones, abscess or emphysematous infection suspected.

### Treatment
- **Empirical IV (intravenous) therapy** by local resistance (EAU (European Association of Urology)): amoxicillin + aminoglycoside, 2nd-generation cephalosporin + aminoglycoside, or IV 3rd-generation cephalosporin. Avoid fluoroquinolones if used in the last 6 months or resistance >10%. Carbapenem or newer agents for ESBL (ceftolozane–tazobactam, ceftazidime–avibactam).
- **Source control is essential:** drain obstruction (**stent or nephrostomy, equally effective**), change catheter, drain abscess, treat stones later.
- Switch to oral when afebrile and stable; de-escalate by culture.
- **Duration:** usually 7–14 days (14 days in men if prostatitis not excluded); 7 days may suffice once afebrile ≥48 hours.
- **Sepsis:** antibiotics within 1 hour, fluids, vasopressors, ICU (intensive care unit).
- Correct the underlying cause, or infection recurs.

### Special forms
- **Also know:** emphysematous pyelonephritis and cystitis (diabetics); pyonephrosis; renal and perinephric abscess; XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis); CAUTI (catheter-associated UTI); funguria; unresolved bacteriuria (resistance, staghorn "critical mass").

**Diagram to draw:** Flowchart: suspect cUTI → culture → imaging → empirical IV → drain obstruction → oral step-down.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Complicated = abnormal tract or compromised host; male sex alone qualifies.
> - Culture mandatory; often MDR.
> - Drain obstruction: stent = nephrostomy.
> - 7–14 days; oral step-down when afebrile.

## ESBL producing enterobacterial UTI
**Asked as:**
- ESBL producing enterobacterial UTI — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*
- ESBL producing enterobacterial UTI. — *Practice Set 5 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)* (also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3f)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- **ESBLs (extended-spectrum β-lactamases):** **plasmid-encoded** enzymes that hydrolyse penicillins, **third-generation cephalosporins** and aztreonam. They are **inhibited by clavulanate** and spare **carbapenems** and cephamycins.
- Mainly *E. coli* and *Klebsiella* (Enterobacterales); first reported 1983.

### Mechanism and spread
- Older types **TEM** and **SHV**; now **CTX-M** dominates worldwide. Spread through the community is linked to *E. coli* **ST131**, whose hallmark is fluoroquinolone resistance (fluoroquinolone resistance is its hallmark).
- Plasmids carry co-resistance genes: **fluoroquinolones, aminoglycosides, TMP-SMX (trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole)**. So oral options are few.
- India has one of the highest prevalence rates.

### Risk factors
- Prior antibiotics (especially cephalosporins, quinolones); recent hospital stay; **indwelling catheter**, urological instrumentation; recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection); diabetes; nursing-home residence; travel to high-prevalence areas; transrectal prostate biopsy.

### Detection
- Screening: reduced susceptibility to ceftriaxone, cefotaxime or ceftazidime.
- **Phenotypic confirmation:** clavulanate synergy (**combination disc** or double-disc synergy test); automated systems.
- Genotypic PCR (polymerase chain reaction) for CTX-M.

### Treatment
| Infection | Options |
|---|---|
| **Cystitis** | **Nitrofurantoin** 5 days, **fosfomycin** 3 g single dose (E. coli), **pivmecillinam**; TMP-SMX or quinolone only if susceptible; single-dose aminoglycoside |
| **Pyelonephritis, complicated UTI** | Quinolone or TMP-SMX if susceptible; else (otherwise) **aminoglycoside** |
| **Urosepsis, bacteraemia** | **Carbapenem** (meropenem, ertapenem), then oral step-down by culture |

- **MERINO trial (2018):** in ceftriaxone-resistant *E. coli*/*Klebsiella* bacteraemia, **piperacillin–tazobactam** had higher 30-day mortality than meropenem (12.3% vs 3.7%). Avoid pip-taz for serious ESBL infection.
- **Ceftolozane–tazobactam, ceftazidime–avibactam:** active but **reserved** for carbapenem-resistant organisms (stewardship).
- **Source control:** remove or change catheters; drain obstruction.
- Treat **asymptomatic bacteriuria** only before urological procedures or in pregnancy.
- **Febrile prostatitis after transrectal biopsy:** carbapenem is favoured.

### Prevention
- **Antibiotic stewardship:** avoid broad-spectrum cephalosporins and quinolones; narrowest spectrum; shortest effective course; culture before treatment.
- Hand hygiene, contact precautions, catheter care.
- Pre-biopsy rectal swab culture for targeted prophylaxis; transperineal biopsy.

**Also know:** AmpC (not clavulanate-inhibited) and carbapenemases **KPC (*Klebsiella pneumoniae* carbapenemase)**, NDM (New Delhi metallo-β-lactamase) are wider resistance.

**Diagram to draw:** treatment flowchart from culture result to oral or IV (intravenous) choice.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Plasmid CTX-M; resists 3rd-gen (third-generation) cephalosporins; inhibited by clavulanate (clavulanate inhibits).
> - Oral: nitrofurantoin, fosfomycin, pivmecillinam.
> - Serious infection: carbapenem; MERINO: pip-taz (piperacillin–tazobactam) inferior.
> - Stewardship prevents spread.

## Lichen sclerosus (BXO, balanitis xerotica obliterans)
**Asked as:**
- Lichen sclerosis. — *Summer 2020 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*
- BXO — *Practice Set 10 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3c)

*Taken as lichen sclerosus (LS), formerly balanitis xerotica obliterans (BXO).*
*BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans) is taken as genital lichen sclerosus (LS) in the male.*

### Definition
- Chronic, progressive, **scarring inflammatory disease of genital skin**; possibly premalignant. BXO is its late stage in men.

### Epidemiology
- 6–10 times commoner in females (prepubertal and menopausal).
- Men: peak 30–50 years in one chapter, older men in another; also children (boys with phimosis). Commonly found at circumcision for phimosis after infancy. 15–20% have extragenital disease.

### Aetiology
- Cause unknown. Autoimmune links (thyroid disease, vitiligo, alopecia); familial clustering.
- **Chronic occlusion by urine** under the foreskin (dribbling, obesity) definitely increases risk.
- Infection and trauma are proposed but unproven.

### Clinical features
- White, atrophic, sclerotic plaques with loss of architecture; purpura, fissures; itch, pain, painful erection, poor stream, spraying.
- Sequence: prepuce → **phimosis** → glans → **meatal stenosis** (sentinel site) → fossa navicularis and penile urethral stricture.
- **Spares the proximal (bulbar and posterior) urethra.**
- Buried penis from loss of shaft skin.
- **Mechanism of stricture:** voiding against meatal stenosis forces urine into the glands of Littre → inflammation → spongiofibrosis.

### Evaluation
- Clinical diagnosis. **Biopsy** if doubtful or suspicious: hyperkeratosis, epidermal atrophy, basal vacuolation, dermal oedema with homogenised collagen, band-like lymphocytic infiltrate (lymphocytes).
- Uroflowmetry; **RUG (retrograde urethrogram) before meatal surgery**, because a proximal stricture often coexists (may coexist).
- **Differential:** lichen planus, Zoon balanitis, penile intraepithelial neoplasia.

### Management
| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Skin disease | **Clobetasol propionate 0.05%** daily for 1–3 months (another source: for about 3 months), then taper to twice-weekly maintenance; calcineurin inhibitors second line; retinoids for refractory disease |
| Phimosis | **Circumcision**, usually curative |
| Meatal stenosis | Dilatation with clobetasol; extended meatotomy; BMG (buccal mucosal graft) meatoplasty |
| Anterior stricture | **Oral mucosal graft, never genital skin** (LS recurs in skin); staged or single stage |
| Panurethral or unfit | **Perineal urethrostomy** (easy because the proximal urethra is spared); self-dilatation |

- Recurrence 40–50% after medical treatment (recurrence is common); restricture higher than in other aetiologies (restricture rates exceed other aetiologies); treatment follows the patient's goals.

### Malignancy
- Penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma) in **2.3–9%** on long follow-up; HPV (human papillomavirus)-independent type.
- **Lifelong follow-up**; biopsy any non-healing ulcer or nodule.

**Diagram to draw:** penis with LS at (showing) prepuce, glans, meatus and fossa involved, proximal urethra spared.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Genital skin disease; meatus is the sentinel site.
> - Spares proximal urethra; perineal urethrostomy easy.
> - Clobetasol first; oral mucosa, never skin, for repair.
> - SCC in 2.3–9%: lifelong follow-up.

## Drug Fosfomycin
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3c

### Introduction
- Bactericidal **phosphonic acid derivative** antibiotic, chemically unrelated to other classes. Oral form: **fosfomycin trometamol**; intravenous form: fosfomycin disodium.

### Mechanism of action
- Enters bacteria through glycerol-3-phosphate and glucose-6-phosphate transporters.
- Irreversibly inhibits **MurA** (enolpyruvyl transferase), blocking the **first step of peptidoglycan (cell wall) synthesis**. β-lactams and vancomycin act at later steps, so **cross-resistance is limited**.

### Pharmacokinetics
- Oral bioavailability is modest, but the drug is excreted **unchanged in urine**. One 3 g dose keeps high urinary concentrations for about 1–2 days, which permits single-dose therapy. Low tissue levels.
- Concentrations fall in renal failure; avoid at very low creatinine clearance.

### Spectrum
- **Escherichia coli**, including **ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase)** producers; most Enterobacteriaceae (Klebsiella variable); *Enterococcus* including **VRE (vancomycin-resistant enterococcus)**; staphylococci.
- **Not reliable:** *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Acinetobacter*.

### Uses in urology

| Indication | Dose |
|---|---|
| Uncomplicated cystitis in women (first-line, EAU (European Association of Urology) and IDSA (Infectious Diseases Society of America)) | 3 g oral sachet, single dose |
| Cystitis in pregnancy, asymptomatic bacteriuria (safe, category B) | 3 g single dose |
| MDR (multidrug-resistant) lower UTI (urinary tract infection) | Oral option avoiding IV (intravenous) therapy; repeated doses used off-label |
| Prophylaxis for transrectal prostate biopsy | As effective as fluoroquinolone |
| Recurrent UTI prophylaxis | 3 g every 10 days (EAU) |

- **Not for pyelonephritis** or urosepsis orally (poor tissue levels). Emerging data suggest IV fosfomycin may help in bacteraemic MDR E. coli UTI.
- Not needed as prophylaxis for flexible cystoscopy.

### Adverse effects
- Diarrhoea, GI (gastrointestinal) upset, headache, vaginitis; rare hypersensitivity. IV form: **sodium load**, hypokalaemia.

### Resistance
- Transporter mutations, MurA changes, plasmid **fosA** enzymes. Resistance develops quickly in vitro, so systemic infections use combinations.

### Evidence
- Huttner (2018) RCT (randomised controlled trial): nitrofurantoin 5 days gave higher clinical resolution than single-dose fosfomycin (70% vs 58%).

**Diagram to draw:** peptidoglycan synthesis showing fosfomycin (MurA), β-lactams and vancomycin sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Inhibits MurA: first step of cell wall synthesis; no cross-resistance.
> - 3 g single dose for cystitis; active against ESBL E. coli and VRE.
> - Not for pyelonephritis; poor Pseudomonas cover.

## Chronic Prostatitis : diagnosis, classification and management.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3c
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

### Classification: NIH (National Institutes of Health), 1999
| Category | Description |
|---|---|
| I | Acute bacterial prostatitis |
| II | **Chronic bacterial prostatitis (CBP)**: recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), **same organism**; well between episodes |
| IIIA | **CP/CPPS (chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome)**, inflammatory: leucocytes in EPS (expressed prostatic secretion), VB3 (post-massage urine) or semen |
| IIIB | CP/CPPS, non-inflammatory (split has no treatment value) |
| IV | Asymptomatic inflammatory (incidental) |

- **CP/CPPS:** pelvic pain **≥3 of the last 6 months**, no uropathogens; 90–95% of cases. Infection, neurogenic inflammation, **pelvic floor dysfunction**, stress and **central sensitisation** interact.

### Diagnosis
- **Symptoms:** perineal (commonest), testicular, suprapubic, penile or ejaculatory pain; LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms); sexual dysfunction.
- **NIH-CPSI (Chronic Prostatitis Symptom Index):** pain, urinary and quality-of-life domains.
- **DRE (digital rectal examination):** prostate tender in under half; palpate pelvic floor for **trigger points**.
- **Localisation tests:**
  - **Meares–Stamey 4-glass:** VB1 (urethra), VB2 (bladder), EPS, VB3 (prostate).
  - **2-glass** (pre- and post-massage urine): matches 4-glass in >95%.
- Urinalysis, culture, PVR (post-void residual), flow; cytology if irritative symptoms; cystoscopy if low flow or bladder pain.
- **UPOINT phenotyping:** Urinary, Psychosocial, Organ-specific, Infection, Neurological/systemic, Tenderness of muscles.

### Management
**Category II (CBP):** fluoroquinolone (lipid-soluble zwitterion, enters prostate) for **4–6 weeks** (EAU (European Association of Urology)); Chlamydia: azithromycin; resistant: TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) 3 months; refractory: suppression or TURP (transurethral resection of prostate).

**Category III: multimodal, UPOINT-directed** (monotherapy fails)
| Domain | Therapy |
|---|---|
| Urinary | α-blocker if recent onset and α-blocker naive |
| Psychosocial | CBT (cognitive behavioural therapy), stress reduction |
| Organ | 5-ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) if BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); quercetin |
| Infection | Quinolone ≥6 weeks only if treatment-naive, <1 year; no repeat courses |
| Neurological | Pregabalin, amitriptyline, duloxetine |
| Tenderness | **Pelvic floor physiotherapy, myofascial release**, biofeedback |

- **Others:** acupuncture (61.5% vs 38.3% response with sham), aerobic exercise, PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation). **Avoid** TURP or prostatectomy for pain.

**Diagram to draw:** UPOINT snowflake.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - NIH I–IV; III = pain ≥3 of 6 months, no infection.
> - CBP: same organism, fluoroquinolone 4–6 weeks.
> - CP/CPPS: NIH-CPSI to score, UPOINT to treat.

## Lymphogranuloma venereum
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition
- **LGV (lymphogranuloma venereum)** is an invasive, lymphotropic STD (sexually transmitted disease) caused by ***Chlamydia trachomatis* serovars L1, L2 and L3**.
- Of 15 serovars, A–C cause trachoma and D–K anogenital infection. The L serovars invade lymphatics, causing lymphadenitis and scarring.

### Epidemiology
- Endemic in the tropics, including India; increasing as **proctitis in MSM (men who have sex with men)**. Not more frequent or virulent in HIV (human immunodeficiency virus).

### Clinical stages

| Stage | Timing | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary** | 3–30 days | Small painless papule or ulcer, often unnoticed. Rectal inoculation → **proctitis** (bleeding, pain, tenesmus, discharge), now the commonest presentation |
| **Secondary (inguinal syndrome)** | 2–6 weeks later | Fever, malaise; **painful, matted, unilateral inguinal or femoral buboes** that may form fistulae. **Groove sign**: nodes above and below the inguinal ligament |
| **Tertiary** | Untreated | Fibrosis and lymphatic block: **genital elephantiasis** (esthiomene in women), perirectal abscess, fistulae, **rectal stricture** |

- Inguinal disease is commoner in men: the cervix and vagina drain to pelvic nodes.

### Differential diagnosis of genital ulcer
- Syphilis (painless ulcer and nodes); herpes (painful vesicles); chancroid (painful ulcer, suppurative nodes); donovanosis (beefy ulcer, no nodes); **LGV: transient painless lesion, then painful matted nodes**.

### Investigations
- **NAAT (nucleic acid amplification test)** of ulcer, rectal or node aspirate; LGV-specific genotyping confirms where available. Rectal chlamydia NAAT in all proctocolitis.
- Serology not routine. Screen for HIV, syphilis, gonorrhoea; offer PrEP (pre-exposure prophylaxis).

### Treatment
- **Doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 21 days** (first line).
- Alternatives: erythromycin 500 mg four times daily for 21 days, or azithromycin 1 g weekly for 3 weeks (pregnancy; consider test of cure by NAAT).
- Fluctuant buboes: aspirate through healthy skin to prevent sinus formation.
- **Late disease:** stricture dilatation or resection, fistula repair, reduction of elephantiasis.

**Diagram to draw:** groin with buboes above and below the inguinal ligament (groove sign).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Serovars L1–L3; invasive and lymphotropic.
> - Proctitis is now the commonest presentation.
> - Groove sign; esthiomene; rectal stricture.
> - Doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 21 days.

## Xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis) is a rare, severe, **chronic** infection causing diffuse destruction of an enlarged, non-functioning kidney, with **lipid-laden foamy macrophages** (xanthoma cells).
- Up to a fifth of nephrectomies for pyelonephritis. Peak age 50–70 years; women; diabetes is a risk factor.

### Pathogenesis
- **Triad: stone, obstruction, infection.** Stones in up to 80% (half staghorn).
- Low-virulence organisms (**Proteus** commonest, *E. coli*) in an obstructed, ischaemic kidney with an inadequate acute inflammatory response.

### Pathology
- Diffuse (>80%) or segmental (focal).
- Gross: yellow nodules, pus-filled dilated calyces, a **contracted pelvis** encased by fibrosis, frequent **perinephric extension**.
- Microscopy: sheets of foamy histiocytes with lymphocytes, plasma cells and giant cells.
- **Malek staging:** I confined to kidney; II into perinephric fat; III into paranephric tissue or retroperitoneum.

### Clinical features
- Flank pain, fever (about two-thirds), flank mass (about 60%), weight loss; persistent bacteriuria.
- Labs: anaemia, raised inflammatory markers, pyuria; liver dysfunction in up to half. One-third have **sterile urine** (prior antibiotics).

### Imaging
- **CT (computed tomography), most useful:** enlarged kidney, little or no function, central calculus in a contracted pelvis; low-density cavities with rim enhancement (**"bear paw" sign**); extrarenal spread.
- Ultrasound: enlarged kidney with hypoechoic areas. DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan quantifies lost function.
- **Differentials:** RCC (renal cell carcinoma), pyonephrosis, malakoplakia, lymphoma, tuberculosis.

### Management
- Antibiotics to stabilise; drain if septic.
- **Nephrectomy** (with perinephric fat) for diffuse disease; partial nephrectomy for focal disease.
- Surgery is difficult: dense adhesions to psoas, great vessels, bowel and diaphragm. Remove the whole inflammatory mass, because simple drainage leads to **nephrocutaneous fistula**.
- Laparoscopic nephrectomy is feasible; conversion rates are high.
- Frozen section can mimic clear cell carcinoma, and XGP can **coexist with RCC** or urothelial carcinoma. If cancer is not excluded, do nephrectomy.

**Diagram to draw:** CT sketch of an enlarged kidney with central staghorn and "bear paw" cavities.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Stone, obstruction, Proteus infection → foamy macrophages.
> - CT: non-functioning enlarged kidney, contracted pelvis, "bear paw" sign.
> - Mimics RCC; treat by complete nephrectomy, not drainage.

## EPN. Prognostic factors & conserv. mgt.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q3b
(prognostic indicators also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3c)

*Assumed meaning: EPN = emphysematous pyelonephritis.*

### Definition
- Acute **necrotising** infection of renal parenchyma and perirenal tissue by **gas-forming organisms** (*E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *Proteus*). Mostly **diabetics**; obstruction in about a quarter. Mortality 19–43%.
- **Mechanism:** high tissue glucose fermented to CO₂ in poorly perfused tissue.
- **CT (computed tomography)** is the imaging of choice.

### Prognostic factors
**1. Huang–Tseng CT classification (2000)**
| Class | Gas location |
|---|---|
| 1 | Collecting system only |
| 2 | Parenchyma only |
| 3A | Perinephric space |
| 3B | Pararenal space |
| 4 | Bilateral or solitary kidney |

**2. Wan CT pattern**
- **Type I:** streaky gas, **no fluid**: mortality 50–60%.
- **Type II:** fluid with bubbly or loculated gas: <20%. Fluid means a contained collection that can be drained.

**3. Clinical risk factors (Huang–Tseng):** **thrombocytopenia, AKI (acute kidney injury), altered sensorium, shock**.
- **Also adverse:** hypoalbuminaemia, bacteraemia, need for haemodialysis, polymicrobial infection.

### Conservative management (now standard)
**1. Resuscitation:** fluids, inotropes, ICU (intensive care unit) if shocked; **tight glucose control**; correct electrolytes.

**2. Antibiotics:** broad-spectrum IV (intravenous), adjusted to culture; usually several weeks in total.

**3. Drainage**
- **Relieve obstruction:** DJ (double-J) stent or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy).
- **PCD (percutaneous catheter drainage)** of gas and fluid collections under CT or ultrasound; multiple drains if needed; repeat CT to check.

**Algorithm**
```
All: resuscitation + glucose control + IV antibiotics + relieve obstruction
Class 1–2                      -> antibiotics ± PCD
Class 3–4, <2 risk factors     -> antibiotics + PCD
Class 3–4, >=2 risk factors,
or PCD failure                 -> nephrectomy (emergency or delayed)
```
- Emergency nephrectomy carried 40–50% mortality, so drainage-first care replaced it. Delayed nephrectomy for a non-functioning kidney on renal scan.
- **Emphysematous pyelitis** (class 1) often responds to antibiotics alone.

**Diagram to draw:** CT sketches of Huang–Tseng classes.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Huang–Tseng: 1 pelvis, 2 parenchyma, 3A perinephric, 3B pararenal, 4 bilateral/solitary.
> - Risk factors: thrombocytopenia, AKI, altered sensorium, shock.
> - Wan type I (no fluid) 50–60% mortality; drainage first, nephrectomy for failure.

## Papillary necrosis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- **RPN (renal papillary necrosis):** ischaemic necrosis of the renal papillae (inner medulla), which may slough into the collecting system.

### Pathophysiology
- The papilla is vulnerable: it depends on the **vasa recta**, has **low oxygen tension** and a **hypertonic** environment. Almost every cause reduces medullary blood flow or oxygenation.
- **Sickle cell:** hypoxia and hypertonicity make cells sickle and block the vasa recta, causing microinfarcts.
- **Analgesics:** NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) block vasodilator prostaglandins; metabolites concentrate in the medulla.
- **Diabetes:** microangiopathy plus infection.

### Causes ("POSTCARDS")
| Letter | Cause |
|---|---|
| P | Pyelonephritis |
| O | Obstruction |
| S | Sickle cell disease or trait |
| T | Tuberculosis |
| C | Cirrhosis |
| A | Analgesic abuse |
| R | Renal transplant rejection; renal vein thrombosis |
| D | **Diabetes mellitus** |
| S | Systemic vasculitis |
- **Also know:** infant dehydration and hypoxia; shock; renal candidiasis.

### Clinical features
- **Acute:** fever, flank pain, haematuria, **ureteric colic from a sloughed papilla**, sepsis; bilateral cases may cause AKI (acute kidney injury).
- **Chronic or incidental:** polyuria (poor concentration), sterile pyuria, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), CKD (chronic kidney disease). Retained papillae may **calcify** and harbour infection, including fungi.

### Investigations
- Urine microscopy and culture; **strain urine** and send tissue for histology; creatinine, glucose, haemoglobin electrophoresis.
- **CT urography or IVU (intravenous urography):** early contrast tracking into the papilla ("**ball-on-tee**", "**lobster claw**"); "**ring sign**" (contrast around a detached papilla); clubbed calyces; filling defects; triangular ring-shaped calcification.
- Retrograde pyelography when renal function is poor.
- **Differential diagnosis:** tuberculosis, medullary sponge kidney, calyceal diverticulum, upper tract urothelial carcinoma.

### Management
- **Treat the cause:** stop analgesics, control diabetes, hydrate, manage sickle crisis.
- **Culture-guided antibiotics.**
- **Obstruction by a sloughed papilla:** urgent drainage (stent or nephrostomy), then ureteroscopic extraction.
- Remove calcified retained papillae that cause persistent infection.
- **Follow-up:** renal function; analgesic abusers risk urothelial cancer.

**Diagram to draw:** calyx showing ring sign and lobster-claw appearance.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Vasa recta, low oxygen and hypertonicity make the papilla vulnerable.
> - POSTCARDS; diabetes, analgesics, sickle cell, obstruction with infection.
> - Ring sign; sloughed papilla causes colic and obstruction: drain urgently.

## Cephalsporins
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 1, Q3c

*Assumed meaning: cephalosporins (generations, spectrum, urological use).*

### Introduction and mechanism
- **β-lactam** antibiotics. They bind **PBPs (penicillin-binding proteins)** and block peptidoglycan cross-linking, so the cell wall fails: **bactericidal**, time-dependent killing.
- Mostly renal excretion (adjust dose in renal failure); **ceftriaxone** also has biliary excretion (no adjustment).
- Resistance: β-lactamases (**ESBL [extended-spectrum β-lactamase]**, AmpC), altered PBPs, reduced porin entry.

### Generations
| Generation | Examples | Spectrum | Urological use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **First** | Cefazolin (intravenous), cephalexin, cefadroxil (oral) | Gram-positive (not MRSA [methicillin-resistant *Staphylococcus aureus*]); *E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *P. mirabilis* | **Surgical prophylaxis (cefazolin)**; cystitis in pregnancy; paediatric prophylaxis |
| **Second** | Cefuroxime, cefaclor; cefoxitin, cefotetan | Wider Gram-negative; cefoxitin and cefotetan add **anaerobes** | Prophylaxis for vaginal and bowel surgery; SWL (shock-wave lithotripsy) |
| **Third** | Ceftriaxone, cefotaxime, cefixime, cefpodoxime; **ceftazidime** | Most Enterobacterales; **only ceftazidime covers *Pseudomonas*** | Pyelonephritis, urosepsis; **gonorrhoea (ceftriaxone 500 mg IM [intramuscular])**; prostate biopsy prophylaxis |
| **Fourth** | Cefepime | Gram-negatives incl. *Pseudomonas*, stable to AmpC; Gram-positive | Complicated, nosocomial UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **Fifth** | Ceftaroline | MRSA + Gram-negatives | Limited urological role |

- **Newer combinations** for complicated UTI with multidrug resistance: ceftazidime–avibactam, ceftolozane–tazobactam, cefiderocol.

### Gaps in spectrum
- **Enterococci** (intrinsically resistant; never treat with cephalosporins), *Listeria*, MRSA (except ceftaroline), atypical organisms.
- ESBL and AmpC producers hydrolyse third-generation agents.

### Adverse effects
- Hypersensitivity, less than penicillins. Cross-reactivity with third-generation agents is <1%; avoid after immediate (IgE [immunoglobulin E]-mediated) penicillin reaction.
- ***C. difficile* colitis**; "collateral damage" (selection of ESBL organisms).
- Positive Coombs test; bleeding with cefotetan and cefamandole.
- Ceftriaxone: biliary sludge; avoid in jaundiced neonates and with intravenous calcium.

### Paediatric use
- Febrile UTI in neonates: **ampicillin + third-generation cephalosporin** (covers enterococcus). Oral cefixime 8 mg/kg/day. A first-generation agent is a good narrow-spectrum choice for many outpatient UTIs.

**Diagram to draw:** β-lactam ring binding a PBP and blocking peptidoglycan cross-links.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cefazolin = standard surgical prophylaxis.
> - Ceftriaxone misses *Pseudomonas*; ceftazidime and cefepime cover it.
> - No cephalosporin covers enterococci; ESBL hydrolyses third-generation agents.

## Fungal UTI
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 18 SAQ Sheet, Q3a

### Introduction and organisms
- Fungal UTI (urinary tract infection) is rising with antibiotics, catheters and immunosuppression. **Candida** is commonest: *C. albicans*, then *C. glabrata* (often fluconazole-resistant); *C. krusei* is intrinsically resistant.
- Others in AIDS (acquired immunodeficiency syndrome) or transplant patients: Aspergillus, Cryptococcus, Mucorales, Histoplasma.

### Risk factors and routes
- Antibiotics, indwelling catheters and stents, diabetes, steroids, neutropenia, prematurity, parenteral nutrition, obstruction.
- **Ascending** (catheter) causes most candiduria. **Haematogenous** spread from candidaemia: in disseminated candidiasis the kidney is the organ most often involved.

### Clinical spectrum
- Asymptomatic candiduria (most) → cystitis → pyelonephritis → **fungus ball (bezoar)** causing obstruction → renal abscess or candidaemia.

### Diagnosis
- Microscopy: budding yeasts and pseudohyphae (may mimic red cells). Repeat culture: contamination is common.
- Children: treat when repeated cultures exceed **10,000–15,000 CFU (colony-forming units)/mL**.
- Persistent funguria: ultrasound or CT (computed tomography) for bezoar; blood cultures if high risk.

### Management
| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Asymptomatic | Remove or change catheter, stop antibiotics; no antifungal |
| Treat asymptomatic only if | Neutropenia, very-low-birth-weight infant, before a urological procedure |
| Cystitis | Fluconazole 200 mg (3 mg/kg) daily for 2 weeks |
| Pyelonephritis | Fluconazole 200–400 mg (3–6 mg/kg) daily for 2 weeks |
| Fluconazole-resistant (*C. glabrata*, *C. krusei*) | Amphotericin B deoxycholate 0.3–0.6 mg/kg/day for 1–7 days ± flucytosine |
| Resistant cystitis | Amphotericin B bladder irrigation 50 mg/L |
| Fungus ball | PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy) drainage + systemic antifungal + local amphotericin irrigation; endoscopic or open removal if persistent |

- Fluconazole concentrates in urine; **echinocandins and lipid amphotericin do not.**
- **Before procedures:** IDSA (Infectious Diseases Society of America) 2016 treats all candiduria (fluconazole 400 mg daily for several days before and after). AUA (American Urological Association) Best Practice Statement 2019 gives single-dose antifungal prophylaxis only for urothelium-breaching surgery. The two disagree.
- **Also know:** alkalinisation for bezoar; fluconazole caution under 6 months.

**Diagram to draw:** budding yeast with pseudohyphae; fungus ball in renal pelvis with nephrostomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Candida commonest; *C. glabrata* often fluconazole-resistant.
> - Asymptomatic candiduria: remove catheter, no antifungal (except neutropenia, neonate, procedure).
> - Fluconazole 2 weeks; echinocandins fail in urine.
> - Fungus ball: drain, antifungal, remove.

## Antimicrobial resistance
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 18 SAQ Sheet, Q3b
(also answered in part in 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3f)

### Definition
- AMR (antimicrobial resistance): a microbe grows despite drug levels achievable in the patient. Reported as S (sensitive), I (intermediate), R (resistant) with the MIC (minimum inhibitory concentration).
- One of the most serious global health threats.

### Types
| Type | Mechanism | Relevance |
|---|---|---|
| Intrinsic | Species lacks the target | Predictable (enterococci and cephalosporins) |
| Acquired chromosomal | Mutants selected when urine level is close to MIC | Underdosing, poor compliance |
| Plasmid (extrachromosomal) | Horizontal transfer in bowel flora | **Commonest**; multidrug resistance after antibiotics |

### Mechanisms
- **Enzymes:** β-lactamases; aminoglycoside-modifying enzymes.
- **Altered target:** penicillin-binding protein; DNA gyrase mutation (quinolones).
- **Reduced entry** (porin loss) and **efflux pumps**.
- **Bypass:** enterococci scavenge folate (escape TMP-SMX (trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole)).

### Key resistant organisms
- **ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase)** (plasmid, 1983): resists 3rd-generation cephalosporins; often co-resistant to fluoroquinolones and aminoglycosides. *E. coli* ST131 carries it.
- **AmpC; KPC (*Klebsiella pneumoniae* carbapenemase, 2001); NDM (New Delhi metallo-β-lactamase)**: carbapenem resistance.
- MRSA (methicillin-resistant *S. aureus*), VRE (vancomycin-resistant enterococci).

### Drivers in urology
- Overuse of fluoroquinolones and cephalosporins; treating ASB (asymptomatic bacteriuria); long prophylaxis; catheters, stents, stones (biofilm).
- Transrectal biopsy: fluoroquinolone-resistant rectal *E. coli* causes sepsis.

### Prevention: stewardship
1. Culture before treatment; follow the **local antibiogram**.
2. TMP-SMX only if local resistance ≤20%; oral fluoroquinolone for pyelonephritis only if ≤10%.
3. Prefer narrow, urine-confined drugs for cystitis (nitrofurantoin, fosfomycin, pivmecillinam: low resistance).
4. Shortest course; de-escalate to culture.
5. Do not treat ASB, except in pregnancy and before mucosa-breaching procedures.
6. Single-dose surgical prophylaxis; transperineal biopsy or rectal-swab-targeted prophylaxis.
7. Infection control and hand hygiene.
- **Also know:** FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2016 fluoroquinolone warning; *C. difficile*; ESBL: carbapenem, or fosfomycin/nitrofurantoin for cystitis.

### Recent advances
- New β-lactam/β-lactamase inhibitor combinations (ceftazidime–avibactam, meropenem–vaborbactam); cefiderocol; gepotidacin.

**Diagram to draw:** Gram-negative cell showing β-lactamase, porin loss, efflux pump, altered target.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Plasmid-mediated resistance is commonest.
> - ESBL: plasmid, co-resistant; KPC/NDM: carbapenem resistance.
> - TMP-SMX if resistance ≤20%; fluoroquinolone for pyelonephritis if ≤10%.
> - Stewardship: culture, narrow, short, no ASB treatment.

## Biofilm
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 18 SAQ Sheet, Q3c
(also answered in part in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3d)

### Definition
- A **structured microbial community in a self-made polymeric matrix**, adherent to a living or inert surface. The matrix contains exopolysaccharide, extracellular DNA and proteins.

### Stages of formation
1. **Conditioning film:** host proteins coat the device within minutes.
2. **Attachment:** reversible, then irreversible (pili, adhesins, flagella).
3. **Matrix production and microcolonies.**
4. **Maturation:** 3-D (three-dimensional) structure with water channels; cell-to-cell signalling (quorum sensing).
5. **Dispersion:** free bacteria seed new infection.

### Why antibiotics and immunity fail
- Poor drug penetration through the matrix.
- Slow-growing, dormant cells resist drugs that need active growth.
- Altered genes, envelope and targets.
- Survive **1000–1500 times** the usual killing concentration. Neutrophils are excluded.

### Urological examples
| Site | Point |
|---|---|
| Urethral catheter | Forms within minutes; **the catheter, not the bladder, is infected**. Proteus urease makes crystalline biofilm → encrustation and blockage |
| Ureteric stent, nephrostomy | Stent colonised more often than urine shows; nephrostomy changed every 3 months |
| Penile prosthesis, AUS (artificial urinary sphincter) | Revision of an "uninfected" device: about 10% infection |
| Infection stones | Bacteria inside struvite; antibiotics cannot sterilise |
| Chronic bacterial prostatitis | Biofilm commoner in prostatitis *E. coli*; needs longer, penetrating therapy |
| Recurrent cystitis | **Intracellular bacterial communities**: biofilm-like pods inside umbrella cells |

- Common organisms: Pseudomonas, Proteus, *E. coli*, Enterococcus, coagulase-negative staphylococci, Candida.

### Prevention and management
- Avoid devices; shortest dwell; aseptic insertion; closed dependent drainage.
- **Change the catheter** when treating symptomatic CAUTI (catheter-associated UTI (urinary tract infection)); remove infected stent or implant.
- Silver-alloy catheters reduce bacteriuria, not clinical UTI.
- Implants: antibiotic-coated devices, no-touch technique, **antiseptic washout** at revision (infection about 10% → 3%).
- Stones: complete clearance. Chemodissolution for catheter encrustation. Macrolides penetrate biofilm (prostatitis).
- **Also know (research):** quorum-sensing inhibitors, bacteriophages, new coatings.

**Diagram to draw:** five-stage biofilm cycle on a catheter surface.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Biofilm: microbes in self-made matrix on a surface.
> - Survives 1000–1500 times normal antibiotic levels.
> - Treat CAUTI by changing the catheter.
> - Revision washout cuts prosthesis infection from about 10% to 3%.

## Renal mucormycosis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3c

### Introduction
- Mucormycosis: an **angioinvasive** fungal infection by Mucorales (*Rhizopus* commonest; *Mucor*, *Lichtheimia*).
- Kidney involvement occurs in **disseminated** disease, or as **isolated renal mucormycosis**, reported particularly from India, sometimes in apparently immunocompetent young adults. Cases rose during the 2021 COVID-19 (coronavirus disease 2019) wave.

### Risk factors
- Uncontrolled diabetes, especially **ketoacidosis**; corticosteroids; neutropenia and haematological cancer; transplantation; iron overload or deferoxamine; intravenous drug use; AIDS (acquired immunodeficiency syndrome).

### Pathogenesis
- Spores inhaled or ingested → **haematogenous** seeding of the kidney.
- Hyphae invade arteries and veins → **thrombosis → infarction and haemorrhagic necrosis**.
- Acidosis and free iron favour growth (hence the ketoacidosis risk).

### Clinical features
- Fever, flank pain, tender flank mass, haematuria, pyuria with sterile bacterial cultures.
- **Bilateral** disease → AKI (acute kidney injury) or anuria.
- Suspect when "pyelonephritis" fails antibiotics in a high-risk patient.

### Investigations
- **CECT (contrast-enhanced computed tomography):** enlarged kidney(s), wedge-shaped or diffuse **non-enhancing** areas (infarcts), perinephric stranding or collection, renal vessel thrombosis.
- **Tissue diagnosis** (biopsy or nephrectomy specimen): **broad, ribbon-like, aseptate or pauciseptate hyphae branching at right angles**, invading vessels.
- KOH (potassium hydroxide) mount, calcofluor stain, fungal culture (often negative), PCR (polymerase chain reaction).
- Differential: *Aspergillus* has narrow septate hyphae with acute-angle branching.
- Image sinuses, lungs, brain.

### Management (three pillars)
1. **Reverse predisposition:** control glucose, correct ketoacidosis, taper steroids and immunosuppression, stop deferoxamine.
2. **Early surgical debridement:** nephrectomy for a unilateral necrotic kidney; drain collections.
3. **Antifungal:** **liposomal amphotericin B 5 mg/kg/day** first line (ECMM/MSG ERC (European Confederation of Medical Mycology/Mycoses Study Group Education and Research Consortium) guideline, 2019). Step-down or salvage: **posaconazole or isavuconazole**.
- **Fluconazole, voriconazole and echinocandins are inactive.**
- Bilateral: antifungal plus dialysis.

### Prognosis
- Mortality is high, especially with bilateral or disseminated disease and delayed diagnosis.

**Diagram to draw:** right-angle branching aseptate hypha invading a renal vessel.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Angioinvasion → infarction; diabetic ketoacidosis is the key risk.
> - CECT: non-enhancing enlarged kidney; histology: broad aseptate right-angle hyphae.
> - Liposomal amphotericin B 5 mg/kg/day plus nephrectomy and glucose control.
> - Voriconazole and echinocandins do not work.

## Hydatid disease of kidney
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3d

### Introduction
- Cystic echinococcosis (CE) by *Echinococcus granulosus*, a cestode. Dog: definitive host; sheep and humans: intermediate.
- Ingested eggs → liver (commonest), lung, then **kidney (third site, about 2–3%)** via the systemic circulation. Usually single, unilateral, cortical.

### Pathology
- Wall: **pericyst** (host fibrous layer), **laminated membrane** (ectocyst), **germinal layer** (endocyst) producing brood capsules, scolices and daughter cysts ("hydatid sand").
- **Closed** versus **open** cyst (ruptured into the collecting system → **hydatiduria**).

### Clinical features
- Grows 1–2 cm a year; long asymptomatic; flank mass, dull pain.
- **Hydatiduria** ("grape-skin" membranes in urine; pathognomonic) with colic, in a minority.
- Infection; rupture (anaphylaxis). Renal function usually preserved.

### Investigations
- **Serology** (ELISA (enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay)) is adjunctive only (sensitivity 60–90%); eosinophilia; Casoni test obsolete.
- **Ultrasound and CT (computed tomography):** thick-walled cyst, wall calcification, daughter cysts, detached membranes ("water-lily sign"). Stage guides treatment.

| WHO (World Health Organization) stage | Appearance | Activity |
|---|---|---|
| CE1 | Unilocular, double-line wall | Active |
| CE2 | Multivesicular, daughter cysts | Active |
| CE3a / CE3b | Detached membranes / daughter cysts in solid matrix | Transitional |
| CE4 / CE5 | Solid "ball of wool" / calcified wall | Inactive |

- Retrograde pyelogram shows communication; image liver and lungs. **Never aspirate blindly** (anaphylaxis, seeding).

### Management
- **Albendazole 400 mg twice daily**, 1–6 months: alone for small or inoperable cysts; around surgery to reduce seeding (± praziquantel).
- **Surgery is the mainstay** (open, laparoscopic or robotic), nephron-sparing when possible:
  1. Pack the field with scolicide-soaked swabs (hypertonic saline, povidone-iodine).
  2. Inject scolicide only if **not** communicating (chemical injury to the collecting system).
  3. **Pericystectomy, partial nephrectomy or deroofing**; close communications; omentoplasty or drain.
  4. **Nephrectomy** for a non-functioning kidney.
- **PAIR (puncture, aspiration, injection, re-aspiration):** chapter says liver only, not kidney; some series report percutaneous renal treatment.
- Calcified inactive cysts (CE4, CE5) may be observed.

**Diagram to draw:** layers of a hydatid cyst with daughter cysts and a communication into a calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Kidney: third site (about 2–3%).
> - Hydatiduria (grape-skin membranes) is pathognomonic of a communicating cyst.
> - Albendazole 400 mg twice daily around surgery; no scolicide if communicating.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Genitourinary infections, common organisms, bacteraemia and urosepsis: pathophysiology and management (LAQ)

- *E. coli* ~85% community UTI; P pili cause pyelonephritis; *Proteus* causes struvite; *Enterococcus* resists cephalosporins.
- Sepsis = SOFA rise ≥2; septic shock = vasopressor for MAP ≥65 plus lactate >2 mmol/L.
- Obstruction plus infection is the emergency; bladder urine may be sterile.
- Hour-1: lactate, cultures, antibiotics, 30 mL/kg, noradrenaline (SSC 2021).
- Infected obstructed kidney: drain urgently; no definitive stone surgery in sepsis.
- Stent and nephrostomy equally effective (stent = nephrostomy for drainage); delay definitive stone surgery.

#### Describe clinical implication of AIDS on the Genito-Urinary Tract? (LAQ)

- AIDS = CD4 <200; UTI risk rises as CD4 falls.
- HIV-TB: disseminated, less fibrosis, fewer strictures.
- Indinavir stones: radiolucent, may be CT-invisible; stop drug and hydrate.
- PEP within 72 hours for 28 days; circumcision cuts acquisition 50–60%.

#### Principles of antimicrobial therapy in UTI (SAQ)

- Recurrent = ≥2 in 6 months or ≥3 in 12 months.
- Cystitis: nitrofurantoin 5 d, fosfomycin single, TMP-SMX 3 d (resistance ≤20%).
- Pyelonephritis: ciprofloxacin 7 d or levofloxacin 5 d (FQ resistance ≤10%).

#### Renal and perinephric abscess (SAQ)

- Now mostly ascending Gram-negative abscess in a damaged kidney.
- Perinephric: within Gerota fascia; diabetes in a third.
- Fever beyond 4–5 days of antibiotics: get a CT.
- CT is the investigation of choice; follow until resolution.
- ≤3 cm antibiotics; 3–5 cm either; >5 cm percutaneous drainage.
- Percutaneous drainage first line; nephrectomy if non-functioning.

#### Ciprofloxacin (SAQ)

- DNA gyrase and topoisomerase IV; concentration-dependent.
- Use for pyelonephritis only if local resistance ≤10%.
- Tendon rupture, neuropathy, aortic aneurysm; avoid in pregnancy and children.

#### Catheter Associated Urinary Tract Infections (CAUTI) (SAQ)

- Bacteriuria 5–10% per catheter-day; duration is the key risk.
- Diagnosis: symptoms + ≥10³ CFU/mL from a fresh catheter.
- Remove catheter early; closed drainage; treat only symptomatic CAUTI, 7 or 10–14 days.

#### Complicated urinary tract infections. (SAQ)

- Complicated = abnormal tract or compromised host; male sex alone qualifies.
- Culture mandatory; often MDR.
- Drain obstruction: stent = nephrostomy.
- 7–14 days; oral step-down when afebrile.

#### ESBL producing enterobacterial UTI (SAQ)

- Plasmid CTX-M; resists 3rd-gen (third-generation) cephalosporins; inhibited by clavulanate (clavulanate inhibits).
- Oral: nitrofurantoin, fosfomycin, pivmecillinam.
- Serious infection: carbapenem; MERINO: pip-taz (piperacillin–tazobactam) inferior.
- Stewardship prevents spread.

#### Lichen sclerosus (BXO, balanitis xerotica obliterans) (SAQ)

- Genital skin disease; meatus is the sentinel site.
- Spares proximal urethra; perineal urethrostomy easy.
- Clobetasol first; oral mucosa, never skin, for repair.
- SCC in 2.3–9%: lifelong follow-up.

#### Drug Fosfomycin (SAQ)

- Inhibits MurA: first step of cell wall synthesis; no cross-resistance.
- 3 g single dose for cystitis; active against ESBL E. coli and VRE.
- Not for pyelonephritis; poor Pseudomonas cover.

#### Chronic Prostatitis : diagnosis, classification and management. (SAQ)

- NIH I–IV; III = pain ≥3 of 6 months, no infection.
- CBP: same organism, fluoroquinolone 4–6 weeks.
- CP/CPPS: NIH-CPSI to score, UPOINT to treat.

#### Lymphogranuloma venereum (SAQ)

- Serovars L1–L3; invasive and lymphotropic.
- Proctitis is now the commonest presentation.
- Groove sign; esthiomene; rectal stricture.
- Doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 21 days.

#### Xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis. (SAQ)

- Stone, obstruction, Proteus infection → foamy macrophages.
- CT: non-functioning enlarged kidney, contracted pelvis, "bear paw" sign.
- Mimics RCC; treat by complete nephrectomy, not drainage.

#### EPN. Prognostic factors & conserv. mgt. (SAQ)

- Huang–Tseng: 1 pelvis, 2 parenchyma, 3A perinephric, 3B pararenal, 4 bilateral/solitary.
- Risk factors: thrombocytopenia, AKI, altered sensorium, shock.
- Wan type I (no fluid) 50–60% mortality; drainage first, nephrectomy for failure.

#### Papillary necrosis. (SAQ)

- Vasa recta, low oxygen and hypertonicity make the papilla vulnerable.
- POSTCARDS; diabetes, analgesics, sickle cell, obstruction with infection.
- Ring sign; sloughed papilla causes colic and obstruction: drain urgently.

#### Cephalsporins (SAQ)

- Cefazolin = standard surgical prophylaxis.
- Ceftriaxone misses *Pseudomonas*; ceftazidime and cefepime cover it.
- No cephalosporin covers enterococci; ESBL hydrolyses third-generation agents.

#### Fungal UTI (SAQ)

- Candida commonest; *C. glabrata* often fluconazole-resistant.
- Asymptomatic candiduria: remove catheter, no antifungal (except neutropenia, neonate, procedure).
- Fluconazole 2 weeks; echinocandins fail in urine.
- Fungus ball: drain, antifungal, remove.

#### Antimicrobial resistance (SAQ)

- Plasmid-mediated resistance is commonest.
- ESBL: plasmid, co-resistant; KPC/NDM: carbapenem resistance.
- TMP-SMX if resistance ≤20%; fluoroquinolone for pyelonephritis if ≤10%.
- Stewardship: culture, narrow, short, no ASB treatment.

#### Biofilm (SAQ)

- Biofilm: microbes in self-made matrix on a surface.
- Survives 1000–1500 times normal antibiotic levels.
- Treat CAUTI by changing the catheter.
- Revision washout cuts prosthesis infection from about 10% to 3%.

#### Renal mucormycosis (SAQ)

- Angioinvasion → infarction; diabetic ketoacidosis is the key risk.
- CECT: non-enhancing enlarged kidney; histology: broad aseptate right-angle hyphae.
- Liposomal amphotericin B 5 mg/kg/day plus nephrectomy and glucose control.
- Voriconazole and echinocandins do not work.

#### Hydatid disease of kidney (SAQ)

- Kidney: third site (about 2–3%).
- Hydatiduria (grape-skin membranes) is pathognomonic of a communicating cyst.
- Albendazole 400 mg twice daily around surgery; no scolicide if communicating.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Primary (congenital) VUR: VUJ embryology, pathophysiology, natural history and management
**Asked as:**
- Describe embryological development of VU Junction and outline management of primary VUR? — *Summer 2020 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- B/l congenital VUR - Pathophysiology and natural history — *Practice Set 1 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)* (VUR management also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q2 and 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q2)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*"B/l congenital VUR" is read as bilateral primary (congenital) VUR (vesicoureteral reflux), often detected antenatally.*

### Part A: Embryology of the VUJ (vesicoureteric junction)
1. **Ureteric bud (weeks 4–6)** arises from the caudal mesonephric (Wolffian) duct: ureter and duct form a "Y", the common nephric duct is the stem.
2. **Reciprocal induction:** the bud enters the metanephric blastema, branches into the collecting system and induces nephrons.
3. **Weeks 5–6:** the urorectal septum divides the cloaca into urogenital sinus (UGS) and anorectal canal.
4. **Absorption:** the common nephric duct is taken into the UGS (current view: removed by **apoptosis**), so ureter and duct open separately.
5. **Rotation (Mackie and Stephens):** the ureteric orifice moves **cranially and laterally** to the bladder base; the duct stays caudal as the ejaculatory duct (Gartner duct in females).
6. **Trigone:** classically ureteral; lineage tracing shows mostly bladder muscle. **Chwalla membrane** over the orifice ruptures; persistence explains ureterocele.
7. **Maturation:** ureteric muscle joins the detrusor as **Waldeyer's sheath**; the tunnel lengthens with growth, so VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) resolves with age.

| Bud origin | Orifice | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Normal | Trigone corner | Tunnel length to diameter about **5:1** (Paquin); refluxing junctions about 1.4:1 |
| Too low (early contact) | High, **lateral** | Short tunnel → **primary VUR**; abnormal bud–blastema contact → dysplasia |
| Too high (late contact) | Caudal, **medial** | **Ectopic ureter** (bladder neck, urethra, Wolffian structures), often obstructed |
| Two buds | Duplex | **Weigert–Meyer rule:** upper pole caudal-medial (ectopia, ureterocele); lower pole lateral (reflux) |

- **Antireflux mechanism:** passive flap valve, ureteric peristalsis, low bladder pressure.
- **Concept:** bud position fixes both orifice and kidney, so high-grade VUR and dysplasia coexist.

### Part B: Pathophysiology of (bilateral) primary VUR
- **Embryology:** a ureteric bud arising too early over-rotates, so the orifice lies **high and lateral** with a short tunnel. Its abnormal contact with the metanephros causes **renal dysplasia**, so high-grade VUR and dysplasia coexist.
- **Failed UVJ (ureterovesical junction) flap valve:** filling normally compresses the intramural ureter against detrusor backing. Tunnel length to diameter is **5:1** normally versus 1.4:1 in refluxing units (Paquin). High infant male voiding pressure and BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction) add a secondary component.

| Renal damage | Reflux-associated dysplasia | Acquired scar |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Abnormal bud | Infected urine plus VUR |
| Typical patient | Boy, grade IV–V, antenatal hydronephrosis | Girl, recurrent febrile UTI (urinary tract infection), BBD |
| Preventable | No | Yes |

- **Intrarenal reflux** through compound polar papillae needs only 2 mmHg in neonates, so scars are polar. **Sterile reflux rarely scars** unless pressure is high.
- **Also know:** massive bilateral VUR gives the megacystis–megaureter association.

**Diagram to draw:** long oblique tunnel versus short lateral one.

### Part C: Management of primary VUR
- **Primary:** congenitally short tunnel with a normal bladder. **Secondary:** high bladder pressure (PUV (posterior urethral valves), neurogenic bladder, BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction)).
- In about 30% of children with UTI (urinary tract infection) and about 30% of siblings.
- **Infection, not reflux, injures the kidney.** The aim is to prevent febrile UTI and scarring while VUR resolves.

**International grading (VCUG, voiding cystourethrogram)**
| Grade | Findings |
|---|---|
| I | Ureter only, not dilated |
| II | Up to pelvis and calyces; no dilatation (undilated) |
| III | Mild–moderate dilatation; minimal forniceal blunting |
| IV | Moderate dilatation and tortuosity |
| V | Gross dilatation and tortuosity; **papillary impressions lost** |

**Evaluation**
- Urine culture; BP (blood pressure), creatinine, urine protein; BBD history.
- **RBUS (renal and bladder ultrasound)**; VCUG (gold standard); nuclear cystogram for follow-up; **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan** for scars.

**Natural history**
- Resolution comes from **elongation of the intravesical ureter** with growth. Grade II resolves in 63–85%; grade III in about 50%; **grades IV–V and bilateral grade III rarely** (International Reflux Study). Younger age resolves better.
- **Bilaterality and older age delay resolution:** persistence is highest in bilateral grade IV, and in bilateral grade III at 6–10 years. Infants with grade IV–V may still resolve.
- **Consequences of bilateral damage:** adult hypertension in 34–38% of scarred children; CKD (chronic kidney disease), with reflux nephropathy a leading paediatric transplant diagnosis; poor growth; pre-eclampsia (worse with bilateral scars). Correction cannot reverse dysplasia.

**Risk stratification:** high risk = female, under 1 year, bilateral, grade III–V, BBD, scars, multiple febrile UTIs.

**Medical management**
- **Treat BBD first:** timed voiding, constipation treatment, anticholinergics.
- **CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis):** one low dose at night. Trimethoprim or amoxicillin under 2 months; TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) or nitrofurantoin over 2 months.
- Boys with phimosis: circumcision or topical steroid (about 4 circumcisions prevent one UTI).
- **RIVUR (Randomized Intervention for Children with Vesicoureteral Reflux, 2014):** recurrent UTI fell from 23.6% to 12.9% on TMP-SMX; **scarring unchanged**.
- **Swedish Reflux Trial** (grade III–IV): febrile UTI in girls 19% on CAP, 23% after endoscopy, 57% on surveillance; no difference in boys.
- IRSC (International Reflux Study in Children), Birmingham: surgery equalled medical care for scarring.
- Low-risk children: observation without CAP.

**Indications for correction:** breakthrough febrile UTI on CAP; new scars or falling function; persistent high grade; non-adherence; parental choice.

**Endoscopic injection:** Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid) by STING (subureteric Teflon injection) or HIT (hydrodistension implantation technique); about 90% for low grade after one injection (see 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3c).

**Ureteric reimplantation:** open success **95.9%** (Elder 1997).
| Technique | Tunnel | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Cohen** (intravesical) | Cross-trigonal | Commonest; over 98%; later ureteroscopy difficult |
| Politano–Leadbetter | New hiatus above old | Long tunnel; blind hiatus risks bowel |
| Glenn–Anderson | Advanced towards bladder neck | Least kinking; short tunnel |
| **Lich–Gregoir** (extravesical) | Detrusorotomy | No haematuria or spasm; **retention up to 10% after bilateral** |
| RALUR-EV (robotic extravesical) | Y-shaped detrusorotomy | Less pain; approaching open results |

- Ultrasound at 6–12 weeks. Contralateral VUR in about 1 in 10, usually resolves.
- **Guidelines:** AUA (American Urological Association) 2010/2017 and EAU/ESPU (European Association of Urology/European Society for Paediatric Urology): treat BBD, CAP for high risk, correct after breakthrough infection.
- **Follow-up:** BP, growth; scarred women risk pre-eclampsia.

```
Treat BBD -> low risk: observe | high risk: CAP
Breakthrough UTI or new scar -> injection or reimplant
```

**Diagram to draw:** "Y" of ureteric bud and mesonephric duct with orifice migration; grades I–V.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Low (early) bud → lateral orifice → VUR plus dysplasia; high (late) bud → ectopia.
> - One embryological fault: lateral orifice, short tunnel, dysplasia.
> - Tunnel length to diameter 5:1 (Paquin).
> - Dysplasia (boys, congenital) versus scar (girls, infection).
> - Grade IV–V and bilateral grade III rarely resolve.
> - Scarred children: 34–38% adult hypertension.
> - RIVUR: CAP halves recurrent UTI, not scarring.
> - Open reimplantation 95.9%; Cohen over 98%.

## Urological evaluation and management of pediatric kidney transplant patient.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q2

### Introduction
- Children needing KRT (kidney replacement therapy) often have CAKUT (congenital anomalies of the kidney and urinary tract). The urologist must ensure that the bladder that destroyed the native kidneys does not destroy the graft.
- **Five goals:** unobstructed drainage; a capacious, low-pressure reservoir with social continence; volitional emptying; no infection; the fewest operations.

### Causes of ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) in children
- Commonest age at transplant 13–17 years.
- **Younger:** dysplasia, hypoplasia, aplasia and obstructive uropathy, mainly PUV (posterior urethral valves). **Older:** FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis) and neuropathic bladder.

### Pretransplant evaluation
- **Every child:** urological history, renal ultrasound, uroflow with PVR (post-void residual) if toilet-trained.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram)** only with neuropathic bladder, PUV, VUR (vesicoureteral reflux), previous lower-tract surgery, recurrent febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) or unexplained hydronephrosis.
- **Urodynamics** for neuropathic bladder, PUV or large ureterocele, refractory voiding dysfunction, high-grade hydronephrosis or recurrent UTI. They measure capacity, compliance, detrusor activity, emptying and sphincter function, and are repeated.

```
History positive → ultrasound + VCUG → urodynamics
  Poor capacity/compliance → anticholinergic, CIC, cycling → still poor → augment
  Poor emptying → CIC ± catheterisable stoma
  Adequate → clear for transplant
History negative → ultrasound + PVR → normal → clear
```
- An anuric child with no urological history needs no further tests.

### Pretransplant management
**1. Bladder optimisation (drugs → CIC → cycling → augmentation)**
- Commonest problem: low capacity, detrusor overactivity, poor compliance. High pressure harms the kidney even without reflux.
- **Target:** age-expected capacity at detrusor pressure **<40 cm H₂O**.
- Antimuscarinics (oxybutynin titrated; solifenacin), mirabegron, intradetrusor botulinum toxin; α-blockers aid emptying. Better storage may worsen emptying, so add **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)**. Prove volitional emptying by repeated low PVRs.
- A family's adherence to CIC predicts adherence to immunosuppression.

**2. Valve bladder versus neuropathic bladder**
- PUV bladder moves from overactivity to **myogenic failure**: postpone augmentation. Neuropathic bladder may deteriorate under high post-transplant urine output: reconstruct earlier.

**3. Defunctionalised bladder** (anuria or diversion)
- **Bladder cycling:** catheter instillation of increasing saline volumes, with drugs, until age-expected capacity; confirm by urodynamics. **Never use the graft to test the bladder.**

**4. Augmentation**
- Indication: cannot reach **>75% expected capacity at <40 cm H₂O** with CIC every 3–4 hours despite maximal therapy. It does not clearly raise transplant complications.

| Segment | Point |
|---|---|
| Dilated ureter (ureterocystoplasty) | Explore first: urothelium, no mucus or acidosis |
| Ileum, sigmoid | Most used; mucus, stones, infection, malignancy; lifelong follow-up |
| Stomach | Haematuria–dysuria syndrome in anuric patients; rarely used |

- Continent stoma (Mitrofanoff) if the urethra is sensate or difficult; site it medial to the planned incision. Allow **6–12 weeks** healing before transplant.

**5. Infection, nephrectomy and dialysis**
- Treat stasis (double voiding, CIC); distinguish colonisation from UTI; consider circumcision in boys with valves.
- **Native nephrectomy** (default is to keep): uncontrolled hypertension, nephrotic syndrome, massive polyuria, recurrent pyelonephritis, large stones, malignancy risk, very large kidneys, massive VUR. Retroperitoneal route keeps PD (peritoneal dialysis).
- Keep a normal native ureter for later ureteroureterostomy. Intraperitoneal surgery on PD needs temporary haemodialysis.

### At transplantation
- Retroperitoneal Gibson incision; children **<15 kg** may need aorta–caval anastomosis, sometimes transperitoneal.
- **Ureter:** extravesical non-refluxing ureteroneocystostomy (3–4 cm detrusorotomy) preferred; Barry tunnel; ureteroureterostomy to a normal native ureter.
- **Stent:** selective; double-J removed at 4–6 weeks, or early removal at 5–7 days.

### Post-transplant complications

| Complication | Rate | Management |
|---|---|---|
| Ureteric obstruction | 6% | Usually distal ischaemic stenosis; >half within 100 days. Stent or nephrostomy; balloon dilatation; open repair (native ureter, bladder flap) |
| Urine leak | 3% | Early; drain creatinine; catheter, nephrostomy, stent, then reimplantation |
| Graft VUR | 2% (significant) | Febrile UTI → reimplant; asymptomatic → observe; injection works in one-third |
| Stones | 2% | Treat if >1 cm and growing, obstructing or infected |

- **Rising creatinine with hydronephrosis:** catheter drainage 1–2 weeks (bladder cause?) → biopsy if rejection is suspected → stent or nephrostomy. Obstruction and rejection can coexist.
- Also: lymphocele, **PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder)**, graft thrombosis (a leading cause of early graft loss), late infection reflecting bladder dysfunction.

### Follow-up
- Lifelong ultrasound, PVR and bladder surveillance; recurrent UTIs lower graft GFR (glomerular filtration rate).

**Diagram to draw:** extravesical ureteroneocystostomy with detrusor flaps closed over the ureter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder target: age-expected capacity at <40 cm H₂O; augment if <75% despite CIC.
> - PUV bladder heads to myogenic failure; neuropathic bladder needs earlier reconstruction.
> - Never test a bladder with a graft; cycle the defunctionalised bladder.
> - Stenosis 6%, leak 3%, stones 2%, significant reflux 2%.

## Testicular development, gonadal descent, abnormalities of descent and bilateral undescended testis
**Asked as:**
- Write a note on the embryological descent of the gonads, explaining the various phases of descent. Outline the evaluation and management of bilateral undescended testis in a 3 year old child. — *Winter 2024 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)* (also answered in part in 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3a, the treatment algorithm)
- Describe developmental embryology of testes and its physiological descent. Mention various abnormalities associated with testicular descent. — *Practice Set 14 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)* (descent also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q2; maldescent in Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q3b)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

### Development of the testis
- **Indifferent gonad:** the gonadal (germinal) ridge forms at **4 weeks** on the medial urogenital ridge. It has four components: coelomic epithelium, underlying mesenchyme, adjacent mesonephros and **PGCs (primordial germ cells)**.
- PGCs arise in the **yolk sac wall in week 3** and migrate along the dorsal mesentery of the hindgut to the ridge (about 6 weeks).
- **SRY (sex-determining region Y)** on the short arm of Y, partly activated by SF1 (steroidogenic factor 1), switches on **SOX9** in pre-Sertoli cells. Sertoli cells then appear (about 40 days). Without SRY no Sertoli cells form and the gonad becomes an ovary.
- Sertoli cells enclose germ cells as **testicular cords (about 6 weeks)**, which canalise into seminiferous tubules.
- **Rete testis** forms with the cords and joins **5–12 mesonephric tubules**, which become efferent ductules. Full continuity (tubules, rete, efferent ductules, epididymis) is established by the **third month**.
- The testis turns round and a thick **tunica albuginea** forms. Testes become spherical after 7–8 weeks; ovaries stay oblong.

| Hormone | Cell | Action | Male remnants |
|---|---|---|---|
| **AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone)** | Sertoli | Müllerian regression, weeks 8–10 | Appendix testis, prostatic utricle |
| **Testosterone** | Leydig (from 8 weeks; driven by placental hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin), later fetal LH (luteinising hormone)) | Wolffian duct to epididymis, vas, seminal vesicle (weeks 8–12) | Appendix epididymis, paradidymis |
| **DHT (dihydrotestosterone)** | Formed locally by 5α-reductase type 2 | External genitalia, prostate, scrotum (fused by 10–12 weeks) | – |
| **INSL3 (insulin-like peptide 3)** | Leydig | Gubernacular swelling (via RXFP2 receptor) | – |

### Gonadal (physiological) descent: starting point
- At **7–8 weeks** the gonad (testis) lies near the kidney (upper lumbar), held by the **cranial suspensory ligament (CSL)** above and the **gubernaculum** below. The gubernaculum appears at 7 weeks, attaches to the **epididymis** and runs to the future inguinal canal.
- **Female:** CSL persists; the gubernaculum becomes the ovarian and round ligaments; the ovary descends only into the pelvis.

### Phases of testicular descent (classic two-phase model)
| | Transabdominal phase | Inguinoscrotal phase |
|---|---|---|
| Timing | Weeks 10–15 | From the 7th month; inguinal passage rarely before 22 weeks, most testes scrotal after 27 weeks |
| Events | **CSL regresses**; **gubernaculum swells** (outgrowth); its cord shortens, holding the testis at the internal ring while the kidney and abdomen grow away (the kidney moves cranially) | Gubernaculum migrates **3–5 cm** to the scrotum; **processus vaginalis** forms within it; bulb then regresses; processus closes |
| Control | CSL regression by **androgen**; swelling by **INSL3 (insulin-like peptide 3)** from Leydig cells, via its receptor RXFP2 (occurs even in androgen resistance) | **Androgen**, mainly (mostly) indirectly via the **genitofemoral nerve (GFN)** releasing **CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide)**; intra-abdominal pressure |
| Failure gives | Intra-abdominal testis | Canalicular or ectopic testis |

- **Modern view:** the "transabdominal descent" is largely relative, not true migration. Barteczko and Jacob describe **five phases** (5 weeks to 28 weeks). The gubernaculum **swells to make room** (outgrowth), then **shrinks to let the testis pass (follow)** (regression). Testosterone (peak 14–16 weeks) and INSL3 (15–17 weeks) act together on gubernacular and cremaster growth (myogenesis).
- Spontaneous postnatal descent occurs mainly in the first 3–6 months.
- **Also know:** AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone) defects (persistent Müllerian duct syndrome) cause cryptorchidism; Insl3-knockout mice are cryptorchid; scrotum forms by 10–12 weeks under DHT (dihydrotestosterone).

| Failed step | Result |
|---|---|
| CSL retained | Intra-abdominal testis |
| Abnormal or ectopic gubernacular attachment | Canalicular testis; ectopic (superficial inguinal pouch, perineal, femoral, prepenile) |
| Processus not closed | Hernia, hydrocele (patent in 87% of unilateral cases) |
| Fibrous remnant of processus / processus not fully involuted | Acquired (ascending) testis; acquired cryptorchidism |

### Abnormalities of descent

| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| **Undescended (cryptorchid)** | Arrested on the normal path: abdominal, "peeping" at the ring, canalicular, prescrotal. 1–4% of term boys, up to 45% preterm; 75–80% palpable; 60–70% unilateral |
| **Ectopic** | Off the path: **superficial inguinal pouch** (commonest), perineal, femoral, prepubic, penile, contralateral scrotum |
| **Retractile** | Scrotal testis pulled up by the cremaster; stays down after sustained traction; some become acquired |
| **Acquired (ascending)** | Documented scrotal earlier; diagnosed at 8–11 years; fibrous processus remnant |
| Recurrent, secondary, iatrogenic | Re-ascent after spontaneous descent; after hernia repair or orchidopexy |
| **Vanishing** vs agenesis | Lost after formation (perinatal torsion; monorchia, anorchia) vs never formed |

### Associated anomalies and sequelae
- **Testicular:** **TTE (transverse testicular ectopia)**, polyorchidism, splenogonadal fusion; epididymal non-fusion.
- **PMDS (persistent Müllerian duct syndrome):** mutation of AMH or its receptor (AMHR2); bilateral cryptorchidism or TTE.
- **Genital and DSD (differences of sex development):** hypospadias (12–24%); bilateral non-palpable testes need karyotype and 17-OHP (17-hydroxyprogesterone) to exclude **CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)**.
- **Syndromes:** prune belly (all), Klinefelter, androgen insensitivity, hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism, cerebral palsy, gastroschisis, omphalocele, myelomeningocele, PUV (posterior urethral valves).
- **Sequelae:** subfertility (worse if bilateral), **TGCT (testicular germ cell tumour) 2–5-fold**, torsion, hernia.
- **Treatment in brief:** orchidopexy from 6 months, by 18 months; laparoscopy for non-palpable; no imaging, no hormones.

### Bilateral undescended testis at 3 years: evaluation
- This is **late presentation**; surgery is advised from 6 months, preferably by 12 months and by 18 months at the latest.
- **History:** prematurity, testes once scrotal (acquired), hernia, family history, salt-wasting illness.
- **Examination:** warm room; supine, then cross-legged or squatting. Sweep from the anterior superior iliac spine down the canal to find the lowest position; check the superficial inguinal pouch. **Retractile** testis stays in the scrotum after gentle traction. Note penis size, meatus (hypospadias), scrotal development, syndromic features.
- **Bilateral palpable:** no investigations needed; plan orchidopexy.
- **Bilateral non-palpable (or with hypospadias or micropenis): treat as DSD (difference of sex development) until proven otherwise.**
  - **Karyotype**; electrolytes and **17-OHP (17-hydroxyprogesterone)** to exclude **CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)** in a virilised 46,XX child.
  - **Hormones:** LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), testosterone, **AMH and inhibin B**. Measurable AMH or inhibin B confirms testicular tissue; high gonadotrophins with absent AMH suggests **anorchia**. The **hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin) stimulation test** (testosterone rise) is now less used.
  - Pelvic ultrasound for Müllerian structures.
- **Imaging to localise the testis is not recommended:** ultrasound sensitivity about 45%; it never removes the need for surgery.

### Management
- **No hormonal therapy to induce descent:** LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) works in about 20%; hCG may increase germ-cell apoptosis. Adjuvant LHRH (buserelin) in bilateral cases is experimental.
- **Palpable:** inguinal orchidopexy (free the cord, ligate the patent processus, retroperitoneal mobilisation, Prentiss manoeuvre if needed, **subdartos pouch**) or scrotal (Bianchi) orchidopexy for low testes. Both sides usually in one sitting.
- **Non-palpable:** examination under anaesthesia, then **diagnostic laparoscopy**:
```
Laparoscopy
 ├─ Testis seen → laparoscopic orchidopexy
 │     vessels short (testis not within 2–4 cm of ring) → Fowler–Stephens
 │     (clip testicular vessels; testis lives on vasal collaterals;
 │      one-stage, or two-stage with second stage at 6 months) or Shehata traction
 ├─ Vessels and vas enter ring → inguinal exploration, excise nubbin
 └─ Blind-ending vessels → stop (vanished testis)
```
- **Both testes very high or of doubtful viability:** stage the sides, the second at 6 months, to avoid bilateral atrophy. Primary orchidopexy (>90% success) beats Fowler–Stephens (60–97%).
- **Biopsy** only if DSD or dysgenesis is suspected. **Bilateral anorchia:** testosterone at puberty, prostheses later.

### Follow-up and prognosis
- Check position and size at 6 months.
- **Fertility:** worse when bilateral (oligospermia 42%, azoospermia 31% in one large review).
- **Cancer:** about **3-fold** risk (another source: TGCT 2–5-fold); teach self-examination after puberty.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal fetus showing CSL, gubernaculum, processus vaginalis and both phases.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - SRY → SOX9 → Sertoli cells (AMH); Leydig cells → testosterone and INSL3.
> - Transabdominal: CSL regression plus INSL3-driven gubernacular swelling.
> - Inguinoscrotal: androgen via genitofemoral nerve (GFN) and CGRP.
> - Most testes scrotal after 27 weeks.
> - Ectopic = off the path; commonest site superficial inguinal pouch.
> - Bilateral non-palpable = DSD work-up first (karyotype, 17-OHP, AMH).
> - Laparoscopy decides; no imaging, no hormones.

## IVC anomalous development and retrocaval ureter
**Asked as:**
- Describe embryology & clinical implications of IVC anomalous development. — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Retrocaval ureter — *Practice Set 11 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(retrocaval ureter also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3d)

### Introduction
- The **IVC (inferior vena cava)** forms between the 6th and 8th weeks from three pairs of fetal veins by anastomosis, regression and persistence. The final IVC is **right-sided**, so long left veins must cross the aorta to reach it.
- Most anomalies are silent CT (computed tomography) findings. They **bleed at surgery, mimic lymph nodes, trap the ureter and alter IVC filter placement**.

### Embryology
**Three paired venous systems**
- Fetal veins form a collar around each ureter: posterior cardinal and supracardinal (dorsal), subcardinal (ventral).

| Vein | Position | Adult derivatives |
|---|---|---|
| **Posterior cardinal** (first) | Dorsolateral | Iliac confluence; mostly regresses |
| **Subcardinal** | Ventromedial | Right: **suprarenal IVC**; intersubcardinal anastomosis: **left renal vein**; left: adrenal and gonadal veins |
| **Supracardinal** (last) | Dorsomedial | Right: **infrarenal IVC**; thoracic parts: **azygos and hemiazygos** |

**Segments of the definitive IVC (top to bottom)**
1. **Hepatic**: right vitelline vein.
2. **Suprarenal**: right subcardinal vein (via the subcardinal–hepatic anastomosis).
3. **Renal**: subcardinal–supracardinal anastomosis.
4. **Infrarenal**: right supracardinal vein.
5. **Iliac confluence**: posterior cardinal veins.

**Renal collar**
- A venous ring encircles the aorta at renal level. The **ventral (pre-aortic) limb** persists as the left renal vein; the **dorsal (retro-aortic) limb** regresses.
- The right supracardinal vein lies **dorsal** to the ureter, so the normal ureter lies in front of the IVC. Normally the right **supracardinal** vein, **dorsal** to the ureter, forms the infrarenal IVC.

```
Left-sided channels regress -> right-sided persist
  -> single right IVC + pre-aortic left renal vein
```

**Diagram to draw:** three stages: paired cardinal veins, anastomoses with renal collar, definitive IVC.

### Anomalies

| Anomaly | Embryological error | Key features |
|---|---|---|
| **Retrocaval ureter** ("preureteral vena cava") | Infrarenal IVC forms from a vein **ventral** to the ureter | Right only; 1 in 1500 autopsies; male 3:1; presents 3rd–4th decade. **Type I** low loop, fishhook, obstructs; **type II** high loop, sickle, mild |
| **Circumaortic left renal vein** | Dorsal limb persists with ventral | Commonest (2–16%); posterior limb runs lower into lumbar IVC |
| **Retroaortic left renal vein** | Ventral limb regresses, dorsal persists | Less common; may be compressed ("posterior nutcracker") |
| **Left IVC** | Left supracardinal persists, right regresses | Crosses to the right at renal level, in front of the aorta |
| **Duplicated IVC** | Both supracardinals persist | Left IVC joins left renal vein; double right cava can trap the ureter |
