| **Azygos continuation** | Subcardinal–hepatic anastomosis fails | Large azygos; hepatic veins drain directly to right atrium; polysplenia, heterotaxy |
| **Absent infrarenal IVC** | Supracardinal and posterior cardinal failure | Deep vein thrombosis in the young |

- For retrocaval ureter Campbell names a persistent **lumbar subcardinal** (Chapter 52) or **posterior cardinal** vein (Chapter 93, most texts); both mean a ventral vein forms the IVC.

### Retrocaval ureter
**Definition**
- The **right ureter** passes **behind the IVC (inferior vena cava)**, then crosses in front of it from medial to lateral before resuming a normal course.
- Better named **"preureteral vena cava"**: the anomaly is venous; the ureter is trapped.

**Embryology**
- If the **lumbar subcardinal vein** (ventral) persists as the IVC, the ureter is trapped behind it.
- Variant: double right IVC, with the ureter between the limbs.

**Incidence and presentation**
- About **1 in 1500** autopsies; **male 3:1**; presents in the **third–fourth decade** with right flank pain, UTI (urinary tract infection), stones or incidental hydronephrosis.

**Types (Bateson and Atkinson)**
| | **Type I (low loop), commoner** | **Type II (high loop)** |
|---|---|---|
| Crossing | Lower, L3–L4 | Higher, at renal pelvis level |
| Shape | **J or "fishhook"** | Smooth horizontal curve |
| Obstruction | Yes, at iliopsoas edge | Mild or none |

**Investigations**
- **CT urography (computed tomography urography): investigation of choice**; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) alternative.
- IVU (intravenous urography): ureter not seen beyond the J hook. Retrograde: **S-curve** at L3–L4. **Diuretic renogram**: degree of obstruction, split function.
- Pitfall: mistaken for **PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction**, so pyeloplasty fails.

**Management**
- **Non-obstructed (usually type II):** observe.
- **Obstructed:** divide the ureter, transpose it **anterior to the IVC**, and do a spatulated **ureteroureterostomy** (or ureteropyelostomy) over a **double-J stent**. Excise or bypass the **aperistaltic retrocaval segment**; respect the ureteric blood supply.
- **Laparoscopic or robotic** (transperitoneal or retroperitoneal) now preferred. Nephrectomy if non-functioning.
- **Also know:** retroiliac ureter (L5–S1); bilateral cases in situs inversus.

**Diagram to draw:** right ureter hooking behind the IVC with a fishhook pelvis.

### Clinical implications
**1. Diagnosis**
- Venous-phase CT or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging). A left or duplicated IVC mimics **para-aortic lymphadenopathy**; a large azygos mimics a mediastinal mass, risking needless biopsy.
- Retrocaval ureter: S-shaped or fishhook ureter at L3–L4 on retrograde pyelography; CT urography confirms; diuretic renography grades obstruction.

**2. RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)**
- Preoperative CT must look for circumaortic or retroaortic veins and retrocaval ureter. A left or duplicated IVC lies in the left template; a retroaortic limb tears when lumbar vessels are divided.

**3. Radical nephrectomy**
- The hidden posterior limb of a circumaortic vein bleeds when the artery is approached from behind: ligate both limbs. Caval thrombus in a duplicated IVC needs rerouting of the left IVC.

**4. Living donor nephrectomy**
- The left kidney is preferred for its long vein. A circumaortic or retroaortic vein shortens it and adds bleeding risk; CT angiography is mandatory, and the right kidney may be chosen.

**5. Retrocaval ureter repair**
- Observe non-obstructed type II. For obstruction, **divide the ureter, transpose it in front of the IVC and re-anastomose** over a stent, excising the aperistaltic segment; laparoscopic or robotic. A pyeloplasty that misses it fails.

**6. Interventional and vascular**
- **IVC filter:** with duplication, emboli bypass a single filter, so filter both cavae or place it suprarenally.
- Aortic surgery risks retroaortic vein injury. A retroaortic vein can cause haematuria or left varicocele.
- **Also know:** variable left adrenal vein entry (adrenal vein sampling); right gonadal vein into right renal vein in up to 10%.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Infrarenal IVC = right supracardinal; suprarenal = right subcardinal; hepatic = vitelline.
> - Circumaortic left renal vein is commonest (2–16%): ligate both limbs.
> - Retrocaval ureter: venous anomaly (persistent subcardinal vein); right side only; 1 in 1500; male 3:1; transpose anterior to IVC.
> - Type I fishhook obstructs; type II does not.
> - CT urography; anterior transposition and ureteroureterostomy.
> - Duplicated IVC: filter both cavae; do not mistake it for nodes.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Glandular hypospadias
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 1, Q3b

*"Glandular" is read as glanular hypospadias: meatus on the glans.*

### Definition
- The mildest **distal (anterior)** hypospadias: the meatus opens on the ventral glans, proximal to the normal tip.
- Hypospadias occurs in 1 in 150–300 male births; distal forms are 70–85%.
- Usual features: **dorsal hooded foreskin**, ventral glans tilt, little or no curvature. **MIP (megameatus intact prepuce)** variant (up to 5%): wide meatus with normal foreskin.

### Embryology
- Urethral folds close from proximal to distal during weeks 8–16 under **DHT (dihydrotestosterone)**.
- Glanular urethra: ectodermal ingrowth (classic) or endodermal plate ("two-zipper", current view). Arrest at the last step gives a glanular meatus.

### Evaluation
- Examine meatus, plate, glans size, curvature, torsion; retract the foreskin before any **newborn circumcision**.
- **GMS (glans–meatus–shaft) score** (each 1–4, total 3–12) predicts complications.
- No workup needed for isolated distal hypospadias; DSD (difference of sexual development) workup if a testis is undescended.

### Indications and timing
- Deflected or spraying stream, meatal stenosis, curvature, cosmesis. A straight penis that voids standing may need no surgery; counsel parents.
- **Age 6–18 months** (AAP, American Academy of Pediatrics, 6–12 months).

### Operations
| Procedure | Suitable for | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| Urethromeatoplasty | Stenotic meatus, blind pit | Heineke–Mikulicz: vertical incision, horizontal closure |
| **MAGPI** (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty, Duckett 1981) | Glanular meatus, mobile skin | Advance meatus, two-layer glanuloplasty; retracts if used more proximally |
| MIV (M inverted V) glansplasty | Glanular or coronal | Avoids MAGPI retraction |
| **GAP** (glans approximation) | Wide deep groove, fish-mouth meatus (MIP) | Tubularise and close glans wings |
| **TIP** (tubularised incised plate, Snodgrass 1994) | Narrow plate | Most used; slit-like meatus |
| Mathieu flip-flap; Thiersch–Duplay | Selected | Stenosis, fistula |

- Preputioplasty or circumcision; dartos barrier layer.

### Complications
- Meatal stenosis or retraction, fistula, **glans dehiscence**, residual curvature, poor cosmesis. Distal repairs succeed in about 80%.

**Diagram to draw:** MAGPI steps (meatal advancement, glans wings closed beneath).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Glanular = most distal hypospadias; dorsal hood, minimal curvature.
> - MAGPI only for a truly glanular meatus; GAP for MIP; TIP is the workhorse.
> - Repair at 6–18 months; may be left alone if function is normal.

## TIP repair in hypospadias
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3b
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3c; extended indications in Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q3c)

### Introduction
- **TIP (tubularised incised plate) urethroplasty**, Snodgrass (1994), built on Rich's hinging incision (1989).
- Used for **>90% of distal** and **about 80% of midshaft** repairs.

### Principle
- A **deep midline relaxing incision** widens a narrow urethral plate so it can be tubularised **without tension**.
- The raw incised area **heals by re-epithelialisation** without a graft, giving a **vertical slit-like meatus**.

### Indications and prerequisites
- Distal and midshaft hypospadias; redo cases with a healthy plate; curvature **<30°** corrected by degloving or dorsal plication.
- **Glans width <14 mm** raises complications: consider preoperative testosterone.
- **POST (plate objective scoring tool):** a poor plate favours a dorsal inlay graft.
- Not for curvature needing plate division; proximal TIP largely abandoned.

### Operative steps
1. Glans traction suture; tourniquet.
2. U-incision around plate and meatus; deglove; artificial erection.
3. Mobilise glans wings deeply off the plate.
4. **Midline plate incision** from meatus to plate end, down to corpora.
5. Tubularise over a **6–8 Fr stent** with subepithelial 6-0/7-0 absorbable sutures, distal to proximal, leaving an oval meatus.
6. **Dartos flap** (dorsal preputial) over the neourethra as a barrier layer.
7. Two-layer glansplasty; mucosal collar; Byars flaps or preputioplasty.
8. Stent 5–7 days; dressing; oxybutynin for bladder spasm.

### Complications and outcomes
- Pfistermuller meta-analysis (2015, 4,675 boys): complications 10.6%, reoperation 4.5%; **fistula 5.7%**, **meatal stenosis 3.6%**, stricture 1.3%.
- Glans dehiscence; meatal stenosis commoner than after Mathieu.
- Proximal and redo cases do worse; a dartos layer reduces fistula.

### Modifications
- **Dorsal inlay graft (Snodgraft)**: inner preputial graft in the incised bed for a shallow plate.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-sections of plate incision, tubularisation and dartos cover.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Snodgrass 1994: incise, tubularise, heals by epithelialisation.
> - Glans <14 mm and poor plate raise complications.
> - Fistula 5.7%, meatal stenosis 3.6% (Pfistermuller 2015).

## Vanishing testes
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 1, Q3b

### Definition
- A testis that **developed normally and was then lost**, usually from **torsion or a vascular accident in fetal life**, after genital masculinisation but before scrotal fixation.
- Unilateral loss gives **monorchia**; bilateral loss gives **anorchia** (testicular regression syndrome), which is rare.
- Excised "nubbins" contain haemosiderin.

### Incidence
- **15–40% of nonpalpable testes** at surgery.

### Clinical features
- **Contralateral compensatory hypertrophy:** volume >2 mL (sensitivity 72%, specificity 100%) or length about 19–20 mm suggests a solitary testis.
- No palpable intrascrotal "appendage" tissue; sometimes a scrotal nubbin.
- These aid counselling but **do not replace exploration**.
- **Anorchia:** micropenis in nearly half.

### Evaluation
- **Imaging** (ultrasound, MRI [magnetic resonance imaging]) is not recommended: it does not change the operation.
- **Bilateral nonpalpable:** exclude DSD (difference of sex development) and CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia) first.
  - Anorchia: high FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and LH (luteinising hormone); low AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone) and inhibin B; no rise after hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin). Most reliable during mini-puberty.

### Management: diagnostic laparoscopy (after examination under anaesthesia)

| Laparoscopic finding | Action |
|---|---|
| **Blind-ending vessels and vas** above a closed ring | Diagnosis confirmed; stop |
| Vessels and vas entering the internal ring | Inguinal or scrotal exploration; excise the nubbin |
| Testis seen | Orchidopexy (single stage or Fowler–Stephens) |
| Neither vas nor vessels | Search up to the kidney (agenesis very rare) |

- **Excise the remnant:** viable germ cells or tubules in 5–15%.
- **Scrotal-first exploration** is reasonable with a palpable nubbin and contralateral hypertrophy (length ≥1.8 cm); laparoscopy is then needed in about a quarter.
- **Contralateral fixation:** debated; not required by the AUA (American Urological Association) algorithm.
- **Prosthesis:** offer at least 6 months after scrotal surgery or after puberty, inguinal approach.
- **Anorchia:** testosterone from puberty. **Also know:** agenesis shows ipsilateral Müllerian persistence.

**Diagram to draw:** laparoscopic view of blind-ending vessels at a closed ring.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prenatal torsion after masculinisation; 15–40% of nonpalpable testes.
> - Contralateral hypertrophy (>2 mL) suggests it, but explore anyway.
> - Blind-ending vessels confirm; excise remnant (germ cells 5–15%).
> - Anorchia: high FSH/LH, low AMH and inhibin B.

## Pelvicalyceal anomalies other than PUJ obstruction
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 1, Q3d

### Introduction
- Congenital pelvicalyceal variants occur in about 4% of people.
- **Key question:** is there obstruction? Dilatation without obstruction needs no treatment; stasis causes stones and infection.

### Anomalies

| Anomaly | Features | Management |
|---|---|---|
| **Calyceal diverticulum** | Transitional-lined intrarenal cavity joined to a calyx (Type I, commoner, small) or pelvis (Type II, larger, symptomatic) by a narrow neck; 4.5 per 1000; congenital (persistent 3rd–4th generation ureteric branches) or acquired. **Stones in almost 40%**; milk of calcium | Asymptomatic: observe. Pain, infection, stones: ureteroscopic neck incision, **percutaneous ablation with fulguration**, laparoscopic marsupialisation; SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) ineffective |
| **Hydrocalycosis** | Dilated major calyx connected to the pelvis; infundibular obstruction by vessel, stenosis, scarring or achalasia | Symptomatic only: infundibulotomy or dilatation |
| **Megacalycosis** | **Non-obstructive**; malformed papillae; **12–20 calyces**; pelvis normal; male 6:1; normal diuretic renogram | **None**; may coexist with segmental megaureter |
| **Infundibular / infundibulopelvic stenosis** | Narrow infundibula ± small pelvis; usually bilateral, with VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), dysplasia; over a third reach renal failure (hyperfiltration) | Monitor creatinine and eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate); ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors; surgery only if obstruction harms function: endoscopic, calicocalicostomy, ureterocalicostomy |
| **Extrarenal pelvis** | Large pelvis outside the sinus; mimics dilatation | None |
| **Bifid pelvis** | About 10%; normal variant | None |
| **Unipapillary kidney** | Single papilla and calyx; rare; usually hypoplastic kidney | None |

- **Also know:** acquired infundibular stenosis (tuberculosis, post-PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)); Fraley syndrome (crossing artery on upper infundibulum).

### Evaluation
- **Ultrasound:** cyst-like space; milk of calcium layers and shifts with position.
- **CT (computed tomography) urography or MRU (magnetic resonance urography):** **delayed contrast pooling** in a diverticulum.
- **Diuretic renogram (MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine)):** separates obstruction (hydrocalycosis) from non-obstruction (megacalycosis, extrarenal pelvis).
- **Retrograde pyelography:** maps the neck.

### Differential diagnosis
- Obstructive hydronephrosis, papillary necrosis, pyelonephritic clubbing, tuberculosis, renal cyst.
- **Stones:** retrograde first (find, dilate or laser-incise the ostium); percutaneous for lower-pole diverticula.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney showing diverticulum with narrow neck, hydrocalyx and megacalyces.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Diverticulum: Type I to calyx, Type II to pelvis; stones in 40%.
> - Megacalycosis: non-obstructive, 12–20 calyces, no treatment.
> - Infundibulopelvic stenosis: bilateral, renal failure risk.

## Methods of orchidopexy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 21 SAQ Sheet, Q3f
(treatment algorithm of undescended testis answered in 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3a)

### Principles
- Operate **after 6 months, ideally before 18 months** (Kollin RCT [randomised controlled trial]: surgery at 9 months gave better testicular growth than at 3 years). No hormones.
- Goals: tension-free **dependent scrotal** testis with intact blood supply; ligate the patent processus.

### Palpable testis
- **Inguinal orchidopexy:** groin incision → open external oblique (spare ilioinguinal nerve) → free cord, divide gubernaculum → separate and **ligate sac at internal ring** → retroperitoneal mobilisation, divide lateral bands → **Prentiss manoeuvre** (medial transposition under inferior epigastric vessels) → **subdartos pouch**. Transalbugineal sutures unnecessary.
- **Trans-scrotal (Bianchi) orchidopexy:** high scrotal incision; sac ligated through it; inguinal conversion 3.5–4.4%; atrophy 0.3–0.6%; less pain.

### Non-palpable testis: examination under anaesthesia, then laparoscopy

| Method | Principle | Success |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary laparoscopic** | Mobilise vessels, preserve vasal collaterals, new hiatus medial to epigastric vessels | >90% (97%) |
| **Open transabdominal (Jones and Bagley)** | Preperitoneal high mobilisation | 95% |
| **Fowler–Stephens** (one- or two-stage) | Divide testicular vessels high; testis survives on **vasal and gubernacular collaterals**; second stage after 6 months | 60–97% (one-stage 63%, two-stage 68%) |
| **Shehata traction** | Fix testis near opposite anterior superior iliac spine; vessels lengthen; orchidopexy at 12 weeks | 84–85%; atrophy 10% |
| **Microvascular autotransplantation** | Testicular vessels to inferior epigastric vessels | 96% |
| **Orchidectomy** | Postpubertal, dysgenetic or atrophic testis | — |

- Fowler–Stephens for testes **not within 2–4 cm of the internal ring**.
- **Also know:** staged (two-stage) inguinal orchidopexy; Dessanti PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene) sleeve traction; gubernaculum-sparing technique; redo approaches (Redman lateral, Cartwright fascial patch).

### Complications
- **Atrophy** (highest after Fowler–Stephens), retraction or reascent, vas or vessel injury, haematoma, infection; torsion very rare after subdartos fixation.

### Follow-up
- Size and position at 6 months; counsel on fertility and cancer risk; self-examination after puberty. Offer prosthesis for an atrophic testis.

**Diagram to draw:** Fowler–Stephens collateral supply: testicular artery clipped, deferential and cremasteric arteries intact.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Orchidopexy at 6–18 months; subdartos pouch.
> - Prentiss manoeuvre gives extra length.
> - Fowler–Stephens relies on vasal collaterals.

## Hypospadias repairs – single-stage versus staged techniques
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 22 SAQ Sheet, Q3e
(present concepts also in Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- Choice depends on **curvature after degloving**, plate quality and glans size, not on meatal position alone.
- Algorithm: deglove, artificial erection. Straight or <30°: dorsal plication, single-stage. >30°: divide plate, ventral corporotomy ± graft, then staged.

### Single-stage techniques
| Group | Examples | Indication |
|---|---|---|
| Advancement | **MAGPI** (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty, Duckett 1981); MIV (M inverted V) | Glanular meatus, no curvature |
| Tubularisation | **TIP** (tubularised incised plate, Snodgrass 1994); Thiersch–Duplay; GAP (glans approximation procedure) | Distal–midshaft; TIP in >90% of distal |
| Flap | Mathieu (flip-flap); **OIF** (onlay island flap) | Thin plate |
| Proximal one-stage | TPIF (transverse preputial island flap, Duckett tube); proximal OIF | Curvature corrected; good tissue |
| Graft | Dorsal buccal inlay | Narrow plate |
- Pros: one anaesthetic. Proximal TIP is abandoned: devascularised plate, stricture, recurrent curvature.

### Staged techniques
- Now the approach of choice for most proximal hypospadias. **Rationale:** a urethroplasty over a fresh corporal graft fails; stage 1 builds a supple, vascular bed.

| Type | Stage 1 | Stage 2 (≥6 months) |
|---|---|---|
| **Bracka / STAG** (staged tubularised autograft) | Correct curvature, divide plate, quilt inner preputial or buccal graft | Tubularise, glansplasty, tunica vaginalis cover |
| **Byars flaps** | Split dorsal prepuce, transpose ventrally | Tubularise |
| Distal-first (Ulaanbaatar) | Curvature and distal urethra | Join proximal urethra |
- Repeat artificial erection before stage 2.

### Comparison of outcomes
| Measure | Single-stage | Staged |
|---|---|---|
| Distal success | 83–95% | – |
| Proximal complications | About 60% (long TIP, mixed) | STAG about 23%; Byars 43–81% |
| Recurrent curvature | Higher after plication | 0–10% |
| Penile length, perception | Lower | Better |
| Extra procedures | Fewer planned | 25–75% need more |
- Fistula is the commonest complication. Long, rigid neourethra without spongiosum raises resistance (Poiseuille's law), causing fistula and diverticulum.

**Diagram to draw:** STAG stage 1 graft and stage 2 tubularisation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Curvature >30° after degloving: stage.
> - TIP for distal; STAG has lowest proximal complications.
> - Stage 2 after ≥6 months.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Primary (congenital) VUR: VUJ embryology, pathophysiology, natural history and management (LAQ)

- Low (early) bud → lateral orifice → VUR plus dysplasia; high (late) bud → ectopia.
- One embryological fault: lateral orifice, short tunnel, dysplasia.
- Tunnel length to diameter 5:1 (Paquin).
- Dysplasia (boys, congenital) versus scar (girls, infection).
- Grade IV–V and bilateral grade III rarely resolve.
- Scarred children: 34–38% adult hypertension.
- RIVUR: CAP halves recurrent UTI, not scarring.
- Open reimplantation 95.9%; Cohen over 98%.

#### Urological evaluation and management of pediatric kidney transplant patient. (LAQ)

- Bladder target: age-expected capacity at <40 cm H₂O; augment if <75% despite CIC.
- PUV bladder heads to myogenic failure; neuropathic bladder needs earlier reconstruction.
- Never test a bladder with a graft; cycle the defunctionalised bladder.
- Stenosis 6%, leak 3%, stones 2%, significant reflux 2%.

#### Testicular development, gonadal descent, abnormalities of descent and bilateral undescended testis (LAQ)

- SRY → SOX9 → Sertoli cells (AMH); Leydig cells → testosterone and INSL3.
- Transabdominal: CSL regression plus INSL3-driven gubernacular swelling.
- Inguinoscrotal: androgen via genitofemoral nerve (GFN) and CGRP.
- Most testes scrotal after 27 weeks.
- Ectopic = off the path; commonest site superficial inguinal pouch.
- Bilateral non-palpable = DSD work-up first (karyotype, 17-OHP, AMH).
- Laparoscopy decides; no imaging, no hormones.

#### IVC anomalous development and retrocaval ureter (LAQ)

- Infrarenal IVC = right supracardinal; suprarenal = right subcardinal; hepatic = vitelline.
- Circumaortic left renal vein is commonest (2–16%): ligate both limbs.
- Retrocaval ureter: venous anomaly (persistent subcardinal vein); right side only; 1 in 1500; male 3:1; transpose anterior to IVC.
- Type I fishhook obstructs; type II does not.
- CT urography; anterior transposition and ureteroureterostomy.
- Duplicated IVC: filter both cavae; do not mistake it for nodes.

#### Glandular hypospadias (SAQ)

- Glanular = most distal hypospadias; dorsal hood, minimal curvature.
- MAGPI only for a truly glanular meatus; GAP for MIP; TIP is the workhorse.
- Repair at 6–18 months; may be left alone if function is normal.

#### TIP repair in hypospadias (SAQ)

- Snodgrass 1994: incise, tubularise, heals by epithelialisation.
- Glans <14 mm and poor plate raise complications.
- Fistula 5.7%, meatal stenosis 3.6% (Pfistermuller 2015).

#### Vanishing testes (SAQ)

- Prenatal torsion after masculinisation; 15–40% of nonpalpable testes.
- Contralateral hypertrophy (>2 mL) suggests it, but explore anyway.
- Blind-ending vessels confirm; excise remnant (germ cells 5–15%).
- Anorchia: high FSH/LH, low AMH and inhibin B.

#### Pelvicalyceal anomalies other than PUJ obstruction (SAQ)

- Diverticulum: Type I to calyx, Type II to pelvis; stones in 40%.
- Megacalycosis: non-obstructive, 12–20 calyces, no treatment.
- Infundibulopelvic stenosis: bilateral, renal failure risk.

#### Methods of orchidopexy (SAQ)

- Orchidopexy at 6–18 months; subdartos pouch.
- Prentiss manoeuvre gives extra length.
- Fowler–Stephens relies on vasal collaterals.

#### Hypospadias repairs – single-stage versus staged techniques (SAQ)

- Curvature >30° after degloving: stage.
- TIP for distal; STAG has lowest proximal complications.
- Stage 2 after ≥6 months.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe embryology of bladder. What are the proposed theories for extrophy epispadias complex? Describe skeletal abnormalities.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q2
(bladder embryology also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### A. Embryology of the bladder
**1. Division of the cloaca (weeks 5–6)**
- The **urorectal septum** (Tourneux and Rathke folds) grows caudally and divides the endodermal cloaca into a dorsal **anorectal canal** and a ventral **UGS (urogenital sinus)**. It meets the cloacal membrane, which perforates to form the urogenital and anal openings.
- The **entry of the Wolffian (mesonephric) ducts** divides the UGS:
  - **Cranial vesicourethral canal** (continuous with the allantois) → bladder and pelvic urethra.
  - **Caudal (definitive) UGS** → phallic urethra in males; vestibule and distal vagina in females.

**2. Wall layers**
- Urothelium from UGS endoderm. Lamina propria, detrusor and adventitia from surrounding splanchnic mesenchyme, by epithelial–mesenchymal signalling.

**3. Trigone (weeks 5–6)**
- The common excretory ducts dilate and are absorbed into the UGS. **Apoptosis** separates the ureters from the Wolffian ducts.
- Ureteric orifices migrate **cranially and laterally**; Wolffian ducts end as ejaculatory ducts at the verumontanum.
- Classic view: trigone muscle is ureteric. Current view (mouse lineage tracing): **mostly bladder smooth muscle**.
- **Weigert–Meyer:** bud too low → lateral orifice → VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); too high → caudal, medial, ectopic orifice.

**4. Urachus:** the bladder descends into the pelvis (months 4–5); the allantois obliterates into the **urachus** (median umbilical ligament).

**5. Function:** urine from about week 10; after week 16 amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine.

### B. Exstrophy–epispadias complex (EEC): theories
- **Spectrum:** glanular epispadias → classic bladder exstrophy (CBE, >50%; about 1 in 46,000; male:female 2.3:1) → cloacal exstrophy.
- **Core defect:** the **cloacal membrane is not reinforced by mesodermal ingrowth**. This mesoderm normally forms the lower abdominal muscles and pelvic bones, so the thin membrane ruptures. **Timing and extent of rupture** decide the variant.

| Theory | Proposed defect | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **Cloacal membrane overdevelopment (Marshall–Muecke)** | Abnormally large membrane in week 4 blocks medial mesenchymal migration | Rupture before septum descent → cloacal exstrophy; after → bladder exstrophy; limited → epispadias |
| Lateral body-wall fold failure | Folds fail to meet in midline | Abdomen and pelvis → cloacal; pelvis alone → classic |
| Caudal genital hillocks | Genital tubercles fuse **below** rather than above the membrane | Dorsal urethral defect (epispadias) |
| Abnormal body-stalk insertion | Caudal insertion prevents mesenchyme interposition | Superficial unstable membrane disintegrates |
| **Bony pelvis first** | Pelvic ring primordium fails to rotate | Pelvic structures cannot meet → bladder herniates |

- All theories converge on **absent infraumbilical midline mesoderm**.
- **Also know (aetiology):** multifactorial; recurrence in offspring of an affected parent 1 in 70; 22q11.2 duplication (commonest variant), ISL1 susceptibility gene, p63 under-expression; IVF (in vitro fertilisation) and high-dose early progesterone raise risk; maternal smoking linked to cloacal exstrophy.

### C. Skeletal abnormalities
**1. Classic bladder exstrophy (3D [three-dimensional] CT [computed tomography] studies)**
- **Rotational:** posterior pelvis externally rotated (mean 12° each side); anterior segment externally rotated (mean 18° each side); sacroiliac joint rotated; acetabular and femoral retroversion.
- **Dimensional:** **pubic diastasis** (mean 4.8 cm); **anterior pubic segment shortened by 30%**; intertriradiate distance increased by about 31%; posterior segment length normal; larger sacrum.
- Bone histology is normal, so growth potential is normal.
- **Clinical effects:** short pendular penis (anterior segment short and splayed); wide hips, waddling gait and out-toeing in early childhood. These cause little disability and improve with growth.
- **Pelvic floor:** levator ani displaced posteriorly, flattened; anterior anus; later pelvic organ prolapse.
- **Spine:** minor variants (spina bifida occulta, lumbarisation, sacralisation) fairly common; scoliosis and dysraphism uncommon.

**2. Cloacal exstrophy**
- Widest, **asymmetric** diastasis (about twice CBE), sacroiliac malformation, hip dislocation.
- **Spinal anomalies in 85–100%** (lumbar myelodysplasia commonest; lipomeningocele); MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for all.
- Limb defects: **clubfoot** (commonest), limb shortening, tibial and fibular deformity.

**3. Isolated epispadias:** male symphysis widened (less than CBE); female symphysis usually closed or narrow.

**Surgical significance: pelvic osteotomy**
- Closing the pelvic ring places the bladder neck deep in the pelvis, reduces tension and helps continence.
- **Indications:** diastasis >4 cm or poorly malleable pelvis; closure after 72 hours; failed closure; all cloacal exstrophy. Under 72 hours with malleable pubes: closure without osteotomy and traction.
- **Combined bilateral anterior innominate + vertical iliac osteotomy** (most used) with external fixator.
- Epispadias repair and continence surgery: see Practice Set 6 Paper 3, Q2.

**Diagram to draw:** cloacal membrane with urorectal septum and failed mesodermal ingrowth; exstrophy pelvis with diastasis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Vesicourethral canal → bladder; urachus from allantois.
> - Marshall–Muecke: overdeveloped cloacal membrane; rupture timing decides variant.
> - CBE: external rotation 12°/18°, anterior pubis 30% short, diastasis ~4.8 cm.
> - Cloacal exstrophy: spinal anomalies 85–100%; osteotomy always.

## Prune Belly Syndrome. Antenatal diagnosis, pathophysiology and postnatal management.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 1, Q1
(also answered as an SAQ in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

### Definition
- **PBS (prune belly syndrome)**, also called Eagle–Barrett or triad syndrome: **deficient abdominal wall muscles**, **bilateral intra-abdominal testes** and a **dilated, poorly contracting urinary tract**.
- 1 in 29,000–44,000 live births; **95% male**.
- **Survival depends on renal dysplasia and lung hypoplasia, not on the abdominal wall.**

### Antenatal diagnosis
- **Ultrasound** may suggest PBS from 11–14 weeks, but the classic signs are not consistent even at 30 weeks.
- **Findings:** huge bladder filling the abdomen; bilateral hydroureteronephrosis; urachal diverticulum; dilated posterior urethra; echogenic kidneys with cortical cysts (dysplasia); irregular abdominal circumference; fetal ascites; oligohydramnios when severe.
- **Differential diagnosis:** PUV (posterior urethral valves), urethral atresia, megacystis–megaureter.
- Fetal dilatation does **not** predict postnatal renal function.
- **Prenatal management:** intervention has no proven renal benefit. Vesicoamniotic shunting is justified only for **urethral atresia with progressive oligohydramnios**, or to decompress the abdomen and avoid dystocia. Termination is hard to justify except with early, severe oligohydramnios. Plan delivery in a centre with neonatal intensive care.

### Pathophysiology
| Theory (none proven) | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Early urethral obstruction | Back-pressure dilates the tract and stretches the wall; but most have no obstruction after birth |
| Lateral plate mesoderm defect | This mesoderm forms ureters, bladder, prostate, urethra and gubernaculum, so one defect explains the triad |
| Intrinsic urinary tract defect | Primary ureteric dilatation and fetal ascites |
| Yolk sac defect | Primary defect of the yolk sac |
- **Genetics:** mostly sporadic, normal karyotype; smooth-muscle genes **MYOCD (myocardin)** and **FLNA (filamin A)** in multiplex families.
- **Kidneys:** dysplasia in about 50% (Potter type II from mesenchymal defect; type IV cystic from obstruction).
- **Ureters:** long and tortuous, worse distally. Collagen replaces smooth muscle, so walls do not coapt and peristalsis fails: **a failed pump, not a blocked pipe**. The result is stasis and infection. **VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) in about 75%.** The proximal ureter has better muscle.
- **Bladder:** large, smooth, collagen-rich; urachal pseudodiverticulum; splayed trigone. Normal compliance but poor contractility, so voiding is "unbalanced", with residual urine.
- **Prostate and urethra:** prostatic hypoplasia dilates the posterior urethra; distal obstruction in about 20%; **megalourethra** (scaphoid: spongiosum deficient; fusiform: all erectile bodies, more severe).
- **Testes:** intra-abdominal; azoospermia; paternity only by sperm retrieval and ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).
- **Extra-genitourinary (about 75%):** pulmonary hypoplasia; cardiac about 10%; gastrointestinal at least 30% (malrotation); orthopaedic up to 65% (talipes, hip dysplasia).

### Classification (Woodard)
| Category | Features | Management |
|---|---|---|
| I | Oligohydramnios, pulmonary hypoplasia, dysplasia, urethral atresia | Supportive; bladder drainage only |
| II | Full triad, moderate or unilateral dysplasia | Individualised (controversial group) |
| III | Mild or incomplete; stable function (majority) | Surveillance |
- **Also know:** RUBACE score (Renal, Ureter, Bladder, Abdominal wall, Cryptorchidism, Extra-genitourinary) grades morbidity in survivors; pseudo-prune presents late with renal failure.

### Postnatal management
**Neonatal (neonatology, nephrology, urology, cardiology):**
1. Heart and lungs first: **chest radiograph** for pneumothorax and hypoplasia.
2. Creatinine, BUN (blood urea nitrogen), electrolytes; follow the **trend**, because the first value reflects the mother.
3. Renal and bladder ultrasound once stable.
4. Antibiotic prophylaxis; **circumcision**; strict asepsis.
5. **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram)** only with renal insufficiency or suspected outlet obstruction.
6. **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) at 4–6 weeks** for split function; MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) for drainage.
7. Outlet obstruction: catheter or suprapubic tube.

**Urinary tract:**
- **Conservative** for category III: surveillance, treat infection.
- **Surgery for:** recurrent upper tract infection, progressive dilatation or renal decline, high-grade VUR, true obstruction.
- **Temporary diversion:** wide **cutaneous vesicostomy (Blocksom)**. Avoid high ureterostomy, which wastes the good proximal ureter.
- **Reconstruction after 3–6 months** (lung maturity): excise distal ureter, taper and reimplant the healthier proximal ureter; reduction cystoplasty (capacity recurs). **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)** or Mitrofanoff for poor emptying.

**Orchidopexy:** **bilateral transabdominal at about 6 months**, often with other surgery; later, **staged Fowler–Stephens** (better than single-stage) or laparoscopic.

**Abdominoplasty** (cosmesis; better cough, voiding, defecation):
| Technique | Key feature |
|---|---|
| Randolph | Transverse; excises worst lower wall |
| Ehrlich | Vertical; umbilicus kept; vest-over-pants overlap |
| Monfort | Vertical; umbilicus kept; central fascial plate with lateral flaps overlapped; transperitoneal access for combined surgery |
- **Also know:** Furness (extraperitoneal plication); Dénes; corset.

### Prognosis and follow-up
- About 20% die perinatally; **up to 30% develop chronic renal failure**. **Nadir creatinine <0.7 mg/dL at 1 year** predicts stable function. Lifelong timed voiding, cultures and imaging; transplant when needed.

**Diagram to draw:** fetal ultrasound sketch: huge bladder, dilated posterior urethra, bilateral hydroureteronephrosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Triad; 95% male; dysplasia and lungs decide survival.
> - Ureter is a failed pump (collagen); VUR about 75%; keep the proximal ureter.
> - Woodard I–III; vesicostomy for drainage; orchidopexy at about 6 months.
> - Nadir creatinine <0.7 mg/dL is favourable; up to 30% reach renal failure.

## Describe neurophysiology of lower urinary tract and various types of voiding dysfunction in children
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q1
(frequency and urgency in a child also in 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3c)

### Introduction
- The LUT (lower urinary tract) has two opposite functions: **storage** at low pressure with a closed outlet, and **emptying** by a coordinated detrusor contraction with a relaxed outlet. Bladder and sphincter act reciprocally.

### Peripheral innervation
| | Parasympathetic | Sympathetic | Somatic |
|---|---|---|---|
| Origin | **S2–S4** (sacral parasympathetic nucleus) | Thoracolumbar (T10–L2) | **Onuf nucleus** (S2–S4 ventral horn) |
| Nerve | Pelvic | Hypogastric | Pudendal |
| Bladder | Contracts (ACh [acetylcholine] → **M3**; ATP) | Relaxes (noradrenaline → **β3**) | – |
| Outlet | Relaxes urethra (**NO**, nitric oxide) | Contracts bladder neck (**α1**) | Contracts EUS (external urethral sphincter; nicotinic) |
| Role | Voiding | Storage | Voluntary closure |

- All three nerves carry afferents. **Aδ fibres** (tension receptors in muscle) signal normal fullness. **Unmyelinated C fibres** are "silent" normally; after inflammation, SCI (spinal cord injury) or obstruction they are recruited and drive overactivity (basis of capsaicin and resiniferatoxin).
- Detrusor has more M2 receptors, but **M3 mediates contraction**.

### Reflex and central control
- **Storage (spinal reflexes):** low afferent activity → sympathetic outflow (detrusor inhibition, bladder-neck closure) and the **guarding reflex** (pudendal → rising sphincter activity with filling).
- **Voiding (spinobulbospinal reflex):** high afferent activity → **PAG (periaqueductal grey)** → **PMC (pontine micturition centre, Barrington nucleus)** → sphincter relaxes first, then detrusor contracts. PMC is an on–off switch.
- **Forebrain decides:** insula senses fullness; **medial prefrontal cortex** tonically holds the switch "off" until voiding is socially appropriate.
- Lesion patterns: above pons → overactivity, coordinated sphincter; between pons and sacral cord → overactivity with **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)**; sacral/peripheral → underactive bladder.
- **Central transmitters:** glutamate excitatory; GABA (γ-aminobutyric acid) and glycine inhibitory; serotonin inhibits voiding; dopamine tonically inhibits the PMC (lost in Parkinson disease).

```
Filling → Aδ afferents → sacral cord → PAG
  low traffic: spinal storage reflexes (sympathetic + guarding)
  high traffic + cortex "permits": PMC → EUS relaxes → detrusor contracts
```

### Maturation in children
- Bladder visible on fetal ultrasound by 14 weeks. Infants void reflexly but **wake before voiding** (cortical arousal gates voiding).
- Infant voiding is often interrupted (transient dyscoordination) with detrusor pressure often **>100 cmH2O**; frequent small voids largely disappear by **2 years**.
- Capacity rises: **EBC (expected bladder capacity) = (age + 2) × 30 mL** (infants: weight in kg × 7 mL).
- Toilet training: bowel control → daytime bladder → night-time bladder. Persisting dyscoordination after toddlerhood is dysfunction, not immaturity.

### Types of voiding dysfunction in children
**A. Non-neurogenic (functional) LUT dysfunction (ICCS [International Children's Continence Society])**

| Type | Key feature |
|---|---|
| **OAB (overactive bladder)** | Urgency ± frequency (≥8 voids a day), urgency incontinence; tower flow |
| **Voiding postponement** | Habitual holding; holding manoeuvres; infrequent voids (≤3 a day) |
| **Dysfunctional voiding** | Habitual sphincter/pelvic-floor contraction during voiding in a neurologically normal child; **staccato** flow |
| **Underactive bladder** | Needs abdominal straining to void; interrupted flow |
| Giggle incontinence | Complete emptying with laughter |
| Vaginal reflux | Small leak just after voiding in girls |
| **Enuresis** | Wetting in sleep, ≥5 years; monosymptomatic or non-monosymptomatic |

- **BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction):** LUT dysfunction plus constipation; rectal loading worsens symptoms and UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Hinman (non-neurogenic neurogenic bladder):** severe end of dysfunctional voiding → trabeculated bladder, VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), renal damage.

**B. Neurogenic LUT dysfunction**
- Causes: **myelomeningocele** (commonest), occult dysraphism/**tethered cord**, sacral agenesis, anorectal malformation, cerebral palsy, SCI, transverse myelitis, pelvic tumour surgery (sacrococcygeal teratoma).
- Pattern = detrusor (overactive or underactive) × outlet (competent, incompetent or dyssynergic). Bony level does not predict bladder function.
- **Hostile bladder:** **DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) ≥40 cmH2O** (McGuire 1981), poor compliance or NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity) with DSD.

**C. Anatomical:** **PUV (posterior urethral valves)** "valve bladder": overactivity → poor compliance → myogenic failure; ectopic ureter.

### Evaluation
- Bladder and bowel diary, Bristol stool chart, spine and neurological examination, urinalysis.
- **Uroflowmetry with PVR (post-void residual) and patch EMG (electromyography)**: bell (normal), tower (OAB), staccato (dysfunctional voiding), interrupted (underactive/DSD), plateau (obstruction).
- Invasive (video)urodynamics only for neurogenic, anatomical or refractory cases.

### Management principles
- **Treat constipation first**; standard urotherapy (timed voiding, posture, fluids) for 4 weeks.
- Dysfunctional voiding: **biofeedback**; α-blocker; sphincter botulinum toxin if refractory. OAB: **oxybutynin 0.3–0.6 mg/kg/day** (maximum 15 mg); mirabegron; botulinum toxin; parasacral TENS (transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation).
- Enuresis: alarm or desmopressin.
- Neurogenic: **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation; Lapides 1971) + antimuscarinic** → intradetrusor botulinum toxin → augmentation.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder with pelvic, hypogastric and pudendal nerves, plus brainstem loop (PAG–PMC).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - S2–S4 pelvic (voiding), T10–L2 hypogastric (storage), Onuf/pudendal (EUS).
> - PMC = switch; medial prefrontal cortex holds it off.
> - EBC = (age + 2) × 30 mL; staccato = dysfunctional voiding.
> - DLPP ≥40 cmH2O = hostile; treat bowel first.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Embryology of Posterior Urethral Valves.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- PUV (posterior urethral valves): commonest cause of bladder outlet obstruction in boys (1.4–2.2 per 10,000 births). Morgagni described them (1769); Hugh Hampton Young named and classified them (1919).

### Normal development
- Weeks 5–6: the **urorectal septum** divides the cloaca into the anorectal canal and the **urogenital sinus**.
- The **Wolffian (mesonephric) ducts** join the cloaca just before division. Their orifices normally migrate to the **verumontanum**, leaving small folds (plicae colliculi).
- The **urogenital membrane** normally disappears completely.
- Fetal urine starts at 10–14 weeks; after 16 weeks amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine. So obstruction in this period damages bladder, kidneys and lungs.

### Young types and their origin
| Type | Anatomy | Embryology |
|---|---|---|
| **I (~95%)** | Leaflets from the verumontanum fuse anteriorly just proximal to the external sphincter | Abnormal insertion of the Wolffian duct ends into the anterolateral cloacal wall: hypertrophied inferior urethral crest |
| **II** | Folds from verumontanum to bladder neck | Not real (Stephens 1955): secondary bladder-neck hypertrophy |
| **III (5–10%)** | Annular diaphragm with a central hole | **Persistent urogenital membrane** |

### Other theories
- Hypertrophied mucosal folds (oldest); cloacal remnants; Wolffian–Müllerian maldevelopment.
- **COPUM (congenital obstructing posterior urethral membrane, Dewan):** one oblique membrane, torn by the first catheter into apparent type I leaflets. Plausible but unproven.
- **Cobb's collar (Moormann's ring):** a congenital ring-like narrowing of the proximal bulbar urethra, just distal to the external sphincter; some authors group it with type III valves.
- **Genetics:** mostly sporadic; chromosome 11 duplications; copy number variants (worse renal outcome); TBX5, PTK7.

### Consequences
- Thick bladder, dilated posterior urethra (**keyhole sign**), hydroureteronephrosis, **renal dysplasia**, oligohydramnios, pulmonary hypoplasia. Pop-offs (VUR (vesicoureteral reflux), urinoma, ascites) relieve pressure.

**Diagram to draw:** posterior urethra with type I leaflets versus a type III membrane.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Type I (95%): abnormal Wolffian duct insertion; type III: persistent urogenital membrane; type II not real.
> - Urorectal septum divides the cloaca at weeks 5–6.
> - COPUM: one membrane torn into leaflets by catheterisation.
> - Dysplasia is fixed in utero; ablation cannot reverse it.

## Anomalies detected on Prenatal scan between 16th and 20th weeks of gestation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3d

### Introduction
- The **anomaly scan is done at 18–20 weeks** (ACOG (American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists)); by 20 weeks most urological anomalies are visible.
- **Why this window:** after about 16 weeks amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine, so **oligohydramnios reflects renal failure or outlet obstruction**. The bladder should be seen filling and emptying.
- Survey kidneys, collecting system, ureters, bladder, genitalia, amniotic fluid, abdominal wall and spine. AFI (amniotic fluid index): normal 8–18 cm; oligohydramnios <5–6 cm.

### UTD (urinary tract dilation) risk, 16–27 weeks
| Parameter | A1 (low) | A2–3 (any one) |
|---|---|---|
| APRPD (anteroposterior renal pelvic diameter) | 4 to <7 mm | ≥7 mm |
| Calyces | Central or none | Peripheral |
| Parenchyma, ureter, bladder | Normal | Abnormal |
| Oligohydramnios | No | Unexplained |
- A1: rescan after 32 weeks. A2–3: scan every 4 weeks. Both: postnatal scan after 48 hours.
- Causes: transient (50–70%), PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, VUR (vesicoureteral reflux), megaureter, duplex, LUTO (lower urinary tract obstruction).

### Anomalies and signs
| Anomaly | Signs |
|---|---|
| **PUV (posterior urethral valves)** | Male; thick bladder; **keyhole sign**; bilateral hydroureteronephrosis; oligohydramnios |
| **Bilateral renal agenesis** | **Anhydramnios after 16 weeks**, no bladder, no renal arteries: lethal Potter sequence |
| **MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney)** | Non-communicating cysts, no central pelvis |
| **ARPKD (autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease)** | Large, homogeneously **bright** kidneys; oligohydramnios |
| **Bladder exstrophy** | **Bladder never seen**, low cord insertion, lower abdominal mass, wide pubis |
| **Prune belly** | Thin-walled large bladder, empty scrotum |
| **Myelomeningocele** | Spinal defect, lemon and banana signs |
- **Also know:** duplex kidney with ureterocele (intravesical cyst); cloacal exstrophy (adds omphalocele, spinal defects); hydrocolpos; ambiguous genitalia; renal ectopia.

### Implications
- Severe LUTO: fetal MRI (magnetic resonance imaging), urine biochemistry, vesicoamniotic shunt or fetal cystoscopy at 18–24 weeks. **PLUTO** (shunt trial): better survival, not renal function. Plan delivery with a multidisciplinary team.

**Diagram to draw:** fetal keyhole sign.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Anomaly scan 18–20 weeks; after 16 weeks amniotic fluid = fetal urine.
> - UTD A2–3 = APRPD ≥7 mm or any abnormal feature.
> - Keyhole + oligohydramnios = PUV; absent bladder = exstrophy or agenesis.
> - ARPKD large bright kidneys; MCDK non-communicating cysts.

## USG in neonates
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 1, Q3d

*Assumed meaning: ultrasonography (USG) of the neonatal urinary tract, including antenatal hydronephrosis follow-up.*

### Why ultrasound first
- No radiation or sedation; repeatable. Shows **anatomy, not function**.
- **Technique:** higher-frequency probes than in adults; kidneys supine and prone; length against age nomograms; bladder full and after voiding; Doppler.

### Normal neonatal kidney (pitfalls)
- Cortex **iso- or hyperechoic** to liver; **prominent hypoechoic pyramids mimic dilated calyces or cysts**.
- **Dilation is under-read in the first 48 hours** (physiological dehydration).

### Antenatal hydronephrosis follow-up
- **First scan after 48 hours**; earlier if solitary kidney or suspected PUV (posterior urethral valves).
- Normal postnatal **APD (anteroposterior pelvic diameter) <10 mm**, no calyceal or ureteric dilation.

| UTD (urinary tract dilation) risk | Findings | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **P1** (SFU [Society for Fetal Urology] 1–2) | APD 10–15 mm, central calyces | Repeat US at 3–6 months; no VCUG or prophylaxis |
| **P2** (SFU 3) | APD ≥15 mm, peripheral calyces or dilated ureter | Repeat US; VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) and prophylaxis at discretion |
| **P3** (SFU 4) | Abnormal parenchyma or bladder | Repeat US at 1 month; VCUG and prophylaxis |

- **SFU grades:** 0 none; 1 pelvis only; 2 major calyces; 3 major and minor calyces; 4 grade 3 plus parenchymal thinning.

### Other neonatal indications
| Condition | Ultrasound finding |
|---|---|
| PUV | Thick bladder, **keyhole sign**, bilateral hydroureteronephrosis |
| MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney) | Non-communicating cysts of varying size, scant parenchyma |
| ARPKD (autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease) | Large, bilaterally echogenic kidneys |
| Masses | Mesoblastic nephroma; adrenal haemorrhage; renal vein thrombosis (large kidney, hypertension) |
| Duplex anomalies | Ureterocele, ectopic ureter, megaureter |
| Spine | Occult dysraphism before arch ossification (up to 6 months) |
| DSD (differences of sex development) | Müllerian structures, gonads |

**Also know:** hydrometrocolpos, scrotal masses, contrast-enhanced voiding urosonography.

### Limitations
- Normal US (ultrasound) does not exclude VUR (vesicoureteral reflux). Function needs MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renography (after renal maturation) or DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid).

**Diagram to draw:** SFU grades 1–4 as kidney outlines.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - First postnatal scan after 48 h; normal APD <10 mm.
> - Echogenic cortex and prominent pyramids are normal in neonates.
> - Keyhole sign + thick bladder = PUV; spinal US only up to 6 months.

## Nocturnal enuresis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 10 Paper 1, Q3a
(also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q3e)

### Definition (ICCS [International Children's Continence Society])
- Intermittent incontinence **during sleep** in a child **aged 5 years or more**.
- **Primary:** never dry. **Secondary:** wetting after at least **6 months** dry (look for stressors, diabetes, UTI [urinary tract infection], neurological disease).
- **MNE (monosymptomatic):** no daytime LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms). **NMNE (non-monosymptomatic):** with daytime LUTS.

### Epidemiology
- About **5–10% at 7 years**; falls with age; about 2% of adults; familial.

### Pathophysiology: three-way mismatch
1. **Nocturnal polyuria** (poor night-time ADH [antidiuretic hormone] rise).
2. **Small nocturnal bladder capacity** or DO (detrusor overactivity).
3. **Failure to arouse** from sleep.
- **Why sleep apnoea matters:** obstructed breathing makes arousal difficult and releases atrial natriuretic peptide, which raises urine output.
- Comorbidities: constipation, sleep-disordered breathing, ADHD (attention deficit hyperactivity disorder).

### Evaluation
- History, examination (spine, genitalia, abdomen), urinalysis.
- **Bladder diary:** voided volumes against EBC (expected bladder capacity) = (age + 2) × 30 mL; night output by weighing nappies.
- Uroflowmetry, PVR (post-void residual), ultrasound only for NMNE or before anticholinergics.

### Management
- **Treat daytime LUTS and constipation first.** Education; waking, fluid restriction and punishment do not work.

| | **Alarm** | **Desmopressin** |
|---|---|---|
| Target | Arousal (conditioning) | Night urine output |
| Use | Nightly; until 14 dry nights | 0.2–0.4 mg tablet or 120–240 µg melt, 1 hour before bed |
| Response | 50–70%; durable | Rule of thirds; relapses off drug |
| Caution | Motivated family | Limit evening fluid (hyponatraemia) |
- **Failure:** switch or combine. **Second line:** add oxybutynin after checking PVR and bowels. **Resistant:** imipramine (cardiotoxic; ECG [electrocardiogram]), off-label β3-agonists, adenotonsillectomy for sleep apnoea.
- **Adults:** new enuresis may signal high-pressure chronic retention: catheterise and treat obstruction.

**Diagram to draw:** triad: nocturnal polyuria, small capacity, arousal failure.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≥5 years; secondary after 6 months dry.
> - Alarm (durable) and desmopressin (rule of thirds) are first line.
> - Treat daytime symptoms and constipation first.

## Neurogenic bladder in myelodysplasia
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 22 SAQ Sheet, Q3b
(prenatal and neonatal assessment also in 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3e)

### Introduction
- Myelodysplasia (NTD [neural tube defect], mainly **myelomeningocele**) is the commonest cause of neurogenic LUT (lower urinary tract) dysfunction in children. Lesions: lumbosacral 47%, lumbar 26%, sacral 20%.
- **Bony or motor level does not predict bladder function**; the lesion can progress (re-tethering), so urodynamic testing is essential.
- About 10% of deaths in SB (spina bifida) are urological. Folic acid 400 µg daily before conception prevents 50–70% of NTDs.

### Patterns
- Detrusor: overactive (NDO, neurogenic detrusor overactivity) or underactive. Outlet: competent, incompetent or dyssynergic.
- Newborns: most have detrusor contractions; over a third are acontractile. Needle EMG (electromyography): intact sacral arc about 40%, partial denervation 25%, complete 35%.

### Evaluation (EAU [European Association of Urology] 2022)
- Renal and bladder ultrasound after birth, then serially; **UDS (urodynamics) within 3 months**; baseline DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid); creatinine (cystatin C better with low muscle mass); VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or video-UDS for VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), which is dilating in 7%.

| Risk (McGuire 1981) | Finding |
|---|---|
| Hostile | DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) or end-filling pressure ≥40 cm H₂O |
| Intermediate | NDO, poor compliance, 25–39 cm H₂O |
| Safe | Below 25 cm H₂O |

### Management (kidneys first, then continence)
- **Early CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation; Lapides 1971) plus oxybutynin** for hostile bladders. Hydronephrosis 0–15% versus 18–80% expectant; ESRD (end-stage renal disease) 0–1.6% versus about 30%.
- Others: solifenacin, mirabegron; **botulinum toxin A** about 10 U/kg (maximum 360 U), continence 65–87%.
- Surgery: vesicostomy (bridge); **augmentation cystoplasty**; Mitrofanoff channel; bladder-neck sling, reconstruction or artificial sphincter.
- VUR: CIC and antimuscarinic first; avoid Credé; reimplant during augmentation if grade ≥III.
- **Also know:** latex allergy; bowel programme; sexual dysfunction (about 75% of men have ED [erectile dysfunction]; sildenafil helps); precocious puberty.

**Diagram to draw:** UDS trace with DLPP above 40 cm H₂O.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Level does not predict bladder: UDS within 3 months.
> - DLPP ≥40 cm H₂O = hostile (McGuire 1981).
> - Early CIC plus antimuscarinic protects kidneys.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe embryology of bladder. What are the proposed theories for extrophy epispadias complex? Describe skeletal abnormalities. (LAQ)

- Vesicourethral canal → bladder; urachus from allantois.
- Marshall–Muecke: overdeveloped cloacal membrane; rupture timing decides variant.
- CBE: external rotation 12°/18°, anterior pubis 30% short, diastasis ~4.8 cm.
- Cloacal exstrophy: spinal anomalies 85–100%; osteotomy always.

#### Prune Belly Syndrome. Antenatal diagnosis, pathophysiology and postnatal management. (LAQ)

- Triad; 95% male; dysplasia and lungs decide survival.
- Ureter is a failed pump (collagen); VUR about 75%; keep the proximal ureter.
- Woodard I–III; vesicostomy for drainage; orchidopexy at about 6 months.
- Nadir creatinine <0.7 mg/dL is favourable; up to 30% reach renal failure.

#### Describe neurophysiology of lower urinary tract and various types of voiding dysfunction in children (LAQ)

- S2–S4 pelvic (voiding), T10–L2 hypogastric (storage), Onuf/pudendal (EUS).
- PMC = switch; medial prefrontal cortex holds it off.
- EBC = (age + 2) × 30 mL; staccato = dysfunctional voiding.
- DLPP ≥40 cmH2O = hostile; treat bowel first.

#### Embryology of Posterior Urethral Valves. (SAQ)

- Type I (95%): abnormal Wolffian duct insertion; type III: persistent urogenital membrane; type II not real.
- Urorectal septum divides the cloaca at weeks 5–6.
- COPUM: one membrane torn into leaflets by catheterisation.
- Dysplasia is fixed in utero; ablation cannot reverse it.

#### Anomalies detected on Prenatal scan between 16th and 20th weeks of gestation (SAQ)

- Anomaly scan 18–20 weeks; after 16 weeks amniotic fluid = fetal urine.
- UTD A2–3 = APRPD ≥7 mm or any abnormal feature.
- Keyhole + oligohydramnios = PUV; absent bladder = exstrophy or agenesis.
- ARPKD large bright kidneys; MCDK non-communicating cysts.

#### USG in neonates (SAQ)

- First postnatal scan after 48 h; normal APD <10 mm.
- Echogenic cortex and prominent pyramids are normal in neonates.
- Keyhole sign + thick bladder = PUV; spinal US only up to 6 months.

#### Nocturnal enuresis (SAQ)

- ≥5 years; secondary after 6 months dry.
- Alarm (durable) and desmopressin (rule of thirds) are first line.
- Treat daytime symptoms and constipation first.

#### Neurogenic bladder in myelodysplasia (SAQ)

- Level does not predict bladder: UDS within 3 months.
- DLPP ≥40 cm H₂O = hostile (McGuire 1981).
- Early CIC plus antimuscarinic protects kidneys.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Female pelvic floor anatomy: prolapse, SUI surgery and fertility-sparing treatment of procidentia
**Asked as:**
- Describe female pelvic anatomy in relation to prolapse and SUI — *Summer 2015 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe anatomy of pelvic floor in females. Discuss fertility sparing treatment options for procedentia. — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Describe anatomy of pelvic floor in females. Discuss fertility preserving treatment options for procidentia — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe anatomy of pelvic floor in females and its importance in surgeries described for SUI — *Practice Set 11 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

(pelvic floor anatomy also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q2 and 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q2; also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q1)

*"Procedentia" is read as procidentia (complete uterine prolapse); "fertility sparing" / "fertility preserving" means uterus-preserving treatment for a woman who wants future pregnancy.*

### Introduction
- **SUI (stress urinary incontinence):** involuntary leakage on effort, coughing or sneezing.
- Support comes from the **levator ani, endopelvic fascia and ligaments, perineal membrane and perineal body**, anchored to the **bony pelvis** (pelvic organs are supported by the **levator ani**, the **endopelvic fascia and its ligaments** and the **perineal membrane and perineal body**). Failure causes **pelvic organ prolapse (POP)** and **stress urinary incontinence (SUI)**. Every SUI operation uses one of these anchors.

### A. Bony pelvis and surgical anchors
- **Bony landmarks** and dangers: **sacrospinous ligament** (pudendal bundle), **Cooper's ligament** (bleeding), **sacral promontory** (left common iliac vein, right ureter).
- **Sacrospinous ligament (SSL)**, 7–8 cm, ischial spine to sacrum: the pudendal nerve and vessels pass behind the spine, the sacral plexus just posterior.
- **Sacral promontory**: left common iliac vein and right ureter each about 2.7 cm away.

### B. Pelvic floor muscles: pelvic diaphragm (levator ani + coccygeus)

| Muscle | Attachment | Function |
|---|---|---|
| Puborectalis | Back of pubis, loops behind anorectal junction (sling behind anorectal junction) | Anorectal angle; anorectal sling |
| Pubococcygeus (pubovaginal part) | Posterior pubis and arcus tendineus → viscera (vagina, perineal body, anus), anococcygeal raphe (coccygeus is separate) | Slings urethra and vagina; closes hiatus, lifts urethra |
| Iliococcygeus | ATLA (arcus tendineus levator ani), ischial spine → raphe, coccyx | Horizontal **levator plate** |
| Coccygeus (not levator) | Ischial spine → sacrum, coccyx | Lies on sacrospinous ligament |

- Levator tone (tonic contraction) closes the **urogenital hiatus**, through which all POP passes; the uterus and upper vagina rest on the levator plate.
- Nerve to levator ani **(S3–S5)** runs on the pelvic surface; **pudendal nerve (S2–S4)** supplies the sphincters and perineal muscles.
- **Muscle fails first, fascia second:** once injury (birth injury, avulsion) opens the hiatus, ligaments carry the load and fail; the fascia then carries the whole load and stretches.
- Levator defects in up to **20% of primiparous women**; avulsion predicts advanced prolapse.

### C. Endopelvic fascia and ligaments
- **Cardinal ligament** (side wall to cervix; carries the uterine artery) and **uterosacral ligament** (sacrum to cervix): the **cardinal–uterosacral complex** suspends the apex; **rectovaginal fascia** posteriorly. The ureter is 4 cm from the uterosacral ligament near the sacrum but 0.9 cm near the cervix (ureter close to uterosacral at the cervix).
- **Pubocervical (pubovesical) fascia** attaches laterally to the **ATFP (arcus tendineus fasciae pelvis)**, the "white line" from the lower pubis to just above the ischial spine.
- **Rectovaginal fascia** supports the posterior wall; **pubourethral ligaments** suspend the urethra from the pubis / anchor the mid-urethra; **urethropelvic ligaments** anchor it to the ATFP.

### D. Perineal membrane, perineal body and urethra
- The perineal membrane spans the pubic arch, pierced by urethra and vagina (urethra and vagina pierce it); above it lies the striated urogenital sphincter. The perineal body anchors the distal vagina.
- Continence zone: **proximal and mid-urethra**; striated sphincter (compressor urethrae, urethrovaginal sphincter) plus oestrogen-sensitive submucosa (~30% of closure).
- **Striated urogenital sphincter**: sphincter urethrae (mid-urethra), **compressor urethrae** and **urethrovaginal sphincter**.
- Urethra 4–5 cm; continence zone is the **proximal and mid-urethra**. The **oestrogen-dependent (oestrogen-sensitive) vascular submucosa** gives up to 30% (~30%) of closure force.

### E. DeLancey's levels of support (1992)
| Level | Region | Structures | Defect causes / loss produces |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I – suspension** | Cervix/upper third | Cardinal–uterosacral complex | **Uterine or vault prolapse**, enterocele |
| **II – attachment** | Middle third | Paravaginal attachment to ATFP, levator fascia | Cystocele (central or paravaginal), rectocele; anterior or posterior wall prolapse |
| **III – fusion** | Distal third | Urethra, perineal membrane, perineal body | **Urethral hypermobility**, perineal descent, distal rectocele |
- **Central cystocele:** midline weakness, rugae lost. **Paravaginal cystocele:** ATFP detachment (Richardson 1981), rugae preserved.
- Level I is the keystone: always correct the apex.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal female pelvis with levator plate and DeLancey levels I–III; hammock cross-section.

### F. Theories of continence and the operations they produced
| Theory | Mechanism | Implication / surgical answer |
|---|---|---|
| **Pressure transmission (Enhorning 1961)** | Hypermobile urethra leaves (drops out of) the abdominal pressure zone | Restore position: Burch, MMK (Marshall–Marchetti–Krantz) |
| **Hammock (DeLancey 1994)** | Urethra lies on anterior vaginal wall and endopelvic fascia attached to ATFP and levator; abdominal pressure compresses it against this layer (a firm backing); repositioning not essential | Restore backing |
| **Integral theory (Petros & Ulmsten 1990)** | Mid-urethral pubourethral ligament laxity (at the **mid-urethra**) | Tension-free mid-urethral sling |
| **Intrinsic sphincter deficiency (ISD) (McGuire 1978)** | Urethral function fails (urethra fails to close); low VLPP (Valsalva leak point pressure) <60 cmH₂O | Sling, bulking, AUS (artificial urinary sphincter) |
- ISD: Valsalva leak point pressure <60 or maximal urethral closure pressure <20 cmH₂O. Most SUI has (includes) some ISD. So slings work in hypermobility, but Burch does poorly in severe ISD / fails in ISD with a fixed urethra.

### G. Innervation
- **Pudendal nerve (S2–4):** sphincters and perineum (perineal muscles); through Alcock's canal near the ischial spine; stretched in delivery.
- **Nerve to levator ani (S3–5):** runs on the muscle's pelvic surface, separate from pudendal; neuropathy in about a quarter of primiparous women.
- Autonomic pelvic splanchnic and hypogastric nerves: injured in radical pelvic surgery.

### H. Why prolapse occurs: risk factors (Bump & Norton)
- Muscle fails first (birth trauma, levator avulsion, denervation). The hiatus widens, the ligaments carry the whole load and stretch.
- Collagen disorders (Ehlers–Danlos, Marfan) add risk; other risks: vaginal birth, forceps, age, obesity, constipation.
- Predisposing: genetics, collagen disorders (Ehlers–Danlos, Marfan). Inciting: **vaginal delivery** (strongest; forceps worse), hysterectomy. Promoting: obesity, constipation, cough. Decompensating: ageing (age).

### I. POP-Q (Pelvic Organ Prolapse Quantification, Bump 1996)
- Nine points in cm relative to the **hymen (0)**: inside negative, outside positive; measured on maximal strain, recorded on a 3 × 3 grid.

| Point | Definition |
|---|---|
| Aa / Ap | Anterior / posterior wall 3 cm inside hymen (−3 to +3) |
| Ba / Bp | Most distal point of anterior / posterior wall (−3 if no prolapse) |
| C | Cervix or vault cuff |
| D | Posterior fornix (omitted after hysterectomy) |
| gh, pb, tvl | Genital hiatus, perineal body, total vaginal length (at rest) |

| Stage | Most distal point |
|---|---|
| 0 | No prolapse |
| I | >1 cm above hymen |
| II | Within ±1 cm of hymen |
| III | >1 cm below hymen, < (tvl – 2) cm |
| IV | Complete eversion (≥ tvl – 2) |
- **Also know:** Baden–Walker grades 0–4; stage I–II often normal (POSST study).

### J. Examination
- Lithotomy and **standing** with Valsalva; Sims speculum for each compartment; lateral sulci (paravaginal defect); rectovaginal examination.
- **Cough stress test**, repeated with prolapse reduced (**occult SUI**); **Q-tip** angle change >30° = hypermobility; levator strength and avulsion; urodynamics if ISD or mixed symptoms.

### K. How anatomy guides surgery
| Defect | Operation | Principle |
|---|---|---|
| SUI | **Mid-urethral sling**: retropubic (Ulmsten 1995, TVT [tension-free vaginal tape]) or transobturator (Delorme 2001) | Mid-urethral support |
| SUI | **Burch colposuspension** | Fascia to Cooper's ligament |
| SUI – ISD | Autologous sling at bladder neck, bulking | Compression |
| Paravaginal / central cystocele | Paravaginal repair / anterior colporrhaphy | Reattach to ATFP / midline plication |
| Apical | **Sacrocolpopexy** (most durable) | Mesh to ligament over S1 |
| Apical (vaginal) | **Sacrospinous fixation** 1.5–3 cm medial to spine; uterosacral suspension (cystoscopy for ureters) | Avoids pudendal bundle |
| Rectocele | Posterior or site-specific repair, perineorrhaphy | Levels II–III |
| Elderly, not sexually active | Colpocleisis | Obliteration |
- **April 2019:** the US FDA (Food and Drug Administration) stopped transvaginal mesh for anterior POP; slings unaffected.

#### Importance in SUI surgery

| Operation | Anatomy used | Anatomical hazards |
|---|---|---|
| **Burch colposuspension** | Space of Retzius (avascular: symphysis in front, bladder behind, endopelvic fascia below). Paravaginal fascia at mid-urethra and bladder neck sutured to **Cooper's (iliopectineal) ligament** | Bleeding from Cooper's ligament and perivesical veins; ureteric kinking (cystoscopy); needs a mobile vagina |
| Paravaginal repair / VOS (vagino-obturator shelf) | Lateral vaginal sulcus to **ATFP** or obturator fascia (level II) | Obturator vessels and nerve |
| **Retropubic MUS (mid-urethral sling), TVT** | Tape under mid-urethra (integral theory), through **endopelvic fascia** and Retzius; suprapubic exits about 2 cm from midline | Bladder perforation (4.5%), vessels, bowel |
| **TOT (transobturator tape)** | Through obturator membrane, **obturator internus and externus**; exit at adductor longus tendon notch | Groin pain (6.4%), obturator neurovascular bundle; bladder injury rare (0.6%) |
| **SIMS (single-incision mini-sling)** | Anchors in obturator internus or endopelvic fascia | Works by restricting mobility |
| **Autologous PVS (pubovaginal sling)** | Rectus fascia at the **bladder neck** (proximal urethra), arms to rectus | Voiding dysfunction; best for ISD |
| Bulking agents | **Submucosa** coaptation | Repeated injections |

- **Why mid-urethra:** it is the zone of maximal closure pressure and pubourethral support. A loose tape lets the posterior urethral wall be compressed (and kinked) during stress.
- **TOMUS (2010):** retropubic 80.8% vs transobturator 77.7% objective success, not different.
- **SISTEr:** autologous sling more successful than Burch at 2 years, with more voiding dysfunction.
- **Also know:** needle suspensions abandoned (sutures pulled through); anterior colporrhaphy is not an SUI operation; levator palpable through the lateral vaginal walls for PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training) assessment; uncorrected level I loss causes recurrent prolapse after SUI repair.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section of urethra on the vaginal hammock (DeLancey) with Burch, bladder-neck sling and mid-urethral sling sites.

### L. Procidentia: definition and assessment
- **Procidentia**: the uterus lies wholly outside the hymen — complete uterine (or vault) prolapse outside the hymen, **POP-Q (pelvic organ prolapse quantification) stage 4**.
- Assess all compartments, occult SUI (stress urinary incontinence) with prolapse reduced, PVR (post-void residual), hydronephrosis (ureteric kinking at the hiatus), cervical length and ulceration.
- Abnormal bleeding or cervical pathology needs gynaecological evaluation first, because it may argue against keeping the uterus.

### M. Fertility-sparing (fertility-preserving) options
**Principle:** restore **level I** support while keeping uterus and cervix. Defer definitive surgery until the family is complete where possible, because pregnancy and delivery cause recurrence.

**1. Conservative (first line)**
- PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training), weight loss, treat constipation.
- **Pessary**: space-filling type (Gellhorn, cube) for procidentia; removed for conception; regular review about 6-monthly for discharge and erosion.

**2. Uterus-preserving surgery**

| Operation | Technique | Fertility points |
|---|---|---|
| **Sacrospinous hysteropexy** (vaginal) | Cervix sutured to (into) the SSL **1.5–3 cm medial to the ischial spine**, into the ligament | Non-inferior to vaginal hysterectomy with uterosacral suspension (SAVE U trial); later data suggest superiority. Buttock pain, pudendal injury |
| **Uterosacral hysteropexy** (vaginal or laparoscopic) | Shorten and reattach uterosacral ligaments to cervix, sutures lateral to medial | No mesh; cystoscopy for ureteric kinking |
| **Sacrohysteropexy** (open, laparoscopic, robotic) | Mesh from posterior cervix to anterior longitudinal ligament over the promontory | Most durable apical support; later pregnancy (delivery) usually by caesarean |
| **Manchester (Fothergill)** | Cervical amputation, cardinal ligaments sutured in front of the stump, anterior colporrhaphy | Suits elongated cervix, but risks **cervical stenosis, incompetence, miscarriage, preterm labour**: avoid if pregnancy wanted |

- **Also know:** Indian abdominal (cervicopexy) slings for young nulliparous prolapse (Shirodkar, Purandare, Khanna, Virkud). **Le Fort colpocleisis** keeps the uterus but not fertility (elderly only).

**3. Choosing**
```
Procidentia, wants pregnancy -> PFMT + pessary
  -> fails -> sacrospinous hysteropexy or
     laparoscopic sacrohysteropexy (avoid Manchester)
  -> recurrence after childbearing -> hysterectomy
     + vault suspension or sacrocolpopexy
```

### N. Complications and follow-up
- Recurrence (mostly posterior), de novo SUI, mesh exposure (3.4–5.4% after sacrocolpopexy; another source: 3.4–5.4% vaginal mesh exposure in meta-analyses), ureteric kinking. The retained uterus needs cervical screening.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Levator ani = puborectalis, pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus; S3–S5.
> - DeLancey: I suspension (uterosacral–cardinal; failure causes uterine prolapse), II attachment (ATFP), III fusion (urethral hypermobility); level I is the keystone.
> - Enhorning 1961, DeLancey 1994, Petros & Ulmsten 1990, McGuire ISD 1978.
> - Integral theory → mid-urethral sling; hammock → backing, not repositioning.
> - Burch → Cooper's ligament; TOT → obturator foramen; PVS → bladder neck.
> - Central cystocele rugae lost, paravaginal preserved; POP-Q II = ±1 cm of hymen.
> - Pessary first; sacrospinous hysteropexy 1.5–3 cm medial to the ischial spine.
> - Manchester amputates the cervix: poor choice for future pregnancy.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Leak point pressures (VLPP, ALPP, DLPP) in SUI: clinical implications
**Asked as:**
- VLPP in SUI — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*
- Leak point pressures-clinical implication. — *Summer 2020 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- **LPP (leak point pressure):** pressure at which leakage is first seen, spontaneous or provoked. Two independent types:

| Feature | ALPP (abdominal LPP), incl. VLPP (Valsalva LPP) and CLPP (cough LPP) | DLPP (detrusor LPP) |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | Total Pves (vesical pressure) at leak during raised abdominal pressure, **no detrusor contraction** | Lowest Pdet (detrusor pressure) at leak, **without contraction or straining** |
| Measures | Sphincter strength (sphincter vs abdominal pressure) | Bladder pressure against (vs) outlet resistance |
| Bad result | **Low** (weak sphincter) (**low = bad**) | **High** (danger to upper tracts) (**high (>40 cmH₂O) = bad**: upper-tract risk (McGuire 1981)) |
| Used in | SUI (stress urinary incontinence) | Neurogenic or low-compliance bladder |

- **VLPP (Valsalva leak point pressure):** the **intravesical pressure** at which urine leaks during Valsalva, **without a detrusor contraction**.
- It is a type of **ALPP (abdominal leak point pressure)**, which measures the urethra's ability to resist abdominal pressure. Lower value = weaker sphincter.
- Seen only in SUI (stress urinary incontinence); **no normal value** (continent women never leak).

### VLPP: technique
1. Small dual-lumen urethral catheter plus rectal catheter; zero to atmosphere.
2. Patient **standing or sitting**; fill to **150 mL** (original method; some use 200 mL).
3. Ask for a **graded Valsalva**; record the **lowest total Pves (vesical pressure)** at visible leak (include baseline, not the change).
4. No leak: repeat every 50 mL to capacity; then (at capacity) **remove the urethral catheter** and read pressure from the rectal line.
5. Report volume, catheter size and provocation (Valsalva or cough).

### VLPP: interpretation and clinical implications (McGuire 1993)
| VLPP (cmH₂O) | Inference (McGuire 1993) |
|---|---|
| **<60** | **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)** |
| **60–90** | Equivocal; mixed |
| **>90** (≥90) | **Urethral hypermobility**, little ISD |

- Low VLPP (ISD) traditionally favours a sling or bulking agent over colposuspension.

### Limitations
- Not standardised: falls with **larger catheters** and **higher volumes**.
- **VLPP is lower than CLPP (cough leak point pressure)** in the same woman (VLPP < CLPP); state the method.
- Invalid during DO (detrusor overactivity) or poor compliance; prolapse raises it (reduce it).
- ISD and hypermobility coexist; VLPP **does not predict surgical outcome** (poor predictor of surgical outcome).
- **VALUE trial (Nager 2012):** for uncomplicated SUI, office evaluation was non-inferior to urodynamics before surgery. AUA/SUFU (Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) 2023 / AUA (American Urological Association) 2023 SUI guideline: urodynamics optional in the index patient.
- **Guidelines:** EAU (European Association of Urology): do not use LPP to grade severity.

### DLPP: clinical implications
- **McGuire (1981):** DLPP **>40 cmH₂O** in myelodysplastic children → hydronephrosis or VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) in **85%**.
- A strong outlet keeps the patient dry but transmits pressure to the kidneys; a weak outlet "pops off" safely.
- **Hostile bladder** (≥40 cmH₂O) → CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) plus antimuscarinic; botulinum toxin, augmentation or vesicostomy if needed.
- **Caveat:** 40 is not a validated "safe" value; 20 cmH₂O is more sensitive in children.

**Also know:** DO leak point pressure (ICS (International Continence Society) 2017); MUCP (maximum urethral closure pressure) ≤20 cmH₂O suggests ISD.

**Diagram to draw:** Pves, Pabd and Pdet traces (tracing) marking ALPP and DLPP, with leak marked on Valsalva.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - VLPP: Pves at leak with Valsalva, no detrusor contraction; 150 mL.
> - ALPP low = weak sphincter; DLPP high = upper-tract danger.
> - VLPP <60 ISD; 60–90 equivocal; >90 (≥90) hypermobility.
> - VLPP < CLPP; catheter size and volume alter it.
> - DLPP >40: upper-tract danger; 85% upper-tract change (McGuire 1981).
> - Lower DLPP is safer; no validated safe cut-off.

## Burch colposuspension
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3b
(related: 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q2)

### Definition
- Retropubic suspension (**Burch, 1961**) of the paraurethral and para-bladder-neck vaginal wall to **Cooper's (iliopectineal) ligament**; Tanagho (1976) modified it.

### Principle
- SUI (stress urinary incontinence) from **urethral hypermobility**: the urethra drops out of the retropubic position. Elevation restores **pressure transmission** and a backstop; scarring holds it.
- It treats hypermobility, not ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency).

### Indications
- SUI or stress-predominant MUI (mixed urinary incontinence) with hypermobility, especially with **concomitant abdominal surgery** (sacrocolpopexy), **mesh refusal**, or poor vaginal access.
- **Contraindications:** immobile urethra or vagina, ISD alone, pure urgency incontinence.

### Work-up
