- Cough stress test, PVR (post-void residual), urinalysis; urodynamics only if SUI is not demonstrated.

### Steps (open)
1. Low lithotomy, Foley; Pfannenstiel incision; enter the **space of Retzius**.
2. Vaginal finger elevates the vagina; sweep bladder medially. **Avoid midline dissection** over urethra.
3. Two to four 0 or 1-0 delayed-absorbable or non-absorbable sutures each side through vaginal wall (not lumen): distal at mid-urethra, proximal at bladder neck, placed laterally.
4. Pass through Cooper's ligament; tie with vaginal elevation, without tension; leave about **two fingerbreadths** between bladder neck and pubis.
5. **Cystoscopy:** ureteric efflux, no suture in bladder.
- Laparoscopic and robotic versions match open short-term results.

### Outcomes
- Cure 85–90% at 1 year, about 70% at 5 years (Cochrane).
- **SISTEr (2007):** autologous fascial sling more effective but more voiding dysfunction and UTI.
- **Ward and Hilton:** TVT (tension-free vaginal tape) equivalent to colposuspension; meta-analyses favour midurethral slings.
- **CARE (2006):** prophylactic Burch at sacrocolpopexy reduced postoperative SUI.

### Complications
- Voiding dysfunction 2–27%, de novo storage symptoms 8–27%, **de novo prolapse** 2.5–27%; bladder or ureteric injury; bleeding.
- **Also know:** MMK (Marshall–Marchetti–Krantz; osteitis pubis 0.7–2.5%), paravaginal repair, VOS (vagino-obturator shelf); failure predictors: BMI (body mass index) >25, age >60, prior surgery.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis with sutures from paravaginal fascia to Cooper's ligament.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Burch 1961: vaginal wall to Cooper's ligament; for hypermobility.
> - Cure 85–90% at 1 year, ~70% at 5 years.
> - Niche: concomitant abdominal surgery, mesh avoidance.

## Management of perforated mid urethral sling
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3a

### Terminology (IUGA/ICS, International Urogynecological Association / International Continence Society)
- **Perforation:** mesh in a hollow organ (bladder, urethra). **Exposure:** mesh seen through unhealed vaginal epithelium. **Extrusion:** passage out of a tissue. "Erosion" is no longer used.
- Two settings: **intraoperative trocar injury** and **late mesh perforation**.

### A. Intraoperative trocar injury
- Bladder injury: 4.5% retropubic versus **0.6% transobturator**; registries 2.7–3.8%.
- **Detection:** cystoscopy with a **70° lens and full bladder distension** after every trocar pass.

| Injury | Management |
|---|---|
| **Bladder** | Withdraw trocar, **repass more laterally**, confirm on cystoscopy, complete the sling; catheter overnight to 72 h. Benign; no later mesh perforation. |
| **Urethra** | **Do not place mesh** (AUA/SUFU, American Urological Association / Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction). Repair, catheter drainage; **staged** anti-incontinence surgery after healing. |

### B. Late perforation of bladder or urethra
- **Presentation:** recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), haematuria, urgency, pain, stones on mesh, voiding dysfunction, fistula. Often months later.
- **Evaluation:** examination, **cystourethroscopy**, translabial ultrasound (best imaging for mesh); exclude fistula.

**Management**
- **Bladder:** endoscopic excision (scissors, resection or holmium laser) flush with the wall; or transvesical laparoscopic or robotic excision; open cystotomy if large.
- **Urethra:** transvaginal excision of mesh in the field and layered urethral repair; **Martius flap** if complex or fistula.
- **Continence after excision:** SUI recurs in many (44–100% after synthetic sling revision). Options: concomitant **autologous pubovaginal sling** (Shah 2013: good results) or staged. A damaged urethra predicts poor continence.
- Avoid urethral dilation after mesh slings; it can cause perforation.

### Prevention
- Empty bladder; trocar hugging the pubis; bottom-up retropubic route; transobturator route when suitable.

**Also know:** Amid type I macroporous polypropylene integrates best; chronic pain or infection may need excision from both sides of the bone.

**Diagram to draw:** retropubic and transobturator trocar paths with bladder at risk.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder trocar injury: repass laterally, continue.
> - Urethral injury: abandon mesh; stage the repair.
> - Late perforation: excise mesh, repair, consider autologous sling.

## TOT in SUI
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 1, Q3d
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3f)

### Introduction
- **TOT (transobturator tape):** a **MUS (mid-urethral sling)** of macroporous monofilament polypropylene passed through the obturator foramina instead of the retropubic space. Delorme (2001) designed it to avoid bladder, bowel and vessel injury.

### Mechanism
- **Integral theory:** pubourethral ligaments and the suburethral hammock maintain continence at the mid-urethra.
- A loose tape supports the posterior urethral wall, which is compressed against it on straining. It anchors in **obturator internus and externus**.

### Indications and evaluation
- **SUI (stress urinary incontinence)** or stress-predominant mixed incontinence after failed PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training).
- History, examination, **objective stress test**, urinalysis, PVR (post-void residual); urodynamics only if complicated. Counsel about permanent mesh and alternatives.

### Technique (outside-in)
1. Lithotomy, hips hyperflexed; 1.5 cm midline vaginal incision 1.5 cm from the meatus; dissect to the ischiopubic ramus.
2. Groin stab incision at the notch between inferior pubic ramus and **adductor longus tendon**.
3. Trocar perforates the obturator membrane (felt as a give), turns medially onto the finger and exits the vaginal incision.
4. Cystourethroscopy; check the vaginal sulci.
5. Tension-free: scissors pass easily between tape and urethra.
- **Inside-out** (de Leval): fewer vaginal perforations, more voiding dysfunction.

### Outcomes
- **TOMUS (2010):** objective success 77.7% vs 80.8% retropubic, not significantly different.
- Poorer with **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)** or a **fixed urethra**: retropubic sling preferred.

| | TOT | Retropubic |
|---|---|---|
| Bladder perforation | 0.6% | 4.5% |
| Groin pain | 6.4% | 1.3% |
| Voiding dysfunction | Less | More |
| Long-term repeat surgery | Possibly more | Less |

### Complications
- Groin or thigh pain, vaginal mesh exposure, voiding dysfunction (sling incision), de novo urgency, UTI (urinary tract infection), rarely thigh abscess.
- **Also know:** SIMS (single-incision mini-sling); alternatives: Burch colposuspension, autologous fascial sling, bulking agents.

**Diagram to draw:** pelvis with tape under the mid-urethra through both obturator foramina.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Delorme 2001; anchors in obturator muscles.
> - TOMUS: equivalent to retropubic (77.7% vs 80.8%).
> - Fewer bladder injuries, more groin pain; weaker in ISD.

## Describe clinical presentation of pelic floor prolapse. diagnostic evaluation and brief discussion of various surgical options.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3d
*Assumed meaning: pelvic organ prolapse (POP) in women.*
(pelvic floor anatomy also in Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q1)

### Definition
- POP (pelvic organ prolapse): descent of the anterior wall, posterior wall, uterus/cervix or vault. Anterior > posterior > apical. It is a disease only if symptomatic.
- Cause: loss of **DeLancey support** (level I uterosacral–cardinal apex; II paravaginal to the ATFP [arcus tendineus fasciae pelvis]; III perineal body) and levator ani injury. Risks: vaginal delivery, parity, age, obesity.

### Clinical presentation
- **Vaginal bulge** (most specific), pressure, heaviness, worse by evening.
- **Splinting/digitation** to void or defecate (high-grade POP).
- Urinary: voiding difficulty (urethral kinking), OAB (overactive bladder); SUI (stress urinary incontinence) in ~40%, may be **occult**.
- Bowel (rectocele): obstructed defecation, stool trapping. Sexual dysfunction, discharge, ulceration; procidentia may cause hydronephrosis.

### Diagnostic evaluation
- Examine supine and standing on strain; split speculum per compartment.
- **POP-Q (Pelvic Organ Prolapse Quantification, 1996):** points Aa, Ba, C, D, Ap, Bp measured from the hymen (above negative), plus gh, pb, tvl.

| Stage | Leading edge |
|---|---|
| 0 | No prolapse |
| I | < −1 cm |
| II | −1 to +1 cm |
| III | > +1 cm but < (tvl − 2) cm |
| IV | Complete eversion |

- Urinalysis, PVR (post-void residual), diary; **cough test or urodynamics with prolapse reduced** for occult SUI; renal ultrasound (procidentia); dynamic MRI or translabial ultrasound if complex or recurrent; pessary trial confirms POP causes the symptoms.

### Surgical options
| Type | Operations |
|---|---|
| **Restorative (native tissue)** | Anterior colporrhaphy; paravaginal repair; posterior midline plication; perineorrhaphy |
| **Apical (vaginal)** | **Uterosacral suspension**, **sacrospinous fixation** (OPTIMAL: similar), vaginal hysterectomy; uterus-sparing hysteropexy, Manchester |
| **Compensatory (abdominal)** | **Sacrocolpopexy** (open, laparoscopic, robotic): most durable; mesh exposure ~3–10% |
| **Obliterative** | **Le Fort / total colpocleisis**: elderly, not sexually active |

- Transvaginal synthetic mesh for POP is withdrawn (FDA order, 2019). Add a sling for occult SUI (OPUS, CARE trials).

**Diagram to draw:** DeLancey three levels of support.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bulge is most specific; splinting means high grade.
> - POP-Q stage II = −1 to +1 cm.
> - Sacrocolpopexy most durable; colpocleisis for elderly.

## Female urethral diverticula
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 1, Q3e

### Definition
- **UD (urethral diverticulum):** a urine-filled periurethral sac within the periurethral fascia, connected to the urethra by an **ostium**.
- Third to seventh decades; prevalence up to 1–6%.

### Aetiology
- Mostly **acquired**. **Routh theory** (most accepted): infection of **periurethral glands** leads to duct obstruction, suburethral abscess, rupture into the lumen and an epithelialised sac.
- Hence the ostium is **posterolateral at mid or distal urethra** in ≥90%.
- **Also know:** childbirth trauma theory (but 20–30% are nulliparous).

### Clinical features
- "Three Ds" (dysuria, dyspareunia, post-void dribbling) in only about 5%.
- Commonest: frequency, urgency, pain, **recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection)**.
- Tender anterior vaginal wall mass; about one-third incontinent.
- **Complications:** stones (4–10%); 10% show metaplasia, dysplasia or carcinoma (**adenocarcinoma** commonest).

### Evaluation
- Urine culture; **cystourethroscopy** (ostium seen in under half).
- **Imaging sensitivity:** **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) 97%**, double-balloon urethrography 95%, ultrasound 82%, VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) 74%.
- MRI shows saddlebag or circumferential extension.
- **Videourodynamics:** true SUI (stress urinary incontinence) versus dribbling from the sac.
- **Differential diagnosis:** Skene cyst (distal, distorts meatus, no communication), Gartner cyst, leiomyoma, caruncle, bulking agent.

### Classification
- **L/N/S/C3 (Leach):** location, number, size, configuration, communication, continence.
- **Also know:** Leng–McGuire (absent fascia = pseudodiverticulum).

### Management
- **Asymptomatic or unfit:** suppressive antibiotics, post-void stripping; counsel about cancer.
- **Symptomatic: transvaginal diverticulectomy.**
- **Principles:**
  1. Sterile urine; topical oestrogen if atrophic.
  2. **Inverted-U** anterior vaginal wall flap.
  3. **Preserve periurethral fascia** as a separate layer.
  4. Excise the **entire sac** and close the ostium.
  5. **Watertight** urethral closure over a catheter (as small as 12 Fr).
  6. **Multilayered, non-overlapping** closure; obliterate dead space.
  7. **Martius flap** if poor tissue.
- **Bothersome SUI:** concomitant **autologous pubovaginal sling**; **never synthetic mesh**.
- **Also know:** Spence–Duckett marsupialisation for distal lesions only.
- Pericatheter VCUG at 14–21 days.

### Complications
- Recurrence 10–22%; de novo SUI 7–16%; urethrovaginal fistula 1–8%; stricture; recurrent UTI.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal section of a mid-urethral sac and its ostium.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Periurethral gland infection (Routh); posterolateral ostium.
> - MRI most sensitive (97%).
> - Preserve fascia; non-overlapping layers; Martius flap.
> - No synthetic mesh at diverticulectomy.

## Endoscopic procedure of SUI
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 1, Q3f

*Read as endoscopic injection of urethral bulking agents.*

### Introduction
- **SUI (stress urinary incontinence)** results from urethral hypermobility and **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)**.
- **Bulking agents**, injected submucosally under cystoscopic vision, are the least invasive surgical option.

### Mechanism
- Bulk at the proximal urethra improves **mucosal coaptation** and resistance.
- It does not correct hypermobility, but most SUI includes some ISD.

### Indications
- ISD with a **fixed, immobile urethra** (low VLPP [Valsalva leak point pressure] <60 cmH₂O, historically).
- Women wanting a **low-risk** procedure; elderly or unfit; future pregnancy.
- Persistent leakage after a sling; neurogenic SUI (safer for bladder compliance than a sling).
- **Avoid in:** active UTI (urinary tract infection), untreated detrusor overactivity.

### Agents

| Agent | Note |
|---|---|
| Polyacrylamide hydrogel (Bulkamid) | Non-particulate |
| Polydimethylsiloxane (Macroplastique) | Silicone particles |
| Calcium hydroxylapatite; carbon-coated zirconium beads | Particulate |
| Bovine collagen (skin test); PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene); autologous fat | Withdrawn or abandoned: allergy, migration, granuloma, embolism |

### Technique
1. Sterile urine; local anaesthesia; cystoscope (0–30° lens) in the urethra.
2. **Transurethral:** needle into the submucosa about 1–1.5 cm distal to the bladder neck; or **periurethral:** needle alongside the urethra, watched cystoscopically.
3. Inject at 2–4 sites until the mucosal cushions **coapt in the midline**.
4. Avoid an indwelling catheter (it moulds the bulk); use CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation) for retention.

### Outcomes
- Early improvement is common, but cure is lower than with slings and **declines with time**; repeat injections are often needed.
- **Cochrane review (Kirchin 2017):** limited evidence of benefit.
- **AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction):** an option, with counselling on durability.
- **Men:** EAU (European Association of Urology): **do not offer** for post-prostatectomy incontinence.

### Complications
- Transient retention, UTI, dysuria, haematuria, urgency.
- Sterile abscess, migration, erosion; may mimic a urethral diverticulum on imaging.

**Diagram to draw:** cystoscopic view of coapted mucosal cushions after injection.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bulking improves coaptation; does not correct hypermobility.
> - Best for fixed urethra with ISD, or unfit patients.
> - Less durable than slings; repeat injections common.
> - Not recommended for post-prostatectomy incontinence.

## Eponymous techniques of VVF repair
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 22 SAQ Sheet, Q3c
(management of VVF also in Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q2)

### Introduction
- VVF (vesicovaginal fistula): an epithelialised bladder–vagina tract. **Principles:** exclude ureteric involvement and cancer; tension-free, multilayer, non-overlapping, watertight closure; interposition when needed; drainage ≥10–14 days. **The first repair has the best chance.**

### Transvaginal techniques
- **Sims:** first successful systematic vaginal repair (silver wire).
- **Flap-splitting (Raz)**:
  - Stent ureters if close; Foley through the tract for traction.
  - J or U incision with a 1–3 mm cuff; raise anterior and posterior vaginal flaps.
  - Layer 1: bladder (3-0 or 4-0), with the tract edge. Layer 2: perpendicular perivesical layer. Fill test 200–300 mL.
  - Optional Martius or peritoneal flap; advance the opposite vaginal flap ≥2 cm beyond. Closure ≥90%.
- **Latzko (1942), high partial colpocleisis**:
  - Denude vaginal epithelium 1–2 cm around the tract; the bladder is **not opened or dissected**.
  - Close vaginal walls over the tract in layers.
  - ≥90% success in non-irradiated post-hysterectomy apical VVF; favoured near ureters or after radiation. Drawback: **vaginal shortening and dyspareunia**.
- **Also know:** vaginal cuff excision (all apical scar excised as an inverted cone).

### Abdominal technique
- **O'Conor and Sokol (1951)**, suprapubic extraperitoneal:
  - Midline cystotomy at the dome, **bivalved down** to the fistula; ureteric catheters.
  - Excise the tract; separate bladder from vagina 2–3 cm beyond.
  - Close vagina; omentum or peritoneum between; close bladder in layers; suprapubic catheter.
- Modifications: limited-cystotomy transvesical; extravesical. Laparoscopic (1994) and robotic (2005) versions give equal closure with less morbidity.

### Interposition flaps
| Flap | Blood supply | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Martius (1928)**, labial fibrofatty pad | Internal pudendal (inferior), external pudendal (superior) | Mid–distal VVF; does not reach the apex |
| Peritoneal | Local | High apical VVF |
| Omental J-flap | Gastroepiploic | Abdominal, radiation |
| Gracilis, Singapore | Myocutaneous | Refractory cases |
- RCT (randomised controlled trial): Martius adds nothing in simple VVF.

**Diagram to draw:** Latzko denuded area versus flap-splitting layers.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Latzko: colpocleisis, bladder not opened, vagina shortened.
> - O'Conor: bivalve bladder to the fistula, interpose omentum.
> - Martius for distal, omentum or peritoneum for apical VVF.

## Endometriosis in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 22 SAQ Sheet, Q3d

### Definition and epidemiology
- Endometriosis is endometrial glands and stroma outside the uterus. Urinary tract involvement occurs in about 1–5% of women with endometriosis, mostly with deep disease.
- Sites: **bladder** (commonest, about 80%) > ureter (about 15%) > kidney, urethra (rare).

### Pathogenesis
- Retrograde menstruation (Sampson), coelomic metaplasia, lymphovascular spread, and **iatrogenic implantation** at pelvic surgery (e.g. caesarean section).
- Bladder lesions start on the **serosa** (vesicouterine pouch) and invade the detrusor.
- Ureteric disease: **extrinsic** (periureteric fibrosis, commoner) or intrinsic (muscularis or mucosa); usually distal, left more often.

### Clinical features
- **Bladder:** cyclical haematuria or frequency, urgency and dysuria with menses; suprapubic pain. Differential: BPS (bladder pain syndrome), vesicouterine fistula (menouria).
- **Ureter:** often **silent**; hydronephrosis and loss of renal function may go unnoticed.

### Evaluation
- Transvaginal and transabdominal ultrasound gauge size and depth; **renal ultrasound in every woman with deep endometriosis**.
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** extent; high T1 signal from blood products.
- **Cystoscopy:** limited value (disease is extravesical); may show a bluish submucosal lesion. Biopsy excludes carcinoma.
- CTU (computed tomography urography) and MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram for obstruction and split function.
- Staging: rASRM (revised American Society for Reproductive Medicine) I–IV; #Enzian records bladder and ureter.

### Management (with gynaecologist)
- **Medical:** progestins (dienogest), combined pill, GnRH (gonadotrophin-releasing hormone) analogues. Symptomatic relief; not for obstructive ureteric disease.
- **Bladder:** **partial cystectomy** (full-thickness excision with margin), laparoscopic or robotic; stent ureters if near. TUR (transurethral resection) is incomplete, with recurrence and perforation risk.
- **Ureter:**
  - Extrinsic, mild: ureterolysis.
  - Intrinsic or stricture: segmental resection with ureteroureterostomy, or **reimplantation with psoas hitch or Boari flap**.
  - Non-functioning kidney: nephrectomy.
- Treat all pelvic disease at the same operation to lower recurrence.
- **Also know:** risk factor for VVF (vesicovaginal fistula) and ureteric injury at hysterectomy; rare malignant change (clear-cell, endometrioid).

**Diagram to draw:** endometriotic nodule in the vesicouterine pouch invading the detrusor.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder commonest; cyclical haematuria.
> - Ureteric disease silent: screen kidneys.
> - Partial cystectomy, not TUR.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 6 · Female Urology

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Female pelvic floor anatomy: prolapse, SUI surgery and fertility-sparing treatment of procidentia (LAQ)

- Levator ani = puborectalis, pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus; S3–S5.
- DeLancey: I suspension (uterosacral–cardinal; failure causes uterine prolapse), II attachment (ATFP), III fusion (urethral hypermobility); level I is the keystone.
- Enhorning 1961, DeLancey 1994, Petros & Ulmsten 1990, McGuire ISD 1978.
- Integral theory → mid-urethral sling; hammock → backing, not repositioning.
- Burch → Cooper's ligament; TOT → obturator foramen; PVS → bladder neck.
- Central cystocele rugae lost, paravaginal preserved; POP-Q II = ±1 cm of hymen.
- Pessary first; sacrospinous hysteropexy 1.5–3 cm medial to the ischial spine.
- Manchester amputates the cervix: poor choice for future pregnancy.

#### Leak point pressures (VLPP, ALPP, DLPP) in SUI: clinical implications (SAQ)

- VLPP: Pves at leak with Valsalva, no detrusor contraction; 150 mL.
- ALPP low = weak sphincter; DLPP high = upper-tract danger.
- VLPP <60 ISD; 60–90 equivocal; >90 (≥90) hypermobility.
- VLPP < CLPP; catheter size and volume alter it.
- DLPP >40: upper-tract danger; 85% upper-tract change (McGuire 1981).
- Lower DLPP is safer; no validated safe cut-off.

#### Burch colposuspension (SAQ)

- Burch 1961: vaginal wall to Cooper's ligament; for hypermobility.
- Cure 85–90% at 1 year, ~70% at 5 years.
- Niche: concomitant abdominal surgery, mesh avoidance.

#### Management of perforated mid urethral sling (SAQ)

- Bladder trocar injury: repass laterally, continue.
- Urethral injury: abandon mesh; stage the repair.
- Late perforation: excise mesh, repair, consider autologous sling.

#### TOT in SUI (SAQ)

- Delorme 2001; anchors in obturator muscles.
- TOMUS: equivalent to retropubic (77.7% vs 80.8%).
- Fewer bladder injuries, more groin pain; weaker in ISD.

#### Describe clinical presentation of pelic floor prolapse. diagnostic evaluation and brief discussion of various surgical options. (SAQ)

- Bulge is most specific; splinting means high grade.
- POP-Q stage II = −1 to +1 cm.
- Sacrocolpopexy most durable; colpocleisis for elderly.

#### Female urethral diverticula (SAQ)

- Periurethral gland infection (Routh); posterolateral ostium.
- MRI most sensitive (97%).
- Preserve fascia; non-overlapping layers; Martius flap.
- No synthetic mesh at diverticulectomy.

#### Endoscopic procedure of SUI (SAQ)

- Bulking improves coaptation; does not correct hypermobility.
- Best for fixed urethra with ISD, or unfit patients.
- Less durable than slings; repeat injections common.
- Not recommended for post-prostatectomy incontinence.

#### Eponymous techniques of VVF repair (SAQ)

- Latzko: colpocleisis, bladder not opened, vagina shortened.
- O'Conor: bivalve bladder to the fistula, interpose omentum.
- Martius for distal, omentum or peritoneum for apical VVF.

#### Endometriosis in urology (SAQ)

- Bladder commonest; cyclical haematuria.
- Ureteric disease silent: screen kidneys.
- Partial cystectomy, not TUR.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe pathophysiology of OAB. Briefly outline management &describe nonmedical non-pharmacological management
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q2
(neuromodulation also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3e and 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3b)

### Definition
- **OAB (overactive bladder), ICS (International Continence Society):** urgency, usually with frequency and nocturia, with or without UUI (urgency urinary incontinence), without UTI (urinary tract infection) or other obvious pathology.
- OAB is a **symptom** diagnosis. DO (detrusor overactivity) is a **urodynamic** finding; only 44–69% of patients with urgency show DO.

### Pathophysiology
All mechanisms converge on **more afferent signal reaching awareness**: the bladder turns the signal up, or the CNS (central nervous system) fails to turn it down.

| Mechanism | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **Afferent (sensory)** | Aδ fibres normally sense filling. Unmyelinated **C fibres** are "silent", but become spontaneously active and hypersensitive in pathological states |
| **Urothelial** | Urothelium acts as a transducer: mechanical, inflammatory or chemical stimuli release ATP (adenosine triphosphate), acetylcholine and other mediators onto suburothelial afferents |
| **Myogenic** | Patchy denervation causes receptor upregulation and spontaneous contraction; exaggerated **micromotions** cause urgency, and coordinated spread across the wall gives DO |
| **Neurogenic / central** | Forebrain and PAG–PMC (periaqueductal grey–pontine micturition centre) normally inhibit voiding; loss of this inhibition lets "afferent noise" be felt as urgency |
| **Other** | Bladder–bowel crosstalk (constipation), ischaemia–reperfusion, chronic inflammation, altered urinary microbiome |

- **Risk factors:** ageing, female sex, BOO (bladder outlet obstruction: an animal model, but only an indirect cause in humans; DO persists in up to 60% after TURP [transurethral resection of the prostate]), anxiety and depression, metabolic syndrome, poor toileting habits.

### Brief outline of management
- **Assessment:** history, examination, **urinalysis** (minimum); bladder diary (3–7 days); questionnaires; PVR (post-void residual) when indicated. Urodynamics only if it will change an invasive decision.
- **Ladder (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction]):** not a compulsory sequence.
```
Education + behavioural therapy (first line)
        ↓  (4–12 weeks)
Antimuscarinic or β3-agonist (mirabegron, vibegron)
        ↓
PTNS / onabotulinumtoxinA / SNM (third line)
        ↓
Augmentation cystoplasty or urinary diversion
```
- Antimuscarinics: dry mouth, constipation; **dementia risk with use >3 months**, so β3-agonists are preferred in the elderly.

### Non-pharmacological management
**1. Education and lifestyle**
- Explain normal bladder function; set realistic goals.
- Reduce fluid intake by about 25% if excessive; cut caffeine; treat constipation.
- **Weight loss:** about 8 kg loss in obese women reduced UUI episodes.

**2. Bladder training (BT)** (breaks the cycle of urgency, frequent voiding, smaller capacity)
1. Keep a bladder diary.
2. Start a fixed interval (often 1 hour while awake).
3. Void by the clock, not by urge.
4. Increase by 15–30 minutes a week as control improves.
5. Target interval 3–4 hours.
- Works by **uncoupling the void from the urge**.

**3. PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training) and urge suppression**
- Kegel exercises: quick 1–2 s flicks and 5–10 s holds, twice daily.
- A pelvic floor contraction **reflexly inhibits the detrusor**. Urge suppression: stop, stay still, squeeze quickly several times, relax, let the urge pass, then walk calmly to the toilet.
- PFMT and BT reduce incontinence episodes by **50–80%**.
- **Also know:** biofeedback helps patients who cannot find the muscle; electrical stimulation at 5–20 Hz inhibits the detrusor; timed and prompted voiding for cognitively impaired patients.

**4. PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation)**
- 34-gauge needle 3–4 cm above the medial malleolus; 20 Hz, 200 μs; confirm great-toe flexion or sole tingling. **30 min weekly for 12 weeks**, then maintenance.
- Posterior tibial nerve (L4–S3) shares sacral roots with the bladder. **SUmiT trial:** 55% vs 21% (sham) responders.

**5. SNM (sacral neuromodulation)**
- Mechanism: stimulates **afferents** that modulate brainstem and forebrain control.
- **Test phase:** PNE (percutaneous nerve evaluation, 5–7 days) or **staged tined lead** at **S3** (anal "bellows" and great-toe flexion). ≥50% improvement on diary → implant pulse generator in the buttock.
- **InSite trial:** 61% vs 42% (standard medical therapy). Revision >30% at 5 years; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional rechargeable devices now available.

**6. Intradetrusor onabotulinumtoxinA**
- Cleaves SNAP-25 (synaptosomal-associated protein 25), blocking acetylcholine release; also reduces afferent signalling.
- **100 U in 10 mL at 20 sites** cystoscopically; effect lasts about 6–9 months.
- About 25% become completely dry. Risks: UTI about 20%, retention needing CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation). **ROSETTA:** greater UUI reduction than SNM at 6 months, but more UTI.

**7. Last resort:** augmentation cystoplasty or urinary diversion.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder showing urothelial afferents, C fibres and micromotions, with the brainstem and forebrain inhibitory pathways.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - OAB is defined by urgency; DO is a urodynamic finding.
> - Final pathway: increased afferent signal (urothelial, myogenic) or failed central inhibition.
> - BT and PFMT with urge suppression first: 50–80% fewer leaks.
> - PTNS (SUmiT 55% vs 21%), SNM (InSite 61% vs 42%), Botox 100 U.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia (DESD / DSD)
**Asked as:**
- External Sphincter Dyssynergia — *Summer 2016 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Detrusor sphincter dyssynergia — *Practice Set 10 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- **DESD / DSD (detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia):** the striated sphincter contracts involuntarily (involuntary contraction) during detrusor contraction, causing functional obstruction, high pressure and poor emptying.
- **It requires a lesion between the pons and the sacral cord:** suprasacral spinal cord injury (SCI), MS (multiple sclerosis), transverse myelitis, myelomeningocele. Brain lesions (stroke, Parkinson's) do not cause it (**never with brain lesions**).
- Lesions (at or) above T6 may add bladder-neck (**smooth sphincter**) dyssynergia and autonomic dysreflexia.

### Blaivas classification
| Type | EMG (electromyography) pattern | Lesion |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Activity rises with detrusor pressure, then relaxes suddenly at peak | Incomplete |
| 2 | Sporadic contractions throughout voiding | Complete |
| 3 | Crescendo–decrescendo contraction; obstruction throughout | Complete |
- Alternative: intermittent versus continuous (continuous means a complete lesion). Type has no relation to lesion level (type is unrelated to level).

### Diagnosis
- **Pressure–flow study with EMG:** EMG rises during the detrusor contraction, with high pressure and interrupted flow. Needle EMG is more reliable than surface.
- **VUDS (videourodynamics):** narrowed (narrow) membranous urethra with dilated posterior urethra; shows VUR (vesicoureteral reflux).
- **Exclude:** guarding reflex (normal); **pseudodyssynergia** (straining or suppressing a contraction); dysfunctional voiding (learned, no neurological lesion; Hinman syndrome in children).

### Significance
- Worse when complete, continuous and **in men**: about half of untreated men get VUR, hydronephrosis, stones or urosepsis. Other risk factors: high filling pressure, indwelling catheter.
- **Management logic:** DSD is both a storage and an emptying failure. Lower bladder pressure, then empty it.
- DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) ≥40 cmH₂O endangers the kidneys.

### Management
- **First line:** CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) plus antimuscarinic or β3-agonist; then intradetrusor botulinum toxin.
- **CIC impossible (men):** sphincterotomy or sphincteric botulinum toxin, with condom drainage. The goal is DLPP <40; DLPP ≥40 afterwards means failure; repeat procedures are common.
- Others: suprapubic catheter, Brindley stimulator with deafferentation, diversion. Urethral stents are abandoned; oral drugs disappoint. Credé and Valsalva voiding contraindicated.
- **Also know:** α-blockers for bladder-neck dyssynergia; lifelong upper-tract surveillance.

**Diagram to draw:** urodynamic trace with rising detrusor pressure (Pdet), EMG bursts and interrupted flow.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lesion between pons and sacral cord; never brain lesions.
> - Blaivas 1 incomplete; 2 and 3 complete.
> - Worst in men; DLPP ≥40 cmH₂O hostile.
> - CIC plus detrusor relaxation first; sphincterotomy if CIC impossible.

## Compare and contrast various detrusor relaxants in management of OAB
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3e
(mirabegron also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3d)

### Introduction and mechanisms
- In OAB (overactive bladder), drugs follow behavioural therapy. Two classes: **antimuscarinics** and **β3-adrenoceptor agonists**.
- **Antimuscarinics:** acetylcholine acts on **M3** receptors (raising intracellular calcium) to contract the detrusor; the commoner M2 enhances it. Blockade reduces involuntary contractions and afferent signalling.
- **β3 agonists:** β3 is the commonest detrusor adrenoceptor. Activation raises **cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate)** and relaxes the detrusor during storage, sparing the voiding contraction.

### Agents

| Drug | Distinguishing feature | Usual dose |
|---|---|---|
| Oxybutynin | Non-selective; direct relaxant; metabolite causes dry mouth | 5 mg two to three times daily; extended-release (ER), patch |
| Tolterodine | Bladder over salivary selective | ER 4 mg daily |
| Fesoterodine | Prodrug of tolterodine's active metabolite | 4–8 mg daily |
| Solifenacin | 10-fold M3 > M2; long half-life | 5–10 mg daily |
| Darifenacin | About 30-fold M3-selective | 7.5–15 mg daily |
| Trospium | Quaternary; does not cross the blood–brain barrier | 20 mg twice daily |
| β3: mirabegron | Inhibits CYP2D6 (a liver enzyme): metoprolol interaction | 25–50 mg daily |
| β3: vibegron | No CYP2D6 interaction | 75 mg daily |

### Comparison

| Feature | Antimuscarinics | β3 agonists |
|---|---|---|
| Efficacy | Similar | Similar (TAURUS: mirabegron ≈ tolterodine) |
| Dry mouth, constipation | Common; main reason for stopping | Near placebo |
| Cognition | Central M1 blockade; **dementia risk 10–65% higher with use >3 months** | No anticholinergic burden |
| Retention | Up to 3% in men | Low |
| Contraindications | Urinary or gastric retention, uncontrolled narrow-angle glaucoma | Uncontrolled hypertension (≥180/110 mmHg) |

- **Choice:** in the elderly, prefer a β3 agonist or ER trospium, darifenacin or fesoterodine; ER forms cause less dry mouth.

### Combination
- Solifenacin plus mirabegron beats each alone (**SYNERGY**); in non-responders it equals solifenacin 10 mg with less dry mouth (**BESIDE**). In men, add to an α-blocker; watch PVR (post-void residual).
- **Also know:** intravesical oxybutynin; imipramine; refractory OAB: onabotulinumtoxinA 100 U, neuromodulation.

**Diagram to draw:** detrusor cell: acetylcholine → M3 → contraction; noradrenaline → β3 → cAMP → relaxation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - M3 mediates contraction; β3 raises cAMP to relax.
> - Equal efficacy; β3 avoids dry mouth and dementia risk (10–65% with antimuscarinics >3 months).
> - Solifenacin + mirabegron (SYNERGY) works.

## EMG
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 9 Paper 1, Q3b

*EMG is taken as urodynamic electromyography of the striated urethral/anal sphincter and pelvic floor.*

### Definition and principle
- EMG (electromyography) records the electrical potentials of depolarising striated muscle membranes, here the **external urethral sphincter and pelvic floor**, during filling and voiding.
- It shows **coordination** between detrusor and sphincter, not sphincter strength. The bladder neck (smooth muscle) is invisible to EMG.
- External anal sphincter activity mirrors the urethral sphincter, except in some neurological diseases.

### Electrodes
| Surface (patch) | Concentric needle |
|---|---|
| Perianal patches at 3 and 9 o'clock (ICS [International Continence Society] standard: two skin electrodes plus reference) | Needle into the sphincter (girls beside the meatus; boys transperineal) |
| Comfortable; poorer signal; movement artefact | Best signal; painful; dislodges |
| Coordination only | Also motor unit analysis: acute versus chronic denervation |

### Normal pattern
- Filling: activity **rises gradually** (recruitment, guarding reflex).
- Voiding: EMG **falls silent before** detrusor pressure rises and stays silent until flow ends.
- **Lag time** (uroflow with patch EMG in children): relaxation to flow 2–6 s is normal; <2 s suggests DO (detrusor overactivity); >6 s suggests primary bladder neck dysfunction.

### Abnormal patterns
| Finding | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)** | Sphincter contracts during detrusor contraction; needs a **suprasacral** cord lesion (SCI [spinal cord injury], MS [multiple sclerosis]). Blaivas types 1–3 |
| Dysfunctional voiding | Same tracing without neurological lesion (learned) |
| Pseudodyssynergia | EMG rise from straining or trying to suppress a contraction |
| Denervation potentials | Sacral cord or cauda equina lesion; MSA (multiple system atrophy) |
| Complex repetitive discharges, decelerating bursts | **Fowler syndrome** (retention in young women); predicts SNM (sacral neuromodulation) response |

### Clinical uses
- Neurogenic bladder (spina bifida, SCI); voiding dysfunction in children; female retention; before sphincter surgery.
- **Limitation:** surface EMG misdiagnoses some dysfunctional voiders; **VUDS (videourodynamics)** is more accurate.

**Diagram to draw:** tracing of Pdet, flow and EMG: EMG silent during normal voiding versus active in DSD.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - EMG judges coordination, not strength.
> - True DSD needs a suprasacral lesion.
> - Lag time 2–6 s normal.
> - Fowler syndrome: complex repetitive discharges on needle EMG.

## Botulinum toxin in urology: mechanism, indications and doses
**Asked as:**
- Botulimum toxin — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- MOA of botulinum toxin. Indications and dosages in UROLOGY — *Practice Set 13 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 9: also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3d. Practice Set 13: also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3d and 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3b)

*"Botulimum" is read as botulinum toxin.*

### Introduction
- Neurotoxin of *Clostridium botulinum*, serotypes A–G. Urology uses **onabotulinumtoxinA (BoNT-A)**, the only licensed formulation: a heavy chain (100 kDa) and a zinc-dependent light chain (50 kDa).

### Mechanism of action
1. The heavy chain binds the cholinergic nerve terminal and the toxin is internalised.
2. The light chain (a zinc protease) **cleaves SNAP-25** (synaptosomal-associated protein 25), a SNARE docking protein. Vesicles cannot fuse, so **acetylcholine release stops**: chemical denervation.
- **Afferent action:** less ATP (adenosine triphosphate), substance P and CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide) release; fewer TRPV1 (capsaicin) and P2X3 (purinergic) sensory receptors, so urgency falls (urgency and pain fall).
- Effect wanes (lasts 6–9 months) as nerve terminals sprout (6–9 months; until terminals sprout), so injections are repeated; repeat injections stay effective.

### Technique
- Cystoscopic (intra)detrusor injection, **20 sites (100 U, in 10 mL) or 30 sites (200 U, in 30 mL)**, usually sparing trigone (trigone usually spared); check PVR (post-void residual) at 2 weeks.

### Urological uses: indications and doses
| Indication | Dose and evidence |
|---|---|
| **Idiopathic OAB (overactive bladder)**, third line | **100 U** (EMBARK). ABC trial: equal to antimuscarinic, more complete dryness. ROSETTA: comparable to SNM (sacral neuromodulation), more UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity):** SCI (spinal cord injury), MS (multiple sclerosis) | **200 U** (DIGNITY): less incontinence, larger capacity, lower pressure; works with or without antimuscarinics |
| **Paediatric NDO** (spina bifida) | About **10 U/kg (maximum 360 U)**, about 30 sites, in published series (Campbell series); FDA (Food and Drug Administration) label 6 U/kg, maximum 200 U |
| **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)** | Intrasphincteric injection, mainly men |
| **IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome)** | Intradetrusor, often trigonal; AUA option, off-label (option) |
| **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** | 100–300 U intraprostatic: **no benefit** over placebo; large RCTs (randomised controlled trials) equal to saline; not used |

- **Also know:** pelvic floor spasm; chronic orchialgia (RCT negative).

### Complications and contraindications
- **Retention needing CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation)**, commoner (more) in NDO; each 10 mL of baseline PVR raises the risk about 27%. The patient must accept CISC beforehand.
- UTI (urinary tract infection), haematuria; rarely generalised weakness. Contraindications (avoid in): active UTI, myasthenia gravis, pregnancy.

**Diagram to draw:** cholinergic synapse showing (with) SNAP-25 cleavage by the light chain.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cleaves SNAP-25; efferent and afferent action.
> - 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO.
> - Children 10 U/kg (maximum 360 U); FDA label 6 U/kg (maximum 200 U).
> - Main risks: retention needing CISC (the main risk), UTI.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe pathophysiology of OAB. Briefly outline management &describe nonmedical non-pharmacological management (LAQ)

- OAB is defined by urgency; DO is a urodynamic finding.
- Final pathway: increased afferent signal (urothelial, myogenic) or failed central inhibition.
- BT and PFMT with urge suppression first: 50–80% fewer leaks.
- PTNS (SUmiT 55% vs 21%), SNM (InSite 61% vs 42%), Botox 100 U.

#### Detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia (DESD / DSD) (SAQ)

- Lesion between pons and sacral cord; never brain lesions.
- Blaivas 1 incomplete; 2 and 3 complete.
- Worst in men; DLPP ≥40 cmH₂O hostile.
- CIC plus detrusor relaxation first; sphincterotomy if CIC impossible.

#### Compare and contrast various detrusor relaxants in management of OAB (SAQ)

- M3 mediates contraction; β3 raises cAMP to relax.
- Equal efficacy; β3 avoids dry mouth and dementia risk (10–65% with antimuscarinics >3 months).
- Solifenacin + mirabegron (SYNERGY) works.

#### EMG (SAQ)

- EMG judges coordination, not strength.
- True DSD needs a suprasacral lesion.
- Lag time 2–6 s normal.
- Fowler syndrome: complex repetitive discharges on needle EMG.

#### Botulinum toxin in urology: mechanism, indications and doses (SAQ)

- Cleaves SNAP-25; efferent and afferent action.
- 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO.
- Children 10 U/kg (maximum 360 U); FDA label 6 U/kg (maximum 200 U).
- Main risks: retention needing CISC (the main risk), UTI.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Spermatogenesis, azoospermia, non-obstructive azoospermia and maturation arrest
**Asked as:**
- Describe physiology of spermatogenesis and discuss management of azoospermia. — *Winter 2017 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Non obstructive azoospermia — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*
- Maturation Arrest. — *Practice Set 10 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*Maturation Arrest taken as spermatogenic maturation arrest on testicular histology.*

### Hormonal control: HPG (hypothalamic–pituitary–gonadal) axis
- Hypothalamus releases **GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone)** in pulses; pulsatility is essential (constant agonist exposure shuts the axis down). Kisspeptin triggers puberty.
- **LH (luteinising hormone)** → Leydig cells → testosterone (about 5 mg/day). **FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)** → Sertoli cells.
- Feedback: testosterone (and oestradiol) on LH; **inhibin B** from Sertoli cells on FSH. So FSH and inhibin B report the tubules, LH reports Leydig cells.
- **Intratesticular testosterone** is about 50-fold higher than serum (held by **ABP (androgen-binding protein)**). This is why exogenous testosterone suppresses spermatogenesis. Intratesticular testosterone is essential for postmeiotic maturation.
- Testis is kept 2–4 °C below core temperature by the pampiniform countercurrent exchange.

### Sertoli cells and blood–testis barrier
- Nurse cells: nourish germ cells, secrete ABP, inhibin, fluid; phagocytose residual bodies.
- **Sertoli–Sertoli tight junctions** divide the tubule into a **basal** compartment (spermatogonia) and an **adluminal** compartment (spermatocytes, spermatids). Post-meiotic cells are "foreign" to the immune system; the barrier and local tolerance protect them.

### Phases of spermatogenesis (about 64 days; 42–76 days)
| Phase | Events |
|---|---|
| **1. Proliferation (spermatocytogenesis)** | Dark type A (Ad, reserve stem cells) self-renew; pale type A (Ap) divide every 16 days into type B spermatogonia → primary spermatocytes. Daughter cells stay joined by cytoplasmic bridges |
| **2. Meiosis** | Primary spermatocytes cross into the adluminal compartment. Meiosis I (reduction division; crossing-over at synapsis) → secondary spermatocytes → meiosis II → 4 haploid round spermatids |
| **3. Spermiogenesis** | No division: acrosome from Golgi, flagellum from centriole, nucleus condenses to about 10%, mitochondria form the midpiece, cytoplasm shed |
| **4. Spermiation** | Sertoli cell releases sperm into the lumen |

- **Cycle, stage, wave:** six stages in humans; cycles arranged as a helical wave, so production is continuous (about 123 million sperm a day).
- Defective meiotic recombination causes maturation arrest and sperm aneuploidy.

### Epididymal maturation and transport
- Testicular sperm are immotile. During 2–12 days of epididymal transit they gain **progressive motility** and fertilising ability; stored in the cauda.
- **Capacitation** and the acrosome reaction occur in the female tract.
- **Clinical relevance:** a new semen sample reflects events about 3 months earlier.

### Azoospermia: definition and classification
- No sperm in the **centrifuged** pellet (ejaculate) on **two** semen analyses (samples); 10–15% of infertile men.
- **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia):** due to **failure of spermatogenesis** (testicular failure). About 60% of azoospermic men.

| Type | Causes | Clues |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-testicular | Kallmann syndrome, pituitary tumour, hyperprolactinaemia, **exogenous testosterone/anabolic steroids** | Low FSH, LH, testosterone |
| Testicular (NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia)) | Klinefelter, Y microdeletion, cryptorchidism, mumps, chemotherapy/radiation | Small testes, high FSH |
| Post-testicular (OA (obstructive azoospermia)) | CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas), epididymal obstruction, vasectomy, hernia repair, EDO (ejaculatory duct obstruction) | Normal testes and FSH |
| Ejaculatory | Retrograde ejaculation, anejaculation | Low volume |

#### Causes of NOA
- **Genetic:** **Klinefelter (47,XXY)**, the commonest; **Y microdeletion** (AZF (azoospermia factor) a, b, c); other karyotype anomalies.
- **Acquired:** cryptorchidism, mumps orchitis, torsion, **chemotherapy or radiation**, trauma, varicocele.
- **Idiopathic**: commonest overall.
- *Note:* hypogonadotropic hypogonadism is pre-testicular and treatable medically (hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) ± FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)).

### Evaluation
```
Azoospermia (2 centrifuged samples)
 ├─ Vasa absent → CBAVD → CFTR (cystic fibrosis gene) testing, both partners; renal scan
 ├─ Volume <1 mL → post-ejaculate urine: sperm = retrograde; none → TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) for EDO
 └─ Normal volume: FSH ≤7.6 IU/L + testis long axis >4.6 cm → obstructive
                   FSH >7.6 + testis ≤4.6 cm → spermatogenic failure
```
- History (testosterone use, surgery, chemotherapy), examination (testes, vasa, epididymis, varicocele), hormones (FSH, LH, testosterone, oestradiol, prolactin).
- NOA: small soft testes, palpable vasa, normal ejaculate volume. **FSH >7.6 IU/L and testis long axis ≤4.6 cm** point to NOA (FSH ≤7.6 with normal testes suggests obstruction).
- **Genetics (AUA/ASRM (American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine)):** karyotype and **Y microdeletion** in all NOA (in all azoospermic men); counselling. AZF (azoospermia factor) **a or b complete deletion: no sperm, do not operate**; AZFc: sperm often found.
- TRUS: seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide or midline cyst suggests EDO.
- **Histology patterns (three patterns in NOA):**

| Pattern | Histology / meaning | Micro-TESE retrieval |
|---|---|---|
| Hypospermatogenesis | All stages (present), fewer cells | Best |
| **Maturation arrest** | Halts at one (a germ-cell) stage | About 40% |
| SCO (Sertoli cell–only) | No germ cells | About 30% |

### Maturation arrest
#### Definition
- A histological pattern of **spermatogenic failure** in which germ cells develop normally up to one stage and **the sequence halts** at a premeiotic, meiotic or postmeiotic step. No mature sperm, or very few, are made.

#### Classification
- **Early:** arrest at spermatogonia or **primary spermatocyte** (meiotic failure).
- **Late:** arrest at the **spermatid** stage.
- **Complete** (all tubules) versus **incomplete** (foci progress to sperm): incomplete gives better retrieval.

#### Causes
- **Genetic:** complete **AZFb (azoospermia factor b) deletion** → arrest; meiotic recombination defects in about half of men with maturation arrest; karyotype anomalies.
- **Acquired:** heat (varicocele, cryptorchidism), chemotherapy, radiation, drugs, orchitis; idiopathic often.

#### Clinical features and diagnosis
- Azoospermia or severe oligospermia, normal semen volume, palpable vasa.
- **Testis size and FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) are often normal**, so it can mimic obstruction (FSH ≤7.6 IU/L, long axis >4.6 cm).
- **Diagnosis needs testicular biopsy** (Bouin's fixative, never formalin), usually done as sperm extraction.
- **Karyotype and Y microdeletion** testing in all azoospermic men (AUA/ASRM).

#### Management of maturation arrest
- Optimise: treat varicocele, stop exogenous testosterone; off-label hormonal stimulation (clomiphene, hCG [human chorionic gonadotropin]) unproven.
- **Micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction)** with ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection): retrieval **about 40%** (versus 30% in SCO).
- **Complete AZFb deletion: retrieval highly unlikely; do not operate.**
- No sperm: donor sperm or adoption. Round spermatid injection is experimental.
- Genetic counselling: sons inherit Y deletions.

### Management of azoospermia
**1. Pre-testicular (hypogonadotropic)**
- Stop exogenous testosterone. **hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) 1500–5000 IU 2–3 times weekly ± FSH**; pulsatile GnRH in hypothalamic failure; dopamine agonist for prolactinoma.

**2. Obstructive**
- Microsurgical **vasovasostomy** or **vasoepididymostomy** (clear watery fluid or sperm parts in vasal fluid → vasovasostomy; thick toothpaste-like fluid without sperm → vasoepididymostomy).
- **TURED (transurethral resection of the ejaculatory ducts)** for EDO.
- CBAVD or unreconstructable: **MESA (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration)** or PESA (percutaneous epididymal sperm aspiration) + **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**; freeze sperm at reconstruction.

**3. Non-obstructive**
- **Optimisation (before retrieval):** stop exogenous testosterone; repair a clinical varicocele (no definitive evidence in NOA); optimise hormones: hormonal therapy off-label (clomiphene, anastrozole, hCG for low testosterone).
- **Micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction)** + ICSI.
  - **Principle:** spermatogenesis in NOA is **focal**. Under ×20–25 magnification, **larger, opaque** (dilated opaque) **tubules** are chosen under the microscope; least tissue removed, blood supply preserved.
  - Sperm found in **about 50%** (about half); better than conventional TESE (Klinefelter 68%, Sertoli-cell-only 30%).

| Predictor | Retrieval (%) |
|---|---|
| Post-orchidopexy | 74 |
| AZFc deletion | 70 |
| Klinefelter | 68 |
| Post-chemotherapy | 53 |
| **AZFa or AZFb complete deletion** | **0: do not operate** |

  - FSH and testis size **do not** reliably predict retrieval.
- **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection):** fresh or frozen testicular sperm. Live birth per cycle modest; miscarriage higher (sperm DNA damage). Male offspring of AZF deletion carriers inherit it.

**4. Ejaculatory**
- Retrograde: sympathomimetics or sperm from alkalinised urine. Anejaculation: vibratory or electroejaculation.

**5. Failure (no sperm):** donor sperm or adoption; genetic counselling.

- **Also know:** spermatogonial stem cell renewal by GDNF (glial cell line–derived neurotrophic factor); in-vitro spermatogenesis and stem-cell transplantation are experimental.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section of a seminiferous tubule showing basal and adluminal compartments with germ cells maturing towards the lumen. For NOA: opened testis under microscope: plump tubules versus thin Sertoli cell–only tubules. For maturation arrest: seminiferous tubule showing germ cells up to spermatocytes with no spermatids or sperm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Spermatogenesis about 64 days; Ap divide every 16 days; 6 stages.
> - Intratesticular testosterone 50 × serum; exogenous testosterone causes azoospermia.
> - FSH ≤7.6 IU/L and testis >4.6 cm → obstructive; FSH >7.6 and testis ≤4.6 cm suggest NOA.
> - Karyotype and Y microdeletion in all.
> - AZFa/b deletion: do not operate (no retrieval); micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA; SCO 30%.
> - Maturation arrest: arrest at one stage; early (spermatocyte) or late (spermatid).
> - FSH and testis size may be normal: biopsy diagnoses.
> - Micro-TESE retrieval in maturation arrest about 40%; none with complete AZFb deletion.

## Physiology of erection, neurotransmitters, and evaluation of the diabetic with ED
**Asked as:**
- Write in detail about physiology of erection. How will you evaluate a diabetic with ED? — *Practice Set 5 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Neurotransmitters in erectile function — *Practice Set 12 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(erection physiology also in Practice Set 5 Paper 1, Q2)

### Introduction
- Erection is **cavernous smooth-muscle relaxation**: low cytosolic Ca²⁺ dilates helicine arteries and sinusoids; expanding sinusoids compress emissary veins (veno-occlusion).
- Pathways: parasympathetic **S2–S4** cavernous nerves (erection), sympathetic **T11–L2** (detumescence), pudendal (sensation, ischiocavernosus rigidity).

### Functional anatomy
- **Corpora cavernosa:** sinusoids lined by endothelium, separated by **trabecular smooth muscle**. Flaccid tone depends on smooth-muscle contraction.
- **Tunica albuginea:** inner circular and outer longitudinal layers. **Emissary veins** pass obliquely between them and are compressed when the corpora expand: the **veno-occlusive mechanism**.
- **Arteries:** internal pudendal → common penile → **cavernous (helicine branches)**, dorsal and bulbourethral arteries. Accessory pudendal arteries in about one-third.
- Spongiosum and glans have a thin or absent tunica, so no veno-occlusion; they stay low-pressure.

### Haemodynamic phases
| Phase | Events | Intracavernous pressure |
|---|---|---|
| Flaccid | Smooth muscle contracted; low inflow | Low |
| Latent (filling) | Arterial dilation; **inflow peaks** | Barely rises |
| Tumescence | Sinusoids fill; venous outflow compressed | Rising |
| Full erection | Maximal veno-occlusion | About 100 mmHg |
| Rigid | **Ischiocavernosus** contraction (pudendal nerve) | Several hundred mmHg |
| Detumescence | Initial, slow, fast phases | Falls to baseline |

### Molecular mechanism and peripheral neurotransmitters
- **Relaxation (erection):** NO (nitric oxide) from **nNOS (neuronal nitric oxide synthase)** in cavernous nerves initiates erection; endothelial NOS (eNOS) sustains it.
  - NO → soluble guanylyl cyclase → **cGMP (cyclic guanosine monophosphate)** → protein kinase G (PKG) → K⁺ channels open, Ca²⁺ channels close, Ca²⁺ sequestered → **low cytosolic Ca²⁺** → relaxation.
  - Second route: PGE1 (prostaglandin E1), VIP (vasoactive intestinal peptide) → **cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate)** → protein kinase A.
  - **PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5)** degrades (breaks down) cGMP and ends relaxation (ends / terminates erection); PDE5 inhibitors block it.

#### Peripheral pro-erectile transmitters
| Transmitter | Source / mechanism |
|---|---|
| **NO (nitric oxide)** | Principal. **nNOS (neuronal NOS)** initiates; **eNOS (endothelial)** sustains. NO → soluble guanylyl cyclase → **cGMP** → PKG → K⁺ channels open, Ca²⁺ channels close → relaxation |
| ACh (acetylcholine) | Indirect: endothelial NO release, inhibits adrenergic nerves; atropine does not block erection |
| PGE1 (prostaglandin E1), VIP (vasoactive intestinal peptide), CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide) | **cAMP** → PKA (protein kinase A); VIP role minimal in man |
| CO, H2S, EDHF (endothelium-derived hyperpolarising factor) | Gaseous/endothelial relaxants via cGMP or K⁺ channels |

- O2 is a NOS substrate: low flaccid Po2 (~35 mmHg) limits NO; erect Po2 ~90 mmHg.
- **Also know:** carbon monoxide and hydrogen sulfide also raise cGMP; acetylcholine acts indirectly through endothelial NO (atropine does not block erection).

#### Anti-erectile transmitters (flaccidity and detumescence)
- **Contraction (flaccidity):** **noradrenaline (α1)** (principal), endothelin-1, angiotensin II → raised Ca²⁺ → calmodulin → myosin light-chain kinase → contraction.
  - **Noradrenaline (α1)**: principal; α2 prejunctional reduces noradrenaline and NO release.
  - **Endothelin-1** (ET-A), angiotensin II (AT1), PGF2α, thromboxane A2: Gq → IP3 → Ca²⁺ rise → Ca²⁺–calmodulin → MLC (myosin light-chain) kinase → contraction.
- **Rho-kinase** inhibits myosin (MLC) phosphatase (calcium sensitisation) and maintains (sustaining) tone.
- **Detumescence:** NO release stops, PDE5 degrades cGMP and sympathetic discharge contracts the smooth muscle, so emissary veins reopen.
- Gap junctions (connexin-43) synchronise the smooth muscle.

### Neural control
| Pathway | Origin | Role |
|---|---|---|
| Parasympathetic | **S2–S4** → pelvic plexus → cavernous nerves | Erection |
| Sympathetic | **T11–L2** → hypogastric nerves | Detumescence, emission |
| Somatic | Pudendal nerve (Onuf nucleus) | Penile sensation; ischiocavernosus rigidity |

- **Three types:** **psychogenic** (brain, chiefly by reducing sympathetic tone), **reflexogenic** (genital touch, sacral arc; preserved in upper cord injury), **nocturnal** (REM [rapid eye movement] sleep).
- **Central:** MPOA (medial preoptic area) and **PVN (paraventricular nucleus)**. Dopamine and oxytocin promote; serotonin, prolactin, GABA (γ-aminobutyric acid) and opioids inhibit.

#### Central neurotransmitters (PVN [paraventricular nucleus], MPOA [medial preoptic area])
| Promote | Inhibit |
|---|---|
| **Dopamine** (D2 in PVN; apomorphine) | **Serotonin** (5-HT1A, 5-HT2; but 5-HT2C pro-erectile) |
| **Oxytocin** | GABA (γ-aminobutyric acid) |
| Central NO | Opioids (high dose), cannabinoids |
| Central noradrenaline; melanocortins (MC4R) | **Prolactin** |

- Dopamine and oxytocin converge on NO in the PVN. Noradrenaline is pro-sexual centrally but anti-erectile peripherally.

#### Clinical relevance
- PDE5 inhibitors, intracavernosal alprostadil (cAMP), papaverine, phenylephrine for priapism (α1), SSRIs (selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors) cause dysfunction, hyperprolactinaemia lowers libido.

**Diagram to draw:** NO–cGMP pathway in the cavernous smooth-muscle cell.

### Evaluation of a diabetic with ED (erectile dysfunction)
**Why diabetics:** ED is commoner, earlier and more severe in DM (diabetes mellitus). Mechanisms: endothelial NO loss, autonomic neuropathy, arteriopathy, smooth-muscle loss and fibrosis (veno-occlusive failure), AGEs (advanced glycation end products) and low testosterone. ED is a **sentinel of cardiovascular disease**.

**1. History**
- Onset, duration (≥3 months), severity, morning erections, libido, ejaculation; partner.
- **IIEF-5 (International Index of Erectile Function, SHIM):** 5–7 severe, 8–11 moderate, 12–16 mild-to-moderate, 17–21 mild, 22–25 no ED.
- Diabetes: type, duration, **glycaemic control**, complications (retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy, foot disease), autonomic symptoms (postural giddiness, gastroparesis, bladder dysfunction).
- Cardiovascular: angina, exercise tolerance, smoking, hypertension, dyslipidaemia, nitrates.
- Drugs: antihypertensives, antidepressants. Depression screen.

**2. Examination**
- BMI, waist, blood pressure, peripheral pulses.
- Genitalia: Peyronie plaques, testicular size, gynaecomastia.
- Neurological: genital sensation, **bulbocavernosus reflex**, foot sensation. Penile neuropathy can exist without foot neuropathy.

**3. Laboratory**
- **HbA1c (glycated haemoglobin)** and fasting glucose, **lipid profile**, creatinine/eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate), urine albumin.
- **Morning total testosterone**; if low, repeat with free testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), prolactin. TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone) if indicated.

**4. Cardiovascular risk stratification (Princeton Consensus)**
- **High risk** (unstable angina, recent infarction, uncontrolled hypertension, some arrhythmias): defer sexual activity and treatment; cardiology referral. **Indeterminate:** cardiac assessment (e.g. stress test), then restratify. **Low:** start treatment.

**5. Specialised tests (selected cases)**
- **CIS (combined intracavernous injection and stimulation):** alprostadil 10–20 µg; a rigid erection excludes major vascular disease.
- **Penile duplex Doppler:** PSV (peak systolic velocity) **<25 cm/s = arterial insufficiency** (>35 normal); EDV (end-diastolic velocity) **>5 cm/s** and RI (resistive index) **<0.75 = veno-occlusive dysfunction**.
- **NPTR (nocturnal penile tumescence and rigidity), RigiScan:** separates psychogenic from organic ED.
- **Neurophysiological:** bulbocavernosus reflex latency, dorsal nerve conduction velocity, biothesiometry.
- DICC (dynamic infusion cavernosometry and cavernosography) and angiography: rarely, as diffuse diabetic vascular disease is not suited to revascularisation.

```
Diabetic with ED → history + IIEF-5 + examination
   → HbA1c, lipids, testosterone, renal function
   → Princeton cardiac risk → treat risk factors
   → PDE5 inhibitor trial → failure → duplex/CIS → ICI or prosthesis
```

> **Key points to remember:**
> - nNOS initiates, eNOS maintains; PDE5 ends (terminates) erection.
> - Noradrenaline α1 is the main anti-erectile transmitter.
> - Dopamine/oxytocin promote; serotonin/prolactin inhibit.
> - Rigid phase: ischiocavernosus, suprasystolic pressure.
> - Duplex: PSV <25 cm/s arterial; EDV >5 cm/s, RI <0.75 venous leak.
> - Diabetic ED: check HbA1c, testosterone and cardiac risk.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Male reproductive immunology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3e

### Introduction
- Haploid germ cells appear at puberty, after self-tolerance is established, so their antigens are "foreign". The testis is therefore **immune-privileged**.

### Immune privilege
**Anatomical barrier: blood–testis barrier (BTB)**
- **Tight junctions between Sertoli cells**, formed at puberty, divide a **basal compartment** (spermatogonia, plasma-like fluid) from an **adluminal compartment** (later spermatocytes, spermatids). 

**Functional barrier**
- A small antigen leak at the **rete testis, efferent ductules and epididymis** induces suppressor T cells and tolerance; a large load provokes immunity.
- Sertoli and local mediators: galectin-1, TGF-β (transforming growth factor-β), interferon-γ, soluble Fc receptor, **androgens**.
- Insult before puberty does not induce antibodies; the barrier also limits chemotherapy (isolated testicular relapse).

### Antisperm antibodies (ASA)
- **Causes:** obstruction (**vasectomy** commonest), torsion, trauma, biopsy, infection, heat (varicocele, cryptorchidism), genetic (human leukocyte antigen HLA-B28).
- **Effects:** agglutination, poor motility, failure to cross cervical mucus and bind the zona; head-bound antibody matters most.
- **Indications to test:** isolated asthenozoospermia, agglutination, unexplained infertility.

| Test | Method |
|---|---|
| Mixed antiglobulin reaction (MAR) | Fresh semen + immunoglobulin (Ig) G/A-coated beads + antiglobulin; beads stick to antibody-coated motile sperm |
| Immunobead test | Washed sperm + beads coated with anti-human IgG/IgA; gives % and site (head/tail) of binding |
- **≥50% of motile sperm bound** is significant (WHO [World Health Organization] 2010).

| Treatment | Comment |
|---|---|
| Corticosteroids | Trials conflict; risk of avascular necrosis; not recommended |
| Sperm washing + intrauterine insemination | Bypasses cervical mucus; modest benefit (bound antibody remains) |
| **IVF/ICSI (in vitro fertilisation/intracytoplasmic sperm injection)** | Preferred; fertilisation largely unaffected |

### Infection and inflammation
- **Leukocytospermia:** >1 × 10⁶ peroxidase-positive leukocytes/mL. Reactive oxygen species and cytokines damage sperm.
- **Orchitis:** mumps (post-pubertal, atrophy); bacterial (*Chlamydia*/gonococcus if young, *E. coli* if older); tuberculous; **autoimmune**, primary (antibodies with azoospermia) or secondary to vasculitis.
- **Also know:** sperm DNA fragmentation testing; prostatitis harms sperm via oxidative stress and ASA; seminal plasma suppresses female tract immunity.

**Diagram to draw:** Seminiferous tubule with Sertoli tight junctions and compartments.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - BTB = Sertoli tight junctions; plus active tolerance (galectin-1, TGF-β, androgens).
> - ASA: vasectomy commonest; ≥50% bound significant; IVF/ICSI.
> - Leukocytospermia >1 million/mL.

## Management of Peyronie's disease
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- **PD (Peyronie's disease)** (AUA (American Urological Association) 2015): acquired fibrosis of the tunica albuginea, with pain, deformity, ED (erectile dysfunction) and/or distress. Prevalence 3–20%; up to half worsen untreated.

### Phases
- **Acute (active, first 12–18 months):** pain, worsening deformity; aim to stop progression. **Stable:** pain gone, deformity unchanged for ≥3–6 months; aim to straighten.

### Evaluation
- History: pain, deformity, Dupuytren disease, ability to penetrate. **Key question:** "If your penis were straight, would your current rigidity allow intercourse?" (predicts postoperative function).
- Plaque, SPL (stretched penile length); **office intracavernosal injection ± duplex Doppler ultrasound** before any invasive treatment (curvature by goniometer, calcification, vascular status).
- **Kelami grading:** ≤30° mild, 31–60° moderate, >60° severe; note hourglass or hinge. PDQ (Peyronie's Disease Questionnaire).

### Non-surgical treatment
| Option | Evidence/position |
|---|---|
| NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) | For pain (only oral agent AUA supports) |
| Oral vitamin E, tamoxifen, potaba, pentoxifylline | No reliable curvature benefit; not recommended |
| **CCH (collagenase *Clostridium histolyticum*)** | Stable disease, curve 30–90°, intact erections. **IMPRESS I/II:** 34% improvement vs 18% placebo. Up to 4 cycles (2 injections of 0.58 mg) with modelling. Risk: corporal fracture |
| Verapamil, interferon α-2b (intralesional) | May offer; stabilise and modestly improve |
| **Traction therapy** | Gains length, reduces curvature; useful in acute phase |
| ESWT (extracorporeal shock wave therapy) | Pain only; no effect on curvature |

### Surgery
- **Indications:** stable disease (≥12 months, stable ≥6 months), deformity preventing intercourse, failed conservative therapy.

```
Erection adequate (± PDE5I (phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor))?
 ├─ No  → penile prosthesis (inflatable) + manual modelling;
 │        residual >30° → tunical incision; graft if defect >2 cm
 └─ Yes → curve <60–70°, no hourglass/hinge → plication (Nesbit, 16-dot, tunica albuginea plication)
          curve >60–70°, hourglass, hinge, short penis → plaque incision/partial
          excision + graft (pericardium, small intestinal submucosa)
```
- **Plication** shortens the convex side: simple, low ED risk, but loses length.
- **Grafting** restores length and girth but carries higher ED risk (5–54%).

**Diagram to draw:** Nesbit wedge excised opposite the plaque.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ask the "straight penis" question; duplex before surgery.
> - CCH: IMPRESS, stable 30–90° curve, intact erections.
> - Surgery: stable ≥12 months; plication <60–70°, graft complex curves, prosthesis if ED.

## Haematospermia
**Asked as:**
- Hematospermia — *Winter 2025 Paper I Q3c (SAQ)*
- Hematospermia — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)* (also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3c)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- Blood in the ejaculate. It alarms patients but is usually **benign and self-limiting**, most often from non-specific prostatic or seminal vesicle inflammation.
- **Primary (idiopathic):** no cause found, often young men. **Secondary:** a cause is identified.
- **Pseudo-haematospermia:** blood from the partner (partner's blood; condom test) or haematuria.

### Causes

| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Iatrogenic (commonest identifiable)** | Prostate biopsy (about 10–50%, lasts about a month), TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), radiotherapy, BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) procedures |
| Inflammation and infection | Prostatitis, vesiculitis, urethritis, STI (sexually transmitted infection), **genitourinary tuberculosis**, schistosomiasis |
| Obstruction and cysts | Ejaculatory duct obstruction or calculi; seminal vesicle cyst (Zinner syndrome) or utricle cyst |
| Tumours | **Prostate cancer**; rarely urethral, bladder or seminal vesicle |
| Vascular | Prostatic varices, BPH vessels, AV (arteriovenous) malformation |
| Systemic | Hypertension, bleeding disorders, anticoagulants, liver disease; also abstinence, trauma |

### Evaluation
- **History:** episodes, recent biopsy, LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), haematuria, TB (tuberculosis) contact, anticoagulants.
- **Examination:** blood pressure, genitalia, **DRE (digital rectal examination)**.
- **Basic tests:** urinalysis and culture, semen culture, STI screen, **PSA (prostate-specific antigen)** in men over 40, coagulation if indicated.

**Who needs full work-up (full work-up if):** age **≥40**, persistent beyond several weeks or recurrent, haematuria, abnormal DRE or PSA, risk factors for TB or cancer, or a distressed patient.
- **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound):** cysts, calculi, dilated seminal vesicles (>1.5 cm) or ejaculatory ducts (>2.3 mm) suggest obstruction.
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) pelvis:** best for seminal vesicle haemorrhage, cysts and prostate cancer.
- **Cystoscopy and urine cytology**; early-morning urine for acid-fast bacilli.

### Management
- **Young, single episode, normal tests:** **reassure**.
- **Treat the cause:** antibiotics or antitubercular drugs; control BP (blood pressure); correct coagulopathy.
- **Persistent prostatic bleeding:** 5-ARI (5-alpha-reductase inhibitor), e.g. finasteride.
- **Refractory or obstructive:** TURED (transurethral resection of ejaculatory ducts), cyst unroofing or aspiration, **seminal vesiculoscopy** (4–6 Fr small scope through the ejaculatory duct or utricle, irrigation, laser for stones).

**Diagram to draw:** algorithm: <40 and single episode → reassure; ≥40 or persistent → PSA, TRUS/MRI, cystoscopy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Usually benign; prostate biopsy is the commonest identifiable cause.
> - Full work-up if ≥40, persistent or recurrent, or associated haematuria (or with haematuria).
> - Exclude prostate cancer and genitourinary tuberculosis.
> - TRUS: seminal vesicle >1.5 cm or ejaculatory duct >2.3 mm suggests obstruction.

## Testicular biopsy and TESA (testicular sperm aspiration)
**Asked as:**
- TESA — *Practice Set 5 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*
- Testicular biopsy — *Practice Set 11 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

### Definition
- **TESA (testicular sperm aspiration):** percutaneous needle aspiration of seminiferous tubules under local anaesthesia to retrieve sperm for **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**, which needs one sperm per oocyte.

### Indications
**Testicular biopsy**
- **Azoospermia** with **normal testicular size, palpable vasa and normal FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)**: separates **OA (obstructive azoospermia)** from **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia)** (AUA/ASRM, American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine).
- Clues to obstruction: FSH ≤7.6 IU/L and testis long axis >4.6 cm.
- Because ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection) needs one sperm, biopsy is **diagnostic and therapeutic**: plan it as **TESE (testicular sperm extraction)** and **cryopreserve** sperm.
- **Not needed:** CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens) with normal FSH; high antisperm antibodies after iatrogenic vasal injury. Do not operate with complete AZFa or AZFb (azoospermia factor) deletion.

**TESA**
- **OA (obstructive azoospermia) with normal spermatogenesis:** CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens), unreconstructable obstruction, failed vasectomy reversal, or failed epididymal aspiration.
- Anejaculation (spinal cord injury) after failed vibratory stimulation or electroejaculation.
- **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia):** poor yield; **micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction)** preferred.

### Types / comparison of retrieval methods
| Type | Method | Use | Limits |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Open biopsy** | Small albugineal incision, pea-sized sample | **Gold standard** for histology and retrieval | Open procedure |
| Percutaneous core | 14 G biopsy gun, cord block | Fresh retrieval in OA | Blind; epididymis or testicular artery injury |
| Aspiration (TESA, testicular sperm aspiration) | 23 G needle, suction, 3–4 passes | OA; backup to epididymal retrieval | Poor architecture; low yield in NOA |
| **Micro-TESE** (microdissection) | Operating microscope, plump tubules | NOA | Long operation |

| Technique | Site | Best for | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| PESA (percutaneous epididymal sperm aspiration) | Caput epididymis | OA | Motile but few sperm; may obstruct epididymis |
| MESA (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration) | Single tubule at 16–25× | OA | Most motile sperm; many frozen vials |
| **TESA** | Testis, needle | OA | Least invasive; few, immotile sperm |
| Percutaneous biopsy | 14-G biopsy gun | OA | Core of tissue; blind |
| TESE / micro-TESE | Open, larger tubules under microscope | **NOA** | Most sperm, least tissue |

### TESA technique
1. Spermatic cord block with local anaesthetic.
2. Testis fixed; **23-G needle** (or angiocatheter) on a high-suction syringe.
3. Multiple passes in different directions; tubules drawn into the needle (often 10–20 passes needed; another source: 3–4 passes).
4. Immediate wet examination for sperm; repeat until sperm are found. **Cryopreserve** spare sperm.

### Open biopsy technique
1. Local anaesthesia with **spermatic cord block** (or general/spinal); choose the larger testis.
2. 2 cm transverse scrotal incision; open tunica vaginalis.
3. Stay sutures; 3–4 mm albugineal incision avoiding surface vessels (loupes).
4. Excise extruding tubules with iris scissors, **no forceps**.
5. Fix in **Bouin's, Zenker's or glutaraldehyde, never formalin** (distorts histology).
6. **Touch prep**, then wet squash prep for sperm; negative → contralateral biopsy.
7. Close tunica vaginalis separately; no antibiotics.

### Histology
| Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Normal spermatogenesis | Obstruction |
| Hypospermatogenesis | All stages, fewer cells |
| Maturation arrest | Stops at a germ-cell stage |
| SCO (Sertoli cell–only) | No germ cells |
| Tubular sclerosis | End-stage (e.g. Klinefelter) |

### TESA: advantages and limitations
- **Advantages:** office procedure, no microsurgery, cheap, repeatable.
- **Limitations:** few tubules with poor architecture (not diagnostic histology); small numbers of immature, immotile sperm; often too few to freeze; blind.

### Complications
- **Haematoma** (commonest), pain, testicular vascular injury, infection (rare), epididymal injury; **inadvertent epididymal biopsy** (most serious, causes obstruction).

### Outcome
- OA: retrieval almost always succeeds; ICSI is essential for testicular sperm.
- NOA: fine-needle aspiration yields far fewer sperm than micro-TESE.
- Before ICSI: karyotype, Y-microdeletion and CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) testing as indicated.

**Diagram to draw:** testis with 23-G needle on a suction syringe and multiple passes; open biopsy: small albugineal window with extruding tubules snipped off.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TESA: 23-G needle, local anaesthesia, OA with normal spermatogenesis.
> - NOA: micro-TESE best; TESA yield poor.
> - Testicular sperm always need ICSI; cryopreserve extras.
> - Biopsy indication: azoospermia, normal testes, vasa and FSH.
> - Bouin's, never formalin; no forceps; touch prep.
> - Haematoma commonest; epididymal biopsy most serious.

## Premature Ejaculation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 1, Q3f
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3a)

### Definition
- **ISSM (International Society for Sexual Medicine) unified definition (2014):** all three needed:
  1. Ejaculation always or nearly always within **about 1 minute** of vaginal penetration (lifelong), or a bothersome fall in latency, often to **≤3 minutes** (acquired).
  2. Inability to delay ejaculation on all or nearly all penetrations.
  3. Negative personal consequences: distress, frustration, avoidance.
- **Types:** lifelong, acquired, variable and subjective.

### Physiology and aetiology
- Ejaculation = **emission** (sympathetic T10–L2) then **expulsion** (somatic S2–S4, pudendal). **Serotonin brakes and dopamine accelerates**.
- **Lifelong PE (premature ejaculation):** neurobiological and genetic; 5-HT2C hyposensitivity and/or 5-HT1A hypersensitivity.
- **Acquired:** performance anxiety, relationship problems, **ED (erectile dysfunction)**, chronic prostatitis, hyperthyroidism, drug withdrawal.

### Evaluation
- Diagnosis is by **history**: latency, control, bother.
- **IELT (intravaginal ejaculatory latency time):** self-estimate in clinic; stopwatch in trials (population median 5.4 minutes).
- **PEDT (Premature Ejaculation Diagnostic Tool)**; IIEF-5 (International Index of Erectile Function) for ED; examination; TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone) only if suggested.

### Management
