- **Psychosexual education** for all; behavioural therapy (stop–start, squeeze).
- Acquired PE: treat the cause first (ED, prostatitis, thyroid).

| Drug | Dose and timing |
|---|---|
| **Dapoxetine** (short-acting SSRI [selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor]) | 30–60 mg, 1–3 h before |
| Paroxetine (strongest delay) 10–40 mg, sertraline, fluoxetine, citalopram | Daily, off-label; full effect 2–3 weeks; do not stop abruptly |
| Clomipramine | 12.5–50 mg daily or 3–4 h before |
| **Topical lidocaine–prilocaine** spray | At least 5 min before; condom avoids partner numbness |
| PDE5Is (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors) | With ED, alone or with SSRI |

- SSRIs raise synaptic serotonin and delay ejaculation. Avoid in bipolar disorder.
- **Also know:** tramadol (addiction risk); high dropout from drug therapy.

**Diagram to draw:** office algorithm: confirm PE → secondary to ED? → treat cause; else classify lifelong or acquired.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ISSM: about 1 min (lifelong), ≤3 min (acquired) plus poor control plus distress.
> - Lifelong is neurobiological; acquired is usually secondary.
> - Dapoxetine 30–60 mg on demand; paroxetine gives the greatest delay.

## Avanafil
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 21 SAQ Sheet, Q3e

### Introduction
- Second-generation oral **PDE5I (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitor)**; **FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) approved 2012**; Europe 2013. Structure differs most from the other PDE5Is.

### Mechanism
- Sexual stimulation releases NO (nitric oxide), which raises cGMP (cyclic guanosine monophosphate) and relaxes cavernosal smooth muscle. PDE5 breaks down cGMP; avanafil blocks this.
- **Enhances, does not initiate, erection**: stimulation is needed.
- **More selective for PDE5** over PDE6 (retina) and PDE1 than sildenafil, so **no visual disturbance** in the comparison table.

### Pharmacokinetics (Campbell Table 72.7)

| Feature | Avanafil | Sildenafil | Tadalafil |
|---|---|---|---|
| Time to peak | **0.3–0.5 h (fastest)** | 0.8 h | 2 h |
| Half-life | 3–5 h | 3–5 h | 17.5 h |
| Fatty food | Reduces absorption | Reduces | No effect |
| Doses | **50, 100, 200 mg** | 25–100 mg | 5–20 mg |
| Blue vision | No | Yes | Rare |

- Taken about 15–30 minutes before sex; start 100 mg; maximum once daily. Metabolised by CYP3A4 (cytochrome P450 3A4).

### Efficacy
- Effective in general ED (erectile dysfunction), **diabetes** and **after nerve-sparing radical prostatectomy** (REVIVE trial programme): improves IIEF (International Index of Erectile Function) erectile scores and successful-intercourse rates, some within 15 minutes. Efficacy equivalent across PDE5Is; no head-to-head superiority.

### Adverse effects
- Headache, flushing, nasal congestion, dyspepsia; back pain rare.

### Contraindications and interactions
- **Nitrates in any form**: absolute; no nitrate within 12 hours of a dose.
- Avoid strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (ketoconazole, ritonavir); maximum 50 mg with moderate inhibitors.
- **α-blockers:** start 50 mg once stable (additive hypotension).
- Caution: aortic stenosis, recent myocardial infarction or stroke, hypotension, retinitis pigmentosa. Not recommended in severe renal impairment, dialysis or severe hepatic impairment.
- **Also know:** proposed prophylaxis for recurrent ischaemic priapism; can cause priapism rarely.

### Advantages
- **Fastest onset**, fewer visual and muscle effects; suits on-demand spontaneous use.

**Diagram to draw:** NO–cGMP pathway in cavernosal smooth muscle with the PDE5 block.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Peak 0.3–0.5 h; doses 50, 100, 200 mg.
> - Highly PDE5-selective: no blue vision.
> - Nitrates absolutely contraindicated.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Spermatogenesis, azoospermia, non-obstructive azoospermia and maturation arrest (LAQ)

- Spermatogenesis about 64 days; Ap divide every 16 days; 6 stages.
- Intratesticular testosterone 50 × serum; exogenous testosterone causes azoospermia.
- FSH ≤7.6 IU/L and testis >4.6 cm → obstructive; FSH >7.6 and testis ≤4.6 cm suggest NOA.
- Karyotype and Y microdeletion in all.
- AZFa/b deletion: do not operate (no retrieval); micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA; SCO 30%.
- Maturation arrest: arrest at one stage; early (spermatocyte) or late (spermatid).
- FSH and testis size may be normal: biopsy diagnoses.
- Micro-TESE retrieval in maturation arrest about 40%; none with complete AZFb deletion.

#### Physiology of erection, neurotransmitters, and evaluation of the diabetic with ED (LAQ)

- nNOS initiates, eNOS maintains; PDE5 ends (terminates) erection.
- Noradrenaline α1 is the main anti-erectile transmitter.
- Dopamine/oxytocin promote; serotonin/prolactin inhibit.
- Rigid phase: ischiocavernosus, suprasystolic pressure.
- Duplex: PSV <25 cm/s arterial; EDV >5 cm/s, RI <0.75 venous leak.
- Diabetic ED: check HbA1c, testosterone and cardiac risk.

#### Male reproductive immunology (SAQ)

- BTB = Sertoli tight junctions; plus active tolerance (galectin-1, TGF-β, androgens).
- ASA: vasectomy commonest; ≥50% bound significant; IVF/ICSI.
- Leukocytospermia >1 million/mL.

#### Management of Peyronie's disease (SAQ)

- Ask the "straight penis" question; duplex before surgery.
- CCH: IMPRESS, stable 30–90° curve, intact erections.
- Surgery: stable ≥12 months; plication <60–70°, graft complex curves, prosthesis if ED.

#### Haematospermia (SAQ)

- Usually benign; prostate biopsy is the commonest identifiable cause.
- Full work-up if ≥40, persistent or recurrent, or associated haematuria (or with haematuria).
- Exclude prostate cancer and genitourinary tuberculosis.
- TRUS: seminal vesicle >1.5 cm or ejaculatory duct >2.3 mm suggests obstruction.

#### Testicular biopsy and TESA (testicular sperm aspiration) (SAQ)

- TESA: 23-G needle, local anaesthesia, OA with normal spermatogenesis.
- NOA: micro-TESE best; TESA yield poor.
- Testicular sperm always need ICSI; cryopreserve extras.
- Biopsy indication: azoospermia, normal testes, vasa and FSH.
- Bouin's, never formalin; no forceps; touch prep.
- Haematoma commonest; epididymal biopsy most serious.

#### Premature Ejaculation (SAQ)

- ISSM: about 1 min (lifelong), ≤3 min (acquired) plus poor control plus distress.
- Lifelong is neurobiological; acquired is usually secondary.
- Dapoxetine 30–60 mg on demand; paroxetine gives the greatest delay.

#### Avanafil (SAQ)

- Peak 0.3–0.5 h; doses 50, 100, 200 mg.
- Highly PDE5-selective: no blue vision.
- Nitrates absolutely contraindicated.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Male urethra: embryology and anatomy, endoscopic stricture management, congenital anomalies and epispadias
**Asked as:**
- Describe the embryology and anatomy of male Urethra. Describe different endoscopic management techniques of treatment of urethral strictures. — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss anatomy of urethra. Classify its congenital anomalies and discuss management of epispadias. — *Practice Set 5 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)* (male urethral anatomy also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q1; isolated epispadias in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

### Embryology
- **Cloaca** is divided by the **urorectal septum** (weeks 4–7) into the anterior **UGS (urogenital sinus)** and the posterior anorectal canal.
- The UGS has three parts: **vesical** (bladder), **pelvic** (prostatic and membranous urethra) and **phallic** (urethral plate of the penis).
- **Posterior urethra:** from the pelvic UGS. Prostatic buds appear at 10 weeks; the **utricle** (Müllerian remnant) and ejaculatory ducts (Wolffian) open on the verumontanum.
- **Anterior urethra:** Leydig cells make testosterone from about 8 weeks; type 2 5α-reductase converts it to DHT (dihydrotestosterone), which masculinises the genital tubercle. Germ layers: ectoderm gives skin and prepuce, mesoderm the corpora, endoderm the **urethral plate**.
- **Penile urethra: "double zipper"**
  1. **Opening zipper:** the urethral plate canalises proximal to distal into a urethral groove between the urethral folds.
  2. **Closing zipper:** the folds fuse in the ventral midline, forming the tube and the median raphe. Folds fused by 16 weeks; urethra complete by about 18 weeks.

| Glanular urethra | Ectodermal ingrowth theory (Glenister) | Endodermal theory (current) |
|---|---|---|
| Formed by | Surface ectoderm grows into the glans to meet the endodermal urethra | Direct canalisation of the urethral plate inside the glans |
| Epithelium | Mixed ectoderm and endoderm | Endoderm (UGS) along the whole urethra |

- **Clinical link:** the shaft–glans junction (change of mechanism) is the commonest hypospadias site. **Also know:** urethral duplication; PUV (posterior urethral valves); utricular cyst.

### Anatomy of the male urethra
- About 18–20 cm long in adults; normal diameter 8–9 mm (wide). **Posterior urethra** runs from the bladder neck to the **perineal membrane**; **anterior urethra** from there (perineal membrane) to the meatus, inside the corpus spongiosum.

| Segment | Length / features | Lining |
|---|---|---|
| Prostatic | Length of prostate; urethral crest, **verumontanum** with utricle (Müllerian remnant) and ejaculatory ducts; angle divides preprostatic and prostatic parts | Urothelium |
| Membranous | 2–2.5 cm; shortest and narrowest; striated sphincter | Pseudostratified columnar |
| Bulbar | Widens as the bulb; **Cowper ducts at 3 and 9 o'clock**; lies **eccentric, close to (nearer) the dorsum** of spongiosum | Pseudostratified columnar |
| Penile | About 15 cm; central, uniform calibre; glands of Littré | Pseudostratified columnar |
| Fossa navicularis | Glanular; lacuna magna on the roof | Stratified squamous |

- **Sphincters:** (1) **internal (preprostatic) smooth muscle sphincter** at the bladder neck, autonomic, gives continence at rest; (2) **external striated rhabdosphincter**, horseshoe-shaped around the membranous urethra, supplied by the pudendal nerve (mainly S3, some S2; Onuf nucleus), plus its intrinsic smooth muscle.
- **Blood supply (arteries):** internal pudendal → common penile artery → artery to the bulb (proximal; bulbourethral branches) and **retrograde flow via the dorsal arteries through the glans**. This **dual supply** allows bulbar transection in anastomotic urethroplasty.
- **Venous** (veins) to pudendal and prostatic plexuses; **lymph** to internal and external iliac nodes.
- **Female urethra:** about 4 cm; smooth muscle plus striated sphincter over the distal two-thirds; **Skene glands** (prostate homologue) near the meatus; oestrogen-dependent mucosal seal.

### Endoscopic management of stricture
**Principle:** healing after incision or dilation is by **secondary intention**, a race between epithelialisation and wound contraction. No endoscopic method removes **spongiofibrosis**, so cure is limited to short, superficial bulbar strictures.

| Favourable | Unfavourable |
|---|---|
| Bulbar, **<1.5–2 cm** | Penile, fossa, longer |
| No dense spongiofibrosis | Straddle injury, LS (lichen sclerosus) |
| First procedure | Repeat; luminal narrowing >74% |

**1. Dilation**
- Stretches the scar without tearing it (bleeding means tearing).
- **Safest: balloon dilation over a guidewire** under vision; blind filiforms condemned. Sounds or self-dilation for palliation.

**2. DVIU (direct vision internal urethrotomy)**
1. RUG (retrograde urethrogram) and VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) define site and length.
2. Guidewire across; **normal saline** irrigation.
3. **Sachse cold knife** at 12 o'clock, through scar to healthy tissue; avoid deep cuts (corpora: ED (erectile dysfunction), fistula).
4. Catheter **3–7 days**; longer adds nothing.
- **Laser urethrotomy** (holmium, others): **no advantage over cold knife**.
- Results: **Steenkamp and Heyns** (randomised): dilation equals urethrotomy; a second attempt rarely and a third never succeeds. **Santucci (2010):** about 8% stricture-free long term.

**3. Adjuncts against recurrence**
- **CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation)** tapering over 3–6 months: delays recurrence, which returns on stopping.
- **Mitomycin C** injection reduces recurrence; steroid injection, colchicine: weak data.

**4. Urethral stents**
- **UroLume** (permanent): **withdrawn** (ingrowth). **Memokath**, removable stents: small series only.

**5. Optilume (paclitaxel-coated balloon)**
- FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2023. **ROBUST III:** 74.6% vs 26.8% stricture-free at 1 year after failed endoscopy.

**Complications:** recurrence (commonest), bleeding with erections, irrigant extravasation, UTI (urinary tract infection) and sepsis, ED, spongio-cavernous fistula, incontinence.

**Guideline (AUA (American Urological Association) 2016):** urethrotomy or dilation for short (<2 cm) bulbar strictures; **urethroplasty, not repeat endoscopy**, after failure. The reconstructive ladder is abandoned.

### Classification of congenital urethral anomalies
| Group | Anomalies |
|---|---|
| Abnormal meatal position | **Hypospadias** (ventral); **epispadias** (dorsal) |
| Obstructive | **PUV (posterior urethral valves)**, Young types I–III; **anterior urethral valves** (with diverticulum); urethral atresia or hypoplasia (congenital stricture); congenital meatal stenosis |
| Dilatation | **Megalourethra**: scaphoid (spongiosum deficient) or fusiform (all erectile tissue deficient, more severe); anterior urethral diverticulum; Cowper duct cyst (syringocele); enlarged prostatic utricle |
| Duplication | **Effmann classification**: type I blind incomplete; type II complete (IIA2 "Y-type"); type III with caudal duplication. The ventral channel is the functional one |
| Accessory openings | Congenital urethrocutaneous fistula; parameatal urethral cyst |
| Female | Epispadias; urethral prolapse; persistent urogenital sinus |

### Epispadias: definition and classification
- A **dorsal urethral wall defect**, replaced by a broad dorsal mucosal strip (urethral plate); part of the **exstrophy–epispadias complex**, mostly seen with exstrophy.
- Embryology: an overdeveloped cloacal membrane blocks mesenchymal ingrowth; genital tubercles fuse caudal to it.

| | Male | Female |
|---|---|---|
| Incidence | About 1 in 117,000 | About 1 in 484,000 |
| Types | Glanular, penile, **penopubic** (about 70%, incontinent) | **Davis:** vesicular (patulous meatus), subsymphyseal, retrosymphyseal (incontinent) |
| Genitalia | Short, upturned penis with **dorsal chordee** | **Bifid clitoris**, flat glabrous mons |
| VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) | 30–40% | 30–75% |

- The more proximal the meatus, the worse the continence (glanular never incontinent). Pubic diastasis is less than in exstrophy.

### Evaluation
- Renal ultrasound; VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) for reflux; cystoscopy and **bladder capacity** under anaesthesia.
- **Key decision:** use the exstrophy pathway (osteotomy and closure) if pubic diastasis >3 cm, poor intrasymphyseal bar, absent sphincter tissue or tiny bladder.

### Management
**Aims:** continence with safe upper tracts, and a straight penis of adequate length that works for intercourse.
- **Preoperative testosterone** (2 mg/kg intramuscular, 5 and 2 weeks before).

**Stage 1: urethroplasty and penile reconstruction (6–12 months)**
1. Release dorsal chordee: divide suspensory ligaments; free corpora from the inferior pubic rami.
2. **Modified Cantwell–Ransley:** an 18-mm plate strip is outlined; reverse MAGPI (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty) brings the meatus to the tip; the plate is tubularised over an 8-Fr stent and moved **ventral to the corpora**, which are rotated medially over it (diamond corporotomies if curvature persists).
3. Glans wings closed; skin flaps resurface the dorsum; base Z-plasty; stent 10–12 days.
- **Mitchell–Bägli complete penile disassembly:** reliable chordee correction, but often leaves hypospadias.
- Urethroplasty adds outlet resistance, so the bladder grows before stage 2.

**Stage 2: bladder neck reconstruction (4–5 years, capacity about 100 mL)**
- **Young–Dees–Leadbetter** repair with Marshall–Marchetti–Krantz suspension and **ureteric reimplantation**. Osteotomy is rarely needed.
- **Good capacity at presentation:** single-stage repair is possible.
- **Female:** urethroplasty and genitoplasty (clitoral halves and labia joined, mons reconstructed) at 6–12 months; bladder neck repair at 4–5 years.

### Outcomes and complications
- Continence after bladder neck reconstruction: **males 47–82%, females 70–87%**; capacity is the main predictor.
- Early fistula about 1 in 6 (penile base); stricture rare; neourethra catheterisable; erections and paternity preserved.
- Better than exstrophy: the bladder was never exposed or scarred. Failures: injectables, augmentation.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal male urethra with segments, sphincters and a 12 o'clock urethrotomy; Cantwell–Ransley with plate transposed under the corpora.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Double zipper: canalisation, then fold fusion; glanular urethra endodermal.
> - Bulbar urethra eccentric (dorsal); Cowper ducts 3 and 9 o'clock; dual retrograde glanular blood supply.
> - DVIU: bulbar, <1.5–2 cm, first attempt; Santucci about 8% long-term.
> - Optilume ROBUST III 74.6% vs 26.8%.
> - Megalourethra: fusiform worse than scaphoid; duplication: ventral channel functional.
> - About 70% of male epispadias are penopubic; diastasis >3 cm → exstrophy pathway.
> - Cantwell–Ransley at 6–12 months; YDL (Young–Dees–Leadbetter) at about 100 mL.

## Lymphatic drainage of testis and scrotum, RPLND templates and penile cancer implications
**Asked as:**
- Lymphatic drainage of testis and principles for designing templates for RPLND. — *Practice Set 7 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Lymphatic drainage of testes and principles of designing templates of RPLND — *Practice Set 12 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Anatomy of lymphatic drainage of testis and scrotum. Describe management implications of penile malignancies. — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

(lymphatic drainage also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q2 [testis and scrotum]; minimally invasive RPLND (MI-RPLND) in 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3f; Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q2 is the same question as Practice Set 7 Paper 1, Q2; 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q1 [inguinal nodes in penile cancer])

*Second part (Summer 2021) taken as the management implications of lymphatic drainage in penile cancer, i.e. management of the inguinal and pelvic nodes.*

### Embryological basis
- **Why retroperitoneal:** the testis develops at L1–L2 beside the mesonephros (near the kidney) and descends, carrying its vessels and lymphatics (its artery, vein and lymphatics stay retroperitoneal). So its primary drainage (its lymph returns) is to the **retroperitoneal (lumbar) nodes near the renal hilum**, not the groin. The scrotum is skin and drains to the **groin**.

### Lymphatic drainage of the testis (lymphatic pathway)
- **Route:** lymphatics of the testis and the caput and corpus of the epididymis run (up the **spermatic cord**) in the spermatic cord with the testicular (gonadal) vessels, through the internal ring and along the gonadal vessels to the lumbar nodes near the renal hilum (**para-aortic and interaortocaval nodes**). Lymph capillaries do not enter seminiferous tubules.
- Onward: lumbar trunks join at the **cisterna chyli** (in front of L1–L2, behind the right side of the aorta, behind the right crus; hence retrocrural nodes), then the **thoracic duct** to the left innominate vein. This explains **left supraclavicular** metastases.
- Lymph flows (drifts) **cephalad and right to left**.
- **Epididymis:** caput and corpus with the testis; **cauda epididymis** drains with the vas to the **external iliac** nodes.

**Retroperitoneal nodal regions**
| Region | Extent |
|---|---|
| Right paracaval (with precaval, retrocaval) | IVC (inferior vena cava) midline to right ureter |
| Interaortocaval | IVC midline to aortic midline |
| Left para-aortic (with preaortic/pre-aortic, retroaortic) | Aortic midline to left ureter |
- **Also know:** ten mapped regions include right and left common iliac, interiliac and right and left suprahilar.

### Primary landing zones
| | Right testis | Left testis |
|---|---|---|
| Primary (landing zone) | **Interaortocaval** (below renal vessels) | **Left para-aortic** (left hilar) |
| Secondary / next echelon | Right paracaval, precaval, then para-aortic | Interaortocaval; pre-aortic |
| Crossover / contralateral | Small but real to left para-aortic (another source: common; right to left occurs) | None significant (another source: rare; implies bulk; rare to right, bulky disease only) |
- **Crossover is only right to left**, matching the overall lymph flow (because lymph drifts right to left; lymph flows that way).
- **Donohue mapping (1982):** contralateral and suprahilar spread is rare but rises with bulk; interiliac spread is very rare.
- **Pelvic, iliac and inguinal nodes** are involved only when drainage is altered (or with retrograde spread from bulky disease): previous scrotal or inguinal surgery, scrotal violation, or tumour invading the tunica vaginalis (reaching the tunica/scrotum). Scrotal skin drains to ipsilateral superficial inguinal nodes. **Hence trans-scrotal orchidectomy is contraindicated** (never biopsy or remove a testis trans-scrotally).
- The retroperitoneum is the first metastatic site in 70–80% (choriocarcinoma spreads by blood). CT (computed tomography) understages 25–35% of stage I NSGCT (non-seminomatous germ cell tumour); 5–9 mm landing-zone nodes are suspicious.

### Lymphatic drainage of the scrotum
- Scrotal lymphatics **do not cross the median raphe** (nothing crosses the raphe); they drain to the **ipsilateral superficial inguinal nodes**.

### Principles of designing RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) templates
**1. Map-based design (mapping studies): follow the side-specific landing zones** to remove likely disease and spare the rest.
- Historical dissection: bilateral infrahilar + suprahilar + interiliac: most men anejaculatory.
- **Ray (1974)** first templated landing zones; **Donohue (1982)** mapping confirmed side-specific spread; **Weissbach and Boedefeld (1987)** reduced left template with frozen section.
- So a template must include the primary landing zone, the next echelon and the known crossover route.

**2. Fixed (standard) boundaries/limits**
- **Superior:** renal hila (renal vessels above). **Lateral:** ureters laterally. **Inferior:** ureter crossing the common iliac artery below. Plus always remove the **gonadal vein and cord stump** to the internal ring.

**3. Omit suprahilar and interiliac regions** without bulky disease (in non-bulky disease, as spread there is rare). This reduces (less) chylous ascites, renal, pancreatic and duodenal injury, and protects the hypogastric plexus.

**4. Templates: unilateral modified templates** leave contralateral sympathetic fibres untouched. Because of right-to-left crossover, both templates take the para-aortic nodes **above the IMA (inferior mesenteric artery)**.
| Template | Includes / content |
|---|---|
| **Right modified** | (Right) paracaval, precaval, retrocaval, interaortocaval, right common iliac; para-aortic **above IMA**; omit para-aortic below IMA |
| **Left modified** | Left para-aortic, preaortic, retroaortic, left common iliac; interaortocaval above IMA; omit paracaval/precaval/retrocaval |
| **(Full) Bilateral infrahilar** | All three regions both sides (paracaval, precaval, retrocaval, interaortocaval, pre-aortic, para-aortic, retroaortic, both common iliacs), between renal vessels, ureters and ureteric crossing of iliacs |
| Historical extended | Plus suprahilar and interiliac (thoracoabdominal); most anejaculatory |
- Omitting para-aortic above the IMA (right) or interaortocaval (left) is controversial.

**5. Nerve sparing / preserve ejaculation:** antegrade ejaculation (emission and bladder-neck closure) depends (depend) on **L1–L4 postganglionic sympathetic fibres** (about three each side) joining the **superior hypogastric plexus at or below the IMA** and running over the sacral promontory. Identify them prospectively before dividing tissue.
- **Unilateral templates** leave contralateral fibres untouched (antegrade ejaculation 75–97%). **Prospective nerve sparing** (Jewett 1988) identifies fibres: 90–100%.

**6. Completeness ("duplicate open RPLND"):** **split-and-roll** over the great vessels, **division of (divide) lumbar vessels** to clear retrocaval and retroaortic tissue, clip lymphatics at the cephalad limit (left renal vein) to prevent chylous ascites (chylous leak). Incomplete RPLND or mass excision gives worse outcomes (GETUG T93BP; 33% local relapse after post-chemotherapy mass-only resection).

**7. Extend if needed:** enlarged nodes at operation → full bilateral template (frozen section, Weissbach 1987). Convert to bilateral if nodes are suspicious at surgery or the primary has somatic-type malignancy.

### Template by clinical setting (setting decides extent)
| Setting | Template |
|---|---|
| **Primary RPLND** (high-risk stage I or low-volume stage II NSGCT [non-seminomatous germ cell tumour], normal markers) | Default: modified unilateral nerve-sparing **or** full bilateral infrahilar nerve-sparing; no randomised trial. Modified allowed: low-volume, clinically negative nodes. Main risk: out-of-template nodes 3–23% (right > left) |
| **PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy)**, residual mass >1 cm, normal markers | **Full bilateral infrahilar + (residual) mass** (standard). Main risk: crossover with bulk; viable tissue unrecognisable |
| Modified PC template allowed only if | Mass ≤5 cm in landing zone, normal markers, IGCCCG (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group) good or intermediate risk |
- Against unilateral templates: 3–23% of node-positive men have nodes outside the modified template, more with right tumours.
- *Sources differ:* Campbell testis-neoplasm chapter calls full bilateral nerve-sparing the standard; the surgery chapter accepts either.

### Outcomes and complications
- Primary RPLND: pN+ about 20–30%; cancer-specific survival (CSS) about 98.8–100%; retroperitoneal recurrence <2% after full template.
- **Antegrade ejaculation >90%** with templates plus nerve sparing (modified template alone: early 75–87%, refined 97%).
- Complications: ileus (commonest), wound infection, chylous ascites (0.2–2.1% primary; 2–12% post-chemotherapy), pulmonary (bleomycin lung toxicity; low FiO₂), ejaculatory loss.
- **Also know:** nephrectomy needed at PC-RPLND (left-sided mass >10 cm); GETUG T93BP showed incomplete RPLND predicts relapse.

### Recent advances
- **Robotic RPLND** for low-volume disease (uses the same templates, same rules).
- RPLND for low-volume (small-volume) stage II seminoma (**SEMS, PRIMETEST**); serum **miR-371a-3p** for selection.

### Clinical implications: testis and scrotum
- **Never operate trans-scrotally** on a testicular tumour. Scrotal violation opens skin lymphatics, so tumour can reach inguinal and pelvic nodes; use **radical inguinal orchidectomy** with high cord clamping.
- Inguinal or pelvic spread from the testis means altered drainage: scrotal violation, previous orchidopexy or hernia repair, scrotal invasion.
- RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) templates and para-aortic or "dog-leg" radiotherapy fields follow the landing zones.
- Scrotal skin cancer is staged at the ipsilateral groin.

### Lymphatic drainage of the penis
| Superficial system | Deep system |
|---|---|
| Prepuce and shaft skin | Glans, frenulum, corpora |
| Dorsal network divides at the base → both superficial inguinal groups | Trunks circle the corona, run under Buck's fascia → presymphyseal plexus → femoral triangle nodes |

- **Two rules:** drainage is **bilateral and unpredictable in side** (presymphyseal crossover), but **orderly in sequence**: superficial inguinal → deep inguinal → external iliac and obturator. **No skip metastases.**
- **Superficial nodes** (4–25, above fascia lata) lie in **Daseler's five zones**; sentinel nodes are mostly **superomedial**, then central. **Deep nodes** (1–3) lie medial to the femoral vein; **Cloquet's node** is the highest.

### Management implications in penile cancer
**1. Prognosis and timing**
- Nodal status is the **strongest prognostic factor**. Penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma) has a long **locoregional phase**, so the groin is a **curable station**.
- **Kroon (2005):** early ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection) for impalpable nodes gave 3-year disease-specific survival about **84% vs 35%** when delayed until palpable.
- Manage both groins **within 3 months**; remove **at least 8 nodes per side**.

**2. Bilateral management**
- Crossover means **both groins** are staged or treated; with one palpable groin, the other holds micrometastasis in about 30%.

**3. Impalpable groin (cN0): risk-based**
| Risk | EAU-ASCO (European Association of Urology–American Society of Clinical Oncology) 2023 | Groin |
|---|---|---|
| Low | pTis, pTa, pT1a G1 | Surveillance |
| Intermediate | pT1a G2 | Surveillance if compliant, or surgical staging |
| High | **pT1b or higher**, G3 | **DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy)**; ILND if DSNB unavailable |
- NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): pT1b G1–2 is intermediate risk and is staged surgically (ILND first).
- **DSNB:** ultrasound ± FNAC (fine-needle aspiration cytology) first, because a tumour-replaced node diverts flow (false negative). Then technetium-99m nanocolloid, blue dye and gamma probe.
- **No-skip spread and superomedial sentinels** justify **modified ILND (Catalona)**: smaller field, saphenous vein preserved; positive frozen section → complete radical ILND.

**4. Palpable nodes (cN+)**
- About half are reactive, but do not delay with antibiotics. Ultrasound-guided **FNAC**; positive → **radical ILND** on that side, stage the other.
- **Bulky (≥4 cm), fixed or bilateral nodes:** neoadjuvant **TIP** (paclitaxel, ifosfamide, cisplatin) × 4, then surgery in responders.

**5. Pelvic nodes**
- Stepwise spread means a negative groin needs no pelvic dissection.
- **PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)**, ipsilateral, if **more than 2 inguinal nodes or ENE (extranodal extension)**. ENE in any node is pN3.

**6. Complications of ILND**
- Wound infection, **flap necrosis**, **lymphocele**, **lymphoedema**, DVT (deep vein thrombosis). Reduced by modified templates, saphenous sparing and **VEIL (video-endoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy)**.

**Also know:** adjuvant therapy for pN2–3; Cabanas sentinel biopsy abandoned.

**Diagram to draw:** great vessels (retroperitoneum showing) with the three nodal regions, right (interaortocaval) and left (para-aortic) landing zones, and the right-to-left crossover arrow; outline the two modified templates (right and left), IMA level and hypogastric plexus. Beside it, the femoral triangle with Daseler's zones and Cloquet's node.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Right testis to (→) interaortocaval; left to left para-aortic; crossover only right to left.
> - Limits/boundaries: renal vessels (hila), ureters, ureteric crossing of iliacs (ureter–iliac crossing); omit suprahilar and interiliac.
> - Nerve-sparing of L1–L4 fibres gives antegrade ejaculation >90%; the fibres meet at the IMA: unilateral templates spare ejaculation.
> - PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy): full bilateral template remains standard unless mass ≤5 cm in landing zone.
> - Scrotum and penis → inguinal nodes; never operate trans-scrotally.
> - Penile drainage is bilateral and stepwise without skips: stage both groins, PLND only if inguinal nodes positive.
> - pT1b or higher: DSNB or ILND within 3 months; Kroon 84% vs 35%.

## Enlist various grafts used in urology and role of BMG in urology
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q2
(BMG uses also in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3b; vascular grafts in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q2)

### Graft versus flap
| | Graft | Flap |
|---|---|---|
| Blood supply | Separated; gets a new supply from the bed | Moved with its own pedicle |
| Depends on | A well-vascularised host bed | Its pedicle |
| Example | BMG (buccal mucosal graft) | Penile fasciocutaneous island flap |

### Graft take (about 96 hours)
| Phase | Duration | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| **Imbibition** | about 48 h | Graft "drinks" nutrients from the bed; graft is cooler than core |
| **Inosculation** | about 48 h | Bed and graft vessels connect; true microcirculation returns |
- Take fails with poor beds (scar, radiation), haematoma, seroma, infection or shear. Hence **quilting or pie-crusting** and **immobilisation for 5–10 days** (bolster or vacuum dressing).

### Grafts used in urology
| Group | Graft | Main uses |
|---|---|---|
| **Skin** | **STSG** (split-thickness skin graft), meshed | Staged urethroplasty only; penile and scrotal cover (Fournier gangrene, buried penis) |
| | **FTSG** (full-thickness skin graft) | Fossa navicularis, penile urethra (avoid in lichen sclerosus) |
| | Posterior auricular (Wolfe) FTSG | Staged hypospadias repair |
| **Mucosa** | **Buccal, lingual, labial (oral mucosa)** | Urethra, hypospadias, ureter |
| | Bladder epithelium | Urethra, hypospadias |
| | Rectal (colonic) mucosa | Panurethral stricture when oral mucosa is unavailable |
| **Other autologous** | Dermis | Tunica albuginea in Peyronie disease |
| | Tunica vaginalis | Peyronie disease, urethral cover; easy harvest |
| | Saphenous vein | Peyronie defects; vascular repair |
| | Rectus fascia, fascia lata | Pubovaginal sling |
| **Allograft / xenograft** | Pericardium, porcine SIS, porcine dermis, cadaveric fascia | Peyronie grafting ("off the shelf"); slings |
| **Synthetic** | Polypropylene (macroporous type I mesh) | Mid-urethral sling, prolapse |
| | Dacron, PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene) | Vascular grafts; not advised for Peyronie (infection, fibrosis) |
| **Newer** | Collagen fleece (TachoSil); acellular matrix, tissue-engineered oral mucosa | Peyronie; experimental urethra |

- Vein is not a true graft: it survives by direct endothelial perfusion and its vasa vasorum.
- **Peyronie grafts:** pericardium (thin, strong, does not contract) and SIS (cut about 25% oversize; contracts) are commonest; AUA gives no preference.
- **STSG versus FTSG:** STSG carries little dermis, takes easily, can be meshed (up to sixfold) but contracts. FTSG carries full dermis, contracts less, looks better but is more "fastidious" in take.

### Role of BMG
**Why it works**
- Thick, non-keratinised, elastin-rich epithelium used to a **wet environment**; resists infection.
- Thin, **highly vascular lamina propria** with a panlaminar plexus, so imbibition and inosculation are rapid.
- Hairless; little contraction; handles and holds sutures well; low donor morbidity.

**Harvest**
1. Nasotracheal intubation; mouth gag; mark the graft (often about 4–5 × 2 cm per cheek).
2. Stay 1 cm from the commissure and avoid **Stensen's (parotid) duct** opposite the upper second molar.
3. Infiltrate, incise, raise the mucosa off buccinator; haemostasis.
4. Donor site: leave open or close; both acceptable.
5. Back table: **defat** the graft, cut about 10% oversize, fenestrate with a needle.
- **Lingual** (under the tongue) and **labial** grafts are alternatives. Buccal and lingual give equal urethroplasty success; lip mucosa is thinner, suits the glans.

**Uses in urethroplasty**
| Technique | Principle | Best for |
|---|---|---|
| **Dorsal onlay** (Barbagli, 1995) | Dorsal urethrotomy; graft spread-fixed on corpora | Bulbar; reliable bed |
| **Ventral onlay** | Ventral urethrotomy; graft covered by spongioplasty | Bulbar with healthy spongiosum |
| **Dorsal inlay** (Asopa) | Ventral approach; dorsal plate incised; graft inlaid | Penile and bulbar |
| **Augmented anastomosis** | Excise narrowest part, floor anastomosis, graft roof | Long, dense bulbar stricture |
| **Kulkarni** (2012) | One-sided dissection; penile invagination; dorsal graft | Panurethral, one stage |
| **Staged** (Johanson, Bracka) | Stage 1 graft; tubularise after 6 months or more | Lichen sclerosus, failed hypospadias |
| **BMG perineal urethrostomy** | Graft completes dorsal wall to skin | Panurethral or failed repairs |

- Dorsal and ventral results are similar; onlay beats tubes. **Skin is avoided in lichen sclerosus** because the disease recurs in skin.

**Other uses**
- **Ureter:** BMG onlay ureteroplasty (first human report 1999), now robotic with an **omental wrap**; for long proximal or mid strictures in dense scar. CORRUS (Collaborative of Reconstructive Robotic Ureteral Surgery) failure about 15–25% by site.
- Redo and staged hypospadias; urethral closure in rectourethral fistula; bladder neck contracture; female urethral stricture; Peyronie grafting (small defects only).

**Complications**
- **Donor:** pain, swelling (first month), numbness (up to 6 months), mouth tightness, salivary duct injury.
- **Recipient:** graft failure, restricture, sacculation, fistula, ED.

**Outcomes**
- Bulbar BMG urethroplasty about 85–90% success; most failures within 12 months.

**Diagram to draw:** bulbar cross-section showing dorsal onlay, ventral onlay and dorsal inlay positions.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Take: imbibition 48 h, then inosculation 48 h; graft lives on its bed.
> - BMG: non-keratinised, vascular lamina propria, wet-adapted.
> - Avoid Stensen's duct; buccal equals lingual in success.
> - Staged BMG, not skin, in lichen sclerosus.

## Describe in brief the anatomy of the retroperitoneum. Explain the principles of laparoscopic retroperitoneal surgery while mentioning the salient steps of retroperitoneal laparoscopic nephrectomy.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q1
(related: 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q2; 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3b)

### Anatomy: definition and boundaries
- The space behind the parietal peritoneum. **Anterior:** posterior peritoneal reflection; **posterior:** posterior abdominal wall; **cranial:** diaphragm; **caudal:** extraperitoneal pelvis.

| Group | Contents |
|---|---|
| Primarily retroperitoneal (formed there) | Kidneys, ureters, adrenals, aorta, IVC (inferior vena cava) |
| Secondarily retroperitoneal (mesentery fused to the wall) | Ascending and descending colon, duodenum D2–D3, pancreas (not the tail) |
| Vessels, lymphatics, nerves | Lumbar nodes, cisterna chyli, sympathetic trunks, autonomic plexuses, lumbosacral plexus |

### Posterior abdominal wall
- **Flank layers:** external oblique, internal oblique, transversus abdominis, transversalis fascia. **Posterior:** psoas, quadratus lumborum, erector spinae.
- **Lumbodorsal (thoracolumbar) fascia:** three layers that fuse into one at the **tip of the 12th rib**, the standard entry point for retroperitoneoscopy.
- **Ribs:** access above the 12th rib risks the pleura; the intercostal bundle runs under the rib above.

### Fasciae and spaces (three embryological strata)
| Space | Boundaries | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| Anterior pararenal | Posterior peritoneum and anterior renal fascia | Colon, duodenum, pancreas |
| Perirenal | Anterior (Toldt) and posterior (Zuckerkandl) laminae of Gerota's fascia | Kidney, adrenal, perirenal fat, hilum, upper ureter, gonadal vessels |
| Posterior pararenal | Posterior lamina and transversalis fascia | Fat only |

- The laminae fuse laterally as the **lateroconal fascia**. The perirenal cone is **open inferiorly**, so urine, blood or pus track into the pelvis.
- Perirenal fat is fine and pale; pararenal fat is coarse and yellow-orange.

### Vessels, lymphatics and nerves
- **Aorta** enters at T12. Unpaired anterior branches supply the gut (coeliac, SMA (superior mesenteric artery), IMA (inferior mesenteric artery)); paired lateral branches supply adrenals, kidneys (L1–L2) and gonads; lumbar arteries go to the wall.
- At the hilum the **renal vein lies anterior to the artery**. The **left renal vein is long** (gonadal, adrenal and lumbar tributaries); the right is short.
- Lymph flows right to left; **cisterna chyli** at L1–L2.
- **Nerves at risk:** sympathetic trunk (medial to psoas), genitofemoral (on psoas), subcostal, iliohypogastric, ilioinguinal (on quadratus); hypogastric plexus (ejaculation).

### Principles of laparoscopic retroperitoneal surgery
1. **No natural cavity:** the working space must be created (finger and balloon dissection) before insufflation.
2. **Psoas is the key landmark:** it forms the floor. Keep it horizontal on screen; the kidney in Gerota's fascia lies anterior, and the peritoneum is pushed medially.
3. **Direct posterior route to the hilum:** the renal artery, posterior to the vein, is met first, allowing early arterial control.
4. **Peritoneum stays intact:** no bowel mobilisation or adhesiolysis; no spill of tumour, urine or pus into the peritoneum.
5. **Small space:** closely spaced ports, limited triangulation, lens smudging.
6. **Gas behaviour:** CO₂ (carbon dioxide) absorbs through fat, so hypercarbia and subcutaneous emphysema are commoner; haemodynamic swings are smaller than transperitoneal.

| Preferred for | Limitations |
|---|---|
| Adhesions, peritoneal dialysis, morbid obesity, pregnancy; posterior tumours, pyeloplasty, cyst surgery, renal biopsy | Small space, unfamiliar landmarks, harder suturing, learning curve |

- **Evidence:** randomised trials and meta-analysis: blood loss, complications and oncology similar to transperitoneal; the consistent advantage is **shorter operative time**.

### Salient steps: retroperitoneal laparoscopic nephrectomy
1. **Position:** full flank (90°), table flexed, axillary roll; surgeon behind the patient.
2. **Access (open Hasson):** 1.5–2.5-cm incision just below the 12th rib tip; split muscle, open the lumbodorsal fascia; finger confirms psoas behind and Gerota-covered lower pole in front.
3. **Space creation:** balloon dilator (commercial, or Gaur glove balloon with 600–800 mL saline) anterior to psoas, posterior to Gerota's fascia; blunt-tip balloon-cuff trocar seals the port.
4. **Ports:** two more trocars under vision, one off the 12th rib tip, one posterior and superior to the camera.
5. **Dissection:** along psoas, find the ureter and gonadal vein medially; lift ureter and lower pole to put the hilum on stretch; see arterial pulsation.
6. **Hilum:** clip or staple the **artery first**, then the vein; on the left a **lumbar vein** usually needs division.
7. **Mobilise:** upper pole (adrenal preserved or taken), then lateral and anterior attachments; radical = outside Gerota's fascia.
8. **Extract:** clip ureter, bag the specimen, remove via an extended port; close.
- **Problems:** peritoneal tear (space collapses), emphysema, pneumothorax, vascular injury, flank hernia (intramuscular balloon), rhabdomyolysis (about 1%). **Bail-out:** open the peritoneum and convert.

**Diagram to draw:** transverse section showing the three spaces, Gerota's laminae and the balloon anterior to psoas.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Three spaces: anterior pararenal, perirenal, posterior pararenal; perirenal cone open below.
> - Entry at the 12th rib tip, where the lumbodorsal fascia fuses.
> - Psoas is the landmark; artery met first.
> - Main advantage: shorter operative time, peritoneum untouched.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Surgical management of primary scrotal cancer
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3e
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

### Introduction
- Rare; **SCC (squamous cell carcinoma)** commonest. **Percivall Pott (1775)**: chimney sweeps, the first occupational cancer.
- **Risks:** soot, tar, mineral oils, arsenic, PUVA (psoralen + ultraviolet A), HPV (human papillomavirus).
- **Others:** EMPD (extramammary Paget disease), BCC (basal cell carcinoma), melanoma, sarcoma.
- Drains to **superficial inguinal nodes, crossing the midline**.

### Evaluation
- Painless nodule or ulcer on the anterolateral scrotum, often presenting late; **biopsy** confirms.
- Examine both groins (lymphatics cross the midline); FNAC (fine-needle aspiration cytology) of nodes; CT (computed tomography) of abdomen and pelvis for staging.

### Staging (Lowe)
| Stage | Extent |
|---|---|
| A1 | Confined to scrotum |
| A2 | Invades testis, cord, penis, pubis or perineum |
| B | Inguinal nodes |
| C | Pelvic nodes |
| D | Distant metastases |

### Primary tumour
1. **Wide local excision, 2 cm margin**, full thickness with dartos; frozen sections.
2. Testes spared unless invaded; hemi- or total scrotectomy for large lesions.
3. A2: en bloc removal of involved testis, penis, perineum or pubis.
4. Positive margin: re-excise.

### Reconstruction (Chapter 87)
| Defect | Management |
|---|---|
| <50%, no tension | Primary closure |
| <50%, tension | Scrotal advancement flap or secondary intention |
| >50% | Testes in thigh pockets; later STSG (split-thickness skin graft) or flap |

### Nodes (as in penile cancer)
- **Palpable, cytology positive:** ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection) ± pelvic dissection. Nodal status is the main prognostic factor.
- **Impalpable:** surveillance; sentinel node biopsy in high-grade tumours.
- **Bulky or fixed:** neoadjuvant cisplatin-based chemotherapy, then surgery; RT (radiotherapy) for palliation.
- Routine prophylactic dissection is not advised.

### Other types
- **EMPD:** wide excision guided by mapping biopsies; screen for other cancers.
- **Also know:** BCC, excision or Mohs surgery; melanoma, excision by Breslow depth ± sentinel node; sarcoma, wide excision.

**Diagram to draw:** elliptical excision with 2 cm margin and bilateral inguinal drainage.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pott 1775: chimney sweeps.
> - 2 cm margin; testes spared.
> - Nodes decide prognosis.

## Varicocele – Pathophysiology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition and epidemiology
- Abnormal dilatation and tortuosity of the **pampiniform plexus** of the internal spermatic veins.
- About 15% of men, 35% with primary and 75–81% with secondary infertility. Appears after age 10, peaks at Tanner 3. About 90% left-sided.
- **Venous anatomy:** anterior (internal spermatic), middle (deferential) and posterior (cremasteric) groups; the last two give return after repair and cause recurrence.

### Why it develops
1. **Left-sided anatomy:** the left vein enters the left renal vein at a right angle, 8–10 cm higher than the right enters the IVC (inferior vena cava). The taller column overloads its valves. The right vein enters obliquely, which acts as a valve.
2. **Valve incompetence** or absence; continuous reflux predicts progression.
3. **Nutcracker:** left renal vein compressed between aorta and SMA (superior mesenteric artery).
4. **Collateral pathways**; **genetic** (4–8 times commoner in first-degree relatives); tall, thin habitus.
- **Secondary:** a **right-sided or non-decompressing** varicocele suggests a retroperitoneal or renal mass, so image the abdomen.

### How it damages the testis

| Mechanism | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **Heat (primary)** | Lost countercurrent heat exchange; the testis normally runs 2–4 °C below core |
| Oxidative stress | Raised reactive oxygen species → sperm DNA fragmentation |
| Hypoperfusion and stasis | Hypoxia, toxin accumulation |
| Apoptosis | Germ cell loss |
| Leydig dysfunction | Lower testosterone than controls |
- The injury is **progressive and duration-dependent**, and may affect **both** testes.

### Consequences
- **Testicular hypotrophy** (asymmetry above about 20% guides adolescent repair); poor semen; hypogonadism; ache on standing (2–11%).
- Repair improves semen, DNA fragmentation, testosterone and pregnancy (**Madgar RCT [randomised controlled trial], 1995:** 44% versus 10% at 1 year).

### Grading
| Grade | Finding |
|---|---|
| Subclinical | Doppler only (not treated) |
| I | Palpable with Valsalva only |
| II | Palpable, not visible |
| III | Visible |

**Diagram to draw:** left and right testicular veins draining into renal vein and IVC.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Left vein: right-angled entry, 8–10 cm higher column; 90% left.
> - Heat (lost countercurrent exchange) is the main injury; also oxidative DNA damage.
> - Right or non-decompressing varicocele: image for a renal mass.
> - Injury is progressive; subclinical varicocele is not treated.

## Treatment of submeatal stricture urethra in males
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 1, Q3c
(meatal stenosis also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)

*"Submeatal stricture" is read as a stricture of the meatus and fossa navicularis (glanular urethra).*

### Causes
- **LS (lichen sclerosus, formerly BXO [balanitis xerotica obliterans]):** main inflammatory cause today; begins at the glans and meatus and progresses proximally.
- **Iatrogenic:** catheter or instrument trauma, TURP (transurethral resection of prostate).
- **Failed hypospadias repair**; post-circumcision ammoniacal meatitis (children).

### Evaluation
- Spraying stream, straining; uroflowmetry.
- **RUG (retrograde urethrogram) before any meatal surgery**: a proximal stricture often coexists, especially in LS.
- Biopsy suspicious lesions: LS turns malignant (squamous cell carcinoma) in up to 8%.

### Treatment
| Option | Method | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **Topical steroid** | Clobetasol 0.05% once or twice daily for 1–2 months, then alternate days | Early LS |
| **Dilation or self-calibration** | Meatal dilator lubricated with clobetasol | Elderly, unfit; seldom durable |
| **Ventral meatotomy** | Crush and incise ventrally; **three sutures** (apex and each side), mucosa to glans skin; dilate afterwards | Simple stenosis; in LS extend it with an inverted-V relaxing incision |
| **Dorsal meatotomy** (Y-V plasty) | Excise the scar ridge, advance a flap | Effective; cosmesis poorer |
| **Fossa navicularis reconstruction (flaps)** | **Jordan flap:** ventral urethrotomy through the stenosis; **transverse ventral penile skin island on dartos** transposed and inverted into the defect. **De Sy:** ventral longitudinal island advanced | Non-LS; excellent function and cosmesis |
| **Graft** | **BMG (buccal mucosal graft)** inlay or onlay, one or two stages; glans wings developed | **LS: oral mucosa, never genital skin** |
| **Perineal urethrostomy** | Bypass | Pan-anterior LS, failed repairs, elderly |

- LS affects genital skin, so skin flaps recur; it spares the proximal urethra, so perineal urethrostomy works.
- Children: ventral meatotomy plus parental dilation for 7–10 days.

### Complications
- Restenosis (commoner in LS), bleeding, spraying, poor cosmesis.

**Diagram to draw:** Jordan flap: transverse ventral skin island on dartos inverted into the ventral meatotomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RUG first; adult meatal stenosis is usually the tip of a fossa stricture.
> - Non-LS: meatotomy or Jordan/De Sy skin island flap.
> - LS: clobetasol, buccal mucosa (never genital skin), perineal urethrostomy if extensive.

## Perineal urethrostomy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3c
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### Definition
- PU (perineal urethrostomy): the proximal bulbar urethra is opened onto perineal skin as a permanent or staged new meatus; the patient voids sitting. It **bypasses**, rather than reconstructs, a diseased anterior urethra.

### Indications
- **Complex anterior strictures:** LS (lichen sclerosus) panurethral disease, multiple failed urethroplasties, failed hypospadias repair.
- **Elderly or high-risk men** unwilling or unfit for staged reconstruction.
- **First stage** of staged (Johanson-type) urethroplasty.
- After **penectomy** or distal urethrectomy for cancer.
- Temporary: after phalloplasty strictures; Fournier gangrene; resectoscope access in morbid obesity.
- AUA (American Urological Association) guideline (2016): PU is an acceptable long-term alternative to urethroplasty.
- LS spares the proximal urethra, so PU suits LS.

### Contraindications and limits
- Disease reaching the proximal bulbar or membranous urethra; prior pelvic radiation and LS raise restricture.

### Techniques
**1. Classical (Blandy) PU:** inverted-U perineal skin flap advanced into a ventral bulbar urethrotomy.

**2. BMG (buccal mucosal graft) PU, steps:**
1. High lithotomy; inverted-U incision; split bulbospongiosus in the midline.
2. Free and **transect** the spongiosum; **dorsal urethrotomy** into healthy mucosa.
3. Bring healthy ventral urethra to skin; calibrate proximally to 30 Fr; close distal urethra.
4. Quilt a BMG on the corpora to complete the dorsal wall up to the skin.
- The graft prevents the circumferential scar that narrows a deep PU.

### Complications and outcomes
- **Stenosis of the urethrostomy** (main failure): revision or dilation.
- Spraying, dribbling, perineal wetness, wound infection, body-image concerns.
- Compared with staged reconstruction it is a shorter, single operation with less morbidity.
- High mid-term success and satisfaction in selected patients.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal perineum with the inverted-U flap sutured into the opened bulbar urethra.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PU bypasses the diseased anterior urethra; patient voids sitting.
> - Ideal for LS, failed repairs and elderly men.
> - BMG PU prevents circumferential scarring; calibrate to 30 Fr.

## Testicular microlithiasis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- **TM (testicular microlithiasis):** multiple small (<3 mm) bright echogenic foci **without acoustic shadowing** scattered in the testicular parenchyma on ultrasound.
- **Classic TM:** ≥5 microliths per testis (or per image); **limited TM:** <5, of no clinical significance.

### Pathology
- Laminated calcified bodies **inside seminiferous tubules**, formed on degenerated tubular cells that Sertoli cells fail to clear. Affected tubules are often obliterated.

### Prevalence

| Population | Prevalence |
|---|---|
| General population | 5–10% (population-based 4.2%, rising with age) |
| Multicentre review, >37,000 males | 2.9% |
| Boys with cryptorchidism | 3.5% |
| Previously cryptorchid testis vs contralateral | 14% vs 19% (no difference) |

### Associations
- Cryptorchidism, subfertility, **GCNIS (germ cell neoplasia in situ)** and TGCT (testicular germ cell tumour), Klinefelter syndrome, Down syndrome.

### Cancer significance
- Tumours were found in 4.64% with TM vs 0.33% without, but cryptorchidism was not accounted for.
- **TM is not clearly an independent risk factor** for TGCT. An EAU (European Association of Urology) paediatric panel review of several hundred children found only one cancer through puberty.
- **Exception:** contralateral TM in a man with previous GCT (germ cell tumour) signals a higher GCNIS risk.

### Management
- **Isolated TM, no risk factors:** reassure; **no further evaluation, no routine ultrasound follow-up**.
- **TM with risk factors** (previous GCT, cryptorchidism, infertility, atrophic testis): teach **monthly self-examination from puberty**; ultrasound only for a palpable change.
- **Biopsy:** not for TM alone. Contralateral biopsy at orchidectomy is considered when GCNIS risk is high: atrophic testis (<12 mL), cryptorchidism, age <40.
- Counsel carefully: incidental TM causes anxiety and unnecessary tests.

### Differential diagnosis on ultrasound
- Coarse calcification, "burned-out" tumour scar, stellate scar (these need orchidectomy if intratesticular), scrotal pearls, rete testis ectasia.

**Also know:** routine screening ultrasound for infertility is discouraged because of incidental TM; follow-up schedules remain undefined.

**Diagram to draw:** ultrasound sketch of a testis with scattered non-shadowing echogenic dots.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Classic TM ≥5 microliths; limited <5.
> - Not an independent cancer risk factor in isolation.
> - Self-examination only with risk factors; no routine imaging.

## Growing teratoma syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 20 SAQ Sheet, Q3a

### Definition
- GTS (growing teratoma syndrome; Logothetis, 1982): metastatic masses of an NSGCT (non-seminomatous germ cell tumour) **enlarge during or after chemotherapy while AFP (alpha-fetoprotein) and hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin) are normal or falling**. Resection shows **mature teratoma only**.
- Uncommon: about 2–8% of metastatic NSGCT.

### Pathogenesis
- Teratoma is **histologically benign but genetically malignant** (i(12p), aneuploidy) and **chemoresistant**.
- Chemotherapy kills the sensitive elements (embryonal, yolk sac), so markers fall; the teratoma keeps growing, mostly by enlarging cysts. Chemotherapy-induced differentiation is a second theory.
- Main risk factor: teratoma in the orchidectomy specimen.

### Clinical features
- Retroperitoneum commonest; also lung, mediastinum, supraclavicular nodes.
- Often silent; or compression: ureteric obstruction, IVC (inferior vena cava) compression or thrombosis, bowel obstruction, back pain.

### Evaluation
- Markers before every cycle; **rising markers mean viable cancer, not GTS**.
- CT (computed tomography): enlarging, well-defined, **cystic** mass, sometimes with fat or calcification.
- **FDG-PET (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography) has no role**: teratoma is not FDG-avid.
- **Differential:** progressing viable GCT (germ cell tumour; markers rise); malignant transformation (somatic sarcoma or carcinoma); necrosis or fibrosis (mass shrinks).

### Management
- **Stop chemotherapy**; do not escalate to salvage chemotherapy (ineffective).
- **Prompt complete surgical resection**: PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy retroperitoneal lymph node dissection), with resection of every other site; adjacent vessel, kidney or bowel resection may be needed. Delay lets the mass become unresectable.
- Radiotherapy is ineffective.
- **Outcome:** favourable after complete resection (long-term survival 75–90% after resection of residual teratoma). Incomplete resection leads to regrowth, **malignant transformation** (rhabdomyosarcoma commonest) or **late relapse**.
- **Also know:** CDK4/6 (cyclin-dependent kinase 4/6) inhibitors studied for unresectable teratoma; lifelong CT and marker follow-up.

**Diagram to draw:** graph of mass size rising while marker curve falls during chemotherapy cycles.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Enlarging mass plus normal or falling markers equals GTS.
> - Teratoma is chemoresistant and not FDG-avid.
> - Treatment is complete surgical resection, not more chemotherapy.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Male urethra: embryology and anatomy, endoscopic stricture management, congenital anomalies and epispadias (LAQ)

- Double zipper: canalisation, then fold fusion; glanular urethra endodermal.
- Bulbar urethra eccentric (dorsal); Cowper ducts 3 and 9 o'clock; dual retrograde glanular blood supply.
- DVIU: bulbar, <1.5–2 cm, first attempt; Santucci about 8% long-term.
- Optilume ROBUST III 74.6% vs 26.8%.
- Megalourethra: fusiform worse than scaphoid; duplication: ventral channel functional.
- About 70% of male epispadias are penopubic; diastasis >3 cm → exstrophy pathway.
- Cantwell–Ransley at 6–12 months; YDL (Young–Dees–Leadbetter) at about 100 mL.

#### Lymphatic drainage of testis and scrotum, RPLND templates and penile cancer implications (LAQ)

- Right testis to (→) interaortocaval; left to left para-aortic; crossover only right to left.
- Limits/boundaries: renal vessels (hila), ureters, ureteric crossing of iliacs (ureter–iliac crossing); omit suprahilar and interiliac.
- Nerve-sparing of L1–L4 fibres gives antegrade ejaculation >90%; the fibres meet at the IMA: unilateral templates spare ejaculation.
- PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy): full bilateral template remains standard unless mass ≤5 cm in landing zone.
- Scrotum and penis → inguinal nodes; never operate trans-scrotally.
- Penile drainage is bilateral and stepwise without skips: stage both groins, PLND only if inguinal nodes positive.
- pT1b or higher: DSNB or ILND within 3 months; Kroon 84% vs 35%.

#### Enlist various grafts used in urology and role of BMG in urology (LAQ)

- Take: imbibition 48 h, then inosculation 48 h; graft lives on its bed.
- BMG: non-keratinised, vascular lamina propria, wet-adapted.
- Avoid Stensen's duct; buccal equals lingual in success.
- Staged BMG, not skin, in lichen sclerosus.

#### Describe in brief the anatomy of the retroperitoneum. Explain the principles of laparoscopic retroperitoneal surgery while mentioning the salient steps of retroperitoneal laparoscopic nephrectomy. (LAQ)

- Three spaces: anterior pararenal, perirenal, posterior pararenal; perirenal cone open below.
- Entry at the 12th rib tip, where the lumbodorsal fascia fuses.
- Psoas is the landmark; artery met first.
- Main advantage: shorter operative time, peritoneum untouched.

#### Surgical management of primary scrotal cancer (SAQ)

- Pott 1775: chimney sweeps.
- 2 cm margin; testes spared.
- Nodes decide prognosis.

#### Varicocele – Pathophysiology (SAQ)

- Left vein: right-angled entry, 8–10 cm higher column; 90% left.
- Heat (lost countercurrent exchange) is the main injury; also oxidative DNA damage.
- Right or non-decompressing varicocele: image for a renal mass.
- Injury is progressive; subclinical varicocele is not treated.

#### Treatment of submeatal stricture urethra in males (SAQ)

- RUG first; adult meatal stenosis is usually the tip of a fossa stricture.
- Non-LS: meatotomy or Jordan/De Sy skin island flap.
- LS: clobetasol, buccal mucosa (never genital skin), perineal urethrostomy if extensive.

#### Perineal urethrostomy. (SAQ)

- PU bypasses the diseased anterior urethra; patient voids sitting.
- Ideal for LS, failed repairs and elderly men.
- BMG PU prevents circumferential scarring; calibrate to 30 Fr.

#### Testicular microlithiasis (SAQ)

- Classic TM ≥5 microliths; limited <5.
- Not an independent cancer risk factor in isolation.
- Self-examination only with risk factors; no routine imaging.

#### Growing teratoma syndrome (SAQ)

- Enlarging mass plus normal or falling markers equals GTS.
- Teratoma is chemoresistant and not FDG-avid.
- Treatment is complete surgical resection, not more chemotherapy.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe Pathogenesis of Congenital Pelvi Ureteric Junction Obstruction its diagnosis and Management.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q1
(related: 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3e; 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q2)

### Definition
- PUJO (pelviureteric junction obstruction) is impaired flow of urine from the pelvis into the ureter. **Koff:** obstruction is "any restriction to urinary outflow that, if left untreated, will lead to progressive kidney damage". **Dilatation is not obstruction.**
- About 1 in 1500 live births; commonest cause of antenatal hydronephrosis. Male and left predominance; **bilateral in 10–40%**.

### Pathogenesis

| Type | Lesion | Typical patient |
|---|---|---|
| **Intrinsic** (commonest) | Short adynamic segment: interrupted circular muscle, excess collagen, fewer pacemaker (interstitial Cajal-like) cells. The peristaltic wave cannot cross, so the pelvis empties poorly | Neonate or infant, antenatal finding |
| **Extrinsic** | **Lower-pole crossing vessel** anterior to the PUJ; the ureter kinks over it | Older child or adult with intermittent pain |
| Secondary | Kinking by a tortuous ureter in high-grade VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); fibroepithelial polyp; high insertion | Any age |

- **Crossing vessels** are persistent embryonic renal arteries. They occur at a fifth to a quarter of **normal** PUJs, so a vessel alone does not prove cause.
- **Also know:** proposed incomplete ureteric recanalisation; horseshoe kidney and duplex lower moiety associations.

### Effects of obstruction (pathophysiology)
- Pelvic pressure rises; filtration and perfusion fall.
- **Immature kidney:** apoptosis and impaired nephrogenesis. **Mature kidney:** hypertrophy, then fibrosis (TGF-β [transforming growth factor-beta] signalling). Fibrosis, not dilatation, causes loss of function.
- **Also know:** concentrating defect, salt wasting; rarely renin-mediated hypertension.

### Clinical features
- **Antenatal ultrasound** (commonest); infants: UTI (urinary tract infection), flank mass, failure to thrive.
- Older children and adults: **intermittent loin pain with vomiting (Dietl crisis)**, haematuria after minor trauma, stones, infection.
- **Associated anomalies (up to 50%):** contralateral PUJO, dysplasia or MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney), VUR (up to 40%).

### Diagnosis
**1. Antenatal grading (APD, anteroposterior renal pelvic diameter)**

| | Second trimester | Third trimester |
|---|---|---|
| Mild | 4 to <7 mm | 4 to <9 mm |
| Moderate | 7–10 mm | 9–15 mm |
| Severe | >10 mm | >15 mm |
- **UTD (urinary tract dilation) 2014 consensus:** six parameters (APD, central/peripheral calyces, parenchymal thickness and appearance, ureter, bladder). Prenatal A1 (low) or A2–3; postnatal **P1** (APD 10–<15 mm, central calyces), **P2** (≥15 mm or peripheral calyces or ureter), **P3** (abnormal parenchyma).
- **SFU (Society for Fetal Urology) grades 0–4:** 1 pelvis just seen; 2 pelvis plus major calyces; 3 all calyces, normal parenchyma; **4 parenchymal thinning**.

**2. Postnatal ultrasound** after **48 hours** (neonatal dehydration under-reads dilatation): large pelvis, rounded calyces, ureter not seen.

**3. MAG3 (technetium-99m mercaptoacetyltriglycine) diuretic renogram** ("well-tempered": hydrated, catheterised, furosemide)
- **DRF (differential renal function)** (normal 45–55%); drainage half-time <10 min normal, 10–20 equivocal, **>20 min obstructed** (adult chapter: >40 min).

**4. Others:** VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) by UTI risk; MRU (magnetic resonance urography) or CT (computed tomography) angiography for vessels; **Whitaker test** (10 mL/min; >15–20 cm H₂O obstructed) if equivocal.

### Management
**Conservative:** most resolve, about **one-third need surgery**; serial ultrasound and renogram.

**Indications for pyeloplasty**
- **DRF <40%** or a **fall of >10%** on serial scans.
- **Increasing APD** or SFU 4 / P3 with poor drainage.
- **Breakthrough UTI**, symptoms, stones, hypertension.
- Bilateral or solitary kidney.

**Anderson–Hynes dismembered pyeloplasty: gold standard (~95% success)**
1. Expose the PUJ; free the lower pole and look for a crossing vessel.
2. Stay sutures; transect the pelvis; reduce a redundant pelvis.
3. **Spatulate the ureter laterally** into healthy ureter; keep the narrow segment as a "no-touch" handle.
4. **Transpose** the PUJ anterior to any vessel (never divide it).
5. Watertight, tension-free, dependent anastomosis (5-0/6-0 absorbable); excise the segment.
6. Double-J or externalised stent (1–4 weeks equivalent); Foley 24–48 h with a double-J.

- **Route:** open preferred in infants; laparoscopic equals open with less pain; **robotic** (easier suturing, shorter learning curve) is now primary at most major centres.

- **Alternatives:** Foley Y-V plasty (high insertion); Culp spiral or vertical flap (long segment); **vascular hitch** (crossing vessel with normal PUJ); **endopyelotomy** (73% overall; **42% with vs 86% without a crossing vessel**), mainly for secondary PUJO.
- **Nephrectomy:** DRF **<15% in adults** (children: no agreed cut-off).

### Complications and follow-up
- Urine leak, stent problems, UTI; early failure from oedema or clot (temporary nephrostomy).
- **Late failure:** redo pyeloplasty (best), endopyelotomy or ureterocalicostomy. Renogram and ultrasound after stent removal.

### Recent advances
- Robotic redo with buccal graft and ICG (indocyanine green) fluorescence; pyramidal thickness ≤3 mm predicts surgery; urinary biomarkers.

**Diagram to draw:** Anderson–Hynes steps: transection, lateral spatulation, transposition anterior to the crossing vessel, dependent anastomosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Intrinsic adynamic segment (infants) vs crossing vessel (older children, intermittent pain).
> - SFU 4 = parenchymal thinning; UTD P3 = abnormal parenchyma.
> - Operate for DRF <40%, fall >10%, rising APD, UTI or symptoms.
> - Anderson–Hynes ~95%; never divide a crossing vessel.

## Discuss pathophysiology of renal stones and also describe how to prevent its recurrence
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q1
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

### Introduction
- Stone disease is a **chronic metabolic disease** with recurrence up to **50% within 5 years**. Removal is surgical; recurrence is metabolic.
- Calcium oxalate (with or without apatite) forms about 80%; uric acid and struvite about 7% each; cystine 1–3%.

### Risk factors
- **Epidemiological:** male sex, hot climate (low urine volume), animal protein and salt, family history.
- **Systemic:** metabolic syndrome and diabetes (insulin resistance lowers urine pH), gout, hyperparathyroidism, RTA (renal tubular acidosis), bowel disease or bypass, sarcoidosis.
- **Stasis:** PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, horseshoe kidney, medullary sponge kidney. **Drugs:** indinavir, topiramate, vitamin C and D excess.

### Physicochemistry: state of saturation
| State | Concentration product (CP) | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| Undersaturated | Below Ksp (solubility product) | No crystals; stones may dissolve |
| **Metastable** | Between Ksp and Kf (formation product) | No spontaneous nucleation, but growth on existing crystals; inhibitors act here. **Zone of therapy** |
| Unstable | Above Kf | Spontaneous nucleation, growth, aggregation |

### Steps of stone formation
1. **Supersaturation:** low volume; high calcium, oxalate or uric acid; low citrate.
2. **Nucleation:** homogeneous, or **heterogeneous** on debris or other crystals (needs less energy).
3. **Growth and aggregation:** aggregates, not single crystals, can block a tubule within the 5–7 minute transit (**free-particle theory**).
4. **Retention (fixed-particle theory):** **Randall plaque** (calcium apatite) starts in the basement membrane of the thin loop of Henle, spreads through the interstitium, erodes the papillary urothelium and anchors calcium oxalate growth (idiopathic formers). **Ductal plugs** (crystals in collecting ducts) point to a secondary cause: enteric hyperoxaluria, distal RTA, hyperparathyroidism, cystinuria.

### Inhibitors and matrix
- **Inhibitors:** **citrate** (complexes calcium), **magnesium** (complexes oxalate), pyrophosphate, glycosaminoglycans, nephrocalcin, **Tamm–Horsfall protein**, osteopontin. **No inhibitor of uric acid** exists. Matrix is about 2.5% of stone weight.

### Pathophysiology by urinary abnormality
| Abnormality (definition) | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Hypercalciuria** (>200 mg/day, or >4 mg/kg/day) | Absorptive (gut), renal leak (secondary high PTH [parathyroid hormone]), resorptive (primary hyperparathyroidism): Pak classification |
| Hyperoxaluria (>40 mg/day) | **Enteric:** malabsorbed fat binds calcium, freeing oxalate; **primary:** hepatic enzyme defect (PH1, AGXT gene) |
| Hyperuricosuria (>600 mg/day) | Urate promotes calcium oxalate nucleation |
| **Hypocitraturia** (<320 mg/day) | Distal RTA, diarrhoea, hypokalaemia, animal protein (acid load) |
| **Uric acid stones** | **Low urine pH (<5.5)** more than excess uric acid; pKa 5.5 |
| **Cystine** | Autosomal recessive defect of tubular cystine reabsorption |
| **Struvite** | **Urease** (Proteus) splits urea → ammonia, pH >7.2 → magnesium ammonium phosphate and carbonate apatite |

### Prevention of recurrence
**1. Screen every first-time former (AUA [American Urological Association], EAU [European Association of Urology])**
- History, serum electrolytes, creatinine, calcium, uric acid; urine pH and culture; **stone analysis**; imaging. This sorts patients into high and low risk.

**2. General measures (all formers)**
- **Fluid for urine ≥2.5 L/day** (drink about 3 L). Borghi 1996: 5-year recurrence 12.1% vs 27%.
- **Normal calcium**, low sodium, less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables (Borghi 2002: halved recurrence versus a low-calcium diet, which raises oxalate absorption).
- Limit high-oxalate foods (spinach, nuts, chocolate); weight loss.

**3. Metabolic evaluation (high-risk or recurrent formers, children)**
- One or two **24-hour urines**: volume, pH, calcium, oxalate, uric acid, citrate, sodium, potassium, creatinine. Therapy targeted to these results reduces stone events.

**4. Selective drug therapy**
| Abnormality | Treatment | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| Hypercalciuria | **Thiazide:** hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg twice daily, chlorthalidone 25–50 mg, indapamide 2.5 mg | Distal calcium reabsorption; add potassium citrate (hypokalaemia causes hypocitraturia) |
| Hypocitraturia, distal RTA | **Potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day** | Alkali load reduces tubular citrate reabsorption |
| Hyperuricosuria with calcium stones | Allopurinol 300 mg/day | Watch hypersensitivity |
| Uric acid stones | Potassium citrate to **pH 6.0–6.5** (avoid >7.0) | pH disease; allopurinol only for hyperuricosuria |
| Enteric hyperoxaluria | **Calcium with meals**, low-oxalate, low-fat diet, magnesium, liquid citrate | Binds oxalate in gut |
| Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 | Pyridoxine; **lumasiran** (RNA [ribonucleic acid] interference) | ILLUMINATE-A |
| Cystinuria | Urine ≥3 L/day, pH **7.0–7.5**; **tiopronin** | Cystine <250 mg/L; thiol forms soluble mixed disulfide |
| Struvite | **Complete clearance**, antibiotics; AHA (acetohydroxamic acid) 250 mg three times daily if unfit for surgery | Urease inhibitor; thrombosis |

**5. Monitoring**
- 24-hour urine within **6 months** of starting therapy, then yearly; reanalyse any new stone.

### Recent advances
- **ROKS (Recurrence of Kidney Stone) nomogram** predicts individual risk. **Also know:** NOSTONE (2023): hydrochlorothiazide did not reduce recurrence; citrus juices are adjuncts; *Oxalobacter formigenes* loss.

**Diagram to draw:** saturation zones (Ksp, Kf) and Randall plaque on a renal papilla.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf: where inhibitors and therapy act.
> - Randall plaque anchors idiopathic calcium oxalate stones; ductal plugs suggest a secondary cause.
> - Urine ≥2.5 L/day; normal calcium, low salt and animal protein (Borghi).
> - Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Flexible Ureterorenoscopy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- Flexible ureterorenoscopy (fURS) or RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) inspects and treats the whole upper tract retrogradely. Marshall reported it in 1964; active deflection and a working channel made it practical.

### Instrument
| Feature | Details |
|---|---|
| Tip | 4.5–7.5 Fr, tapered (digital tips up to about 8.5 Fr) |
| Working channel | 3.6 Fr (laser fibre, basket) |
| Deflection | ≥270° both ways; lower pole needs about 140° (up to 175°) |
| Types | **Fibreoptic** (smallest, image degrades); **digital** (chip on tip, better image, not more durable); **single-use** (LithoVue 2016, 7.7 Fr; cheaper below about 99 cases a year) |
- Repair is needed about every 15 cases.

### Indications
- **Stones:** renal <2 cm; lower pole <1 cm (or >1 cm as an alternative to PCNL, percutaneous nephrolithotomy); patients who cannot stop anticoagulants; failed SWL (shock wave lithotripsy); obesity.
- **Others:** diagnosis and ablation of low-grade UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma); filling defects, positive cytology; endopyelotomy, short strictures; calyceal diverticulum; essential haematuria; foreign bodies.

### Technique
1. Sterile urine; single-dose antibiotic.
2. Cystoscopy; **safety guidewire**; retrograde pyelogram.
3. **Access sheath** (usually 12/14 Fr) over a stiff wire under fluoroscopy: lowers intrarenal pressure, allows repeated passes; severe ureteric injury in about 1 in 8.
4. Low-pressure irrigation; **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) or thulium fibre laser**: dusting or fragment-and-basket. Keep power ≤10 W in ureter, ≤25 W in kidney (tissue damage above 43 °C).
5. Inspect ureter on withdrawal; stent unless uncomplicated.
- **Difficult lower pole:** steep infundibulopelvic angle, long (>10 mm) or narrow (<5 mm) infundibulum (EAU (European Association of Urology) factors against SWL; they also hinder fURS). Relocate the stone to an upper calyx with a basket.

### Complications
- Sepsis about 5%; stricture about 3%; bleeding or subcapsular haematoma <1%; perforation; avulsion <1%; stent symptoms; scope damage.

### Recent advances
- Suction access sheaths, intrarenal pressure sensors, thulium laser, robotic fURS.

**Diagram to draw:** flexible scope deflected into the lower pole calyx through an access sheath.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 3.6 Fr channel; ≥270° deflection.
> - Access sheath lowers pressure but injures ureter in about 1 in 8.
> - Laser power ≤10 W ureter, ≤25 W kidney.

## Distal Renal tubular acidosis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q3d

### Definition
- **Distal (type 1) RTA (renal tubular acidosis):** the collecting duct cannot secrete H⁺ despite systemic acidosis. Result: **hypokalaemic, hyperchloraemic, normal anion gap metabolic acidosis** with **urine pH >6**. The only acidosis with alkaline urine.

### Normal acidification and the defect
- **α-intercalated cells** secrete H⁺ by apical H⁺-ATPase; bicarbonate exits by basolateral Cl⁻/HCO₃⁻ exchanger (AE1). Normally urine pH can fall to 4.5.
- In distal RTA, potassium is secreted in place of H⁺ → hypokalaemia.

### Causes
| Inherited | Acquired (secondary) |
|---|---|
| **SLC4A1** (AE1): mostly dominant | Sjögren syndrome, SLE (systemic lupus erythematosus) |
| **ATP6V1B1, ATP6V0A4** (H⁺-ATPase): recessive, **sensorineural deafness** | Obstructive uropathy, pyelonephritis, medullary sponge kidney |
| **CA2**: mixed proximal–distal | Hypercalciuria, hyperparathyroidism, amphotericin B |

### Why stones form (urological importance)
- **Stones in up to 70%**: **calcium phosphate** stones and **nephrocalcinosis**. Stones lead to diagnosis in up to half.
- **Profound hypocitraturia** (most important): acidosis increases proximal citrate reabsorption.
- **Hypercalciuria:** bone buffering releases calcium. **High urine pH** precipitates calcium phosphate.

### Clinical features
- Stones, nephrocalcinosis, weakness (hypokalaemia), osteomalacia or rickets; children fail to thrive.
- **Incomplete distal RTA:** normal bicarbonate, but urine pH cannot fall below 5.5 after an acid load.

### Diagnosis
- Low bicarbonate, high chloride, low potassium; urine pH >6 on a fasting morning sample.
- **UAG (urine anion gap) = (Na⁺ + K⁺) − Cl⁻:** **positive** (low ammonium excretion) → renal cause; negative in diarrhoea.
- **Ammonium chloride loading** (0.1 g/kg orally; hourly urine pH): **failure to fall below 5.5** confirms. Furosemide–fludrocortisone test is better tolerated.
- 24-hour urine: low citrate, high calcium; ultrasound for nephrocalcinosis.

### Treatment
- **Potassium citrate** (about 1–2 mEq/kg/day in divided doses): corrects acidosis, hypokalaemia and hypocitraturia, reduces calcium excretion and stone formation. Sodium alkali is less suitable because sodium raises urinary calcium.
- Treat the cause; manage stones endourologically.
