- **Also know:** proximal (type 2) RTA (bicarbonate wasting, Fanconi, few stones); type 4 (hyperkalaemic, low urine pH; diabetes, obstruction); topiramate and acetazolamide mimic distal RTA.

**Diagram to draw:** α-intercalated cell with H⁺-ATPase, AE1 and the stone mechanism.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urine pH >6 with acidosis; hypokalaemia; positive UAG.
> - Calcium phosphate stones in up to 70%; hypocitraturia key.
> - NH₄Cl test: pH fails to fall below 5.5.
> - Potassium citrate is the treatment.

## Radionuclide studies and diuretic renography in PUJ obstruction
**Asked as:**
- Radionuclide studies for PUJ obstruction. — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Radionuclear studies in PUJ obstruction — *Practice Set 4 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Diureteric renography — *Practice Set 2 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 4: also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3e. Practice Set 2: PUJ obstruction renography also in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3e; tracers: see Practice Set 2 Paper 1, Q3e)

*"Diureteric" is read as diuretic renography.*

### Introduction / principle
- **Dilatation is not obstruction.** PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction is impaired drainage that damages the kidney. **Diuretic renography** is the functional standard (functional gold standard for PUJ obstruction) and best follow-up test (best follow-up study).
- A capacious pelvis retains tracer passively and furosemide washes it out; an obstructed system cannot empty even under diuresis.

### Tracers
| Tracer | Handling | Use |
|---|---|---|
| ⁹⁹ᵐTc-**MAG3** (technetium-99m mercaptoacetyltriglycine) | Tubular secretion | **Agent of choice** (tracer of choice): split function and drainage; good images (good curves) even with poor function |
| ⁹⁹ᵐTc-**DTPA** (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid) | Glomerular filtration | GFR (glomerular filtration rate); poor in renal impairment; an alternative to MAG3 |
| ⁹⁹ᵐTc-**DMSA** (dimercaptosuccinic acid) | Cortical retention | Static split function and scars; no drainage data |

### "Well-tempered" diuretic renogram (standard protocol)
| Element | How | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **Hydration** | Oral, or IV (intravenous) fluid in children (about 15 mL/kg) | Dehydration mimics obstruction |
| **Bladder catheter** | Urethral catheter | Removes full-bladder (back-pressure) and reflux (VUR, vesicoureteral reflux) effects |
| **Furosemide** | 0.5 mg/kg (adult maximum 40 mg) in adults; 1 mg/kg in children | Maximal diuresis |
| **Timing** | **F+20** (classic; 20 min after tracer, when the pelvis is full; Campbell authors use F+15), **F0** (with tracer) or **F−15** (15 min before, so the system is already in diuresis) | Diuretic before filling (early diuretic into an unfilled system) misleads; always state timing |
| **Regions of interest** | Regions of interest over cortex (for split) and pelvis (dilated pelvis for drainage); then upright images | Wrong placement gives spurious values |

- Well-tempered: hydrate, catheter, standard diuretic dose and timing.
- First study after about 4–6 weeks of age (immature function).

### Outputs
- **DRF (differential renal function)** from counts at 1–3 min (1–3 minute counts); normal 45–55%.
- **Drainage half-time (T½, half-clearance time)** after furosemide, from maximal filling, not from injection:

| T½ after furosemide | Interpretation |
|---|---|
| <10 min (minutes) | Not obstructed |
| 10–20 min (minutes) | Equivocal (indeterminate) |
| >20 min (minutes) | Obstructed |

- **O'Reilly curves:** I normal; II obstructed (keeps rising); IIIa dilated, non-obstructed (falls after furosemide); IIIb equivocal.
- **Also know:** P40 (activity remaining / left at 40 min) raises sensitivity; output efficiency; NORA (normalised residual activity); a **biphasic curve** and poor upright (post-void) drainage predict progression.

### Indications
- PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, antenatal hydronephrosis, megaureter, after pyeloplasty, transplant ureter.

### Indications for pyeloplasty from renography
- Consider surgery for:
- **DRF <40%**, **fall in DRF** on serial comparable scans (**>10%** on serial scans; ≥5% in children; 10% in older series), **T½ >20 min** (minutes) or upright retention; also (poor drainage with) symptoms, infection, increasing dilatation.
- Adult DRF <15%: likely non-salvageable; nephrostomy and reassess, or nephrectomy.

### Pitfalls
- Dehydration, early diuretic (mistimed diuretic), full bladder, wrong ROI (region of interest), very poor function, very capacious pelvis.
- One Campbell chapter calls T½ >40 min obstructed; children have no agreed (paediatric T½) cut-off (false positives up to 44%; neonatal false-positive rates reach 44%); one Campbell chapter uses **T½ >40 min** in adults, so **serial trends and curve shape** matter / decide.

### Follow-up and alternatives
- After pyeloplasty: renogram 1 month after stent removal; spontaneous drainage means success.
- Equivocal (cases) → **Whitaker test** (>15–20 cm H₂O obstructed) or MRU (magnetic resonance urography).

**Diagram to draw:** normal, obstructed and dilated non-obstructed renogram curves (O'Reilly curves I, II, IIIa and IIIb) with furosemide arrow.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - MAG3 is the tracer of choice; hydrate and catheterise.
> - Well-tempered: hydrate, catheter, standard diuretic dose and timing.
> - Furosemide 0.5 mg/kg (adult maximum 40 mg); 1 mg/kg in children.
> - DRF normal 45–55%; T½ <10 normal (not obstructed); 10–20 indeterminate; >20 min obstructed.
> - Operate (surgery) for DRF <40%, fall >10% (falling DRF), or poor drainage with symptoms.

## Prone vs Supine PCNL: Indications, positioning, technique, specific complications, advantages and disadvantages of each.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3f
(mini-PCNL: see 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1)

### Indications for PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)
- Renal stones **>2 cm**, staghorn, lower pole >1 cm, calyceal diverticulum, failed retrograde route or urinary diversion, UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) obstruction.

### Comparison
| Feature | Prone (Goodwin, 1955) | Supine (Valdivia-Uría, 1987) |
|---|---|---|
| Positioning | Lithotomy for ureteric catheter, then turned prone; chest and pelvic bolsters (horizontal bring kidney down more); arms ≤90°, face and knees padded | Ipsilateral flank at table edge on a bolster, arm across chest; **Galdakao-modified (Scoffone)**: legs in lithotomy for simultaneous retrograde access |
| Best suited | Standard anatomy; **upper pole and posterior calyx** access; multiple tracts | Obese, cardiopulmonary disease, kyphosis; ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery); ileal conduit or continent channel |
| Technique | Posterior calyx puncture under fluoroscopy or ultrasound; tract more vertical; large working area | Puncture in posterior axillary line; tract more horizontal and longer; kidney more mobile; air outlines anterior calyces |
| Drainage | Against gravity; higher pelvic pressure | **Gravity drains fragments**, low intrarenal pressure |
| Anaesthesia | Airway access difficult; turning needed | **Airway retained**; no repositioning |

### Specific complications
- **Prone:** ocular injury, neuropraxia (brachial plexus), pressure injury, airway tube dislodgement while turning.
- **Supine:** **colon injury** (if puncture too anterior), access failure to upper pole, kidney displacement during dilatation.
- **Both:** bleeding (transfusion now about 1%), pleural injury above the 12th rib, sepsis, bowel, spleen or liver injury.

### Advantages and disadvantages
| | Prone | Supine |
|---|---|---|
| Advantages | Wide working area, easy upper pole access, familiar | Single position, shorter time, ECIRS, gravity clearance, safer anaesthesia |
| Disadvantages | Repositioning, positioning injuries, needs split-leg table for ECIRS | Smaller working area, collapsed system, longer tract, harder upper pole |

- **Evidence:** stone-free rates and complications similar; supine often shortens operating time; choice is mainly surgeon preference.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section showing prone (vertical) and supine (horizontal) tracts.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prone: posterior and upper pole access, big working space.
> - Supine: airway, ECIRS, gravity drainage, no turning.
> - Similar outcomes; choose by anatomy and experience.

## Metabolic Evaluation in Stone Disease
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3b
(also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

### Rationale
- Stones are a chronic metabolic disease: about **50% recur within 10 years**. Evaluation finds a treatable cause and directs targeted therapy, which reduces stone events.

### Step 1: Screening evaluation (every first-time former; AUA [American Urological Association], EAU [European Association of Urology])

| Component | What is assessed |
|---|---|
| History | GI (gastrointestinal) disease, gout, sarcoidosis, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), family history; diet; drugs (calcium, vitamins C and D, acetazolamide, steroids) |
| Blood | Creatinine, sodium, potassium, calcium, uric acid, bicarbonate; PTH (parathyroid hormone) if calcium is high |
| Urine | pH, crystals (hexagonal = cystine), culture |
| **Stone analysis** | Infrared spectroscopy or X-ray diffraction; uric acid, cystine and struvite point to a cause |
| Imaging | NCCT (non-contrast computed tomography): burden |

### Step 2: Extended evaluation (high-risk or recurrent formers)
- **High risk:** children, recurrent stones, solitary kidney, GI disease or bariatric surgery, hyperparathyroidism, RTA (renal tubular acidosis), metabolic syndrome, gout, family history.
- **One or two 24-hour urines** on a usual diet, ≥4 weeks after an episode, stone- and infection-free; creatinine confirms completeness.

| Parameter | Target per day | Abnormality |
|---|---|---|
| Volume | **>2.5 L** | Low volume (commonest) |
| Calcium | <4 mg/kg (about 250 mg women, 300 mg men) | Hypercalciuria |
| Oxalate | **<40 mg** | Dietary, enteric, primary |
| Citrate | **>320 mg** | Hypocitraturia (RTA, diarrhoea) |
| Uric acid | <600 mg | Hyperuricosuria |
| pH | 5.8–6.2 | <5.5 uric acid; >6.5 calcium phosphate or infection |
| Sodium | <100 mmol | Raises urine calcium |
| Cystine | Negative screen | Cystinuria |

- **Special tests:** ammonium chloride load (urine pH stays >5.5 in distal RTA); quantitative cystine.
- Fast and calcium-load subtyping of hypercalciuria is no longer advised (does not change treatment).

### Follow-up
- Repeat 24-hour urine **within 6 months** of starting therapy, then yearly; blood tests for drug side effects.
- Pregnancy: defer 24-hour urine.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: first stone → screening → low risk (general advice) or high risk (24-hour urine → targeted therapy).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Screening for all first-time formers; 24-hour urine for high-risk.
> - Targets: volume >2.5 L, oxalate <40 mg, citrate >320 mg.
> - Repeat 24-hour urine within 6 months of therapy.

## What are nanobacteria? Describe their role in urolithiasis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 1, Q3e

### Definition
- **Nanobacteria**, now called **CNPs (calcifying nanoparticles)**, are spherical particles far smaller than bacteria with a **calcium apatite shell**, described in bovine and human serum by **Kajander and Çiftçioglu (1998)** as *Nanobacterium sanguineum*.
- **Their living nature is disputed.** Proposed as the smallest self-replicating bacteria, but later work showed "replication" is mineral nucleation, and that they are **mineralo-protein complexes** (apatite with serum proteins such as fetuin-A). Their bacterial DNA proved to be contamination. Most now regard them as **non-living**.

### Claimed properties
| Property | Claim |
|---|---|
| Size | Pass through bacteria-retaining filters |
| "Growth" | Very slow, in serum-containing medium |
| Shell | Apatite; resistant to heat and gamma irradiation |
| Detection | Electron microscopy, von Kossa and Hoechst staining, specific antibodies, culture |
| Susceptibility | Tetracycline and EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) inhibit them |

### Proposed role in urolithiasis
- **Nidus theory:** CNPs in blood or urine are filtered or excreted, deposit apatite in renal tubular cells and interstitium, and act as a **nucleating nidus** for crystal growth.
- **Link to Randall plaque:** idiopathic calcium oxalate stones grow on **Randall plaque**, an interstitial **apatite** deposit that starts in the basement membrane of the thin loop of Henle and erodes through the papilla. CNPs are **associated with**, but **not proven to cause**, Randall plaque (Campbell, Chapter 95).
- Early studies found apatite nanoparticles in a large majority of stones; rat studies reported renal calcification after injection.
- Also implicated in atherosclerotic calcification, polycystic kidney cysts and chronic prostatitis.

### Therapeutic implications (unproven)
- Tetracycline-based regimens with EDTA were tried in stones and chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome; no RCT (randomised controlled trial) evidence supports routine use.

### Current position
- Not in stone guidelines; pathogenesis is explained by supersaturation, inhibitors, Randall plaque and ductal plugs.

**Diagram to draw:** renal papilla with interstitial apatite (Randall plaque) eroding through urothelium, CNPs as nidus, and calcium oxalate stone attached.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Nanobacteria = calcifying nanoparticles; apatite shell; living nature disputed.
> - Proposed nidus for apatite and Randall plaque; association, not causation.
> - Tetracycline/EDTA therapy unproven.

## Tear drop bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 1, Q3a

### Definition
- A **tear-drop (pear-shaped or inverted-pear) bladder** is a bladder that is symmetrically **compressed from both sides** and elongated upwards on cystogram, IVU (intravenous urogram) or CT (computed tomography).
- It is a radiological sign of a **bilateral perivesical mass or collection**, not a disease itself.

### Causes
| Group | Causes |
|---|---|
| **Collections** | **Pelvic haematoma** (pelvic fracture: commonest in trauma); urinoma (extraperitoneal leak); bilateral lymphocele (after lymphadenectomy or transplant); pelvic abscess |
| **Fat** | **Pelvic lipomatosis** |
| **Muscle** | **Iliopsoas hypertrophy** (muscular young men) |
| **Vascular** | Dilated pelvic venous collaterals (IVC [inferior vena cava] occlusion); bilateral iliac artery aneurysms |
| **Nodes and tumour** | Pelvic lymphadenopathy (lymphoma, metastases); pelvic retroperitoneal fibrosis; pelvic tumours |

### Pelvic lipomatosis (the classic cause)
- Overgrowth of benign, non-encapsulated fat in the perivesical and perirectal spaces; mainly in men, more in Black men.
- **Presentation:** LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), suprapubic pain, constipation; often incidental.
- **Imaging:**
  - Plain film: pelvic lucency. IVU: elevated, elongated pear-shaped bladder; **medial deviation of both ureters**; hydronephrosis.
  - Barium enema: straightened, elongated rectosigmoid.
  - **CT or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** fat density is diagnostic.
- **Cystoscopy:** high bladder neck, difficult passage; often **cystitis glandularis**, which can progress to adenocarcinoma. Biopsy and surveillance are needed.
- **Management:** observe if no obstruction. Upper-tract obstruction → stent or nephrostomy, ureteric reimplantation or urinary diversion. Weight loss rarely helps; surgical excision of fat is difficult.

### Evaluation of a tear-drop bladder
- History: trauma, pelvic surgery or transplant, malignancy.
- **CT** shows the cause (blood, fat, fluid, muscle, nodes, vessels). Ultrasound for collections; aspiration (creatinine for urinoma, lymph for lymphocele).

### Management
- Treat the cause: pelvic haematoma usually observed; lymphocele drained or marsupialised (laparoscopic fenestration); urinoma drained and leak repaired.

**Diagram to draw:** cystogram outline of a bladder squeezed into a tear drop by bilateral pelvic haematoma.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tear-drop bladder = bilateral extrinsic compression.
> - Trauma: pelvic haematoma; non-trauma: pelvic lipomatosis.
> - Lipomatosis: fat on CT, medially deviated ureters, cystitis glandularis.

## Retrograde endopyelotomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 9 Paper 1, Q3f

### Definition and principle
- Endoscopic **full-thickness incision of the UPJ (ureteropelvic junction)** from inside the ureter, through to peripelvic fat, followed by stenting so the ureter heals with a wider lumen (Davis "intubated ureterotomy" concept, 1943).
- **Retrograde ureteroscopic holmium laser endopyelotomy** is now the standard endourological route: direct vision without a percutaneous tract.

### Indications and contraindications
| Favourable | Unfavourable / contraindicated |
|---|---|
| Functionally significant UPJO (UPJ obstruction); short segment | **Long stenotic segment** |
| **Secondary UPJO** (failed pyeloplasty) | **Upper-tract stones** (treat percutaneously instead) |
| Moderate hydronephrosis, fair function | **Crossing vessel**, **gross hydronephrosis**, poor function |
- Pre-operative CT (computed tomography) angiography or Doppler to look for crossing vessels.

### Technique
1. General anaesthesia; retrograde pyelogram; hydrophilic safety wire into the pelvis.
2. Access sheath; flexible ureteroscope to the UPJ (semirigid in some women). Dilate a narrow intramural ureter or pre-stent.
3. Holmium laser, 200-µm fibre, 0.8–1.2 J, 10–15 Hz.
4. **Lateral incision** (no crossing vessels laterally), deepened into fat and extended into normal ureter and pelvis.
5. Optional balloon dilation to 24 Fr; DJ (double-J) stent and Foley catheter.
6. Stent out after some weeks; **diuretic renogram 4 weeks later**; follow up for 24–36 months.

### Results
- Holmium series 70–80% at 5 years (range 44–89%). Excluding crossing vessels >4 mm raises success to about 90%.
- Crossing vessel present versus absent: 42% versus 86% (antegrade series).
- Still below pyeloplasty (about 95%), but most cost-effective endourological option.

### Complications
- Urine leak, stent migration, infection, bleeding (lateral cut spares vessels), stricture (rare with small scopes); ureteroarterial fistula reported.
- **Also know:** antegrade (percutaneous) endopyelotomy for UPJO with stones; cautery wire balloon (more haemorrhage); balloon dilation alone (about 42% success); failure then needs pyeloplasty.

**Diagram to draw:** UPJ with lateral full-thickness incision and crossing vessel lying anterior/posterior.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lateral full-thickness incision over a stent.
> - Crossing vessel and gross hydronephrosis predict failure.
> - Success 70–80%, below pyeloplasty (about 95%).

## Autotransplantation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 10 Paper 1, Q3c
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3c and 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3c)

*Taken as renal autotransplantation.*

### Definition and principle
- Removing a kidney and re-implanting it, usually in the **ipsilateral iliac fossa** on the iliac vessels, with **bench (ex vivo) surgery** if needed. First done in 1963 for proximal ureteric injury.
- Bringing the kidney to the bladder replaces the ureter without bowel.

### Indications
| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Ureteric loss (commonest)** | Long iatrogenic or traumatic loss; failed repairs (**delayed setting, never acute**) |
| **RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis)** | Ureterolysis impossible |
| **Tumour** | UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) in a solitary kidney; bench partial nephrectomy |
| **Vascular** | Complex renal artery aneurysm or AVM (arteriovenous malformation) unsuitable for endovascular repair; nutcracker |
| **Pain** | Selected loin pain–haematuria syndrome |
- **Preferred over ileal ureter** when the other kidney is poor or bowel unsuitable (creatinine >2 mg/dL, bowel disease, radiation).
- **Contraindications:** poor renal function, severe iliac atherosclerosis, unfit patient.

### Procedure
```
CT (computed tomography) angiography, renogram
→ harvest as for live donation: maximal vessel length,
  preserve ureteric blood supply
→ cold perfusion ± bench repair
→ Gibson incision, extraperitoneal iliac fossa
→ vein to external iliac vein (end-to-side);
  artery to external iliac (end-to-side) or internal iliac (end-to-end)
→ pyelovesicostomy (± Boari flap), ureteroneocystostomy,
  or native ureter left in continuity
→ stent, catheter, drain
```
- Laparoscopic harvest speeds recovery; robotic autotransplantation emerging.

### Complications and outcome
- Arterial or venous thrombosis, bleeding, urine leak, anastomotic stricture, lymphocele, infection.
- **Follow-up:** Doppler ultrasound early; renogram and blood pressure later.
- **Renal unit loss 5–10%**; major (Clavien–Dindo ≥III) complications about 15–20%; **success about 90%**.

**Also know:** alternatives: Boari flap, TUU (transureteroureterostomy), ileal ureter, Yang–Monti.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney in the iliac fossa with vascular anastomoses and pyelovesicostomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - For long ureteric loss when the other kidney is poor or bowel unsuitable.
> - Delayed setting only; bench surgery for vascular or tumour cases.
> - Unit loss 5–10%; success about 90%.

## Retroperitoneal fibrosis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3a
(also answered as an LAQ in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition
- RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis): inflammatory fibrous plaque encasing the aorta, IVC (inferior vena cava) and ureters; centred on the **distal aorta at L4–L5**. Age 40–60; males 2–3 times commoner.

### Aetiology
| Idiopathic (~70%) | Secondary (~30%) |
|---|---|
| Chronic periaortitis (large-vessel vasculitis); **IgG4 (immunoglobulin G4)-related disease**; autoimmune; ceroid antigen | **Drugs** (methysergide, ergots, β-blockers); **malignancy** (lymphoma commonest); radiotherapy; infection (tuberculosis) |

### Pathology
- Early: lymphocytes, plasma cells, capillaries (steroid-responsive). Late: acellular collagen.
- Ureteric obstruction is by compression and **loss of peristalsis**; lumen stays patent. Malignant RPF shows tumour islands: take deep biopsies.

### Clinical features
- **Dull back or flank pain**, weight loss, malaise, hypertension; about half present with **uraemia**; leg oedema or DVT (deep venous thrombosis) from IVC compression.

### Evaluation
- Raised ESR (erythrocyte sedimentation rate) and CRP (C-reactive protein), creatinine, IgG4.
- **CT (computed tomography):** periaortic mass with **medial deviation of mid-ureters**.
- **MRI:** high T2 = active. **PET-CT (positron emission tomography):** best marker of activity.
- **Biopsy** if atypical or malignancy suspected.

### Management
```
Drain (stents or nephrostomy) → stop drug, exclude cancer
  → prednisone → failure / fibrotic disease → ureterolysis
```
- **Prednisone** 60 mg daily (~1 mg/kg), tapered over up to 2 years: ~80% respond; relapse 25–50%.
- **Vaglio RCT (randomised controlled trial; Lancet 2011):** relapse 17% prednisone vs 50% tamoxifen.
- Steroid-sparing: azathioprine, mycophenolate; **rituximab** for refractory or IgG4 disease.
- **Ureterolysis** (open, laparoscopic or robotic; ~15% need it): **bilateral**, biopsies, then **intraperitonealisation**, lateralisation or **omental wrap**.
- Others: long-term metallic stents; autotransplantation; nephrectomy for a dead kidney.

### Follow-up
- ESR/CRP, creatinine and ultrasound; MRI or PET-CT for activity; remove stents once the ureters drain; watch for relapse and steroid side effects.

**Diagram to draw:** periaortic plaque pulling both ureters medially.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 70% idiopathic; methysergide and lymphoma classic.
> - Medially deviated ureters; PET-CT shows activity.
> - Prednisone first (Vaglio 2011 beat tamoxifen).
> - Bilateral ureterolysis with omental wrap.

## Management of failed pyeloplasty
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition of failure
- Persistent pain, UTI (urinary tract infection) or stones; **obstructive diuretic renogram** (T½ >20 minutes); increasing hydronephrosis; falling split function.
- Dilatation settles slowly; judge by renography.
- About 1 in 9 children need a secondary procedure. Most failures occur within 1–2 years, but up to 30% occur later.

### Causes
- **Ischaemia** (over-stripping) and **tension** at the anastomosis.
- **Missed crossing vessel**; inadequate excision of the narrow segment; non-dependent anastomosis.
- **Urine leak** or infection causing periureteric fibrosis.
- Missed distal stricture or high-grade VUR (vesicoureteral reflux).

### Early failure (first days or weeks)
- Pain, fever, increasing dilatation: usually **oedema or clot**; temporary nephrostomy or stent, and most settle.

### Evaluation of late failure
- **MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) diuretic renogram:** obstruction and split function.
- **CT (computed tomography) angiography:** crossing vessel.
- Antegrade or retrograde pyelography (stricture length); **Whitaker test** if equivocal. Nephrostomy first if infected.

### Treatment options

| Option | When | Results |
|---|---|---|
| **Endopyelotomy** (retrograde or antegrade) | Short stricture, no crossing vessel; first choice after failed open repair | About 70% |
| **Redo pyeloplasty** (open, laparoscopic, robotic) | Long or dense stricture, crossing vessel | About 86–89%; best choice in most children |
| **Ureterocalicostomy** | Scarred, small intrarenal pelvis; dilated lower calyx | About 70% (redo series) |
| **Buccal mucosa onlay** (robotic, omental wrap) | Long scarred UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) | Failure about 15–25% (CORRUS) |
| **Nephrectomy** | Split function <15%, normal other kidney; multiple failures | Definitive |

- **Also know:** ileal ureter or autotransplantation for long ureteric loss.

### Principles of redo surgery
- Transperitoneal route in children (retroperitoneum is scarred).
- Ureteric catheter; wide mobilisation of kidney and ureter; **excise all scar**; spatulate; **tension-free, watertight** anastomosis over a stent; wrap with omentum or perirenal fat.
- **Also know:** near-infrared fluorescence shows perfusion; downward nephropexy.

### Follow-up
- Stent out at 4–6 weeks; renograms for 2–3 years (late failures).

**Diagram to draw:** ureterocalicostomy (lower calyx to spatulated ureter).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Causes: ischaemia, tension, missed crossing vessel, leak.
> - Failed open repair: endourology first; redo pyeloplasty most reliable.
> - Ureterocalicostomy for small scarred pelvis; buccal onlay emerging.
> - Nephrectomy if split function <15%.

## Post-obstructive diuresis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 19 SAQ Sheet, Q3b

### Definition
- POD (post-obstructive diuresis): marked polyuria after relief of obstruction, as the kidney sheds retained salt, water and urea.
- No agreed rate; a common working definition is **>200 mL/h for 2 consecutive hours or >3 L in 24 hours**. Up to 300 mL/h is common.
- **Physiological** (appropriate, self-limited) versus **pathological** (continues after euvolaemia, causing hypovolaemia and electrolyte loss).

### Incidence and setting
- Unilateral obstruction: **about 2%** (the other kidney compensates). Bilateral or solitary kidney: **about 50%**.
- Commonest after catheterising **chronic retention with renal impairment**; also bilateral ureteric obstruction and PUV (posterior urethral valves).

### Mechanisms
1. **Transporter dysregulation:** down-regulation of Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase and aquaporin-2 → concentrating defect (nephrogenic DI (diabetes insipidus)); loss of medullary tonicity.
2. **Volume expansion:** on relief, raised ANP (atrial natriuretic peptide), low aldosterone and sympathetic tone → salt and water loss.
3. **Osmotic diuresis** from retained urea.

### Risk factors
- Bilateral obstruction, raised creatinine, oedema, heart failure, hypertension, confusion.
- After valve ablation: oligohydramnios, preterm birth, raised creatinine.

### Clinical features
- Starts within hours of relief; most settle within 24–48 hours.
- Danger signs: postural hypotension, tachycardia, weight loss, hypernatraemia (free-water loss), low potassium, magnesium, phosphate.

### Management
```
Relieve obstruction (catheter / nephrostomy)
        |
Monitor hourly urine output, vitals, weight
        |
Alert, can drink -> free oral fluids (most resolve)
        |
Output persists / cannot drink / unstable
  -> admit; electrolytes every 4-6 h
  -> intravenous 0.45% saline: about 75% of urine output
  -> replace K, Mg, phosphate; adjust to urine sodium/osmolality
```
- **Do not overcorrect:** replacing every millilitre sustains the diuresis.
- **Gradual bladder decompression has no role**: it does not prevent haematuria or diuresis.
- PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy) for upper-tract obstruction; hydration alone for physiological POD.
- **Also know:** decompression haematuria; neonatal PUV concentrating defect; no approved drug therapy.

**Diagram to draw:** graph of urine output against time after catheterisation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Unilateral about 2%, bilateral about 50%.
> - Mechanisms: aquaporin-2 loss, volume expansion, urea osmotic diuresis.
> - Replace about 75% of output with 0.45% saline; do not overcorrect.
> - Gradual decompression has no role.

## Stone complexity scores
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 20 SAQ Sheet, Q3b

### Purpose
- Grade stone and kidney complexity, chiefly to **predict stone-free rate (SFR) after PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)**. Used for counselling, choosing the surgeon, auditing and comparing series.

### Main scores

| Score | Variables and scoring | Predicts |
|---|---|---|
| **Guy's Stone Score** (Thomas, 2011) | **Grade I:** single stone in mid or lower pole, or pelvis, simple anatomy. **II:** single upper-pole stone, or multiple stones with simple anatomy, or single stone with abnormal anatomy. **III:** multiple stones with abnormal anatomy, calyceal diverticulum stone, or partial staghorn. **IV:** complete staghorn, or any stone in spina bifida or spinal injury | SFR on KUB (kidney, ureter, bladder radiograph); not complications |
| **S.T.O.N.E. nephrolithometry** | **S**ize (area), **T**ract length (≤100 or >100 mm), **O**bstruction (hydronephrosis), **N**umber of involved calyces, **E**ssence (density, ≤950 or >950 HU [Hounsfield units]); total 5–13 | SFR, blood loss, operative time, stay; needs CT (computed tomography) |
| **CROES nomogram** (Clinical Research Office of the Endourological Society) | Stone burden, location, staghorn, number, prior treatment, **annual centre volume** | SFR; only multicentre derivation, but SFR on KUB |
| **S-ReSC** (Seoul National University Renal Stone Complexity) | Number of **9 sites** involved on CT: pelvis, upper and lower major calyces, anterior and posterior minor calyces of the three groups; score 1–9 | SFR; not complications |
| **RUSS** (Resorlu–Unsal Stone Score) | 1 point each: stone >20 mm; lower-pole stone with infundibulopelvic angle <45°; stones in more than one calyx; abnormal anatomy; score 0–4 | SFR after **RIRS** (retrograde intrarenal surgery) |

### Limitations
- Each score added what the previous lacked: anatomy, then CT depth and density, then centre factors, then calyceal distribution.
- Mostly predict clearance, **not complications**. Meta-analysis: heterogeneous; no single generalisable score.
- **Also know:** Triple D score predicts SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) success, not PCNL.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney outline showing the 9 S-ReSC sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Guy's: grades I–IV; complete staghorn or spina bifida is grade IV.
> - S.T.O.N.E.: five variables, score 5–13.
> - CROES includes centre volume; RUSS is for RIRS.

## Calyceal diverticulum – classification and management
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 20 SAQ Sheet, Q3c

### Definition
- Cystic cavity in the renal cortex, **lined by transitional epithelium**, connected to a calyx (or pelvis) by a **narrow neck**. Fills passively with urine.
- About 4.5 per 1000; equal in children and adults; no side or sex preference.

### Classification

| | Type I | Type II |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | Commoner | Less common |
| Connects to | Minor calyx (usually upper pole) | Renal pelvis or major calyx |
| Size, symptoms | Small, usually silent | Larger, often symptomatic |

### Aetiology
- **Congenital** (favoured): persistent third- and fourth-generation ureteric bud branches.
- **Acquired:** cortical abscess draining into a calyx, infundibular stenosis, trauma, reflux.

### Clinical features
- Stasis: **stones in up to 40%**, milk of calcium, infection, pain, haematuria.

### Evaluation
- Ultrasound mimics a simple cyst. **CT (computed tomography) urography with delayed images shows contrast pooling**; milk of calcium layers and shifts with position.
- Retrograde pyelography maps the neck before intervention.
- **Differential:** hydrocalycosis (dilated major calyx from infundibular obstruction), megacalycosis, simple cyst.

### Management
- **Asymptomatic:** observe with ultrasound.
- **Treat** pain, recurrent infection, haematuria, abscess, symptomatic stones.

| Approach | Technique | Best for |
|---|---|---|
| **Ureteroscopy** | Find the ostium, laser-incise or balloon-dilate the neck, remove stones | Upper or mid-pole, small stones |
| **PCNL** (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) | Direct puncture onto stone, clear stones, fulgurate lining, dilate or create neck | Posterior, lower-pole, larger stones; long-term results good |
| **Laparoscopic or robotic** | Unroof, remove stones, close neck, obliterate cavity, pack with fat | Anterior, thin-parenchyma, failed endoscopy |
| Partial nephrectomy | Excision | Rare; destroyed segment |

- Endoscopy keeps the cavity draining by widening the neck; laparoscopy abolishes it by closing the neck.
- **Also know:** SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) gives symptom relief but low stone clearance; fulguration of lining is debated.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with upper-pole diverticulum, narrow neck to calyx, stone inside.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Transitional lined, narrow neck; type I calyceal, type II pelvic.
> - Stones in up to 40%; delayed CT shows pooling.
> - Ureteroscopy or PCNL first; laparoscopy for anterior or failed cases.

## Pelvic lipomatosis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 20 SAQ Sheet, Q3e

### Definition
- Rare benign **overgrowth of non-encapsulated mature fat** in the perivesical and perirectal spaces, compressing the bladder, ureters, rectum and pelvic veins.
- Mostly men aged 20–60; associated with obesity and hypertension. Cause unknown; obesity alone does not explain it (weight loss rarely shrinks the fat). Chronic inflammation and venous obstruction are proposed.

### Clinical features
- LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), haematuria, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), suprapubic or perineal pain, constipation.
- Leg oedema or venous thrombosis from venous compression.
- **Hydronephrosis and renal failure** from ureteric compression in a substantial minority.
- **Proliferative cystitis (cystitis glandularis)** is common; it can transform to **adenocarcinoma**.

### Evaluation
- **IVU (intravenous urography) or cystogram:** elevated, elongated **"pear-" or "teardrop-shaped" bladder**; medially displaced pelvic ureters; high prostate.
- **Barium enema:** elongated, narrowed rectosigmoid.
- **Ultrasound:** echogenic pelvic fat; hydronephrosis.
- Medial deviation of **both** pelvic ureters also occurs in retroperitoneal fibrosis and after abdominoperineal surgery.
- **CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging): diagnostic**; homogeneous fat density surrounding pelvic organs, separates it from tumour.
- **Cystoscopy:** elevated trigone, long prostatic urethra (rigid scope may be difficult); biopsy for cystitis glandularis.
- **Differential of pear-shaped bladder:** pelvic haematoma, lymphadenopathy, liposarcoma, iliopsoas hypertrophy, venous collaterals, pelvic fibrosis.

### Management
- **Surveillance** (often progressive): ultrasound for hydronephrosis, renal function, periodic cystoscopy for cystitis glandularis.
- Weight loss, antibiotics and steroids give limited or no benefit; COX-2 (cyclo-oxygenase-2) inhibitors for cystitis glandularis are case reports only.
- **Ureteric obstruction:** stents or nephrostomy; ureteric reimplantation (difficult, dense fat).
- **Advanced or failed cases:** urinary diversion; cystectomy if adenocarcinoma or unmanageable bladder.
- Surgical excision of fat is difficult: no tissue planes, heavy bleeding.
- Resect cystitis glandularis transurethrally and treat obstruction or inflammation.

**Diagram to draw:** cystogram outline of pear-shaped bladder with medially deviated ureters.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pear-shaped bladder with medial ureters; CT shows fat.
> - Cystitis glandularis common; adenocarcinoma risk.
> - Watch for hydronephrosis; diversion if obstruction progresses.

## Cystinuria
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 21 SAQ Sheet, Q3a

### Definition and genetics
- **Autosomal recessive** defect of proximal tubular (and jejunal) reabsorption of the dibasic amino acids **COLA: cystine, ornithine, lysine, arginine**. Only cystine is insoluble, so only cystine forms stones.
- Transporter subunits and ICC (International Cystinuria Consortium) types:

| Gene | Subunit | Chromosome | Type |
|---|---|---|---|
| SLC3A1 | rBAT (heavy) | 2p | A |
| SLC7A9 | b⁰,⁺AT (light) | 19q | B |
| Both | — | 2 and 19 | AB |

- Fractional excretion of cystine rises from about 0.4% to up to 100%. Incidence 1 in 1000 to 1 in 17,000; **1–3% of adult stones, up to 10% of paediatric stones**; males more severe.

### Pathophysiology
- **Pure supersaturation disease**: no specific inhibitor. Solubility is pH dependent: about 300 mg/L at pH 5, 400 mg/L at pH 7, 1000 mg/L at pH 9.
- Coexisting hypocitraturia (commonest), hyperuricosuria or hypercalciuria cause mixed calcium–cystine stones.

### Clinical features
- First stone at mean about 12 years; recurrent, bilateral, staghorn stones; CKD (chronic kidney disease) from repeated surgery (about a quarter of adults have eGFR [estimated glomerular filtration rate] <60).

### Diagnosis
- **Hexagonal crystals** in urine (always abnormal); cyanide–nitroprusside screen; 24-hour urinary cystine (normal <60 mg/day/1.73 m²; homozygotes often >400).
- Stone: yellow, waxy, faintly radiopaque (sulphur); **resists SWL (shock wave lithotripsy)**.

### Management
- **Fluids:** urine output **≥3 L/day**, including at night.
- **Diet:** sodium ≤2300 mg/day; limit animal protein (not in children).
- **Alkalinise to pH 7.0–7.5** with potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day; avoid >7.5 (calcium phosphate stones) and sodium alkali (raises cystine excretion).
- **Thiols** if cystine stays >250 mg/L: thiol–cystine mixed disulfide is soluble.
  - **Tiopronin** (choice): 300 mg twice daily, up to 1200 mg/day.
  - **D-penicillamine:** 250 mg/day, titrated; rash, proteinuria, nephrotic syndrome, pancytopenia.
  - Captopril: weak evidence.
- Monitor blood count, liver tests, urine protein.
- **Surgery:** ureteroscopy or PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) with laser; nephron-sparing approach.

**Diagram to draw:** proximal tubule cell with rBAT/b⁰,⁺AT transporter and hexagonal crystal.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Autosomal recessive; SLC3A1 (type A), SLC7A9 (type B).
> - Hexagonal crystals; target cystine <250 mg/L.
> - Urine ≥3 L, pH 7.0–7.5, then tiopronin.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe Pathogenesis of Congenital Pelvi Ureteric Junction Obstruction its diagnosis and Management. (LAQ)

- Intrinsic adynamic segment (infants) vs crossing vessel (older children, intermittent pain).
- SFU 4 = parenchymal thinning; UTD P3 = abnormal parenchyma.
- Operate for DRF <40%, fall >10%, rising APD, UTI or symptoms.
- Anderson–Hynes ~95%; never divide a crossing vessel.

#### Discuss pathophysiology of renal stones and also describe how to prevent its recurrence (LAQ)

- Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf: where inhibitors and therapy act.
- Randall plaque anchors idiopathic calcium oxalate stones; ductal plugs suggest a secondary cause.
- Urine ≥2.5 L/day; normal calcium, low salt and animal protein (Borghi).
- Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.

#### Flexible Ureterorenoscopy (SAQ)

- 3.6 Fr channel; ≥270° deflection.
- Access sheath lowers pressure but injures ureter in about 1 in 8.
- Laser power ≤10 W ureter, ≤25 W kidney.

#### Distal Renal tubular acidosis. (SAQ)

- Urine pH >6 with acidosis; hypokalaemia; positive UAG.
- Calcium phosphate stones in up to 70%; hypocitraturia key.
- NH₄Cl test: pH fails to fall below 5.5.
- Potassium citrate is the treatment.

#### Radionuclide studies and diuretic renography in PUJ obstruction (SAQ)

- MAG3 is the tracer of choice; hydrate and catheterise.
- Well-tempered: hydrate, catheter, standard diuretic dose and timing.
- Furosemide 0.5 mg/kg (adult maximum 40 mg); 1 mg/kg in children.
- DRF normal 45–55%; T½ <10 normal (not obstructed); 10–20 indeterminate; >20 min obstructed.
- Operate (surgery) for DRF <40%, fall >10% (falling DRF), or poor drainage with symptoms.

#### Prone vs Supine PCNL: Indications, positioning, technique, specific complications, advantages and disadvantages of each. (SAQ)

- Prone: posterior and upper pole access, big working space.
- Supine: airway, ECIRS, gravity drainage, no turning.
- Similar outcomes; choose by anatomy and experience.

#### Metabolic Evaluation in Stone Disease (SAQ)

- Screening for all first-time formers; 24-hour urine for high-risk.
- Targets: volume >2.5 L, oxalate <40 mg, citrate >320 mg.
- Repeat 24-hour urine within 6 months of therapy.

#### What are nanobacteria? Describe their role in urolithiasis. (SAQ)

- Nanobacteria = calcifying nanoparticles; apatite shell; living nature disputed.
- Proposed nidus for apatite and Randall plaque; association, not causation.
- Tetracycline/EDTA therapy unproven.

#### Tear drop bladder (SAQ)

- Tear-drop bladder = bilateral extrinsic compression.
- Trauma: pelvic haematoma; non-trauma: pelvic lipomatosis.
- Lipomatosis: fat on CT, medially deviated ureters, cystitis glandularis.

#### Retrograde endopyelotomy (SAQ)

- Lateral full-thickness incision over a stent.
- Crossing vessel and gross hydronephrosis predict failure.
- Success 70–80%, below pyeloplasty (about 95%).

#### Autotransplantation (SAQ)

- For long ureteric loss when the other kidney is poor or bowel unsuitable.
- Delayed setting only; bench surgery for vascular or tumour cases.
- Unit loss 5–10%; success about 90%.

#### Retroperitoneal fibrosis (SAQ)

- 70% idiopathic; methysergide and lymphoma classic.
- Medially deviated ureters; PET-CT shows activity.
- Prednisone first (Vaglio 2011 beat tamoxifen).
- Bilateral ureterolysis with omental wrap.

#### Management of failed pyeloplasty (SAQ)

- Causes: ischaemia, tension, missed crossing vessel, leak.
- Failed open repair: endourology first; redo pyeloplasty most reliable.
- Ureterocalicostomy for small scarred pelvis; buccal onlay emerging.
- Nephrectomy if split function <15%.

#### Post-obstructive diuresis (SAQ)

- Unilateral about 2%, bilateral about 50%.
- Mechanisms: aquaporin-2 loss, volume expansion, urea osmotic diuresis.
- Replace about 75% of output with 0.45% saline; do not overcorrect.
- Gradual decompression has no role.

#### Stone complexity scores (SAQ)

- Guy's: grades I–IV; complete staghorn or spina bifida is grade IV.
- S.T.O.N.E.: five variables, score 5–13.
- CROES includes centre volume; RUSS is for RIRS.

#### Calyceal diverticulum – classification and management (SAQ)

- Transitional lined, narrow neck; type I calyceal, type II pelvic.
- Stones in up to 40%; delayed CT shows pooling.
- Ureteroscopy or PCNL first; laparoscopy for anterior or failed cases.

#### Pelvic lipomatosis (SAQ)

- Pear-shaped bladder with medial ureters; CT shows fat.
- Cystitis glandularis common; adenocarcinoma risk.
- Watch for hydronephrosis; diversion if obstruction progresses.

#### Cystinuria (SAQ)

- Autosomal recessive; SLC3A1 (type A), SLC7A9 (type B).
- Hexagonal crystals; target cystine <250 mg/L.
- Urine ≥3 L, pH 7.0–7.5, then tiopronin.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Instestinal segments in urology
**Asked as:**
- Instestinal segments in urology — *Summer 2022 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Instestinal segments in urology — *Practice Set 22 SAQ Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)

*Assumed meaning: intestinal segments used in urinary diversion and bladder reconstruction.*

### Introduction
- Bowel is used for conduits, continent pouches, neobladders, augmentation and ureteric replacement. Choice depends on renal function, irradiation, previous surgery, mesenteric length and the reconstruction needed.

### Segments compared

| Segment | Electrolyte disturbance | Advantage | Drawback |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stomach | Hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis | Secretes acid, less mucus; for renal failure, short gut | Haematuria–dysuria syndrome, ulcers |
| Jejunum | Hyponatraemic, hypochloraemic, **hyperkalaemic** acidosis | Outside radiation field | Severe salt loss; avoid |
| Ileum | **Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis** | Mobile, constant supply; default conduit | B12 and bile-salt loss; obstruction ~10% |
| Colon | Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis | Easy antireflux tunnel; transverse colon spared by pelvic radiation | Diarrhoea if ileocaecal valve used |

- **Mechanism (ileum, colon):** NH₄⁺ replaces Na⁺ on the Na⁺/H⁺ exchanger, coupled with Cl⁻/HCO₃⁻ exchange, so ammonium chloride is absorbed. Treat with sodium bicarbonate or citrate (potassium citrate if hypokalaemic); chlorpromazine or nicotinic acid limit chloride transport.

### Other complications
- **Hypokalaemia** (worse with colon); correct potassium before acidosis.
- **Altered sensorium:** hyperammonaemia (drain diversion, lactulose), hypomagnesaemia, drug reabsorption (phenytoin, methotrexate).
- **Osteomalacia:** acidosis, vitamin D resistance, calcium loss; growth retardation in children.
- **Infection, stones** (ileal conduit 10–12%, continent caecal reservoir ~20%), short-gut effects.
- **Cancer:** ureterosigmoidostomy 6–29% after 10–20 years; endoscopy from 5 years.

### Renal function needed for continent diversion
- Creatinine <2 mg/dL; GFR (glomerular filtration rate) >35 mL/min; urine pH ≤5.8 after ammonium chloride load; osmolality ≥600 mOsm/kg.

### Technical principles
- Clear ≤8 cm of mesentery from a segment end; avoid watershed areas (Griffiths, Sudeck points).
- **Also know:** ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery) with no routine mechanical bowel preparation; Wallace (stricture ~3%) and Bricker anastomoses; nipple stoma.

**Diagram to draw:** ileal loop with mesenteric arcade and Wallace anastomosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ileum and colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis (NH₄Cl absorption).
> - Jejunum: hyperkalaemia; stomach: hypochloraemic alkalosis.
> - Continent diversion: GFR >35, pH ≤5.8, osmolality ≥600.

## Diagnosis and management of hemorrhagic cystitis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3d
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3b)

### Definition
- Diffuse inflammation and bleeding of the bladder mucosa, after other sources are excluded.

### Causes
| Cause | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Cyclophosphamide, ifosfamide** | Liver forms **acrolein**, which sloughs urothelium; onset within 48 h |
| **Pelvic radiation** (about 5%; 6 months to 10 years) | **Obliterative endarteritis**, ischaemia, fibrosis |
| **Viral** | BK polyomavirus; adenovirus 11, 35; after transplant |
| Others | Bacteria; busulfan, thiotepa; penicillins; amyloidosis |

### Diagnosis
- History: drugs, radiotherapy, transplant, anticoagulants. Severity: microscopic, gross, clots, clot retention.
- Blood count, coagulation, renal function, urine culture, viral PCR (polymerase chain reaction) if transplanted.
- **Exclude cancer:** cytology, cystoscopy with biopsy, upper tract imaging (CT urography).

### Prevention
- **Mesna** inactivates acrolein; hyperhydration, forced diuresis.

### Management (escalate by severity)
```
Resuscitate, transfuse, correct coagulopathy, treat infection
→ 3-way catheter + CBI (continuous bladder irrigation)
→ clot evacuation, fulguration, biopsy under anaesthesia
→ intravesical alum 1%
→ bleeding after 48 h: hyperbaric oxygen (+ nephrostomies)
→ formalin 1%, then 4% (cystogram first)
→ cystectomy + conduit diversion
(Unstable at any stage: internal iliac embolisation)
```

| Agent | Key point |
|---|---|
| **Alum 1%** | First line; no anaesthesia; avoid in renal failure (aluminium toxicity) |
| Aminocaproic acid | Antifibrinolytic CBI; clot-free bladder only |
| Prostaglandins, silver nitrate, hyaluronate | Second-line options |
| **Formalin 1–4%** | 80–90% success; under anaesthesia, instilled by gravity for 10–15 min; **cystogram to exclude reflux** (occlude ureters if present); risks: contracted bladder, ureteric stricture |

- **HBOT** (hyperbaric oxygen therapy): 100% oxygen at 2–3 atmospheres, 30–40 sessions; raises tissue oxygen and drives neovascularisation; 80–90% response, but many relapse; **RICH-ART** trial improved urinary scores in radiation cystitis. Nephrostomies divert urokinase-rich urine if bleeding continues.
- **Embolisation:** selective anterior division of internal iliac (posterior division causes gluteal pain); initial control about 90%.
- **Cystectomy** last; remove bladder (pyocystis).
- **Viral:** supportive (hydration, irrigation); cidofovir anecdotal.
- **Also know:** tranexamic acid and laser ablation are unproven.

**Diagram to draw:** escalation ladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Acrolein (mesna); radiation endarteritis; BK virus.
> - Exclude bladder cancer.
> - Alum first; formalin after cystogram; HBOT for radiation.

## BCG therapy in Bladder Cancer
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3f
(intravesical therapy also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin), a live attenuated mycobacterium (Morales, 1976), is the only intravesical agent that reduces both **recurrence and progression** of NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer).

### Mechanism
- BCG binds **fibronectin** on the urothelium; cytokines (IFN-γ [interferon-gamma], IL-2 [interleukin-2] and TNF-α [tumour necrosis factor-alpha]) and immune cells follow.
- A **Th1 (T-helper 1)** response gives cell-mediated tumour kill. It needs repeated stimulation, which is why **maintenance** is essential.

### Indications
- **High-risk NMIBC:** HG (high-grade) T1, large/multifocal/recurrent HG Ta, **CIS (carcinoma in situ)** (initial complete response up to 84%).
- **Intermediate risk:** option. Not for low risk.

### Technique and schedule
- Start **2–4 weeks after TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)**; urinalysis first; atraumatic catheter; retain **1–2 hours**.
- **Induction:** weekly × 6.
- **Maintenance (SWOG [Southwest Oncology Group], Lamm 2000):** weekly × 3 at 3 and 6 months, then every 6 months to **3 years** (recurrence-free survival 76.8 vs 35.7 months).
- **EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30962:** intermediate risk, full dose for 1 year; high risk, full dose for 3 years. **NIMBUS:** reduced frequency was inferior.

### Contraindications
- **Absolute:** immediately after TURBT, gross haematuria, traumatic catheterisation (all risk **intravasation**); previous BCG sepsis; total incontinence; immunosuppression (now debated).
- **Relative:** UTI (urinary tract infection), liver disease, prior TB (tuberculosis).

### Toxicity

| Grade | Features | Management |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Cystitis, fever <38.5 °C, <48 h | Symptomatic |
| 2 | Severe or >48 h | Isoniazid + rifampicin; resume at ½–⅓ dose |
| 3 | Sepsis, organ involvement | Stop BCG; add ethambutol; steroids only with cover |
- BCG is resistant to **pyrazinamide**.

### BCG failure
- **BCG-unresponsive:** persistent or recurrent CIS or HG Ta/T1 despite **adequate BCG** (≥5 of 6 induction + ≥2 of 3 maintenance), or HG relapse within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS). **Timely radical cystectomy** is standard.
- **If unfit or unwilling:** pembrolizumab, nadofaragene firadenovec, N-803 + BCG, gemcitabine–docetaxel.
- **Recent:** PD-1/PD-L1 (programmed death-1/ligand-1) inhibitor + BCG in BCG-naive high risk improved event-free survival (CREST, POTOMAC); TAR-200 gemcitabine-releasing device.

**Diagram to draw:** SWOG schedule timeline.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Only BCG reduces progression; maintenance is essential.
> - Never immediately after TURBT; start at 2–4 weeks.
> - Unresponsive: timely cystectomy.

## Cancer in augmented bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 20 SAQ Sheet, Q3f

### Introduction
- Bladder augmentation (enterocystoplasty) is mainly for neurogenic bladder. **Earliest reported tumour 4 years after surgery**, usually after decades; tumours are often **aggressive and advanced at diagnosis**.
- German registry (Kälble, 2011): secondary malignancy after cystoplasty **1.6%**, versus ureterosigmoidostomy 2.6% and ileal conduit 0.02%.

### Pathology
- **Adenocarcinoma** commonest, usually near the **enterovesical anastomosis**; also urothelial carcinoma (aggressive, often metastatic), signet-ring and other types.
- **Nephrogenic adenoma**: benign, probably the commonest tumour after cystoplasty.

### Pathogenesis
- **N-nitroso compounds** from bacteria in stagnant, infected urine (probably enhancers).
- Chronic inflammation at the anastomosis releases growth factors that drive proliferation; perianastomotic urothelium is genetically unstable.
- The underlying disease may itself carry risk: bladder cancer in spina bifida is about 2–4%, mostly stage III or IV, and non-gastric augmentation did not appear to increase that risk.
- Ureterosigmoidostomy is the extreme model: urothelium beside colonic mucosa bathed in faeces; mean latency about 26 years.

### Risk factors
- **Gastrocystoplasty** (highest among augments), **exstrophy**, immunosuppression after transplant, smoking, chronic infection, long latency.

### Clinical features
- **Gross haematuria**, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), pelvic pain, new incontinence, mass. Neurogenic patients may notice symptoms late.

### Surveillance
- Chapters differ: Campbell bladder-surgery chapter advises **endoscopy yearly from 10 years**; paediatric chapter: no consensus, not shown cost-effective (Mayo group: unnecessary). Higher-risk groups benefit most.
- Investigate any haematuria promptly: cystoscopy with biopsy, cytology, CT (computed tomography) urography.
- **Prevention:** regular clean intermittent catheterisation, daily bladder irrigation (less stasis, mucus and stones), treat urea-splitting infection, stop smoking, lifelong transitional follow-up.

### Management
- **Radical cystectomy with excision of the augment** and urinary diversion (conduit or continent pouch), with lymphadenectomy.
- Chemotherapy as for histology; prognosis poor because of late stage.
- **Also know:** stones, perforation, metabolic acidosis also occur after augmentation.

**Diagram to draw:** clam ileocystoplasty with tumour at the enterovesical suture line.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Adenocarcinoma at the anastomosis; earliest at 4 years.
> - Gastric segment, exstrophy, immunosuppression: highest risk.
> - Haematuria needs cystoscopy; treatment is cystectomy.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Instestinal segments in urology (SAQ)

- Ileum and colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis (NH₄Cl absorption).
- Jejunum: hyperkalaemia; stomach: hypochloraemic alkalosis.
- Continent diversion: GFR >35, pH ≤5.8, osmolality ≥600.

#### Diagnosis and management of hemorrhagic cystitis (SAQ)

- Acrolein (mesna); radiation endarteritis; BK virus.
- Exclude bladder cancer.
- Alum first; formalin after cystogram; HBOT for radiation.

#### BCG therapy in Bladder Cancer (SAQ)

- Only BCG reduces progression; maintenance is essential.
- Never immediately after TURBT; start at 2–4 weeks.
- Unresponsive: timely cystectomy.

#### Cancer in augmented bladder (SAQ)

- Adenocarcinoma at the anastomosis; earliest at 4 years.
- Gastric segment, exstrophy, immunosuppression: highest risk.
- Haematuria needs cystoscopy; treatment is cystectomy.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Embryology and anatomy of kidney; nephron-sparing surgery in small renal mass and renal malignancy
**Asked as:**
- Discuss anatomy of kidney. Describe role of nephron sparing surgery in small renal mass. — *Summer 2017 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss anatomy of kidney. Describe role of nephron sparing nephrectomy in small renal mass. — *Practice Set 1 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Describe embryology and anatomy of kidney.. Describe role of nephron sparing surgery in a small renal mass. — *Practice Set 13 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Nepron Sparing Surgery in renal malignancy. — *Winter 2025 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q1; NSS also in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q1 and 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q2)
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q1 and Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q1; NSS techniques: see Practice Set 13 Paper 2, Q1)
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q1; 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q1)

*"Nephron sparing nephrectomy" is read as nephron-sparing surgery (NSS): partial nephrectomy and ablation.*
*"Nepron" is read as nephron.*

### Embryology
- Three successive kidneys form from **intermediate mesoderm**, in cranial-to-caudal order:

| Kidney | Time and site | Fate |
|---|---|---|
| Pronephros | Week 3–4, cervical | Non-functional; regresses |
| Mesonephros | Week 4, thoracolumbar | Functions briefly; tubules become efferent ductules; **mesonephric (Wolffian) duct** persists |
| **Metanephros** | Week 5, sacral | **Definitive kidney** |

- **Reciprocal induction:** the **ureteric bud (UB)** grows from the caudal Wolffian duct (about week 5) into the **metanephric blastema**.
  - UB branches into ureter, pelvis, calyces and collecting ducts.
  - Blastema, induced by UB tips, forms nephrons (glomerulus to distal tubule).
- **Molecular control:** GDNF (glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor) from the blastema acts on RET on the UB; SLIT2/ROBO2 restricts GDNF to one bud. Also WT1, PAX2.
- Urine production is established by 14 weeks; nephrogenesis ends before birth.
- **Ascent and rotation (weeks 6–9):** the kidney rises from the pelvis to the lumbar region and rotates 90° so the hilum faces medially. It takes successively higher arteries; persistent lower ones become accessory arteries.
- **Clinical correlation:** no UB gives agenesis; abnormal induction gives MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney); failed ascent gives ectopia; fused lower poles trapped by the IMA (inferior mesenteric artery) give horseshoe kidney; also malrotation, duplication.

### Surgical anatomy of the kidney
- **Size and position:** about 11 cm, 125–170 g; T12–L3, lies on (the cone-shaped) psoas, so the upper pole is more medial and posterior, and the kidney is angled 30–50° behind the coronal plane. The right kidney is 1–2 cm lower (liver).
- **Relations:**

| Aspect | Right | Left |
|---|---|---|
| Superior | Liver, adrenal, 12th rib | Spleen, stomach, adrenal, 11th–12th ribs |
| Medial/anterior | Duodenum (2nd part), hepatic flexure | Pancreatic tail with splenic vessels, splenic flexure, jejunum |
| Posterior (both) | Diaphragm, psoas, quadratus lumborum, subcostal nerve | Same |

- **Coverings (inside out):** true capsule, perirenal fat, **Gerota fascia**, pararenal fat. Gerota fascia is closed above and laterally but open below. So it contains tumour and abscess (collections), but (yet) fluid can track (tracks) into the pelvis.
- **Hilum (anterior to posterior, front to back):** renal vein, artery, pelvis, then the posterior segmental artery.
- **Vascular anatomy (the basis of partial nephrectomy)**
  - **Arteries:** renal artery from the aorta at L1–L2; the right passes behind the IVC (inferior vena cava).
  - **Five segmental end arteries**: posterior (first branch, behind the pelvis) plus four from the anterior division (apical, upper, middle, lower). With no anastomoses, occlusion causes segmental infarction, but each segment can be clamped or resected separately. Then interlobar (columns of Bertin), arcuate, interlobular arteries.
  - **Accessory arteries** in 25–28%, end arteries too (see Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3c).
  - **Brödel line:** an avascular plane between anterior (two-thirds) and posterior (one-third) territories, just behind the lateral border. Incision here bleeds least (anatrophic nephrolithotomy).
  - **Veins anastomose freely**, so a segmental vein can be ligated safely. The long left renal vein receives adrenal, gonadal and lumbar veins.
- **Parenchyma / internal structure:** cortex and 8–18 pyramids; 0.4–1.2 million nephrons per kidney.
- **Collecting system:** papilla, minor calyces (7–9), major calyces, pelvis. Puncture through the **calyceal fornix**, not the infundibulum (Sampaio). (Collecting system: see Q3d.)
- **Also know:** lymph drains to para-aortic (left) or paracaval/interaortocaval nodes (right); sympathetic T8–L1.

**Diagram to draw:** segmental arteries with the Brödel line on a transverse kidney section.

### Small renal mass (SRM)
- **Definition:** an enhancing renal mass **≤4 cm (cT1a)**, usually (mostly) incidental.
- **Biology:** 20–30% of masses ≤4 cm and about 40% ≤2 cm are benign (oncocytoma, fat-poor angiomyolipoma); most cancers are indolent (under/<2% of the cancers metastasise). About 20% benign, 60% indolent, 20% potentially aggressive.

### Definition and rationale of NSS / why spare nephrons
- NSS (nephron-sparing surgery) is complete local removal of the tumour with **maximal preservation of functioning parenchyma**. PN (partial nephrectomy) is the standard form; thermal ablation is the non-excisional form.
- **Why:** RN (radical nephrectomy) causes CKD (chronic kidney disease), linked to (which raises) cardiovascular events and death. PN gives equal cancer control for small masses (cT1a) with better function and keeps about **90%** of global function (RN 65–70%).

### Work-up / preoperative evaluation
- Multiphase CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) with 3D (three-dimensional) reconstruction; chest imaging; eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) and proteinuria (CKD [chronic kidney disease] stage).
- **Renal mass biopsy (RMB)** if (when) it may change management (non-diagnostic about 14%). Biopsy when it changes management; genetic counselling if age ≤46, multifocal or bilateral.
- **Nephrometry scores** predict complexity and complications:

| R.E.N.A.L. component | Scored as |
|---|---|
| **R**adius | ≤4, 4–7, ≥7 cm |
| **E**xophytic/endophytic | ≥50% exophytic, <50%, entirely endophytic |
| **N**earness to collecting system | ≥7, 4–7, ≤4 mm |
| **A**nterior/posterior | Descriptor |
| **L**ocation to polar lines | Polar, crossing, central |
- Total: low 4–6, moderate 7–9, high 10–12 complexity. **PADUA** and C-index are alternatives.

### Indications for nephron-sparing surgery

| Category | Situations |
|---|---|
| **Imperative** | Solitary (functioning) kidney, bilateral tumours (bilateral RCC (renal cell carcinoma)), RN would mean dialysis |
| **Relative** | Contralateral kidney threatened: stone, stones, (renal artery) stenosis, hydronephrosis, diabetes, severe hypertension; hereditary RCC |
| **Elective** | **cT1a (≤4 cm)** with a normal contralateral kidney: **standard of care** |

- Also used for selected cT1b–T2, complex or hilar tumours when function matters.
- **Relative contraindications:** **<20% of nephron mass retained**; pedicle encasement; collecting-system invasion; **venous tumour thrombus**; cT4; cN1.

### Choosing the option (AUA (American Urological Association) 2021)

| Option | Role |
|---|---|
| **Active surveillance** | <2 cm or predominantly cystic; elderly, comorbid (frail patients), life expectancy <5 years. Imaging every 3–6 months. Intervene if >3 cm or growth >5 mm a year (DISSRM registry data: almost no metastasis/metastases) |
| **Thermal ablation** (cryoablation, radiofrequency) | cT1a **<3 cm** (masses <3 cm), percutaneous, biopsy first. Higher local recurrence (than PN), salvaged by repeat ablation |
| **Partial nephrectomy** | **Preferred / prioritised (prioritise PN) for cT1a** needing treatment. Imperative: solitary kidney, bilateral tumours, CKD or proteinuria; also familial RCC (renal cell carcinoma), young patients |
| **Radical nephrectomy** | RN is preferred only if **all three** apply: high complexity; no CKD or proteinuria; normal contralateral kidney with new (post-RN) eGFR/GFR likely **>45** after RN. Otherwise PN |

- **PN principles:** negative margin (width does not matter); maximise preserved parenchyma; avoid long warm ischaemia; enucleation for familial or multifocal tumours. Approaches, steps and complications: see Practice Set 13 Paper 2, Q1.

### Techniques of PN
**Approach:** open (flank extraperitoneal or transperitoneal), laparoscopic, or robotic **RAPN (robot-assisted PN)**: transperitoneal for anterior and retroperitoneal for posterior tumours; equal oncology.

**Steps**
1. **Early hilar control** of artery, vein and accessory vessels, even if off-clamp is planned (selective or off-clamp possible).
2. Expose / defat the kidney; **intraoperative ultrasound** maps (marks) the tumour borders and depth.
3. Clamp the artery (± vein). Use **ice slush** if ischaemia is expected to be **≥25–30 minutes** (solitary kidney, CKD).
4. Excise with a thin rim of normal parenchyma (or enucleate). **A negative margin matters (is the priority); its width does not.**
5. **Two-layer renorrhaphy** (sliding clip): inner layer closes vessels and collecting system; outer capsular layer over a haemostatic bolster.
6. Unclamp; check haemostasis; close Gerota fascia; drain.

**Excision options**

| | Standard (wedge) resection | Enucleation |
|---|---|---|
| Plane | Through normal parenchyma, thin rim | Along the pseudocapsule |
| Advantage | Fewer positive margins | Less bleeding, maximal parenchyma |
| Use | Default | Hereditary RCC (VHL [von Hippel–Lindau]), familial, multifocal (disease), severe CKD |
- Polar resection or heminephrectomy for large polar tumours.

**Vascular control (ischaemia)**
- Traditional warm ischaemia (traditionally) **<25–30 minutes** (min); ice slush if longer is expected. Function depends mainly on the **quantity of parenchyma preserved**; ischaemia is secondary within accepted limits.
- **Off-clamp, selective or early unclamping:** randomised trials show (gave) no long-term (lasting) eGFR advantage / gain. Hypothermia and mannitol give no benefit with a normal contralateral kidney.
- **Trifecta:** negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min (minutes). RAPN: shorter ischaemia than laparoscopy, less blood loss than open.

### Ablative NSS / other nephron-sparing options
- **Cryoablation** (double freeze–thaw), **RFA (radiofrequency ablation)**, **MWA (microwave ablation)**; mostly percutaneous.
- AUA: alternative for **cT1a <3 cm**, especially elderly or comorbid; biopsy before or at ablation. Recurrence is higher than PN but treatable (salvaged) by repeat ablation.
- **Also know:** SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy) for non-surgical candidates; active surveillance for frail patients.

### Special situations
- **Hereditary RCC:** observe (treat) until the largest tumour is **3 cm**, then enucleate (except HLRCC [hereditary leiomyomatosis and RCC]: wide excision). (von Hippel–Lindau: treat at 3 cm, enucleation.)
- **Bilateral tumours:** bilateral PN, usually staged; if one side needs RN, do the PN first.
- **Solitary kidney:** temporary dialysis about 3.5%; local recurrence 3–5%.
- **Also know:** neoadjuvant TKI (tyrosine kinase inhibitor) downsizing in imperative cases; bilateral Wilms tumour; kidney-sparing upper-tract urothelial cancer surgery.

### Complications of PN
- **Urine leak** (drain, stent); **haemorrhage / bleeding, pseudoaneurysm or AV (arteriovenous) fistula** (**delayed haematuria**: angioembolisation); acute kidney injury.
- **Positive margin 0–7%:** surveillance, not reoperation.
- **Hyperfiltration injury** if >50% of a solitary kidney is removed (lost): (hyperfiltration) proteinuria, FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis); ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor.
- **Follow-up:** AUA risk-based imaging; eGFR and urine protein at every visit.

### Outcomes and evidence
- Elective PN: **local recurrence 1–2%**, cancer-free survival >90%; imperative 3–5%.
- **EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30904 (2011):** RN vs PN (PN versus RN) for ≤5 cm tumours: PN better function, similar oncology (cancer control); overall survival slightly better with (favoured) RN — 10-year overall survival 81% RN vs 76% PN, but the trial is flawed.
- **DISSRM (Delayed Intervention and Surveillance for Small Renal Masses) registry:** surveillance almost never led to metastasis; about 24% had delayed intervention.

### Recent advances
- 3D (three-dimensional) models (randomised: shorter ischaemia, less blood loss); segmental clamping; ICG (indocyanine green) fluorescence for selective clamping; single-port RAPN.

**Diagram to draw:** clamped renal artery, tumour excised with a rim, two-layer renorrhaphy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ureteric bud gives collecting system; blastema gives nephrons (GDNF–RET).
> - Five segmental end arteries; veins anastomose; Brödel line avascular; puncture at the fornix.
> - SRM ≤4 cm: 20–30% benign; PN is standard for cT1a, ablation <3 cm, surveillance <2 cm.
> - Imperative, relative, elective.
> - R.E.N.A.L. 4–6 / 7–9 / 10–12; trifecta includes warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
> - AUA: RN only if complex + no CKD + eGFR/GFR likely >45.
> - Preserved parenchyma, not clamp time, governs function.
> - EORTC 30904: PN better function, similar cancer control.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Role of renal nephrometry in management of RCC.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3f

### Definition
- **Nephrometry scores** grade the anatomical complexity of a renal mass on CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging), to standardise RCC (renal cell carcinoma) reporting and plan treatment.

### R.E.N.A.L. score (Kutikov and Uzzo, 2009)
| Component | 1 point | 2 points | 3 points |
|---|---|---|---|
| **R**adius (maximum diameter) | ≤4 cm | >4 to <7 cm | ≥7 cm |
| **E**xophytic/endophytic | ≥50% exophytic | <50% exophytic | Entirely endophytic |
| **N**earness to collecting system or sinus | ≥7 mm | >4 to <7 mm | ≤4 mm |
| **A**nterior/posterior | Suffix a, p or x (no points) | | |
| **L**ocation to polar lines | Entirely above or below | Crosses a polar line | >50% across, crosses midline, or entirely between |
- Suffix "h": touches main renal vessels. **Total 4–12:** low 4–6, moderate 7–9, high 10–12.

### PADUA (Preoperative Aspects and Dimensions Used for an Anatomical classification) score (Ficarra, 2009)
- Polar location, exophytic rate, renal rim (lateral or medial), renal sinus, collecting system and size, plus anterior/posterior face. **Total 6–14:** 6–7 low, 8–9 intermediate, ≥10 high risk.

### C-index (centrality index)
- Distance from tumour centre to kidney centre ÷ tumour radius. **Lower value = more central, more complex.**

### Role in management
| Decision | Use |
|---|---|
| **Partial vs radical nephrectomy** | Feasibility and risk of PN (partial nephrectomy) vs RN (radical nephrectomy) |
| **Approach** | Open vs minimally invasive; hilar clamping |
| **Predict outcomes** | Warm ischaemia, blood loss, complications, conversion, collecting-system entry; urine leak (R and N components) |
| **Function** | Parenchymal loss, eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) decline |
| **Ablation** | Cryoablation complications rise with complexity |
| **Pathology** | Higher scores: malignancy, higher grade |

- **AUA (American Urological Association) 2021:** with high oncological potential, prefer RN only if PN would be challenging even for experts, there is no CKD (chronic kidney disease) or proteinuria, and new baseline eGFR will likely exceed 45.

### Limitations
- Interobserver variation; ignores patient factors and surgeon experience.

**Also know:** ABC (arterial based complexity), DAP (diameter–axial–polar) scores; **MAP (Mayo Adhesive Probability) score** for adherent perinephric fat.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with polar lines and the five R.E.N.A.L. components.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RENAL 4–12: low 4–6, moderate 7–9, high 10–12.
> - PADUA 6–14; C-index lower = more central.
> - Predicts PN feasibility, ischaemia time and complications.
> - MAP score: adherent perinephric fat.

---

# Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe anatomy of arenal glands AND steps in production of adrenal hormones & perioperative management of a patient of pheochromocytoma
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q1
(pre-operative management also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

*Assumed meaning: "arenal" = adrenal glands.*

### A. Anatomy
**Gross**
- Paired retroperitoneal glands on the upper renal poles (11th–12th ribs), inside Gerota fascia, separated from the kidney by thin connective tissue. Yellow-orange, 4–6 × 2–3 cm, about 5 g each.

| Feature | Right | Left |
|---|---|---|
| Shape, level | Triangular; higher | Crescentic; lower (to L1) |
| Relations | Liver anteriorly, duodenum anteromedially, IVC (inferior vena cava) medially, diaphragmatic crus behind | Pancreas body and splenic vessels anteriorly, aorta medially, crus behind |
| Vein | **Short (<1 cm), into posterior IVC**: tears easily | **Longer, into left renal vein**, often joined by inferior phrenic vein |

**Blood supply**
- **"Many arteries in, one vein out."** Superior adrenal (from inferior phrenic), middle (aorta), inferior (renal artery) arteries break into 10–50 small branches.
- Cortical sinusoids drain into the medulla, so cortisol-rich blood reaches the chromaffin cells. **Cortisol induces PNMT (phenylethanolamine-N-methyltransferase)**, which is why the medulla makes adrenaline.

**Nerves:** preganglionic sympathetic fibres (T11–L2, via splanchnic nerves, no synapse in coeliac ganglion) end directly on chromaffin cells. The medulla is a modified sympathetic ganglion.

**Embryology:** cortex from coelomic mesoderm (adrenogonadal ridge, weeks 3–4); medulla from neural crest. Hence adrenals stay orthotopic in renal agenesis, and adrenal rests occur along the path of gonadal descent.

**Histology (zones from outside in: glomerulosa, fasciculata, reticularis = "salt, sugar, sex")**
| Zone | Share | Product | Main control |
|---|---|---|---|
| Glomerulosa | ~15% of cortex | Aldosterone | Angiotensin II, potassium |
| Fasciculata | ~80% | Cortisol (up to 20 mg/day) | ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) |
| Reticularis | 5–7% | DHEA (dehydroepiandrosterone), DHEA-S, androstenedione | ACTH |
| Medulla | ~10% of gland | Adrenaline 80%, noradrenaline 19%, dopamine 1% | Sympathetic nerves |

### B. Steps in hormone production
**1. Steroids (cortex)**
- Cholesterol (mainly from LDL [low-density lipoprotein]) is carried into mitochondria by **StAR (steroidogenic acute regulatory protein)**: the rate-limiting step, driven by ACTH.
- **Side-chain cleavage enzyme (CYP11A1)** converts it to **pregnenolone**. The enzymes present in each zone then decide the product:
```
Pregnenolone -3βHSD-> Progesterone -21OH-> 11-deoxycorticosterone -11βOH-> Corticosterone -aldosterone synthase (CYP11B2)-> ALDOSTERONE (glomerulosa only)
Pregnenolone -17αOH-> 17-OH pregnenolone/progesterone -21OH-> 11-deoxycortisol -11βOH-> CORTISOL (fasciculata)
17-OH pregnenolone -17,20-lyase-> DHEA -> androstenedione (reticularis)
```
- 3βHSD (3β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase). Steroids are not stored; they diffuse out and act on intracellular receptors.
- **Regulation:** CRH (corticotropin-releasing hormone) → ACTH → cortisol, with negative feedback and morning peak; RAAS (renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system) and potassium for aldosterone.
- **Clinical link:** 21-hydroxylase deficiency is the commonest cause of CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia).

**2. Catecholamines (medulla)**
```
Tyrosine -tyrosine hydroxylase (rate-limiting)-> L-DOPA -decarboxylase-> Dopamine
-dopamine β-hydroxylase-> Noradrenaline -PNMT (cortisol-induced)-> Adrenaline
```
- Stored in vesicles, released by exocytosis. **COMT (catechol-O-methyltransferase)** inside chromaffin cells forms **metanephrine and normetanephrine** continuously, which is why metanephrines are the best test. MAO (monoamine oxidase) and liver form VMA (vanillylmandelic acid).

### C. Perioperative management of phaeochromocytoma
**Why:** handling the tumour causes catecholamine surges (crisis, arrhythmia); vein ligation causes sudden withdrawal (hypotension, hypoglycaemia). Mortality fell from up to 50% to <3% with blockade.

**Pre-operative (7–14 days)**
- Confirm with plasma free or urinary fractionated metanephrines; CT (computed tomography) or MRI; functional imaging (⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE PET [positron emission tomography], MIBG [metaiodobenzylguanidine]) if extra-adrenal or hereditary; **never biopsy**; genetic testing (about one-third hereditary).
- ECG (electrocardiogram) and echocardiography for catecholamine cardiomyopathy.

| Drug | Use |
|---|---|
| **Phenoxybenzamine** (irreversible non-selective α) | First line: 10 mg twice daily, increased every 2–3 days; about 1 mg/kg total |
| Doxazosin, prazosin (selective α1) | Fewer side effects |
| Calcium channel blockers | Alternative strategy or add-on |
| β1-blocker (atenolol, metoprolol) | **Only after α-blockade**, for tachycardia |
| Metyrosine | Blocks tyrosine hydroxylase; add-on; sedation, extrapyramidal effects |

- **Never β before α:** losing β2 vasodilatation leaves α-vasoconstriction unopposed: crisis.
- **Target:** seated BP 120–130/80 mmHg with mild postural drop.
- **Volume expansion:** high-salt diet and fluids; 1–2 L IV (intravenous) fluid the night before. Last phenoxybenzamine dose the night before surgery.

**Intra-operative**
- Arterial and central lines; laparoscopic adrenalectomy (open for large or invasive tumours); minimal handling; **early adrenal vein ligation** after warning the anaesthetist.
- Surges: nitroprusside, nicardipine, phentolamine. Tachyarrhythmia: esmolol, lidocaine. Post-ligation hypotension: fluids ± vasopressors.
- Cortical-sparing adrenalectomy in MEN2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2) and VHL (von Hippel–Lindau).

**Post-operative**
- HDU (high dependency unit): hypotension, **rebound hypoglycaemia** (insulin no longer suppressed), steroid cover if bilateral.
- Metanephrines after surgery, then **yearly for life** (recurrence up to 16%).

**Diagram to draw:** posterior view of both adrenals with three arteries and the two veins.

> **Key points to remember:**
