> - Right vein short into IVC; left vein into left renal vein.
> - StAR is rate-limiting for steroids; tyrosine hydroxylase for catecholamines.
> - Cortisol induces PNMT; COMT forms metanephrines inside the tumour.
> - α-blockade 7–14 days, β only after α, volume expansion; watch glucose.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe anatomy of arenal glands AND steps in production of adrenal hormones & perioperative management of a patient of pheochromocytoma (LAQ)

- Right vein short into IVC; left vein into left renal vein.
- StAR is rate-limiting for steroids; tyrosine hydroxylase for catecholamines.
- Cortisol induces PNMT; COMT forms metanephrines inside the tumour.
- α-blockade 7–14 days, β only after α, volume expansion; watch glucose.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe in details basic patho-physiology of urine production and transport
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q1

### Introduction
- Urine is formed by filtration, reabsorption, secretion and concentration, then propelled to a low-pressure bladder.

### A. Functional anatomy
- Glomerular barrier (fenestrated endothelium, basement membrane, podocytes) restricts by size and negative charge, so albumin is excluded.
- **Juxtamedullary nephrons** (long loops) build the medullary gradient.

### B. Renal blood flow (RBF) and autoregulation
- RBF ~25% of cardiac output. The medulla is hypoxic (10–20 mmHg), endangering the thick ascending limb and S3 segment.
- **Autoregulation** keeps RBF and GFR (glomerular filtration rate) constant at mean arterial pressure 80–180 mmHg:
  - **Myogenic:** the afferent arteriole constricts when stretched and dilates (prostaglandins) when pressure falls; NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) abolish this.
  - **Tubuloglomerular feedback:** low NaCl at the macula densa releases renin; angiotensin II constricts the efferent arteriole. Angiotensin blockade abolishes this.

### C. Glomerular filtration
- GFR ~125 mL/min (180 L/day); filtration fraction ~20%.
- **Net filtration pressure** ≈ glomerular hydrostatic 60 – Bowman's space 18 – oncotic 32 = **~10 mmHg**.
- Efferent constriction lowers RBF but **raises** GFR; afferent constriction lowers both. Obstruction raises Bowman's space pressure, so GFR falls.
- **Also know:** GFR estimated from creatinine or cystatin C (race-free 2021 equations).

### D. Tubular handling
| Segment | Key transporter | Handling | Drug/disease link |
|---|---|---|---|
| Proximal tubule | Na⁺/H⁺ exchanger, SGLT2 (sodium–glucose cotransporter 2) | ~**65%** Na⁺ and water (iso-osmotic); all glucose; most bicarbonate and phosphate; secretes drugs | SGLT2 inhibitors |
| Thin descending limb | Aquaporin-1 | Water out, solute stays: fluid concentrates | – |
| Thick ascending limb | NKCC2 (Na⁺-K⁺-2Cl⁻) | ~**25%** NaCl; water-impermeable "diluting segment" | Loop diuretics |
| Distal convoluted tubule | NCC (Na⁺-Cl⁻ cotransporter) | 5–7% Na⁺; Ca²⁺ (parathyroid hormone) | Thiazides lower urinary Ca²⁺ |
| Collecting duct | ENaC (epithelial Na⁺ channel), AQP2 | Principal cells: Na⁺/K⁺ (aldosterone), water (ADH); intercalated cells: acid | Amiloride, spironolactone; distal RTA (renal tubular acidosis) |

### E. Concentration
- **Countercurrent multiplication:** ascending limbs absorb NaCl without water, raising medullary osmolality from 300 to ~**1200 mOsm/kg**; urea recycling adds about half. Vasa recta exchange without washing out the gradient.
- **Antidiuretic hormone (ADH)** is released by high osmolality or low volume. Signal chain: V2 receptor → cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate) → protein kinase A → aquaporin-2 (AQP2) inserted into the apical membrane → water reabsorbed. Concentration needs both a hypertonic medulla and AQP2; urine osmolality ranges ~50–1200 mOsm/kg.

| Hormone | Site | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Angiotensin II, aldosterone | Efferent arteriole, proximal tubule; collecting duct | Na⁺ retention, K⁺/H⁺ loss, GFR maintained |
| Atrial natriuretic peptide | Afferent arteriole, collecting duct | Natriuresis, lowers renin |
| Parathyroid hormone | Proximal and distal tubule | Phosphaturia, Ca²⁺ retention |

### F. Ureteric transport
- **Pacemaker:** atypical smooth muscle cells at the minor calyces/pelvicalyceal border.
- Peristalsis is **myogenic**: the excised or denervated ureter keeps contracting. Nerves (T11–L1, S2–4) carry only pain.
- Calcium entry triggers action potentials spread by gap junctions; calcium–calmodulin then activates myosin light-chain kinase.
- Walls coapt behind each bolus: 2–6 waves/min at 20–80 cmH₂O. Very high flow makes boluses coalesce, dilating the ureter **without obstruction**.
- Ureterovesical tunnel length:diameter 5:1 prevents reflux; bladder pressure >40 cmH₂O stops efflux.

| Factor | Effect on transport |
|---|---|
| Obstruction | Acute: stronger contractions; chronic: dilatation, lost coaptation |
| Infection | Gram-negative endotoxin inhibits peristalsis (dilatation without obstruction) |
| Pregnancy | Right > left dilatation: compression at pelvic brim, progesterone |
| Drugs | α1-blockers and calcium-channel blockers relax (medical expulsive therapy); NSAIDs lower pelvic pressure in colic |

### G. Bladder storage and voiding
- **Storage** below ~10 cmH₂O: compliance, sympathetic β3 detrusor relaxation, α1 bladder-neck and pudendal sphincter tone.
- **Voiding:** pelvic parasympathetic (S2–4) acetylcholine on M3 receptors contracts the detrusor; nitric oxide relaxes the urethra.

### H. Pathophysiology of obstruction (unilateral: triphasic)
```
Phase 1 (0–1.5 h): RBF up,   pressure up    (afferent dilatation:
                                             prostaglandins, nitric oxide)
Phase 2 (1.5–5 h): RBF down, pressure up    (efferent constriction)
Phase 3 (>5 h):    RBF down, pressure down  (afferent constriction: angiotensin II,
                                             thromboxane A2, endothelin)
```
- Ischaemic injury starts **within 2 hours** of complete block.
- **Tubules:** down-regulated aquaporins and Na⁺ transporters cause concentrating defect, salt wasting and distal RTA.
- **Fibrosis** (final path to failure): stretch, inflammation, TGF-β (transforming growth factor-β), angiotensin II, microRNAs.
- **Bilateral obstruction or solitary kidney:** pressure stays high longer and natriuretic peptides and urea accumulate, so **post-obstructive diuresis** follows relief in ~50% (vs ~2% unilateral).
- **Clinical:** hypertension (~77% bilateral, ~20% unilateral); contralateral hypertrophy; pyelorenal backflow acts as a pop-off; forniceal rupture causes urinoma.
- **Also know:** immature kidneys fail to develop; outlet obstruction causes detrusor hypertrophy then decompensation; diuretic MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram (half-time >40 min = obstructed); Whitaker test (>15–20 cmH₂O = obstructed); tolvaptan in polycystic disease.

**Diagram to draw:** Nephron with transporters; triphasic graph.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RBF 25% of cardiac output; GFR 125 mL/min; net filtration pressure ~10 mmHg.
> - Proximal 65%, loop 25%, distal 5–7% of Na⁺; ADH → V2 → AQP2.
> - Myogenic peristalsis from calyceal pacemaker; post-obstructive diuresis 2% vs 50%.

## Pelvicalyceal (renal collecting system) anatomy, its relevance to PCNL and endourology, and mini-PCNL
**Asked as:**
- Describe anatomy of pelvicalyceal system in detail and its application in endourological surgeries — *Winter 2017 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Anatomy of renal collecting system and its relevance to PCNL — *Practice Set 1 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)* (also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1 and 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q1)
- Describe anatomy of kidney in relation to PCNL and describe indication, contraindication, procedure and complications of mini-PCNL. — *Summer 2022 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q1 (pelvicalyceal anatomy) and 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3d (Miniperc))

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- Papilla → minor calyx → infundibulum → major calyx → renal pelvis → UPJ (ureteropelvic junction); lined by urothelium. Anatomy is very variable, so map it (CT (computed tomography) urogram or pyelogram) before every endourological procedure.
- Safe PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) access depends on the kidney's position, neighbours, vessels and calyces.

### Surgical anatomy relevant to PCNL: position and orientation
- Lies T12–L3 (right 1–2 cm lower); descends about 3 cm on inspiration.
- It lies on the cone-shaped psoas, so the upper pole is medial and posterior and the lower pole lateral and anterior.
- Tract: skin, flank muscles, thoracolumbar fascia, paranephric fat, Gerota fascia, perirenal fat, cortex, then **pyramid and papilla** into the calyx.

### Anatomy of the calyces (collecting system)
- **Minor calyx:** a cup around one papilla; its two side projections are the **fornices**; it drains by an infundibulum to a major calyx and pelvis. **Compound calyces** (2–3 papillae) are commonest at (cluster at) the poles. Average 7–9 calyces per kidney (range 4–19 or more).
- **Distribution:** upper pole usually 3 calyces, interpolar 3–4, lower pole 2–3.
- **Drainage pattern:** upper pole by a single midline infundibulum; lower pole by a single midline infundibulum or paired calyces; hilar region (hilar ones) by **anterior and posterior rows** of paired calyces.
- **Orientation:** the kidney lies on psoas, rotated 30–50° behind the coronal plane (posteriorly), so **posterior calyces point posterolaterally** (towards the prone surgeon). On a pyelogram anterior and posterior calyces (rows) overlap.
- **Brödel vs Hodson pattern (Kaye and Reinke):** in a Brödel-type kidney the posterior calyces lie lateral and the anterior calyces medial; in a Hodson-type kidney the reverse. Brödel type is commoner on the right, Hodson type on the left, so confirm calyx orientation with contrast/air or ultrasound before puncture.

### Renal pelvis
- Pyramidal, apex to the ureter; capacity 3–10 mL. **Intrarenal** (in the sinus), **extrarenal** (larger, longer infundibula, mimics dilatation) or mixed.
- **Duplex (divided) pelvis:** upper and lower parts join at the hilum, with no direct connection between upper and lower calyces (does not link upper and lower calyces). Suspect it when one pole cannot be reached from a puncture.

### Sampaio classification (Sampaio types; 140 resin endocasts)
| Type | Frequency | Midzone (hilar) drainage |
|---|---|---|
| A-I | 45% | Midzone calyces drain into the superior or inferior calyceal group (via a polar group) |
| A-II | 17.2% | **Crossed calyces**: one to the superior and one to the inferior group (to both groups) |
| B-I | 21.4% | Independent major calyceal group (major calyx) from the midzone |
| B-II | 16.4% | 1–4 minor calyces enter the pelvis directly (straight into the pelvis) |

- **Perpendicular minor calyx (11.4%):** drains straight into the pelvis, hidden on a pyelogram (pyelography); narrow neck (≤4 mm) hinders clearance.
- **Crossed calyces:** the limb to the inferior group is **ventral in 87.5%**.

### Vascular relations (the basis of safe access)
- Hilum, anterior to posterior: vein, artery, pelvis, **posterior segmental artery** (the only branch passing behind the pelvis).
- Five segmental **end arteries**; interlobar arteries run in the **columns of Bertin**, hugging the anterior and posterior faces of the infundibula (beside the infundibula).
- **Brödel line:** avascular plane between anterior and posterior arterial territories, just posterior to (behind) the lateral border, along the posterior calyces. Posterior calyces along it are the target.

| Puncture route / site (Sampaio) | Vascular injury |
|---|---|
| Upper-pole infundibulum | 67% (arterial 26%); posterior segmental artery |
| Mid-pole infundibulum | Arterial 23% |
| Lower-pole infundibulum | Infundibular artery (present) in 38% |
| **Calyceal fornix** | Venous <8%, **no arterial injury** |

- So the needle passes **through the papilla along the calyx axis**, never into an infundibulum or the pelvis.

### Relations (the hazards of access)
- **Relations:** pleura reaches the 12th rib posteriorly, lung the 10th intercostal space; liver and spleen descend in inspiration; **retrorenal colon** is commoner at the lower pole and when prone.

| Structure | Relation | Practical point |
|---|---|---|
| Pleura, lung | Pleura reaches the 12th rib; lung the 10th intercostal space | Prefer subcostal or 11th-space access; risk rises above the 12th rib, highest above the 11th; puncture in expiration |
| Liver, spleen | Over the upper poles; descend in inspiration | Risk with supracostal access, inspiration, organomegaly |
| Colon | Retrorenal colon, mainly at the lower pole, commoner prone and on the left | Review CT; ultrasound shows bowel |

### Applications in endourology
**1. PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) access**
- Ideal target: a **posterior calyx with a short, wide infundibulum**, punctured (entered) **through the papilla and fornix** along the calyx axis, never into (through) the infundibulum or pelvis; identified by injected air, C-arm rotation or ultrasound. This passes through a pyramid and avoids interlobar arteries.
- **Upper-pole posterior access** gives the straightest route (best reach) to the pelvis, UPJ and most calyces (staghorn; staghorn branches). Chest risk (pneumothorax, hydrothorax) rises above the 12th rib, highest above the 11th. Puncture in expiration.
- **Lower-pole access** is chest-safe but risks (retrorenal) colon (left) and reaches the UPJ poorly (poor UPJ reach).
- Identifying (identify) posterior calyces: rainbowing the C-arm towards the surgeon shortens them (they shorten when the C-arm rotates towards the surgeon); injected air fills non-dependent (posterior, when prone) calyces (rises posteriorly when prone); ultrasound also shows bowel and pleura.
- **Also know:** traps: perpendicular or crossed calyces, duplex pelvis.

**2. RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery)**
- Entering the lower-pole infundibulum needs about **140° deflection** (104–175°); ≥270° scopes see it, but working there is harder.
- **Unfavourable lower-pole anatomy:** steep infundibulopelvic angle, long calyx (>10 mm), narrow infundibulum (<5 mm). Relocate stones to an upper calyx with a basket.
- Lower-pole stone >1 cm: PCNL preferred (**Lower Pole I, 2001:** stone-free 91% PCNL vs 21% SWL (shock wave lithotripsy)).

**3. Endopyelotomy**
- Incise the UPJ **laterally**, where crossing vessels are absent. A crossing vessel lowers success (42% vs 86%).
- Antegrade route: mid-posterior or superolateral calyx; not lower pole (acute angle to the UPJ).

**4. Infundibulotomy and calyceal diverticulum**
- Incise infundibular stenosis **superiorly or inferiorly**; never anteriorly.
- **Calyceal diverticulum:** cortical cavity joined to the fornix by a narrow neck; treat if symptomatic. Upper or interpolar: ureteroscopy, incise or dilate the neck. Lower pole: direct puncture onto the stone, mini-PCNL; fulguration optional.

**5. Open/anatrophic nephrolithotomy:** incision along the Brödel line.

- **Also know:** horseshoe kidney (medial access, upper pole usually subcostal); pelvic kidney (ultrasound or laparoscopic guidance); transplant kidneys; megacalycosis mimics obstruction; AV (arteriovenous) fistula or pseudoaneurysm → angioembolisation.

### Mini-PCNL: definition

| Term | Tract size |
|---|---|
| Standard | 24–34 Fr |
| **Mini** | 15–22 Fr |
| Ultra-mini | 11–14 Fr |
| Micro-perc | <10 Fr |

- First mini-perc: Jackman, 1998 (13 Fr).

### Indications
- Renal stones 1–2 cm; lower-pole stones >1 cm; failed SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) or RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery).
- Calyceal diverticulum (lower pole), children, poor retrograde access (urinary diversion, ureteric stricture), selected stones >2 cm or extra tracts for staghorns.

### Contraindications
- Untreated UTI (urinary tract infection), uncorrected coagulopathy, pregnancy (EAU, European Association of Urology).
- Tumour in the access tract or suspected malignant renal tumour; no safe window; unfit for anaesthesia.

### Procedure
1. Urine culture, blood count, coagulation, creatinine; **non-contrast CT (computed tomography)**; antibiotic prophylaxis.
2. Lithotomy: ureteric catheter and retrograde pyelogram.
3. Prone, or supine (Galdakao-modified) for single-position retrograde access.
4. 18 G puncture of a posterior calyx under fluoroscopy (bull's-eye or triangulation) or ultrasound; urine return; guidewire and safety wire.
5. One-step, serial or balloon dilatation to 15–22 Fr; place the sheath.
6. Mini-nephroscope; holmium/thulium laser or pneumatic lithotripsy. Fragments wash out by the irrigation **"vacuum-cleaner" effect** or a **suction sheath**.
7. Flexible nephroscopy to clear calyces.
8. Exit: tubeless, small nephrostomy (≤10 Fr: less pain) or double-J stent.

### Advantages and limitations
- Meta-analysis: **non-inferior stone-free rate**, smaller haemoglobin drop, **fewer transfusions**, shorter stay.
- Longer operation, poorer vision. A tight sheath raises **intrarenal pressure**, causing pyelovenous backflow and **fever or sepsis**. Advantages disappear against 24 Fr tracts.

### Complications (grade by Clavien–Dindo)

| Type | Points |
|---|---|
| Bleeding | Acute tract bleeding: tamponade sheath, balloon or Kaye catheter. Delayed: pseudoaneurysm or AV (arteriovenous) fistula, treated by angioembolisation |
| Infection | Fever, SIRS (systemic inflammatory response syndrome), urosepsis |
| Thoracic | Pneumothorax, hydrothorax: chest drain |
| Visceral | Colon (stent plus nephrostomy withdrawn into the colon), spleen, liver, duodenum |
| Collecting system | Perforation, extravasation, later infundibular stenosis; residual stones |
| Others | Irrigation absorption, venous gas embolism, DVT (deep vein thrombosis), neuropraxia, renal function loss, death |

**Diagram to draw:** transverse section of kidney showing anterior and posterior calyces (posterior calyx), Brödel line and the needle through the posterior calyceal fornix.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Sampaio: A-I 45%, A-II (crossed) 17.2%, B-I 21.4%, B-II 16.4%.
> - Fornix puncture: no arterial injury; upper-pole infundibulum 67% vascular injury.
> - Target a posterior calyx with a short, wide infundibulum; upper pole reaches most.
> - Lower pole: steep angle, long calyx >10 mm, infundibulum <5 mm unfavourable.
> - Endopyelotomy lateral; infundibulotomy superior or inferior, never anterior.
> - Pleura to 12th rib; retrorenal colon at the lower pole, commoner prone.
> - Mini 15–22 Fr (Jackman 1998): fewer transfusions, longer operation.
> - High intrarenal pressure in mini-PCNL causes fever and sepsis.

## Lymphatic drainage of the genitourinary system and chyluria (etiopathogenesis and management)
**Asked as:**
- Describe anatomy of lymphatic drainage of genitourinary system, etiopathogenesis and management of Chyluria — *Summer 2018 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Describe lymphatic system of urinary tract with reference to etiopathogenesis of chyluria. — *Practice Set 8 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*
- Management of chyluria — *Practice Set 10 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q1 and 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q2)

### General plan of lymphatic drainage
- Lymph flows **cephalad and right → left** and returns to the left innominate (brachiocephalic) vein.
- Pelvic nodes (external iliac, internal iliac, obturator, presacral) → common iliac → lumbar (para-aortic, interaortocaval, paracaval) nodes → **lumbar trunks**. These join the intestinal trunk at the **cisterna chyli** (in front of L1–L2, behind the right side of the aorta). The **thoracic duct** rises behind the aorta to the left innominate vein.
- Pelvic lymph crosses freely between right and left (the two sides). Organs supplied by lateral aortic branches (kidney, adrenal, ureter, gonad) drain to lumbar nodes.
- **Lumbar groups:** left lumbar (preaortic, left para-aortic, retroaortic), interaortocaval, and right lumbar (precaval, paracaval, retrocaval). They drain organs supplied by lateral aortic branches: kidneys, adrenals, ureters and gonads.
- Preaortic nodes (coeliac, superior and inferior mesenteric) form the **intestinal trunk**, which carries **chyle** from the intestinal lacteals.
- Lumbar and intestinal trunks join at the **cisterna chyli** (in front of L1–L2, behind the right side of the aorta). It continues as the **thoracic duct**, which rises behind the aorta to the left innominate vein.
- **Lymphatic anatomy (summary):** intestinal lacteals → intestinal trunk → **cisterna chyli** (L1–L2) → **thoracic duct** → left subclavian–jugular junction. Renal lumbar lymphatics join the cisterna; valves normally prevent backflow.

### Drainage by organ
| Organ | Primary nodes | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Left kidney** | Left lateral para-aortic | Capsular and deep hilar networks drain to renal vein nodes; may go retrocrural |
| **Right kidney** | Right paracaval, interaortocaval | May cross to left para-aortic |
| **Ureter** | Abdominal: para-aortic (left), paracaval/interaortocaval (right); middle: common iliac; pelvic: common, external, internal iliac | Plexus in muscle and adventitia |
| **Bladder** | Mainly **external iliac**; also internal iliac, obturator | Begins in lamina propria; bilateral cross-drainage |
| **Prostate** | **Obturator and internal iliac**; presacral | Three routes: superolateral (side wall), posterior (presacral), apical (internal pudendal) |
| **Testis** | Left: left para-aortic; right: interaortocaval, then right paracaval | Follows gonadal vessels; crossover only right → left. Epididymal cauda and vas → external iliac |
| **Penis** | Skin → superficial inguinal (both groins); glans and corpora → superficial and deep inguinal, then pelvic | Shaft lymphatics converge dorsally |
| **Scrotum** | Ipsilateral superficial inguinal | Does not cross the raphe |
| **Urethra (male)** | Penile → inguinal; bulbomembranous and prostatic → internal/external iliac, obturator | Female: proximal → pelvic; distal → inguinal |

#### Lymphatic drainage of the urinary tract (intrinsic lymphatics and regional nodes)
| Organ | Intrinsic lymphatics | Regional nodes |
|---|---|---|
| **Kidney** | Superficial capsular and deep hilar networks along the interlobular, arcuate and interlobar arteries, to nodes at the renal vein | **Left:** left lateral para-aortic (sometimes retrocrural, or thoracic duct above the diaphragm). **Right:** interaortocaval and right paracaval (may cross to left para-aortic) |
| **Ureter** | Plexuses in the muscle and adventitia | Abdominal: left para-aortic (left); paracaval and interaortocaval (right). Middle: common iliac. Pelvic: common, external and internal iliac |
| **Bladder** | Begins in the lamina propria; a few perivesical nodes | Mainly **external iliac**; also internal iliac and obturator; cross-drainage to both sides |
| **Prostate** | Periprostatic plexus | Obturator and internal iliac; presacral; external iliac |
| **Urethra** | Submucosal plexus | Male prostatic and membranous: internal iliac, obturator; penile: inguinal, then external iliac. Female: proximal to pelvic, distal to inguinal nodes |

- **Applied:** upper tract cancers spread to lumbar nodes; bladder and prostate cancers to pelvic nodes. Renal drainage is asymmetric: right renal lymph can cross left; left stays left.
- Valves in the renal hilar lymphatics normally stop backflow from the cisterna chyli. **Failure of these valves is the basis of chyluria.**

### Chyluria: definition
- Passage of **chyle** (intestinal lymph rich in chylomicrons, triglyceride and protein) in urine, giving milky urine (making it milky; urine looks milky). Caused by an abnormal **lymphourinary (chylourinary) fistula**, usually at the renal fornices / a renal calyceal fornix (**pyelolymphatic fistula**).

### Etiology
| Parasitic (commonest; India, China) | Non-parasitic |
|---|---|
| ***Wuchereria bancrofti*** (lymphatic filariasis; *Culex* mosquito) | Congenital lymphangiectasia, renal lymphangioma |
| *Brugia* (lymphatic filariasis that spares the genitals; rarely urological) | Trauma, surgery (e.g. partial nephrectomy, RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)) |
|  | Retroperitoneal tumour or fibrosis, tuberculosis, pregnancy |

### Pathogenesis (obstructive theory)
- **Life cycle:** mosquito-borne larvae mature into adult worms that live for years in retroperitoneal and genital lymphatics. Females release microfilariae with **nocturnal periodicity**.
- Adult worms live in retroperitoneal lymphatics and cause lymphangitis and fibrosis. This **obstructs** flow between the intestinal lymph trunks and the thoracic duct.
- Proximal lymphatics dilate and their valves fail, so chyle **refluxes** into renal hilar lymphatics.
- Raised pressure ruptures them into a calyceal fornix → **pyelolymphatic fistula**. Rarely the fistula is in the ureter or bladder.
- **Obstructive (reflux) theory:**
```
Adult worms + host immune reaction -> lymphangitis and fibrosis
 -> obstruction between intestinal trunks/cisterna chyli and thoracic duct
 -> dilatation of retroperitoneal lymphatics, valve incompetence
 -> reflux of chyle into renal hilar and peripelvic lymphatics
 -> raised pressure ruptures thin-walled lymphatics into a calyceal fornix
 -> pyelolymphatic fistula -> chyluria
```
- **Pathogenesis (filarial):** adult *Wuchereria bancrofti* worms and the host reaction cause **lymphangitis and fibrosis** between the intestinal trunks, cisterna chyli and thoracic duct. Lymphatics below the block dilate and their valves fail. Chyle refluxes into renal hilar lymphatics, which rupture into a calyx.
- The fistula is usually renal (hilar lymphatics lie against thin fornices); rarely ureteric or vesical.
- Chyluria appears **earlier than genital elephantiasis**, is usually **intermittent** and may remit spontaneously. It worsens after fatty meals, when lacteal flow is highest. So every treatment is judged against natural remission.
- **Non-parasitic causes** work the same way: lymphatic obstruction (tumour, fibrosis), direct disruption (surgery, trauma) or congenital lymphangiectasia.
- **Effects:** loss of fat, protein and lymphocytes causes weight loss, hypoproteinaemia and lowered immunity. Fibrin in chyle forms clots.

### Clinical features
- Milky urine, worse after fatty meals; **chylous clots** (retention, colic); **haematochyluria**.
- Loss of fat and protein → weight loss, **hypoproteinaemia**, oedema, anaemia, fatigue, lowered immunity.
- **Severity:** mild (intermittent, no systemic effect); moderate (persistent, clots); severe (haematochyluria, clot retention, weight loss, hypoalbuminaemia).

| Grade | Features |
|---|---|
| Mild | Intermittent; no systemic effect |
| Moderate | Persistent; clots |
| Severe | Haematochyluria, clot retention, weight loss, hypoalbuminaemia |

### Evaluation (before treatment)
**1. Confirm chyle**
- **Ether (chloroform) test**: milky urine shaken with ether clears because fat dissolves. **Urine triglycerides** (and protein) positive/raised; Sudan III stains fat globules.
- Distinguish from / exclude **phosphaturia** (clears with acetic acid) and **pyuria** (leucocytes, no clearing).

**2. Filarial status (filarial tests)**
- Night blood smear (often negative at this stage / late), **circulating filarial antigen** (card) test, eosinophilia; PCR (polymerase chain reaction).

**3. Lateralise the side and localise the fistula**
- **Cystoscopy after a fatty meal** shows milky efflux from the ureteric orifice (one, sometimes both).
- **Retrograde pyelography** shows **pyelolymphatic backflow**.
- **Define fistula:** **lymphoscintigraphy** (non-invasive, shows tracer in kidney and bladder), pedal lymphangiography (historical gold standard), MR (magnetic resonance) lymphangiography or CT.

**4. Baseline:** serum albumin, haemoglobin, renal function; urine culture before instillation.

### Management (outline / algorithm)
```
Mild → diet + DEC
Persistent / moderate → add sclerotherapy (can repeat)
Severe or failed sclerotherapy → renal hilar lymphatic disconnection
```
```
Mild               -> low-fat, high-protein diet + MCT oil + DEC
Moderate/persistent -> endoscopic sclerotherapy (silver nitrate 0.5-1%, povidone-iodine)
Severe or failed   -> renal hilar lymphatic disconnection (open, laparoscopic, robotic)
```
```
Confirm chyle -> grade severity
   Mild             -> diet + antifilarial drugs; observe
   Moderate/persist -> add endoscopic sclerotherapy (repeatable)
   Severe or failed -> renal hilar lymphatic disconnection
```

**1. Conservative (all patients)**
- **Low-fat, high-protein diet with MCT (medium-chain triglyceride) oil**, plenty of fluids.
- **Why MCT:** MCTs (medium-chain fats) are absorbed straight/directly into the portal blood, not into lacteals, so chyle formation falls (less chyle is formed).
- **DEC (diethylcarbamazine)** 6 mg/kg/day for 12 days kills microfilariae (another source: 2 mg/kg orally three times daily, traditionally 12 days; a 1-day course is as effective). It has only modest effect on adults.
- **Doxycycline** 200 mg daily for 4–8 weeks kills *Wolbachia* (endosymbiont; anti-*Wolbachia*) and so sterilises or shortens the life of adult worms. Albendazole and ivermectin are alternatives.
- Correct anaemia and protein deficit. Most mild cases settle on this, and many remit spontaneously.

**2. Endoscopic sclerotherapy (renal pelvic instillation sclerotherapy)**
- Ureteric catheter on the affected (leaking) side, head-down position. Instil **silver nitrate** (0.5–1%) or **povidone-iodine**, repeated over several sessions.
- **Mechanism:** chemical lymphangitis and fibrosis seal the fistula.
- **Contraindications:** active UTI (urinary tract infection), bilateral simultaneous instillation (risk of anuria).
- Good initial success/response; recurrence is common, but it is repeatable and avoids surgery.
- **Complications:** flank pain, haematuria, papillary necrosis, ureteric stricture, anuria; rarely renal failure or death (silver nitrate).

**3. Surgery: renal hilar lymphatic disconnection (chylolymphatic disconnection)**
- **Indications:** severe disease, failed sclerotherapy, haematochyluria with clot retention, marked weight loss.
- **Steps:**
  1. Full renal mobilisation (nephrolysis).
  2. **Skeletonise the renal artery and vein**, ligating, clipping or sealing every/all hilar lymphatics.
  3. Strip the upper ureter; remove perinephric fat.
  4. Nephropexy ± optional **omental wrap** around the hilum as a barrier.
  5. **On-table cystoscopy** confirms clear urine/efflux.
- Reflux occurs only through **hilar lymphatics**, so upper-pole dissection can be limited.
- **Approach:** open, laparoscopic (transperitoneal or retroperitoneal) or robotic. **Retroperitoneoscopic** ligation beats open in meta-analysis: shorter operation and stay, less blood loss, faster bowel recovery, fewer complications.
- **Complications:** lymphocele, renal vein injury, recurrence.
- **Also know:** lymphovenous anastomosis; autotransplantation (rare); nephrectomy (now) obsolete; prevent by clipping lymphatics during renal and nodal surgery.

**4. Follow-up and prevention**
- Urine triglycerides and weight; recurrence needs repeat sclerotherapy or surgery.
- Mass drug administration (albendazole with DEC or ivermectin), bednets; clip lymphatics during renal and nodal surgery.

**Diagram to draw:** Retroperitoneal lymphatics with cisterna chyli, obstruction (a block above it / obstructed thoracic duct), dilated hilar lymphatics, and reflux to a pyelolymphatic fistula at a calyceal fornix.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lymph flows right → left; cisterna chyli at L1–L2, behind the right side of the aorta.
> - Bladder drains mainly to external iliac nodes; left kidney to left para-aortic nodes.
> - Chyluria = pyelolymphatic fistula from lymphatic obstruction and valve failure, usually filarial (*W. bancrofti*); often intermittent.
> - Ether test and (urine) triglycerides confirm (ether test clears milky urine); cystoscopy after (a) fatty meal lateralises.
> - Diet (MCT) + DEC (diet with MCT plus DEC) → (silver nitrate) sclerotherapy → laparoscopic hilar lymphatic disconnection; disconnection surgery for severe or refractory cases.
> - Retroperitoneoscopic disconnection beats open.

## Pathophysiology of Renovascular hypertension
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 1, Q2
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q2, with treatment in detail)

### Definitions
- **RVH (renovascular hypertension):** hypertension caused by RAS (renal artery stenosis) and relieved by revascularisation or nephrectomy. RAS is common in older vascular patients; RVH is proved only by cure.
- **Ischaemic nephropathy:** loss of GFR (glomerular filtration rate) from RAS affecting the **whole renal mass** (bilateral RAS or stenosis to a solitary kidney).

### Causes
| | Atherosclerotic RAS | FMD (fibromuscular dysplasia) |
|---|---|---|
| Share | Most (about 90%) | Most of the rest |
| Patient | Older, smokers, diabetic, vascular disease | Young women |
| Site | **Ostium and proximal third** | **Mid and distal artery, branches**; "string of beads" (medial fibroplasia) |
| Course | Progresses to occlusion and renal loss | Rarely occludes |

**Also know:** Takayasu arteritis, neurofibromatosis, midaortic syndrome (children), aneurysm, dissection, embolism, transplant RAS, **Page kidney** (perinephric compression, e.g. subcapsular haematoma).

### The RAAS (renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system)
- **Renin** is released by juxtaglomerular cells of the afferent arteriole in response to:
  1. low afferent arteriolar pressure (intrarenal baroreceptor);
  2. low sodium chloride delivery to the **macula densa**;
  3. sympathetic **β1** stimulation.
- Renin cleaves hepatic **angiotensinogen** to angiotensin I. **ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme)**, mainly in lung endothelium, converts it to **angiotensin II**.
- **Angiotensin II (AT1 receptor) actions:** systemic arteriolar vasoconstriction; **aldosterone** release (sodium retention, potassium loss); proximal tubular sodium reabsorption; thirst and ADH (antidiuretic hormone); sympathetic facilitation; **efferent arteriolar constriction**, which maintains GFR; cardiac and vascular hypertrophy and fibrosis.
- Normally, rising blood pressure (BP) and sodium suppress renin (negative feedback). In RVH the stenosed kidney "sees" low pressure, so this feedback fails.

### Goldblatt experiments
- **Goldblatt (1934)** clipped the canine renal artery and produced sustained hypertension, reversed by early clip removal.

| | **Two-kidney, one-clip (2K1C)** | **One-kidney, one-clip (1K1C)** |
|---|---|---|
| Human equivalent | Unilateral RAS, normal opposite kidney | Bilateral RAS or stenosed solitary kidney |
| Sodium | Normal kidney excretes extra sodium (**pressure natriuresis**) | No normal kidney: sodium and water retained |
| Renin | **High** from stenotic side; contralateral suppressed | Rises, then normalises as volume expands |
| Type | **Renin (angiotensin)-dependent** | **Volume-dependent** |
| ACE inhibitor effect | BP falls; captopril tests positive | Less BP effect; may cause **acute kidney injury** |

### Phases of 2K1C hypertension
1. **Acute phase:** renin-dependent; ACE inhibitor or clip removal reverses it.
2. **Transition:** sodium retention and secondary hyperaldosteronism; renin falls towards normal.
3. **Chronic phase:** hypertension damages the contralateral kidney (nephrosclerosis) and resistance vessels. BP no longer depends on the stenosis, so **revascularisation cures less**. This explains poorer results in long-standing hypertension.

### Other contributing mechanisms
- Renal afferent nerve signals raise **sympathetic activity**.
- Angiotensin II generates **oxidative stress** (reactive oxygen species), lowers **nitric oxide** and raises **endothelin**, worsening vasoconstriction.
- Inflammation and fibrosis; left ventricular hypertrophy and diastolic dysfunction.

### Pathophysiology of ischaemic nephropathy
- The kidney receives about **20–25% of cardiac output**, far more than its metabolic need. **Autoregulation** holds GFR until stenosis is severe (about 70% or more, or a significant translesional pressure gradient).
- Beyond this, GFR depends on angiotensin II-mediated efferent constriction. Blocking it (ACE inhibitor or ARB [angiotensin receptor blocker]) drops GFR: hence **creatinine rise >30%** after these drugs.
- Chronic hypoperfusion causes hypoxia, inflammation, microvascular rarefaction, **tubulointerstitial fibrosis** and atrophy. Atheroemboli and hypertensive injury add damage.
- Early disease is reversible ("hibernating" kidney); established fibrosis is not.
- **Flash pulmonary oedema:** bilateral RAS causes volume retention plus angiotensin II-driven diastolic dysfunction.

### Clinical clues
- Onset <30 years (FMD) or severe hypertension after 55; **resistant** (≥3 drugs) or accelerated hypertension; sudden loss of control.
- Flash pulmonary oedema; kidneys differing by >1.5 cm; abdominal bruit; **hypokalaemia** (secondary hyperaldosteronism).

### Investigations based on pathophysiology
- **Duplex Doppler:** high peak systolic velocity, renal/aortic ratio >3.5. **RI (resistive index) >0.8** means parenchymal disease and poor response.
- **CT (computed tomography) or MR (magnetic resonance) angiography** for anatomy; **catheter angiography** with translesional gradient is the gold standard.
- **Captopril renography:** removing angiotensin II drops GFR in the stenosed kidney (delayed uptake), proving renin-dependence.
- **Renal vein renin ratio >1.5** lateralises.

### Treatment implications (brief)
- **ACE inhibitor or ARB** treats renin-dependent RVH (monitor creatinine and potassium), plus statin, antiplatelet and smoking cessation.
- **ASTRAL (2009)** and **CORAL (2014):** stenting added no benefit over medical therapy in atherosclerotic RAS.
- **FMD:** balloon angioplasty without stent. Revascularise atherosclerotic RAS for flash pulmonary oedema, rapidly falling function or truly resistant hypertension.

**Diagram to draw:** RAAS cascade from renin release to angiotensin II actions, beside the 2K1C and 1K1C models.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Goldblatt 1934: 2K1C = renin-dependent; 1K1C = volume-dependent.
> - Chronic phase: contralateral nephrosclerosis makes RVH stenosis-independent.
> - Angiotensin II keeps GFR by efferent constriction; ACE inhibitor causes acute kidney injury in bilateral RAS.
> - ASTRAL and CORAL: no routine stenting; FMD = angioplasty alone.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Renin-angiotensin system
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition
- Hormonal cascade controlling **blood pressure, extracellular volume, Na⁺/K⁺ balance and GFR (glomerular filtration rate)**.

### Cascade
```
Angiotensinogen (liver)
   ↓ renin (juxtaglomerular cells) – rate-limiting
Angiotensin I
   ↓ ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme), mainly lung
Angiotensin II → AT1 / AT2 receptors
   ↓ ACE2
Angiotensin (1-7) → Mas receptor (protective)
```

### Juxtaglomerular apparatus and renin release
- Granular cells in the afferent arteriole secrete renin and act as baroreceptors; the **macula densa** senses tubular NaCl.

| Renin stimulated by | Renin inhibited by |
|---|---|
| Low renal perfusion pressure (baroreceptor) | High perfusion pressure |
| Low NaCl at macula densa | High NaCl at macula densa |
| Sympathetic β1 activity | Angiotensin II (negative feedback) |
| Prostaglandins, hypovolaemia | Atrial natriuretic peptide, β-blockers, NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) |

### Actions of angiotensin II
- **AT1 receptor:** vasoconstriction; **aldosterone** (distal Na⁺ retention, K⁺ loss); direct proximal Na⁺ reabsorption; thirst and ADH (antidiuretic hormone); **fibrosis**.
- **AT2 receptor:** vasodilatation; fetal urinary tract development.
- **Intrarenal:** efferent constriction maintains GFR when perfusion falls, so ACE inhibitors and ARBs (angiotensin receptor blockers) drop GFR in bilateral or solitary-kidney renal artery stenosis.

### Urological relevance
- **Renovascular hypertension (Goldblatt kidney):** atherosclerosis (older men, ostial) or fibromuscular dysplasia (young women). Diagnose by duplex ultrasound, computed tomography or magnetic resonance angiography, captopril renography. **Renal vein renin ratio >1.5** with contralateral suppression predicts cure by revascularisation or nephrectomy.
- **Page kidney:** subcapsular haematoma compresses parenchyma; ACE inhibitor/ARB, then drainage.
- **Reninoma (juxtaglomerular cell tumour):** young women, ≤3 cm mass, refractory hypertension, **hypokalaemia**; excision cures most.
- **Obstruction:** hypertension (~77% bilateral, ~20% unilateral); angiotensin II drives fibrosis.
- **Also know:** post-traumatic hypertension (arteriovenous fistula, scarring); reflux nephropathy (renin-guided segmental resection); hyperkalaemia with angiotensin blockade in CKD (chronic kidney disease).
- **Drugs:** ACE inhibitors (cough, angio-oedema from bradykinin), ARBs, aliskiren (direct renin inhibitor), aldosterone antagonists; all contraindicated in pregnancy.

**Diagram to draw:** Juxtaglomerular apparatus and cascade.

### Recent advances
- Renal denervation (US approval 2023) for resistant hypertension.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Renin is rate-limiting; released by low pressure, low NaCl, β1.
> - Efferent constriction maintains GFR, so ACE inhibitors risk renal failure in bilateral stenosis.
> - Renin ratio >1.5 lateralises; reninoma excision cures most.

## Patho-physiology of Brain stem death
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3d

### Definition
- **Irreversible loss of the capacity for consciousness and to breathe** from permanent brainstem failure; legally death. Donation after brain death (DBD) keeps circulation until procurement, minimising warm ischaemia.

### Pathophysiology
```
Brain injury → oedema → raised intracranial pressure (ICP)
 → falling cerebral perfusion → herniation ("coning")
 → Pons: Cushing reflex (hypertension, bradycardia)
 → Medulla: catecholamine storm (hypertension, arrhythmia)
 → Loss of sympathetic tone → vasodilatation, hypotension
```
- The storm injures myocardium and causes pulmonary oedema. Loss of antidiuretic hormone causes **diabetes insipidus (DI)**: polyuria, hypernatraemia; thyroid hormone and cortisol fall.
- **Hypothermia** (hypothalamic loss) and **disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)** from brain thromboplastin.

### Diagnosis
- **Preconditions:** apnoeic coma of known irreversible cause; exclude sedatives, relaxants, hypothermia (35–36 °C by protocol), metabolic causes.
- **Brainstem reflexes, all must be absent:**

| Test | How it is done | Cranial nerves |
|---|---|---|
| Pupillary light reflex | Pupils fixed, no response to bright light | II, III |
| Corneal reflex | Touch cornea; no blink | V, VII |
| Oculovestibular (caloric) | ~50 mL ice-cold water into each ear (drum visible); no eye movement | VIII, III, VI |
| Oculocephalic (doll's eye) | Turn head; eyes move with the head | VIII, III, VI |
| Motor response | Central painful stimulus (supraorbital pressure); no facial grimace | V, VII |
| Gag and cough | Stimulate pharynx; pass suction catheter down trachea; no response | IX, X |

- **Apnoea test:** pre-oxygenate with 100% oxygen, disconnect the ventilator with oxygen insufflation, and watch for breathing effort until **arterial CO₂ (PaCO₂) ≥60 mmHg**. No effort = positive. Abort if hypotension, desaturation or arrhythmia.
- **Ancillary tests** (electroencephalography (EEG), cerebral angiography, perfusion scan, transcranial Doppler) are not mandatory; used when clinical testing cannot be completed.

### Indian law
- **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994**, amended 2011, Rules 2014: **4-member board** (hospital in-charge, independent specialist, neurologist/neurosurgeon, treating doctor; no transplant team member); **two tests at least 6 hours apart**.

### Donor management
- **Rule of 100s:** systolic pressure >100 mmHg, urine >100 mL/h, PaO₂ (arterial oxygen tension) >100 mmHg, haemoglobin >100 g/L.
- **Vasopressin** preferred (also treats DI); thyroid hormone, methylprednisolone, insulin.
- **Also know:** cold storage at 4 °C (University of Wisconsin solution); donor exclusions (metastatic cancer, melanoma, untreatable viral infection).

### Recent advances
- Hypothermic machine perfusion reduces delayed graft function.

**Diagram to draw:** Herniation sequence leading to storm, DI, hypothermia, DIC.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Raised ICP → Cushing reflex → storm → hypotension, DI, hypothermia, DIC.
> - Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
> - THOA: 4-member board, two tests ≥6 h apart; Rule of 100s.

## Drugs associated with Acute Renal Failure.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3c

### Introduction
- AKI (acute kidney injury), KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes): creatinine rise ≥0.3 mg/dL in 48 hours or ≥1.5 × baseline in 7 days, or urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 hours.

### Classification by mechanism
| Mechanism | Drugs | How |
|---|---|---|
| **Haemodynamic (prerenal)** | **NSAIDs** (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) | Block prostaglandin afferent dilatation |
| | **ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors, ARBs (angiotensin receptor blockers)** | Remove efferent constriction that holds GFR (glomerular filtration rate) |
| | Ciclosporin, tacrolimus, contrast; diuretics | Afferent vasoconstriction; volume loss |
| **ATN (acute tubular necrosis)** | **Aminoglycosides**, amphotericin B, vancomycin, cisplatin, ifosfamide, contrast | Direct tubular toxicity |
| **AIN (acute interstitial nephritis)** | **β-lactams**, sulfonamides, quinolones, rifampicin, NSAIDs, **PPIs (proton pump inhibitors)**, allopurinol, **checkpoint inhibitors** | Cell-mediated hypersensitivity |
| **Crystals** | Aciclovir, methotrexate, indinavir, sulfadiazine; tumour lysis | Intratubular obstruction |
| **Microangiopathy** | Gemcitabine, VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) inhibitors | Endothelial injury |
| **Rhabdomyolysis** | Statins (with fibrates) | Myoglobin toxicity, precipitation |

- **"Triple whammy":** NSAID + ACE inhibitor/ARB + diuretic in a dehydrated patient.
- **False creatinine rise:** trimethoprim and cimetidine block creatinine secretion.

### Recognition
| Type | Urine |
|---|---|
| Prerenal | FeNa (fractional excretion of sodium) <1%, bland |
| ATN | FeNa >1%, muddy-brown casts |
| AIN | Sterile pyuria, white cell casts, ± rash, fever, eosinophilia |
- AIN appears days to weeks after the drug starts; it is the commonest biopsy lesion with checkpoint inhibitors. Urine eosinophils are unreliable.

### Prevention and management
- Identify risk (CKD (chronic kidney disease), age, diabetes). Dose by eGFR (estimated GFR); monitor drug levels; once-daily aminoglycoside.
- Hydrate before contrast or cisplatin; alkalinise for methotrexate.
- **Stop the culprit**, withhold NSAIDs and ACE inhibitors, restore volume. **Early steroids for AIN** (only about half of antibiotic AIN recovers fully).
- Dialysis for refractory AEIOU: acidosis, electrolytes, intoxication, overload, uraemia.

**Diagram to draw:** glomerulus with afferent (prostaglandin) and efferent (angiotensin II) control.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - NSAIDs block afferent dilatation; ACE inhibitors/ARBs block efferent constriction.
> - ATN: aminoglycosides, amphotericin, cisplatin, contrast.
> - AIN: β-lactams, PPIs, NSAIDs, checkpoint inhibitors; stop and give steroids.
> - Crystals: aciclovir, methotrexate, indinavir.

## GFR estimation in a prospective renal donor and creatinine clearance
**Asked as:**
- Describe methods to estimate GFR in a prospective renal donor — *Summer 2019 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Creatinine clearance — *Practice Set 4 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 4: GFR assessment also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3a)

### Why it matters
- Donor loses about half the nephron mass. **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017** sets GFR (glomerular filtration rate) thresholds and asks for **measurement, not just estimation**, at a decision point.

### Methods (and comparison with other GFR measures)
**1. Serum creatinine and eGFR (estimated GFR)**
- Creatinine is freely filtered (and not reabsorbed), but generation depends on **muscle mass**, diet (cooked meat, creatine supplements) and age; it is also secreted, so it overestimates GFR.
- **CKD-EPI (Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology Collaboration) 2021 creatinine equation:** age and sex, **no race term** (no race); currently recommended; used for staging. MDRD (Modification of Diet in Renal Disease) and Cockcroft–Gault are older.
- Less accurate at GFR >60, where donors lie: a screening test only.

**2. Cystatin C**
- 13-kDa protein made by all nucleated cells; freely filtered, fully reabsorbed and broken down. Independent of muscle mass; affected by thyroid state, steroids, inflammation.
- **Combined creatinine–cystatin C equation** is more accurate than either alone (combined equation most accurate).

**3. Measured 24-hour creatinine clearance**
- **Definition:** creatinine clearance (CrCl) is the volume of plasma cleared of creatinine per minute. It estimates **GFR (glomerular filtration rate)** because creatinine is freely filtered and not reabsorbed.
- Normal about 120 mL/min/1.73 m² in young men, slightly lower in women; falls with age.
- **CrCl = (U × V) / P** ((urine creatinine × volume) ÷ serum creatinine): U = urine creatinine, V = urine flow (mL/min), P = plasma creatinine.
- **Method:** discard the first morning void, collect all urine for 24 hours including the next morning's first void; take serum creatinine during the collection.
- **Completeness check:** creatinine excretion should be **20–25 mg/kg/day in men, 15–20 mg/kg/day in women**.

**Limitations of creatinine clearance**
- **Tubular secretion** of creatinine: small in health but rising as function falls, so CrCl (secretion) **overestimates GFR** in CKD (chronic kidney disease).
- Prone to collection errors (collection errors are common). Creatinine generation depends on **muscle mass**, diet (cooked meat, creatine supplements) and age.
- Drugs blocking secretion (**trimethoprim, cimetidine**) raise serum creatinine without real injury.
- Invalid in AKI (acute kidney injury). **Also know:** cimetidine pretreatment blocks secretion to improve accuracy; averaging urea and creatinine clearance helps in advanced CKD.

**Estimated clearance: Cockcroft–Gault**
- **CrCl = (140 − age) × weight (kg) / (72 × serum creatinine mg/dL)**, × 0.85 in women.
- Used for **drug dosing**; not normalised to body surface area; inaccurate in obesity.

**4. mGFR (measured GFR): reference (standard)**
| Marker | Method / comment |
|---|---|
| **Inulin** | Urinary clearance; gold standard, research only |
| **Iohexol** | Plasma clearance; safe, accurate |
| **Iothalamate** | Urinary or plasma clearance |
| **⁵¹Cr-EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid)** | Plasma clearance |
| **⁹⁹ᵐTc-DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid)** | Plasma sampling, or gamma camera (Gates method); DTPA also gives split function |

**5. Split function**
- DTPA renogram gives differential function; MAG3 or DMSA alternatives. A difference >10% (beyond 45:55) matters: **leave the better kidney with the donor**.

### KDIGO 2017 approach
- Initial **eGFRcr**; confirm with **mGFR, measured creatinine clearance or eGFRcr-cys**.

| GFR (mL/min/1.73 m²) | Decision |
|---|---|
| **≥90** | Accept |
| **60–89** | Individualise (age, lifetime risk) |
| **<60** | Decline |

- Indian practice: **DTPA GFR** with split function plus CT (computed tomography) angiography is standard.

### Clinical uses of creatinine clearance
- Drug dosing; **cisplatin eligibility** (CrCl <60 mL/min usually unfit).
- Extremes of body size, amputees and pregnancy, where eGFR is unreliable.
- Living donor evaluation (confirm with measured GFR).

**Diagram to draw:** plasma tracer clearance curve after a single injection; nephron showing filtration plus a small secreted fraction of creatinine.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - CKD-EPI 2021 race-free; cystatin C adds accuracy.
> - Inulin is gold standard; iohexol or DTPA in practice.
> - KDIGO 2017: ≥90 accept, 60–89 individualise, <60 decline.
> - Better kidney stays with the donor.
> - CrCl = UV/P; check completeness by creatinine excreted per kg.
> - Secretion makes CrCl overestimate GFR as function falls.
> - Cockcroft–Gault: (140 − age) × weight / (72 × SCr), × 0.85 women.

## Berger's Disease.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3a

*Berger's disease is IgA (immunoglobulin A) nephropathy (IgAN).*

### Definition
- **Commonest primary glomerulonephritis (GN) worldwide**, in children too. Dominant **mesangial IgA deposits** on immunofluorescence.
- Commonest cause of **glomerular haematuria** (about one-third). Second–third decades; male predominance.
- **IgA vasculitis** (Henoch–Schönlein purpura) is the systemic form.

### Pathogenesis
- "Multi-hit" model: abnormal (galactose-deficient) IgA1 → autoantibodies → immune complexes → mesangial deposition, complement activation, proliferation. Mucosal (respiratory) infection triggers flares.

### Clinical features
- **Recurrent painless macroscopic haematuria during or within days of an upper respiratory infection** ("synpharyngitic"), or after exercise. Post-streptococcal GN follows infection by weeks.
- Persistent microscopic haematuria, proteinuria, hypertension; occasional loin pain; rarely nephrotic syndrome or RPGN (rapidly progressive GN).

### Evaluation (urologist's role)
- **Dysmorphic RBCs (red blood cells), RBC casts, proteinuria** → glomerular source. **Normal C3 and C4.**
- Without casts, complete the urological evaluation (imaging ± cystoscopy) to exclude a urinary tract cause.
- **Renal biopsy** confirms. Once diagnosed, repeat haematuria work-ups are not needed.

**Oxford MEST-C score (biopsy)**
| Item | Grades |
|---|---|
| M: mesangial hypercellularity | M0, M1 |
| E: endocapillary hypercellularity | E0, E1 |
| S: segmental sclerosis | S0, S1 |
| T: tubular atrophy/interstitial fibrosis | T0 ≤25%, T1 26–50%, T2 >50% |
| C: crescents | C0 none, C1 <25%, C2 ≥25% of glomeruli |

### Prognosis
- Often benign, but **20–30% reach ESKD (end-stage kidney disease)**.
- Poor: persistent proteinuria, hypertension, raised creatinine, older onset, high MEST-C.

### Management
- No cure. **Supportive:** ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor or ARB (angiotensin receptor blocker), BP (blood pressure) control, low salt, stop smoking.
- **Newer drugs:** SGLT2 (sodium–glucose cotransporter 2) inhibitors; sparsentan (dual endothelin–angiotensin receptor blocker); targeted-release budesonide acting on gut mucosal lymphoid tissue.
- Persistent proteinuria above about 0.75–1 g/day despite supportive care: consider a 6-month steroid course (KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2021); weigh infection risk.
- Crescentic RPGN: steroids plus cyclophosphamide.
- **Transplant:** recurs histologically often, clinically rarely.

**Diagram to draw:** glomerulus with mesangial IgA deposits.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Commonest primary GN; haematuria with upper respiratory infection.
> - Normal complement; biopsy shows mesangial IgA.
> - Oxford MEST-C; 20–30% ESKD.
> - ACE inhibitor or ARB is the mainstay.

## Renal allograft rejection.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3d

### Definition
- Recipient immune attack on donor (allo) antigens: **HLA (human leucocyte antigen)** class I (A, B, C) and II (DR, DQ, DP), ABO and non-HLA antigens.
- Acute rejection has fallen from over 60% (pre-ciclosporin) to **below 10%**. **Chronic antibody injury** now limits graft survival.

### Mechanism
- **T-cell**: direct (donor antigen-presenting cells) and indirect pathways → cytotoxic T cells → tubulitis, arteritis.
- **Antibody**: DSA (donor-specific antibody) binds endothelium → complement, leaving **C4d** → microvascular inflammation.

### Types
| Type | Timing | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Hyperacute** | Minutes–hours | Preformed ABO/HLA antibody; thrombosis; graft turns blue. **Prevented by crossmatch** |
| Accelerated | 2–5 days | Memory response in sensitised recipient |
| **Acute TCMR (T-cell-mediated rejection)** | Mostly first 6 months | Interstitial infiltrate, tubulitis, arteritis |
| **Acute AMR (antibody-mediated rejection)** | Any time | DSA, C4d, capillaritis |
| **Chronic active AMR** | Months–years | Transplant glomerulopathy, arterial intimal fibrosis; main cause of late loss |
| Subclinical | — | Histological rejection, stable function |

- **Risk factors:** HLA mismatch, DSA, ABO incompatibility, long ischaemia and DGF (delayed graft function), **non-adherence**, retransplant, previous rejection.

### Diagnosis
- Rising creatinine, oliguria; fever and graft tenderness now rare.
- **Exclude:** obstruction, urine leak, lymphocele, renal artery stenosis or thrombosis (ultrasound with Doppler), CNI (calcineurin inhibitor) toxicity, BK virus nephropathy, recurrent disease.
- **Biopsy is the gold standard (Banff):**

| Banff category | Criteria |
|---|---|
| Borderline | Foci of tubulitis with mild inflammation |
| TCMR IA/IB | Interstitial inflammation >25% with moderate/severe tubulitis |
| TCMR IIA/IIB | Mild-moderate/severe intimal arteritis |
| TCMR III | Transmural arteritis or fibrinoid necrosis |
| Active AMR | Tissue injury + antibody–endothelium interaction (C4d) + circulating DSA |

- **Monitoring:** de novo DSA predicts loss; dd-cfDNA (donor-derived cell-free DNA) has negative predictive value >90%; gene-expression panels.

### Treatment
- **TCMR:** IV (intravenous) pulse methylprednisolone; **ATG (antithymocyte globulin)** for Banff II–III or steroid-resistant.
- **AMR:** not standardised: steroids, **plasmapheresis, IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin)**, rituximab; eculizumab in selected patients; anti-IL-6 (interleukin-6) for chronic active AMR.
- Optimise maintenance (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, steroids); address adherence.

**Diagram to draw:** Banff biopsy features: tubulitis, arteritis, peritubular capillary C4d.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hyperacute rejection prevented by crossmatch.
> - Banff AMR = injury + C4d + DSA.
> - TCMR: pulse steroids, ATG; AMR: plasmapheresis, IVIG.
> - Chronic active AMR is the main cause of late loss.

## Immunosuppressants in renal transplantation (classification, protocols, ciclosporin)
**Asked as:**
- Immunosuppressants. — *Summer 2021 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)
- Classify immunosuppresants. Describe few common protocols. — *Practice Set 3 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)* (also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3f and 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)
- Cyclosporins — *Practice Set 4 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)* (immunosuppressants also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3f; tacrolimus in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3c)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Taken as immunosuppressant drugs (immunosuppressants) in renal transplantation; "Cyclosporins" taken as ciclosporin (cyclosporin A).*

### Principle
- T-cell activation needs three signals: **signal 1** (TCR [T-cell receptor] binding donor antigen → calcineurin → IL-2 [interleukin-2] transcription), **signal 2** (costimulation, CD28–CD80/86) and **signal 3** (IL-2 receptor → mTOR [mammalian target of rapamycin] → proliferation). Each drug blocks a step (drugs block these steps).

### Phases
- **Induction** (peri-transplant), **maintenance** (lifelong), **rescue** (**treatment of rejection**).

### Classification
| Class | Drug(s) | Mechanism / action | Main toxicity |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CNI** (calcineurin inhibitor(s)) | **Tacrolimus**, ciclosporin | Block calcineurin (signal 1) | **Nephrotoxicity**, diabetes, tremor, hypertension; ciclosporin: gum hypertrophy, hirsutism |
| **Antimetabolites** (antiproliferatives) | **Mycophenolate**, azathioprine | Block purine synthesis (IMPDH [inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase]) | Diarrhoea, leucopenia |
| **mTOR inhibitors** | Sirolimus, everolimus | Block signal 3 | **Lymphocele, poor wound healing**, proteinuria, dyslipidaemia; not nephrotoxic, antitumour |
| **Corticosteroids** | Prednisolone, methylprednisolone | Block cytokine transcription | Diabetes, bone loss, cataract |
| **Costimulation blocker** | **Belatacept** | CTLA4-Ig binds CD80/86 (signal 2) | **PTLD** (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder); avoid if EBV (Epstein–Barr virus)-negative |
| **Depleting antibodies** | rATG (rabbit antithymocyte globulin), alemtuzumab | T-cell depletion | Infection, PTLD |
| **Non-depleting** (antibody) | **Basiliximab** | Anti-CD25 (IL-2 receptor) | Few |
| **B-cell, antibody** (antibody/B-cell agents) | Rituximab, IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin), plasmapheresis, bortezomib, eculizumab, imlifidase | Antibody-mediated rejection, desensitisation; antibody removal or blockade | — |

### Standard regimen and common protocols
- **Over 90%** receive induction and **tacrolimus plus mycophenolate** (tacrolimus–mycophenolate); steroids in more than two-thirds. Acute rejection has fallen from ≥60% to <10%.
1. **Standard-risk (most recipients):** basiliximab 20 mg IV (intravenous) on days 0 and 4 + intraoperative methylprednisolone; then **tacrolimus + mycophenolate + tapering prednisolone**.
2. **High-immunological-risk** (sensitised, retransplant, DGF [delayed graft function] expected): **rATG** induction, then triple therapy.
3. **Steroid-avoidance or early withdrawal:** low-risk recipients, usually after depleting induction.
4. **CNI-sparing:** belatacept- or mTOR-based regimens to spare nephrotoxicity; TRANSFORM: everolimus with reduced CNI non-inferior.
5. **ABO-incompatible or HLA (human leucocyte antigen)-sensitised:** desensitisation with rituximab, plasmapheresis and IVIG; imlifidase for crossmatch-positive.
6. **Rejection:** T-cell-mediated → (pulse) IV (intravenous) steroids / methylprednisolone, then rATG (for Banff grade II–III); antibody-mediated → steroids, plasmapheresis, IVIG, rituximab.

- **Monitoring:** **tacrolimus trough monitoring** (trough levels: higher early, lower later); CYP3A4 (cytochrome P450 3A4) interactions (azoles, macrolides raise levels; rifampicin lowers).

### Ciclosporin (cyclosporin A)
#### Introduction
- Ciclosporin is a cyclic peptide of 11 amino acids from the fungus *Tolypocladium inflatum*. It was the first **CNI (calcineurin inhibitor)**.
- Its introduction in the early 1980s cut acute rejection from at least 60% (pre-ciclosporin era) towards today's <10%. Tacrolimus has now largely replaced it.

#### Mechanism
```
Ciclosporin + cyclophilin (cytoplasm)
   → complex inhibits calcineurin
   → NFAT (nuclear factor of activated T cells) stays phosphorylated
   → no IL-2 (interleukin-2) transcription → T-cell activation blocked (signal 1)
```

#### Pharmacokinetics and monitoring
- Variable oral absorption; the **microemulsion form** absorbs more predictably.
- Metabolised by **CYP3A4** (cytochrome P450 3A4) and P-glycoprotein; narrow therapeutic index, so whole-blood **trough (C0) or 2-hour (C2) levels** guide dosing.

#### Uses
- **Transplantation:** maintenance with an antimetabolite and steroids, when tacrolimus is not tolerated.
- **IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome):** refractory **Hunner lesions**, 1–3 mg/kg/day; better than pentosan polysulfate.
- **Others:** retroperitoneal fibrosis after steroid failure; frequently relapsing nephrotic syndrome; severe psoriasis and other dermatoses.

#### Toxicity
| System | Effect |
|---|---|
| Kidney | **Nephrotoxicity**: acute afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction (reversible); chronic striped fibrosis, arteriolar hyalinosis; TMA (thrombotic microangiopathy) |
| Metabolic | Hypertension, hyperkalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, **hyperuricaemia** (gout), dyslipidaemia |
| Cosmetic | **Gingival hyperplasia, hirsutism** |
| Neurological | Tremor, PRES (posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome) |
| Immune | Infection, skin cancer, PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder) |

#### Interactions
- **Levels rise:** azoles, erythromycin and clarithromycin, diltiazem, verapamil, grapefruit.
- **Levels fall:** rifampicin, phenytoin, carbamazepine, St John's wort.
- More nephrotoxicity with NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) and aminoglycosides.

#### Versus tacrolimus
- Ciclosporin: more hirsutism, gum hypertrophy, hypertension and hyperlipidaemia; less diabetes and tremor.
- **ELITE-Symphony (2007):** low-dose tacrolimus gave less rejection and better GFR (glomerular filtration rate) than ciclosporin regimens.

### Urological relevance
- **Infection:** UTI (urinary tract infection), **BK virus** (ureteric stricture, nephropathy), CMV (cytomegalovirus).
- **Malignancy:** skin cancer, PTLD, RCC (renal cell carcinoma, mostly native kidneys), **bladder cancer about 3-fold** and more aggressive.
- **Surgery:** lymphocele and wound problems (mTOR; lymphocele with mTOR inhibitors); stones (CNI, steroids).
- **Urological note:** CNIs promote transplant stones (hyperuricaemia, hyperoxaluria, hypocitraturia).
- **Also know:** belatacept (BENEFIT trial: better glomerular filtration rate), extended-release tacrolimus, TRANSFORM (everolimus with reduced CNI non-inferior).

**Diagram to draw:** antigen-presenting cell (APC)–T cell (synapse) with signals 1–3 and the drug acting at each; T cell with the cyclophilin–calcineurin–NFAT–IL-2 pathway.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tacrolimus + mycophenolate ± steroids (maintenance) after induction (basiliximab or ATG / rATG).
> - CNI nephrotoxic; mTOR inhibitors cause lymphocele and poor healing.
> - Belatacept blocks signal 2; avoid if EBV-negative.
> - Rejection: steroids for T-cell-mediated; plasmapheresis, IVIG, rituximab for antibody-mediated.
> - Ciclosporin: cyclophilin → calcineurin → NFAT → IL-2 blocked.
> - Nephrotoxic; gum hyperplasia and hirsutism; CYP3A4 interactions.
> - Urology uses: transplant, refractory Hunner lesions, retroperitoneal fibrosis.

## Kidney retrieval techniques for organ donation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3c
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q2, with donor selection)

### Types of donor
- **Deceased:** DBD (donation after brain death; circulation maintained) or DCD (donation after circulatory death; warm ischaemia).
- **Living:** related or unrelated (THOA, Transplantation of Human Organs Act, in India).

### A. Deceased multi-organ retrieval (DBD)
1. Midline laparotomy with sternotomy.
2. Mobilise bowel off retroperitoneum (Cattell–Braasch); expose aorta and IVC (inferior vena cava); **look for and preserve accessory renal arteries**.
3. Heparin; cannulate distal aorta; clamp supracoeliac aorta; **vent IVC**; flush cold UW (University of Wisconsin) or HTK (histidine–tryptophan–ketoglutarate) solution; surface ice; open Gerota's fascia laterally.
4. Heart, lungs, liver and pancreas removed first.
5. Kidneys removed **en bloc** with aorta, IVC, perinephric fat and ureters, keeping periureteric tissue.
- **DCD:** super-rapid laparotomy and direct aortic cannulation to cut warm ischaemia; usually machine perfusion.
- **Back table:** separate kidneys; aortic **Carrel patch**; IVC lengthens the short right renal vein; ligate lymphatics; biopsy marginal kidneys; package at 4 °C.
- Paediatric donors <15 kg: en bloc kidneys.

### B. Living donor nephrectomy
- **Principles:** donor safety first; maximal vessel length; keep periureteric tissue and the lower pole–ureter triangle; minimal handling; short warm ischaemia.
- **Left kidney preferred** (longer vein).

| Technique | Features |
|---|---|
| Open (flank or mini-incision) | Complex anatomy, more often right side |
| **Laparoscopic** (Ratner, 1995) | Preferred; less pain, faster recovery |
| **Hand-assisted** | About two-thirds of US cases; tactile control |
| Retroperitoneoscopic, LESS (laparoendoscopic single-site), robotic | Selected centres |
- Sequence: hilar dissection → ureter divided → artery then vein stapled or clipped → rapid extraction → cold flush.
- **Risks:** complications about 16%; mortality 3 per 10,000; conversion 0.26–3.34%.

### Preservation
- Cooling slows metabolism; hypothermic machine perfusion reduces DGF (delayed graft function), not long-term loss.

**Diagram to draw:** aortic cannula, supracoeliac clamp, IVC vent, en bloc kidneys.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cannulate, clamp, vent, cool; kidneys removed last, en bloc.
> - Left kidney preferred for living donation.
> - Machine perfusion reduces DGF.

## Extended Criteria for Selection of Live Renal Donor
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3a
(donor assessment also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3a; marginal donor in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3e)

*Read as the expanded (marginal, "medically complex") living donor.*

### Definition and principle
- A living donor with a risk factor that would once have excluded donation; used because of organ shortage.
- **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017** replaced fixed cut-offs with a **risk-based approach**: the donor's estimated lifetime ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) risk is compared with the centre's acceptable threshold. Donor safety comes first.
- Counsel with figures: donor lifetime ESKD 90 vs 14 per 10,000 matched non-donors (Muzaale 2014); mortality 3 per 10,000.

### Extended criteria

| Factor | Standard | Extended (individualised) | Exclude |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Age** | Adult | Older donors (often >60) acceptable; no upper limit | Under legal age |
| **GFR (glomerular filtration rate)**, mL/min/1.73 m² | ≥90 | **60–89** | <60 |
| **Albuminuria**, mg/day | <30 | **30–100** | >100 |
| **Hypertension** | None | Controlled on 1–2 drugs, no target-organ damage | Uncontrolled, organ damage |
| **Obesity** | BMI (body mass index) <30 | **30–35** | Many centres: >35 |
| **Glucose** | Normal | Prediabetes; selected type 2 diabetes | Type 1 diabetes |
| **Stones** | None | Single past stone, low recurrence risk; small stone removed ex vivo | Bilateral or recurrent; cystinuria, primary hyperoxaluria |
| **Anatomy** | Single vessels | Multiple arteries, simple cyst, mild FMD (fibromuscular dysplasia) | Major anomaly |

- **Why older donors are acceptable:** their remaining lifespan is short, so lifetime ESKD risk is low. Young donors carry the highest lifetime risk.
- **Also know:** isolated microscopic haematuria after negative work-up; treated low-risk cancer; APOL1 (apolipoprotein L1) high-risk genotype; future pregnancy (pre-eclampsia risk).

### Safeguards
- Measured GFR and split function; **the better kidney stays with the donor**.
- CT (computed tomography) angiography; laparoscopic retrieval.
- India: **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act)** authorisation; independent donor advocate.
- **Outcome:** grafts from older living donors do about as well as standard deceased-donor kidneys.

**Diagram to draw:** risk-threshold scale (accept, individualise, decline).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - KDIGO 2017: risk-based lifetime ESKD threshold.
> - GFR 60–89 and albuminuria 30–100 are individualised.
> - Controlled hypertension on 1–2 drugs may donate; type 1 diabetes cannot.

## Vascular complications after renal transplantation, including transplant renal artery stenosis
**Asked as:**
- Vascular Complications after Renal Transplantation in Recipient — *Winter 2025 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*
- Transplant renal artery stenosis — *Practice Set 20 SAQ Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(vascular complications of transplantation also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3d)

### Introduction
- Uncommon but a major cause of **early graft loss**. Doppler ultrasound is the first test.
- **Concept:** no inflow gives anuria; blocked outflow gives a swollen, tender graft with haematuria; reduced inflow gives hypertension and fluid retention.

### Main complications

| | Arterial thrombosis | Venous thrombosis | TRAS (transplant renal artery stenosis) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Incidence | 0.2–5% | 0.1–8.2% | 1–23% (commonest) |
| Timing | First days | First week | Months (usually 3 months–2 years) |
| Presentation | Sudden anuria | Painful swollen graft, haematuria, oliguria | Refractory hypertension, rising creatinine, flash pulmonary oedema |
| Doppler | No arterial flow | **Reversed diastolic flow**, clot in vein | High velocity at stenosis; **parvus-tardus** waveform distally |
| Treatment | Immediate exploration; thrombectomy (catheter thrombolysis described); nephrectomy if non-viable | Immediate exploration, thrombectomy; nephrectomy if necrotic | **Angioplasty or stenting** (first choice); surgical revision if that fails |

- **Thrombosis risks:** extreme donor or recipient age, **cold ischaemia >24 hours**, multiple arteries, technical error (kink, intimal flap), hypovolaemia, rejection, thrombophilia (factor V Leiden, antiphospholipid antibodies).
- **TRAS causes:** anastomotic technique, clamp injury, recipient atherosclerosis (iliac inflow stenosis mimics it). Contrast angiography confirms.

### Transplant renal artery stenosis (TRAS) in detail
#### Definition and incidence
- TRAS (transplant renal artery stenosis): narrowing of the graft artery or its inflow; **commonest vascular complication (1–23%)**, usually 3 months to 2 years after transplant.

#### Types and causes
- **Anastomotic:** suture technique, end-to-end anastomosis to the internal iliac artery, no aortic (Carrel) patch.
- **Post-anastomotic (distal):** clamp or perfusion-cannula intimal injury, kinking, rejection.
- **Pre-anastomotic ("pseudo-TRAS"):** recipient iliac atherosclerosis.
- **Also know:** CMV (cytomegalovirus) infection, delayed graft function, deceased donor.

#### Pathophysiology and clinical features
- Reduced graft perfusion activates the **renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system**.
- **Refractory hypertension**, rising creatinine, fluid retention, **flash pulmonary oedema**; graft bruit.
- Clue: creatinine rises sharply after an ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor.

#### Evaluation
- **Doppler ultrasound first:** high peak velocity at stenosis (about >200–250 cm/s), and distal **parvus-tardus** waveform with low resistive index.
- MRA (magnetic resonance angiography) or CO₂ angiography to spare contrast.
- **Contrast angiography is the gold standard** and allows treatment at once. Iodinated contrast risks nephrotoxicity; gadolinium is avoided at low GFR (glomerular filtration rate).
- **Differential of graft dysfunction with hypertension:** rejection, calcineurin-inhibitor toxicity, ureteric obstruction.

#### Management
- **Medical:** for mild stenosis with stable function: blood pressure control (calcium-channel blockers), statin, antiplatelet; avoid or monitor ACE inhibitors.
- **Percutaneous angioplasty with or without stent: treatment of choice** for significant stenosis (usually >50–60%). High technical success; reverses hypertension and graft dysfunction. Stents reduce restenosis.
- **Surgical revision** (resection and re-anastomosis, interposition graft, bypass) if angioplasty fails or anatomy unsuitable; risks graft loss.
- Follow-up Doppler after angioplasty detects restenosis; untreated TRAS ends in graft loss.

### Other vascular complications
- **Haemorrhage:** hilar vessels or anastomosis; re-explore if unstable.
- **Pseudoaneurysm (<1%):** technical or **mycotic**; rupture risk; endovascular or open repair, often graft nephrectomy, prolonged antibiotics.
- **Post-biopsy AV (arteriovenous) fistula:** persistent haematuria; usually closes; large or with steal: **coil embolisation**. Subcapsular haematoma can cause a **Page kidney**.
- **Graft torsion:** intraperitoneal grafts; prevent with nephropexy.
- **DVT (deep vein thrombosis)** of the iliac vein; **lymphocele** compressing the iliac or renal vein.
- **Limb ischaemia** from iliac dissection; internal iliac use: gluteal claudication, erectile dysfunction.

### Prevention
- Anastomose away from atheroma; check the lie for kinking; avoid clamp injury and kinking; use a Carrel patch where possible.
- Extend a short right renal vein with donor IVC (inferior vena cava).
- Short cold ischaemia; anticoagulate thrombophilic patients; avoid hypovolaemia.
- At reperfusion, a soft kidney suggests poor inflow; a tense, engorged kidney suggests outflow block.

**Diagram to draw:** Doppler waveforms: normal, parvus-tardus (TRAS; distal to stenosis) and reversed diastolic flow (venous thrombosis).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TRAS is the commonest vascular complication; presents months later; angioplasty or stent first (first-line); angiography is gold standard.
> - Refractory hypertension plus rising creatinine: Doppler first.
> - Venous thrombosis: reversed diastolic flow, swollen tender graft.
> - Arterial thrombosis: sudden anuria; explore immediately.

## Management of acute hyperkalaemia
**Asked as:**
- Management of Acute Hyperkalemia — *Winter 2025 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- Mgt. of acute hyperkalemia. — *Practice Set 6 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3e)

### Definition and physiology
- Serum potassium **>5.5 mmol/L**; mild 5.5–5.9, moderate 6.0–6.4, **severe ≥6.5** (UK Kidney Association). Above 6.0 is generally urgent.
- About 98% of potassium is intracellular; the ratio sets the resting membrane potential, so a high level (high levels) causes **arrhythmia and cardiac arrest**.
- Defence: rapid shift into cells (insulin, β₂-agonists via Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase), then renal excretion (needs distal sodium delivery, urine flow, aldosterone).

### Causes

| Mechanism | Examples |
|---|---|
| Pseudohyperkalaemia | Haemolysis, tourniquet, fist clenching, thrombocytosis |
| Reduced excretion | AKI (acute kidney injury), CKD (chronic kidney disease), **obstructive uropathy** (type 4 RTA [renal tubular acidosis]), adrenal insufficiency; drugs: ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors, ARBs (angiotensin receptor blockers), spironolactone, NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs), tacrolimus |
| Shift out of cells | Acidosis, insulin deficiency, β-blockers, suxamethonium |
| Cell release | **Rhabdomyolysis** (prolonged lithotomy or flank position), tumour lysis, crush |
| Urological | **Jejunal conduit** (reabsorbs urinary potassium) |

### Assessment
- Repeat the sample to **exclude pseudohyperkalaemia** if the ECG (electrocardiogram) is normal.
- **ECG changes:** peaked T (earliest) → prolonged PR, wide QRS → loss of P → sine wave → ventricular fibrillation (VF). Ascending weakness.
- Creatinine, glucose, blood gas, CK (creatine kinase); stop potassium sources.

### Emergency treatment (in order)

| Step | Drug and dose | Onset and effect |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Protect the heart** | **Calcium gluconate 10%, 10 mL IV (intravenous)** over 5–10 min; repeat if ECG unchanged | 1–3 min; lasts 30–60 min; does not lower potassium |
| **2. Shift into cells** | **Insulin 10 units regular IV with 50 mL of 50% dextrose** | 15–30 min; lowers about 0.6–1 mmol/L; check (monitor) glucose (hypoglycaemia) |
| | **Salbutamol 20 mg nebulised** | Additive to insulin |
| | Sodium bicarbonate | Only with metabolic acidosis |
| **3. Remove** | Loop diuretic if passing urine; **binders**: sodium zirconium cyclosilicate (about 1 h), patiromer (about 7 h), sodium polystyrene sulfonate (slow; colonic necrosis) | Hours |
