| | **Haemodialysis** | Refractory or oliguric, ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) |

- **Relieve obstruction** (catheter, nephrostomy); watch for post-obstructive diuresis.
- Monitor ECG, potassium and glucose; treat the cause.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: ECG change or K⁺ ≥6.5 → calcium → shift → remove.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Calcium stabilises the membrane but does not lower potassium.
> - Insulin 10 units + 50 mL 50% dextrose; salbutamol 20 mg nebulised.
> - Binders, diuretics and dialysis remove potassium; relieve obstruction.

## Vascular grafts in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 1, Q3e
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q2 and 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

### Introduction
- A vascular graft **replaces, bypasses, extends or widens** a vessel when primary repair would leave tension or narrowing. Patch widens a wall; interposition graft replaces a segment; bypass routes flow around a lesion.

### Types
| Type | Examples | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Autologous vein** | Saphenous, gonadal, donor external iliac vein | Resists infection; small vessels | Limited length |
| **Autologous artery** | Internal iliac, **Carrel aortic patch** | Best for renal artery | Limited supply |
| **Xenograft** | Bovine pericardium | Pliable patch | Patch only |
| **Synthetic** | **PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene)**, Dacron | Large calibre, any length | Infection; venous grafts thrombose |

### Applications
1. **Renal transplantation:** multiple arteries (18–43%) joined by Carrel patch or bench reconstruction; **preserve the lower-pole artery** (supplies the ureter). Short right renal vein extended with donor IVC (inferior vena cava), external iliac or spiral gonadal vein.
2. **RCC (renal cell carcinoma) with IVC thrombus:** residual lumen <50% → **patch cavoplasty**; circumferential wall invasion → **PTFE replacement (16–20 mm)**, then lifelong aspirin.
3. **PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy retroperitoneal lymph node dissection):** IVC narrowing >25% → patch or graft; IVC ligation tolerated. **Aorta never ligated**: PTFE or Dacron replacement (about 1%).
4. **Retroperitoneal sarcoma:** caval resection and graft; thrombosis 10–40%.
5. **Renovascular repair and autotransplantation:** ex vivo bench repair with internal iliac artery or saphenous vein.
6. **Dialysis access:** PTFE AV (arteriovenous) graft when no native fistula is possible.
7. **Penile:** Grayhack saphenous–cavernosal shunt for priapism.

### Principles
- Autologous in contaminated fields and small vessels; synthetic for large, high-flow vessels.
- Venous grafts: use the widest graft. Heparin before clamping; tension-free spatulated anastomosis with monofilament polypropylene; **omental wrap** keeps synthetic grafts away from bowel and urine.

### Complications
- Thrombosis, stenosis, infection (synthetic usually removed), graft–enteric fistula, **ureteroarterial fistula**.

**Diagram to draw:** IVC patch cavoplasty and PTFE caval graft.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Autologous for small or contaminated fields; PTFE or Dacron for large vessels.
> - IVC: lumen <50% → patch; invaded wall → PTFE 16–20 mm.
> - PC-RPLND: IVC may be ligated; aorta must be grafted.

## Hypokalemia
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 1, Q3f

### Definition and physiology
- **Hypokalaemia:** serum potassium **<3.5 mEq/L**; severe <2.5 mEq/L.
- About **98% of body potassium is intracellular**; the ratio sets the resting membrane potential.
- Two defences: **shift into cells** (insulin, β2-adrenergic stimulation, alkalosis) and **renal excretion** (distal nephron). Distal secretion rises with high urine flow, distal sodium delivery, **aldosterone** and alkalosis.

### Causes
| Mechanism | Causes |
|---|---|
| Low intake | Malnutrition |
| **Shift into cells** | Insulin, catecholamines (stress, β-agonists), alkalosis, refeeding, periodic paralysis |
| **GI (gastrointestinal) loss** | Diarrhoea, villous adenoma, **ureterosigmoidostomy and colonic diversions** (colon secretes potassium, ileum absorbs it) |
| **Renal loss, high BP (blood pressure)** | **Primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn)**, renin-secreting juxtaglomerular tumour, Cushing, Liddle, **abiraterone** (mineralocorticoid excess) |
| **Renal loss, normal BP** | Loop and thiazide diuretics, **postobstructive diuresis**, osmotic diuresis, **RTA (renal tubular acidosis) types 1 and 2**, Bartter, Gitelman, hypomagnesaemia, amphotericin, cisplatin |

- **Vomiting:** potassium is lost mainly in urine (alkalosis → kaliuresis), not in gastric juice.

### Clinical features
- Muscle weakness, cramps, ileus, **flaccid paralysis**, rhabdomyolysis.
- Arrhythmias; potentiates digoxin toxicity.
- Polyuria (nephrogenic diabetes insipidus); **hypocitraturia** → calcium stones.
- **ECG (electrocardiogram):** flat T wave, **prominent U wave**, ST depression, prolonged QU; ventricular arrhythmias.

### Evaluation
- Repeat potassium; magnesium, bicarbonate, glucose.
- **24-h urine potassium:** <20 mmol/day suggests extrarenal loss; higher suggests renal loss.
- Renal loss: check blood pressure and acid–base; **aldosterone–renin ratio** if hypertensive (only a minority of Conn patients are hypokalaemic).

### Management
- Treat the cause; stop offending drugs.
- **Potassium chloride** preferred (potassium citrate if acidotic, phosphate if hypophosphataemic). **Oral** 20–40 mEq repeated when possible.
- **Intravenous** for severe or symptomatic cases: usually ≤10 mEq/h peripherally; faster only centrally with ECG monitoring. Never as a bolus.
- **Correct magnesium**, or potassium will not correct.
- **Urinary diversion:** replace potassium **before or with bicarbonate**; correcting acidosis alone drives potassium into cells → paralysis.
- Correct before abiraterone and adrenalectomy for Conn syndrome (spironolactone preoperatively).

**Diagram to draw:** ECG strip showing flat T and U wave.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Shift or loss; urine potassium separates renal from extrarenal.
> - ECG: flat T, U wave.
> - Diversion acidosis: give potassium with or before bicarbonate.
> - Correct magnesium first.

## Lymphocele and postoperative lymphatic leak (chylous ascites)
**Asked as:**
- Lymphocele — *Practice Set 8 Paper I Q3a (SAQ)*
- Postoperative lymphatic leak — *Practice Set 14 Paper I Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Postoperative lymphatic leak taken as lymphocele and chylous ascites after RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection), nephrectomy, pelvic node dissection and transplantation.*

### Definition and types
- **Lymphocele:** a collection of lymph **without an epithelial lining** (a pseudocyst with a fibrous wall), caused by divided lymphatics that keep leaking. It is the **commonest complication of PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)**.
- **Chylous ascites:** lipid-rich lymph in the peritoneum from the cisterna chyli or major trunks.

### Settings and incidence
- **PLND** (radical prostatectomy, cystectomy): radiological in up to 60%, clinically significant 2–5%. An extended template roughly doubles the rate.
- **Renal transplantation:** from lymphatics around the recipient iliac vessels or donor hilum. Radiological 0.6–33.9% (symptomatic 0.03–26%); most appear within 6 months, peaking at 6 weeks.
- Also after RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection; lymphocele ≤1.7%), inguinal dissection, nephrectomy.
- **Chylous ascites:** RPLND: 0.2–2.1% primary, **2–12% post-chemotherapy**; left nephrectomy with hilar dissection.

| Type | Definition | Setting and frequency |
|---|---|---|
| **Lymphocele** | Lymph collection **without epithelial lining** | **PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection):** radiological up to 60%, significant 2–5%. **Transplant:** symptomatic 0.03–26%, peak 6 weeks. **RPLND:** ≤1.7% |
| **Chylous ascites** | Lipid-rich lymph in the peritoneum from the cisterna chyli or major trunks | RPLND: 0.2–2.1% primary, **2–12% post-chemotherapy**; left nephrectomy with hilar dissection |

- **Risk factors:** extensive dissection, extended templates, suprahilar dissection near the cisterna chyli (L1–L2), post-chemotherapy surgery, obesity, heparin prophylaxis, mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors, acute rejection, retransplantation (re-transplant).

### Clinical features
- **Lymphocele:** most are silent (usually silent). Large ones compress:
  - iliac vein: leg oedema, **DVT (deep vein thrombosis)**;
  - bladder: frequency, urgency, pelvic pressure;
  - ureter or graft: hydronephrosis, rising creatinine (graft dysfunction).
- Infection (may become infected): fever, rigors, sepsis.
- **Chylous ascites:** rising girth, early satiety, dyspnoea; milky drain fluid.

### Diagnosis
- **Ultrasound:** anechoic, often septated, thin-walled collection. **CT (computed tomography)** maps it before intervention. Air or rim enhancement means infection.
- **Aspirate:** lymph has creatinine equal to (equals) serum (urinoma: much higher), lymphocytes; culture excludes abscess. Chyle: **triglyceride 2–8 × serum**, protein >3 g/dL.

### Management
**Lymphocele** (treat by symptoms, not size alone)
| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Asymptomatic, <5 cm | Observe; most resolve |
| Symptomatic | **Percutaneous drain** ± sclerotherapy (povidone-iodine, doxycycline) |
| Recurrent or thick-walled | **Laparoscopic fenestration (marsupialisation)** into a peritoneal window (best) |
| Infected | Antibiotics + percutaneous drainage; exclude urine leak |
| Laparoscopy unsafe | Open drainage |

```
Asymptomatic, <5 cm → observe
Symptomatic → percutaneous drain (± sclerotherapy)
Recurrent / thick-walled → laparoscopic peritoneal fenestration (best)
Infected → drainage + antibiotics; exclude urine leak
```

- **Success:** laparoscopic fenestration best, open next, drain or sclerotherapy intermediate, **aspiration alone worst** (reaccumulates, recurs, risks infection).
- Sclerosants (povidone-iodine, doxycycline) may hinder later fenestration.
- Fenestration lets the peritoneum absorb lymph (internal drainage): the definitive cure.
- **Lymphangiography with embolisation:** high success; long-term data awaited.

**Chylous ascites** (stepwise)
1. Paracentesis; drain if it reaccumulates.
2. **Very low-fat, high-protein diet with MCT (medium-chain triglycerides)**, which are absorbed directly into portal blood, bypassing lacteals; plus **octreotide**.
3. Nil by mouth with **TPN (total parenteral nutrition)**.
4. Refractory: lymphangiography with embolisation, peritoneovenous shunt or surgical ligation.
- Continue a low-fat diet for **≥4 weeks** after resolution.

### Prevention
- **Clip or ligate visible lymphatics** (clips often preferred; no robust data) (cephalad at the renal hilum in RPLND; perivascular at transplant). Spare lymphatics over the external iliac artery (leg lymph). Avoid unneeded suprahilar dissection.
- **Peritoneal interposition flap** fixed to the bladder (peritoneal flap after robotic PLND) reduces clinically significant lymphocele. Transperitoneal access is **not** protective (fluid loculates within 48–72 h).
- Drain after transplantation and major nodal dissections.

**Diagram to draw:** pelvic lymphocele compressing the external iliac vein and bladder, with a laparoscopic peritoneal window.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lymphocele: commonest PLND complication; radiological up to 60%, significant 2–5%.
> - Lymph creatinine = serum; urinoma creatinine higher.
> - Laparoscopic fenestration best (is best); aspiration alone worst.
> - Chylous ascites: 2–12% after post-chemotherapy RPLND; triglyceride 2–8 × serum.
> - Ladder: diet with MCT and octreotide → TPN → embolisation or shunt.

## Hyperfiltration injury in AKI and CKD
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 1, Q3c

### Definition
- **Hyperfiltration:** a rise in **SNGFR (single-nephron glomerular filtration rate)** in surviving nephrons when nephron mass falls; whole-kidney GFR (glomerular filtration rate) may look normal.
- **Brenner hypothesis (1982):** the adaptation itself damages glomeruli.

### Physiology
- Net filtration pressure ≈ 10 mmHg (hydrostatic 60 − Bowman's 18 − oncotic 32).
- Afferent dilatation raises flow and filtration; **efferent constriction (angiotensin II) raises filtration pressure**.

### Mechanism
```
Nephron loss → afferent dilatation + efferent constriction
→ raised glomerular capillary pressure and flow (hyperfiltration)
→ glomerular hypertrophy; podocytes cannot divide → stretch, detachment
→ proteinuria → FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis)
→ tubulointerstitial inflammation, fibrosis → more nephron loss (vicious cycle)
```

### In AKI (acute kidney injury)
- Incomplete repair leaves fewer nephrons, which hyperfilter; creatinine may normalise while **renal reserve is lost**.
- This drives the **AKI-to-CKD (chronic kidney disease) transition**; AKI persisting ≥90 days becomes CKD.
- Each perioperative AKI episode is a **modifiable risk factor** for CKD progression.

### In CKD
- Final common pathway of progression; marked by **albuminuria (KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) A stages)**, hypertension, falling eGFR (estimated GFR).

### Urological settings

| Setting | Point |
|---|---|
| Nephrectomy or donation | Compensatory hypertrophy 20–30%; donor function 60–80% of pre-donation |
| Partial nephrectomy, solitary kidney | >50% parenchymal loss → proteinuria, FSGS; marked when total nephron mass falls >80%; ESRD (end-stage renal disease) if <20–30% of one kidney remains |
| Congenital solitary kidney, MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney) | Monitor BP (blood pressure), proteinuria, yearly ultrasound |

- **Also know:** reflux nephropathy, infundibulopelvic stenosis, diabetes, obesity, pregnancy, low birth weight (low nephron endowment).

### Prevention and treatment
- Nephron sparing; avoid NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) and nephrotoxins.
- **BP <130/80 mmHg**; **ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor or ARB (angiotensin receptor blocker)** in proteinuric disease: efferent dilatation lowers glomerular pressure.
- **SGLT2 (sodium–glucose cotransporter-2) inhibitors:** restore tubuloglomerular feedback, causing afferent constriction. **DAPA-CKD** and **EMPA-KIDNEY** slowed progression with or without diabetes.
- Finerenone (type 2 diabetic CKD); dietary protein restriction.

**Diagram to draw:** glomerulus with dilated afferent and constricted efferent arterioles.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hyperfiltration = raised SNGFR; Brenner 1982.
> - Leads to FSGS, proteinuria and the AKI-to-CKD transition.
> - ACE inhibitors and SGLT2 inhibitors lower glomerular pressure.

## Management of CAPD peritonitis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 21 SAQ Sheet, Q3d
(CAPD as a whole answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3e and Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3a)

### Introduction
- CAPD (continuous ambulatory peritoneal dialysis) peritonitis is the leading cause of technique failure and transfer to haemodialysis. Commonest organism: **coagulase-negative *Staphylococcus*** (touch contamination).
- ISPD (International Society for Peritoneal Dialysis) 2022 target: centre rate **≤0.40 episodes per patient-year**.

### Diagnosis (any 2 of 3)
1. Abdominal pain and/or **cloudy effluent**.
2. Effluent **WCC (white cell count) >100/µL with >50% neutrophils** (dwell ≥2 hours).
3. Positive effluent culture.

### Initial steps
- Effluent cell count, Gram stain, **culture in blood-culture bottles** (culture-negative rate should be <15%).
- Examine exit site and tunnel; analgesia; heparin in bags if fibrin.

### Empirical antibiotics
- **Intraperitoneal route preferred** (intravenous if septic); start immediately after samples.
- Cover **Gram-positives** (vancomycin or first-generation cephalosporin, e.g. cefazolin) **and Gram-negatives** (third-generation cephalosporin, e.g. ceftazidime, or aminoglycoside). Choice follows centre sensitivities.
- Intermittent (once daily in long dwell) or continuous dosing; avoid prolonged aminoglycoside (residual function, ototoxicity).
- **Antifungal prophylaxis** (nystatin or fluconazole) during antibiotics.

### Organism-specific duration

| Organism | Duration |
|---|---|
| Coagulase-negative *Staphylococcus*, streptococci, culture-negative | 2 weeks |
| *S. aureus*, enterococcus, Gram-negatives | 3 weeks |
| *Pseudomonas* | 3 weeks, two antipseudomonal drugs |
| Polymicrobial enteric | Add metronidazole; surgical review (bowel perforation) |
| **Fungal** | **Immediate catheter removal** + antifungal |

### Catheter removal
- **Refractory** (effluent not clear after 5 days of right antibiotics), relapsing, fungal, refractory exit-site or tunnel infection.
- Haemodialysis meanwhile; new catheter after at least 2 weeks.

### Definitions
- **Relapse:** same organism (or sterile) within 4 weeks of finishing therapy. **Recurrent:** different organism within 4 weeks. **Repeat:** same organism after 4 weeks.

### Prevention
- Training, exit-site mupirocin or gentamicin, prophylaxis before colonoscopy or gynaecological procedures.

**Diagram to draw:** management flowchart from cloudy bag to catheter removal.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Two of: cloudy fluid or pain, WCC >100/µL with >50% neutrophils, positive culture.
> - Intraperitoneal Gram-positive plus Gram-negative cover.
> - Remove catheter for fungal or refractory (5 days) peritonitis.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe in details basic patho-physiology of urine production and transport (LAQ)

- RBF 25% of cardiac output; GFR 125 mL/min; net filtration pressure ~10 mmHg.
- Proximal 65%, loop 25%, distal 5–7% of Na⁺; ADH → V2 → AQP2.
- Myogenic peristalsis from calyceal pacemaker; post-obstructive diuresis 2% vs 50%.

#### Pelvicalyceal (renal collecting system) anatomy, its relevance to PCNL and endourology, and mini-PCNL (LAQ)

- Sampaio: A-I 45%, A-II (crossed) 17.2%, B-I 21.4%, B-II 16.4%.
- Fornix puncture: no arterial injury; upper-pole infundibulum 67% vascular injury.
- Target a posterior calyx with a short, wide infundibulum; upper pole reaches most.
- Lower pole: steep angle, long calyx >10 mm, infundibulum <5 mm unfavourable.
- Endopyelotomy lateral; infundibulotomy superior or inferior, never anterior.
- Pleura to 12th rib; retrorenal colon at the lower pole, commoner prone.
- Mini 15–22 Fr (Jackman 1998): fewer transfusions, longer operation.
- High intrarenal pressure in mini-PCNL causes fever and sepsis.

#### Lymphatic drainage of the genitourinary system and chyluria (etiopathogenesis and management) (LAQ)

- Lymph flows right → left; cisterna chyli at L1–L2, behind the right side of the aorta.
- Bladder drains mainly to external iliac nodes; left kidney to left para-aortic nodes.
- Chyluria = pyelolymphatic fistula from lymphatic obstruction and valve failure, usually filarial (*W. bancrofti*); often intermittent.
- Ether test and (urine) triglycerides confirm (ether test clears milky urine); cystoscopy after (a) fatty meal lateralises.
- Diet (MCT) + DEC (diet with MCT plus DEC) → (silver nitrate) sclerotherapy → laparoscopic hilar lymphatic disconnection; disconnection surgery for severe or refractory cases.
- Retroperitoneoscopic disconnection beats open.

#### Pathophysiology of Renovascular hypertension (LAQ)

- Goldblatt 1934: 2K1C = renin-dependent; 1K1C = volume-dependent.
- Chronic phase: contralateral nephrosclerosis makes RVH stenosis-independent.
- Angiotensin II keeps GFR by efferent constriction; ACE inhibitor causes acute kidney injury in bilateral RAS.
- ASTRAL and CORAL: no routine stenting; FMD = angioplasty alone.

#### Renin-angiotensin system (SAQ)

- Renin is rate-limiting; released by low pressure, low NaCl, β1.
- Efferent constriction maintains GFR, so ACE inhibitors risk renal failure in bilateral stenosis.
- Renin ratio >1.5 lateralises; reninoma excision cures most.

#### Patho-physiology of Brain stem death (SAQ)

- Raised ICP → Cushing reflex → storm → hypotension, DI, hypothermia, DIC.
- Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
- THOA: 4-member board, two tests ≥6 h apart; Rule of 100s.

#### Drugs associated with Acute Renal Failure. (SAQ)

- NSAIDs block afferent dilatation; ACE inhibitors/ARBs block efferent constriction.
- ATN: aminoglycosides, amphotericin, cisplatin, contrast.
- AIN: β-lactams, PPIs, NSAIDs, checkpoint inhibitors; stop and give steroids.
- Crystals: aciclovir, methotrexate, indinavir.

#### GFR estimation in a prospective renal donor and creatinine clearance (SAQ)

- CKD-EPI 2021 race-free; cystatin C adds accuracy.
- Inulin is gold standard; iohexol or DTPA in practice.
- KDIGO 2017: ≥90 accept, 60–89 individualise, <60 decline.
- Better kidney stays with the donor.
- CrCl = UV/P; check completeness by creatinine excreted per kg.
- Secretion makes CrCl overestimate GFR as function falls.
- Cockcroft–Gault: (140 − age) × weight / (72 × SCr), × 0.85 women.

#### Berger's Disease. (SAQ)

- Commonest primary GN; haematuria with upper respiratory infection.
- Normal complement; biopsy shows mesangial IgA.
- Oxford MEST-C; 20–30% ESKD.
- ACE inhibitor or ARB is the mainstay.

#### Renal allograft rejection. (SAQ)

- Hyperacute rejection prevented by crossmatch.
- Banff AMR = injury + C4d + DSA.
- TCMR: pulse steroids, ATG; AMR: plasmapheresis, IVIG.
- Chronic active AMR is the main cause of late loss.

#### Immunosuppressants in renal transplantation (classification, protocols, ciclosporin) (SAQ)

- Tacrolimus + mycophenolate ± steroids (maintenance) after induction (basiliximab or ATG / rATG).
- CNI nephrotoxic; mTOR inhibitors cause lymphocele and poor healing.
- Belatacept blocks signal 2; avoid if EBV-negative.
- Rejection: steroids for T-cell-mediated; plasmapheresis, IVIG, rituximab for antibody-mediated.
- Ciclosporin: cyclophilin → calcineurin → NFAT → IL-2 blocked.
- Nephrotoxic; gum hyperplasia and hirsutism; CYP3A4 interactions.
- Urology uses: transplant, refractory Hunner lesions, retroperitoneal fibrosis.

#### Kidney retrieval techniques for organ donation (SAQ)

- Cannulate, clamp, vent, cool; kidneys removed last, en bloc.
- Left kidney preferred for living donation.
- Machine perfusion reduces DGF.

#### Extended Criteria for Selection of Live Renal Donor (SAQ)

- KDIGO 2017: risk-based lifetime ESKD threshold.
- GFR 60–89 and albuminuria 30–100 are individualised.
- Controlled hypertension on 1–2 drugs may donate; type 1 diabetes cannot.

#### Vascular complications after renal transplantation, including transplant renal artery stenosis (SAQ)

- TRAS is the commonest vascular complication; presents months later; angioplasty or stent first (first-line); angiography is gold standard.
- Refractory hypertension plus rising creatinine: Doppler first.
- Venous thrombosis: reversed diastolic flow, swollen tender graft.
- Arterial thrombosis: sudden anuria; explore immediately.

#### Management of acute hyperkalaemia (SAQ)

- Calcium stabilises the membrane but does not lower potassium.
- Insulin 10 units + 50 mL 50% dextrose; salbutamol 20 mg nebulised.
- Binders, diuretics and dialysis remove potassium; relieve obstruction.

#### Vascular grafts in urology (SAQ)

- Autologous for small or contaminated fields; PTFE or Dacron for large vessels.
- IVC: lumen <50% → patch; invaded wall → PTFE 16–20 mm.
- PC-RPLND: IVC may be ligated; aorta must be grafted.

#### Hypokalemia (SAQ)

- Shift or loss; urine potassium separates renal from extrarenal.
- ECG: flat T, U wave.
- Diversion acidosis: give potassium with or before bicarbonate.
- Correct magnesium first.

#### Lymphocele and postoperative lymphatic leak (chylous ascites) (SAQ)

- Lymphocele: commonest PLND complication; radiological up to 60%, significant 2–5%.
- Lymph creatinine = serum; urinoma creatinine higher.
- Laparoscopic fenestration best (is best); aspiration alone worst.
- Chylous ascites: 2–12% after post-chemotherapy RPLND; triglyceride 2–8 × serum.
- Ladder: diet with MCT and octreotide → TPN → embolisation or shunt.

#### Hyperfiltration injury in AKI and CKD (SAQ)

- Hyperfiltration = raised SNGFR; Brenner 1982.
- Leads to FSGS, proteinuria and the AKI-to-CKD transition.
- ACE inhibitors and SGLT2 inhibitors lower glomerular pressure.

#### Management of CAPD peritonitis (SAQ)

- Two of: cloudy fluid or pain, WCC >100/µL with >50% neutrophils, positive culture.
- Intraperitoneal Gram-positive plus Gram-negative cover.
- Remove catheter for fungal or refractory (5 days) peritonitis.

# Section 16 · BPH — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Anatomy of prostate and neurovascular bundles: clinical and surgical concepts in localised prostate cancer
**Asked as:**
- Anatomy of prostate and its neurovascular bundles with its clinical details. — *Practice Set 1 Paper I Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe in detail, anatomy of prostate with special reference to concepts in surgical management if localised CA prostate. — *Practice Set 9 Paper I Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

(Practice Set 1: nerve-sparing techniques also in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3a. Practice Set 9: also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q1)

*"if localised" is read as "of localised" prostate cancer.*

### Introduction
- A (20-g) fibromuscular, glandular organ around the proximal urethra, from bladder neck (**base**) to urogenital diaphragm (**apex**), in front of the rectum; about 20 g.
- Buds from the urogenital sinus at week 10, driven by androgen acting on the **mesenchymal** AR (androgen receptor).
- RP (radical prostatectomy) aims at the "trifecta" of cancer control, continence and potency. Each anatomical step either secures a clear margin or saves a named structure (nerves, sphincter, anterior support).

### Relations
- **Anterior:** retropubic space (of Retzius), puboprostatic ligaments, DVC (dorsal venous complex).
- **Posterior:** Denonvilliers fascia, then rectal ampulla, hence DRE (digital rectal examination) and TRUS (transrectal ultrasound).
- **Lateral:** levator ani with its fascia; NVB (neurovascular bundle) posterolaterally.
- **Base:** bladder neck; seminal vesicles behind. **Inside:** the urethra bends about 35° at the **verumontanum**, where the ejaculatory ducts open beside the utricle.

### Zonal anatomy (McNeal 1981)

| Zone | Glandular share (share of glands) | Position | Clinical / surgical relevance |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peripheral (PZ) | About 70% | Posterior and lateral; longest ducts | **70% of cancers**; felt on DRE; biopsy target; posterolateral, so ECE (extracapsular extension) runs along the nerves |
| Central (CZ) | About 25% | Cone around the ejaculatory ducts at the base | Few cancers; mimics HGPIN (high-grade prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia) |
| Transition (TZ) | About 5% | Two lobes beside the preprostatic sphincter; shortest ducts | **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)**; anterior cancers (about 15%) missed by DRE (digital rectal examination) |
| Periurethral glands | Small | Within the preprostatic sphincter | Middle-lobe BPH |
| Anterior fibromuscular stroma (AFMS) | No glands | Anterior | Anterior tumours reach it; margin risk in posterior approaches |

- **Capsule:** no true histological capsule (there is **no true capsule**), so cancer (breaching the gland) shows "extraprostatic extension". In BPH the compressed PZ forms the **surgical capsule**, the plane of enucleation. The capsule transmits growth pressure to the urethra, so TUIP (transurethral incision) relieves obstruction.

### Fascial investments

| Layer | Position | Surgical rule |
|---|---|---|
| **Prostatic fascia** | On the gland; carries DVC tributaries anteriorly | Stays on the prostate in nerve sparing |
| **Levator fascia** | Covers levator; fuses with prostatic fascia as the lateral pelvic fascia | Incised to reach the NVB |
| **Denonvilliers fascia** | Between prostate and rectum; thickest at base and vesicles | Excised completely for the posterior margin |

- **Denonvilliers fascia** stays on the rectum when ECE risk is low; it is excised when posterolateral ECE is suspected.

### Blood supply and lymphatics
- **Inferior vesical artery** ends as: **urethral group** (enters at the vesicoprostatic junction, 5 and 7 o'clock; supplies the adenoma) and **capsular group** (posterolateral, in the lateral pelvic fascia, marking the cavernous nerves).
- **Accessory pudendal arteries** run over the anterolateral prostate; dividing them reduces penile inflow. Preserve them; they may be the main penile supply.
- **Veins:** the deep dorsal vein passes under the pubis into a superficial branch and lateral (**Santorini**) plexuses, draining to the internal iliac vein. They anastomose freely, so tears bleed heavily.
- DVC (dorsal venous complex): CO₂ pneumoperitoneum tamponades it, so robotic blood loss is low.
- **Lymph:** obturator, internal and external iliac, common iliac, presacral nodes.

### Neurovascular bundle
- **Pelvic plexus:** parasympathetic S2–S4 plus sympathetic (T10–L2) via the hypogastric nerves (hypogastric sympathetic); beside the rectum, centred on the seminal vesicle tip. It gives the **cavernous nerves**.
- **NVB of Walsh (Walsh and Donker, 1982):** capsular vessels plus **cavernous nerves**. The nerves are invisible; the vessels mark them (marked by capsular vessels).
- It runs posterolaterally **between prostatic and levator fascia**, **3–5 mm from the capsule**, outside Denonvilliers fascia.
- At the apex it (they) lie(s) at **5 and 7 o'clock**, posterolateral to the sphincter. The nerves then swing to about **3 and 9 o'clock** at the mid-urethra and enter the corpora behind the dorsal penile artery. The apex is where they are most often injured (most nerve injuries occur here).
- Distribution varies (is diffuse): posterolateral bundle, posterior "hammock", anterolateral fibres (which is the basis of high anterior release).
- **Striated sphincter:** a vertical tube over the apex (Oelrich), pudendal nerve; passive continence.

**Diagram to draw:** axial prostate showing the three fasciae, NVBs at 5 and 7 o'clock and the intra-, inter- and extrafascial planes.

### Continence anatomy
| Passive | Active |
|---|---|
| Smooth muscle of bladder neck and urethra; anterior support (endopelvic fascia, detrusor apron, puboprostatic ligaments, DVC) | Horseshoe striated sphincter at the apex, supplied by pudendal nerve branches |

### Clinical details
- **BPH surgery:** TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) stays proximal to the verumontanum (sphincter lies just distal); urethral-group vessels at 5 and 7 o'clock bleed.
- **Cancer:** PZ cancer exits posterolaterally along nerves; anterior TZ tumours are missed by DRE and biopsy, so MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) helps.
- **Radical prostatectomy (RP):**
  1. Incise endopelvic fascia; spare the pubourethral ligament.
  2. Ligate the DVC over the urethra before division.
  3. Preserve accessory pudendal arteries.
  4. Excise Denonvilliers fascia; the apex is the commonest positive-margin site.

| Nerve-sparing plane | Course (lies) | Verdict (trade-off) |
|---|---|---|
| **Interfascial** | Between prostatic and levator fascia | **Standard plane** for organ-confined disease |
| **Intrafascial** | Under the prostatic fascia (between capsule and prostatic fascia) | Most nerves (kept); more positive margins |
| **Extrafascial** | Outside levator (and Denonvilliers) fascia | Widest margin; ED (erectile dysfunction) likely |

- **NVB injury** (injury comes from): incision, entrapment, **heat**, **traction**, so dissect athermally without traction. Excise an NVB for palpable extracapsular disease; margins are equal with or without nerve sparing.
- **Continence:** Retzius-sparing (Bocciardi 2010) and hood techniques preserve anterior support; continence returns earlier.
- **Also know:** rectal injury at the ampulla; PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5) inhibitor rehabilitation.

### Concepts in surgical management of localised cancer
1. **Selection:** RP suits localised disease (mainly intermediate and selected high risk) with a life expectancy of more than 10 years; low risk is usually offered active surveillance.
2. **RARP (robot-assisted RP):** 3D, magnified view and wristed instruments removed the suturing barrier. **Yaxley 2016** RCT (randomised controlled trial): equal early function versus open; less blood loss and shorter stay. Surgeon matters more than platform.
3. **Risk-graded nerve sparing:** each side is graded from full (intrafascial) through partial to none, using grade, positive cores and mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging) capsular contact. **NeuroSAFE** (intraoperative frozen section of the posterolateral surface) aims to allow more nerve sparing without more positive margins.
4. **Anterior-support preservation:**
   - **Retzius-sparing** (Bocciardi, 2010): fully posterior through the pouch of Douglas. Earlier continence at 3 months, equal at 12; more positive margins, so avoid for anterior tumours.
   - **Hood technique** (Tewari, 2021): anterior approach keeping detrusor apron, puboprostatic ligaments and DVC.
5. **Apical dissection:** the apex is the commonest positive-margin site. Keep maximal urethral length and divide sharply without cautery. Posterior (Rocco) reconstruction may aid early continence.
6. **PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection) templates:**

| Template | Nodes | Drainage captured |
|---|---|---|
| Limited | Obturator | Least |
| Standard | + External iliac | About 38% |
| **Extended** | + Internal iliac | About 63% |
| + Common iliac | + Common iliac | About 75% |

- Extended PLND when nodal risk exceeds 5% (EAU [European Association of Urology]). It stages but does not improve survival; lymphocele is the commonest complication.
7. **Focal therapy anatomy:** ablate the **index lesion**. Transrectal HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) suits posterior PZ lesions; anterior lesions need transperineal cryotherapy. Spare sphincter, NVB and rectum.
- **Also know:** single-port robot (extraperitoneal); PSMA PET (prostate-specific membrane antigen positron emission tomography) staging; ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PZ (gives) 70% of cancers; TZ gives BPH; CZ around ejaculatory ducts; no true capsule.
> - NVB between prostatic and levator fascia, 5 and 7 o'clock; apex most vulnerable (the commonest margin and nerve-injury site).
> - Interfascial plane is (the) standard (nerve-sparing plane); avoid heat and traction.
> - Ligate DVC; excise Denonvilliers fascia; spare accessory pudendals.
> - Retzius-sparing: earlier continence, more positive margins.
> - Extended PLND captures about 63% of drainage.

# Section 16 · BPH — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## HoLEP in BPH
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3c

### Principle
- **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate)** removes the whole adenoma along the **surgical capsule**, as in open simple prostatectomy, but endoscopically.
- **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium–aluminium–garnet), 2120 nm**: absorbed by water, pulsed, **0.4 mm penetration**, coagulates vessels. Normal saline irrigation (no TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome).
- Evolution: HoLAP → HoLRP → **HoLEP (1998)**, made practical by the **morcellator**.

### Indications
- **Size-independent** (<30, 30–80, >80 mL); median lobe; **anticoagulated or high bleeding risk** (AUA (American Urological Association) recommendation); alternative to open prostatectomy for large glands.

### Steps
- 60–150 W laser, 550 µm end-firing fibre, 24–28 Fr resectoscope; about 2 J × 40–50 Hz to enucleate.
1. Bladder-neck incisions at 5 and 7 o'clock to capsule (median lobe) or one at 6 o'clock.
2. Perimontanal incision; **early apical release** to spare the sphincter.
3. Develop the capsular plane round to 12 o'clock; detach lobes into the bladder (**en bloc, 2-lobe or 3-lobe**).
4. Haemostasis with a defocused beam.
5. **Morcellation** with a full bladder; 22 Fr three-way catheter.

### Outcomes
- Versus **TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate)** (trials, meta-analyses): **better IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and Qmax (maximum flow rate)**, less bleeding, shorter catheter time and stay; longer operation. The **only endoscopic method superior to TURP**.
- Versus **open prostatectomy** for large glands (**Kuntz, 5-year**): equal function with less blood loss and shorter stay.
- **Durable**: whole transition zone removed; **retreatment rare**.

### Complications
- **Retrograde ejaculation** about 75%; transient storage symptoms and early SUI (stress urinary incontinence).
- BNC (bladder neck contracture) 0–3.2%; stricture; capsular perforation; ureteric orifice injury; **morcellator bladder injury**; retained adenoma.
- Erectile function unchanged.

### Limitation
- **Steep learning curve**: efficiency plateaus after about 50 cases.

### Recent advances
- **Moses pulse modulation**; thulium enucleation; ejaculation-sparing HoLEP; same-day discharge. EAU (European Association of Urology): enucleation is an alternative to TURP, and to open surgery for >80 mL.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal prostate showing enucleation along the capsule.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ho:YAG 2120 nm, water chromophore, 0.4 mm.
> - Size-independent; best for anticoagulated men.
> - Better IPSS and Qmax than TURP; durable; retreatment rare.
> - Retrograde ejaculation about 75%; learning curve about 50 cases.

## Millin's prostatectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3f
(also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### Definition
- **Retropubic transcapsular simple prostatectomy** (Millin, 1945): adenoma enucleated through a transverse anterior capsulotomy **without opening the bladder**. Plane: the surgical capsule (compressed peripheral zone around the transition-zone adenoma).

### Indications
- BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia), gland **>80–100 g**, with refractory LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), retention, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), haematuria or upper-tract damage.
- Prefer suprapubic (Freyer) for a large median lobe, diverticulum or bladder stones.
- EAU (European Association of Urology) and AUA (American Urological Association): simple prostatectomy or HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation) for large glands.

### Work-up
- PSA (prostate-specific antigen), MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) ± biopsy; TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) volume; cystoscopy; urodynamics if bladder function is doubtful.

### Steps
1. Lower midline or Pfannenstiel; **space of Retzius**.
2. Optional prophylactic ligation of DVC (dorsal venous complex) and pedicles (Walsh–Oesterling).
3. **Transverse capsulotomy ~2 cm distal to the bladder neck** (a longitudinal cut may extend into the sphincter).
4. Find the adenoma–capsule plane with scissors; **finger enucleation**.
5. **Divide the apical urethra under vision** proximal to the sphincter.
6. **Figure-of-eight sutures at 5 and 7 o'clock** (prostatic arteries); advance bladder neck mucosa into the fossa.
7. 22 Fr three-way catheter, **watertight capsule closure**, drain.

### Millin versus Freyer

| Aspect | Millin (retropubic) | Freyer (suprapubic) |
|---|---|---|
| Bladder | Not opened | Opened |
| Apex | Divided under vision | Pinched by finger |
| Risk | Venous bleeding | Cystotomy leak |

### Complications
- Bleeding (transfusion <5%): catheter traction, Malament purse-string, O'Conor plication.
- Urine leak, UTI, bladder neck contracture 2–6%, stricture, DVT (deep vein thrombosis).
- **Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%**; ED (erectile dysfunction) 3–5%; incontinence very rare.

### Advantages over TURP (transurethral resection of prostate)
- Complete removal, low retreatment, no TUR syndrome; but incision, longer stay.
- **Recent:** laparoscopic, robotic multiport and single-port transvesical simple prostatectomy: less bleeding, shorter stay.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal view of transverse capsulotomy and enucleation plane.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Millin 1945: capsulotomy ~2 cm below bladder neck; bladder not opened.
> - Apex divided under vision; 5 and 7 o'clock sutures.
> - Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%; transfusion <5%.

## TUR Syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3f
(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3f)

### Definition
- **TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome:** neurological and cardiovascular disturbance from **absorbing hypo-osmolar, non-conducting irrigant** during monopolar TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate). Incidence low and falling.

### Pathophysiology
- Prostatic venous pressure about **10 mmHg**; irrigant at higher pressure enters open sinuses or, after perforation, the periprostatic space.
- Monopolar needs ion-free fluid: **glycine 1.5%**, sorbitol, mannitol or water (all hypo-osmolar).
- **Fluid overload** → hypertension, pulmonary oedema.
- **Dilutional hyponatraemia and hypo-osmolality** → water enters brain → **cerebral oedema**.
- **Glycine toxicity:** ammonia → encephalopathy; retinal inhibitory transmitter → **transient blindness**.

### Risk factors
- Gland **>45 mL**, resection **>90 minutes** (Veterans Affairs Cooperative Study), high irrigation pressure, capsular perforation, sterile water.

### Clinical features
- Awake (spinal) patient: restlessness, confusion, **nausea, vomiting, visual disturbance**.
- **Hypertension with bradycardia**, tachypnoea, later hypotension.
- Severe: seizures, coma, herniation. **Na <120 mEq/L** is dangerous.

### Prevention
- **Bipolar TURP or laser with saline** (NNT [number needed to treat] about 50).
- Bag **~60 cm** above patient (each extra 10 cm more than doubles absorption); continuous flow; low pressure.
- Limit resection time; stage huge glands; check sodium in long cases.
- Spinal anaesthesia for early detection; ethanol-tagged irrigant monitors absorption.

### Treatment
1. Stop surgery, secure haemostasis, insert catheter.
2. ABC (airway, breathing, circulation), oxygen, monitoring; send **sodium**, osmolality, glucose.
3. Overload: **furosemide**; restrict fluids.
4. Severe neurological signs or Na <120: **3% saline 100 mL bolus** (raises Na 2–3 mEq/L), repeated at 10 minutes, maximum 300 mL. **Target rise 4–6 mEq/L.**
5. Limit: **≤12 mEq/L in 24 h** for acute post-TURP hyponatraemia (8–10 mEq/L if symptoms start >48 h after surgery), avoiding **osmotic demyelination**.
6. Seizures: benzodiazepines; ICU (intensive care unit).
- **Also know:** occurs in TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour), PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), hysteroscopy.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: absorption → overload + hyponatraemia → cerebral oedema.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Venous pressure 10 mmHg; gland >45 mL, time >90 min.
> - Glycine: ammonia and transient blindness.
> - 3% saline 100 mL boluses; ≤12 mEq/L in 24 h.
> - Bipolar saline TURP prevents it.

## TURP instruments and techniques
**Asked as:**
- TURP instruments — *Practice Set 14 Paper I Q3e (SAQ)*
- TURP techniques — *Practice Set 21 SAQ Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(irrigation fluids also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)
(irrigation fluids answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3a; complications in Practice Set 9 Paper 2, Q3b)

### Introduction
- **TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate):** an electrified (wire) loop removes transition-zone adenoma **from bladder neck to verumontanum, down to the surgical capsule**. Gold standard for glands up to 80 mL (AUA [American Urological Association] 2023); larger glands need enucleation or simple prostatectomy.

### Resectoscope

| Part | Description |
|---|---|
| **Outer sheath** | 24–28 Fr; **insulated beak** prevents arcing to the urethra |
| **Inner sheath** | Creates **continuous flow**: inflow through the inner, outflow between sheaths (passive or suction). Lower bladder pressure, clear vision, no repeated emptying |
| **Obturator** | Blunt or visual; atraumatic passage |
| **Working element** | Moves the loop. **Passive:** spring holds loop inside sheath; cutting stroke by spring recoil. **Active:** surgeon's thumb pulls the extended loop in |
| **Telescope** | **Hopkins rod-lens**, usually **30°** (12° alternative) |

### Electrodes
- **Cutting loop:** tungsten (monopolar); **platinum–iridium (bipolar plasma)**. Plasma loop (platinum–iridium) cuts at lower temperature and seals vessels.
- Roller ball or barrel (coagulation), vaporisation button, **Collins knife** (TUIP (transurethral incision of the prostate)).

### Energy (energy variants)
- **Generator:** about 500 kHz, too fast to stimulate nerves.
- **Cut:** continuous waveform. **Coagulation:** interrupted, higher voltage. **Blend:** better haemostasis, cuts less well.

| | Monopolar | Bipolar |
|---|---|---|
| Current | Loop → patient → **dispersive pad** | Active and return poles on the loop; stays at loop |
| Irrigant | **Non-conducting, hypo-osmolar irrigant:** glycine 1.5%, sorbitol, water | **Normal saline** |
| TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome | Risk; yes; keep resection <90 min | Virtually none; eliminated |
| Bleeding | More | Less transfusion, clot retention |
| Efficacy | Equal | Equal |

### Imaging and irrigation
- **Light source:** xenon or LED (light-emitting diode) with fibreoptic cable; camera and monitor.
- **Irrigant** at body temperature; bag about **60 cm** high (each extra 10 cm more than doubles absorption); lowest height giving vision.

### Accessories
- **Ellik evacuator or Toomey syringe** for chips.
- Van Buren sounds or Otis urethrotome: dilate meatus 2 Fr above sheath size (prevents stricture).
- **Three-way Foley catheter** (22–24 Fr) with catheter guide; continuous irrigation set.

### Preparation
- Sterile urine; spinal or general anaesthesia; lithotomy with peroneal padding.
- **Inspect bladder first** (tumour, diverticula); identify ureteric orifices, verumontanum, external sphincter.

### Resection techniques
- **Median lobe first**, then lateral lobes.
- **Modified Nesbit "encirclement":** start at 11–9 and 1–3 o'clock; expose but do not resect bladder neck fibres.
- **Floor-first:** channel at 5 or 7 o'clock to the capsule (sets depth early); widen laterally; lateral lobes "fall" into the fossa.
- **Anterior start:** 12 o'clock bladder neck, then quadrants down to the apex.
- **Chips:** long "canoe-like" chips with synchronised rocking.
- **Anterior tissue and apex last** in a bloodless field; leaving a little tissue is better than incontinence.
- Haemostasis: arterial bleeders must be fulgurated; venous ooze settles with catheter traction.
- Evacuate chips (Ellik evacuator); three-way catheter.
- **Also know:** TUVP (transurethral vaporisation, button electrode; ≤80 mL); TUIP (transurethral incision, <30 mL, no median lobe); bipolar enucleation; high bleeding risk: AUA prefers HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation) or PVP (photoselective vaporisation).

### Troubleshooting a loop that will not cut
```
Scope–generator connection → foot pedal →
irrigant matches generator (glycine for monopolar, saline for bipolar) →
patient grounding (pad contact)
```

### Outcomes
- About 80% symptom improvement; low retreatment; retrograde ejaculation about two-thirds.

### Recent advances
- Bipolar plasma resection; chip-on-tip digital optics; laser enucleation scopes; single-use resectoscopes.

**Diagram to draw:** assembled resectoscope labelling sheaths, working element, loop and flow channels.
**Diagram to draw:** resection field with clock positions, bladder neck, verumontanum and sphincter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Continuous-flow 24–28 Fr sheath, 30° telescope.
> - Tungsten loop (monopolar) vs platinum–iridium (bipolar).
> - Glycine 1.5% for monopolar, saline for bipolar; bag 60 cm.
> - Bladder neck to verumontanum, depth to capsule.
> - Median lobe first; apex last.
> - Bipolar saline TURP: no TUR syndrome.

## Methods to estimate prostate size
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 1, Q3f
(TRUS also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3e)

### Why measure
- Chooses treatment: 5ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) if >30 mL (AUA (American Urological Association)) or >40 mL (EAU (European Association of Urology)); type of BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) surgery.
- **PSAD (PSA density)** = PSA (prostate-specific antigen) ÷ volume; >0.15 suggests cancer. Also AUR (acute urinary retention) risk, brachytherapy planning.
- Normal adult gland 20–25 g; about 48 g at 60–70 years. **1 cm³ ≈ 1 g**.

### Methods

| Method | Technique | Accuracy and use |
|---|---|---|
| **DRE (digital rectal examination)** | Estimate size, consistency | **Underestimates** above 30 mL (lateral lobes impalpable); flat gland predicts <30 mL |
| **PSA as surrogate** | Age-specific cut-offs for **>40 mL:** >1.6 ng/mL (50s), >2.0 (60s), >2.3 (70s) | AUC (area under curve) 0.76–0.78; within ±20% of TRUS in most |
| **TAUS (transabdominal ultrasound)** | Full bladder; transverse width and height, sagittal length | Less precise than TRUS; also PVR (post-void residual) and **IPP (intravesical prostatic protrusion)** |
| **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)** | Ellipsoid formula; planimetry | **Method of choice** |
| **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)** | T2-weighted ellipsoid or segmentation (manual or automated) | Most accurate and reproducible |
| **CT (computed tomography)** | Axial images | Overestimates; poor capsule definition |
| **Cystoscopy** | Urethral length, lobes, median lobe | Rough; guides procedure choice |

### TRUS formulas

| Shape assumed | Formula |
|---|---|
| **Ellipsoid (standard)** | **π/6 (0.52) × transverse × AP (anteroposterior) × length** |
| Sphere | π/6 × transverse³ |
| Prolate spheroid | π/6 × transverse² × AP |
| **Planimetry** | Sum of serial transverse areas |

- Transverse and AP at the widest axial section; length sagittally. All formulas correlate >0.90 with specimen weight.
- **Planimetry:** probe on a stepper, images every 3–5 mm, areas summed; most precise; used for brachytherapy.

### IPP grading (suprapubic, sagittal, bladder 100–200 mL)
- Grade I <5 mm, II 5–10 mm, III >10 mm; ≥10 mm predicts BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) and failed trial without catheter.

**Diagram to draw:** TRUS transverse and sagittal views with three measured diameters.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ellipsoid volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; 1 mL ≈ 1 g.
> - DRE underestimates above 30 mL; PSA >1.6/2.0/2.3 by decade predicts >40 mL.
> - Planimetry most precise; MRI most reproducible.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 16 · BPH

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Anatomy of prostate and neurovascular bundles: clinical and surgical concepts in localised prostate cancer (LAQ)

- PZ (gives) 70% of cancers; TZ gives BPH; CZ around ejaculatory ducts; no true capsule.
- NVB between prostatic and levator fascia, 5 and 7 o'clock; apex most vulnerable (the commonest margin and nerve-injury site).
- Interfascial plane is (the) standard (nerve-sparing plane); avoid heat and traction.
- Ligate DVC; excise Denonvilliers fascia; spare accessory pudendals.
- Retzius-sparing: earlier continence, more positive margins.
- Extended PLND captures about 63% of drainage.

#### HoLEP in BPH (SAQ)

- Ho:YAG 2120 nm, water chromophore, 0.4 mm.
- Size-independent; best for anticoagulated men.
- Better IPSS and Qmax than TURP; durable; retreatment rare.
- Retrograde ejaculation about 75%; learning curve about 50 cases.

#### Millin's prostatectomy (SAQ)

- Millin 1945: capsulotomy ~2 cm below bladder neck; bladder not opened.
- Apex divided under vision; 5 and 7 o'clock sutures.
- Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%; transfusion <5%.

#### TUR Syndrome (SAQ)

- Venous pressure 10 mmHg; gland >45 mL, time >90 min.
- Glycine: ammonia and transient blindness.
- 3% saline 100 mL boluses; ≤12 mEq/L in 24 h.
- Bipolar saline TURP prevents it.

#### TURP instruments and techniques (SAQ)

- Continuous-flow 24–28 Fr sheath, 30° telescope.
- Tungsten loop (monopolar) vs platinum–iridium (bipolar).
- Glycine 1.5% for monopolar, saline for bipolar; bag 60 cm.
- Bladder neck to verumontanum, depth to capsule.
- Median lobe first; apex last.
- Bipolar saline TURP: no TUR syndrome.

#### Methods to estimate prostate size (SAQ)

- Ellipsoid volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; 1 mL ≈ 1 g.
- DRE underestimates above 30 mL; PSA >1.6/2.0/2.3 by decade predicts >40 mL.
- Planimetry most precise; MRI most reproducible.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe staging of CA prostate and management of nonorgan confined CA prostate
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 1, Q2
(also answered in Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q1)

### Staging: TNM (AJCC (American Joint Committee on Cancer) 8th edition, 2017)

| Stage | Definition |
|---|---|
| T1 | Not palpable: T1a incidental in ≤5% of resected tissue; T1b >5%; T1c found on needle biopsy |
| T2 | Palpable, confined: T2a ≤ half of one lobe; T2b > half of one lobe; T2c both lobes |
| **T3** | **T3a** extraprostatic extension (including microscopic bladder neck invasion); **T3b** seminal vesicle invasion |
| **T4** | Fixed or invading external sphincter, rectum, bladder, levators, pelvic wall |
| N | N0; **N1 regional (pelvic) nodes** |
| M | **M1a** non-regional nodes; **M1b** bone; **M1c** other sites |

- **Traps:** seminal vesicle invasion is T3b, not T4; non-regional nodes are M1a.
- **ISUP (International Society of Urological Pathology) grade groups (GG):** GG1 ≤6; GG2 3+4; GG3 4+3; GG4 8; GG5 9–10.
- **Risk groups (EAU (European Association of Urology)):** high risk = PSA (prostate-specific antigen) >20 ng/mL, GG4–5 or cT2c; **locally advanced = cT3–4 or cN1**. AJCC prognostic groups combine T, N, M, PSA and grade.

### Staging work-up
- DRE (digital rectal examination) sets clinical T stage but under- and over-stages. PSA predicts extent: organ-confined about 80% if PSA <4, under 50% if >10; nodes in about 75% if >50 ng/mL.
- **mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging):** extraprostatic extension and seminal vesicle invasion; best specificity in higher-risk men.
- **High risk:** bone scan plus CT (computed tomography) or MRI; **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography)**. **proPSMA:** accuracy 92% vs 65% for conventional imaging.
- Nomograms (Partin, Kattan, CAPRA (Cancer of the Prostate Risk Assessment)) and genomic classifiers (Decipher) refine risk.

### Management principle
- Non-organ-confined disease (T3–4 and/or N1, M0) has a high chance of micrometastases. **Single-modality treatment usually fails**, so treatment is **multimodal**: local therapy plus systemic ADT (androgen deprivation therapy).

### A. Locally advanced (cT3–4 N0 M0)
**1. EBRT (external beam radiotherapy, RT) plus long-term ADT: standard**
- Dose-escalated IMRT (intensity-modulated radiotherapy) with image guidance, about 76–78 Gy, with **ADT for 18–36 months** (NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network); EAU 2–3 years). Neoadjuvant ADT shrinks the target and is synergistic with radiation.
- **EORTC 22863:** 3 years of ADT with RT raised 5-year overall survival (78% vs 62%). **RTOG 92-02:** 28 vs 4 months; survival gain in Gleason 8–10.
- **SPCG-7 and MRC PR07/NCIC PR.3:** adding RT to ADT reduced cancer death (RR (relative risk) 0.44; HR (hazard ratio) 0.46). ADT alone is inferior.
- **Brachytherapy boost** improves biochemical control (ASCENDE-RT).
- **Very high risk:** add **abiraterone for 2 years** (STAMPEDE M0: metastasis-free survival HR 0.53). Eligible: N1, or two of cT3–4, Gleason ≥8, PSA ≥40 ng/mL.

**2. RP (radical prostatectomy) with ePLND (extended pelvic lymph node dissection): selected men**
- Fit, life expectancy >10 years, resectable (mainly cT3a). Clinical overstaging occurs in 7–27%; 10-year disease-specific survival about 80–90%.
- Neoadjuvant ADT downstages clinically but gives **no survival gain**.
- **After surgery:** pT3 or positive margin: **observation with early salvage RT** (RADICALS-RT, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17); adjuvant RT only for very high-risk pathology (GG4–5, pT3b, margins).

**3. Other options**
- **ADT alone:** only for men unfit or unwilling for local therapy; immediate if symptomatic, high PSA or rapid doubling.
- **Docetaxel with RT and ADT:** RTOG 0521 overall survival HR 0.69; not routine.
- Cryotherapy or HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound): investigational in high risk.

### B. Node-positive (N1 M0)
- **cN1:** RT to prostate and pelvic nodes with long-term ADT plus 2 years of abiraterone; RP with ePLND in selected men.
- **pN1 after prostatectomy:** **ECOG 7887**: immediate ADT improved overall survival; ADT plus pelvic RT beats ADT alone. Observation is an option for ≤2 nodes with undetectable PSA.

### C. Metastatic (M1), in brief
- ADT plus an **ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor)** (abiraterone, enzalutamide, apalutamide) or a triplet with docetaxel; prostate RT for low-volume disease (see 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3b).

```
Non-organ-confined, M0
 ├─ Fit, cT3a, resectable → RP + ePLND → early salvage RT ± ADT
 ├─ cT3–4 or cN1 (most) → EBRT + ADT 18–36 mo
 │       very high risk / N1 → + abiraterone 2 years
 └─ Unfit for local therapy → ADT (immediate if symptomatic)
```

### Local complications and follow-up
- Bladder outlet obstruction (channel TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate)), ureteric obstruction (stent or nephrostomy), haematuria (palliative RT).
- PSA every 3–6 months; bone health, metabolic and cardiovascular monitoring on ADT.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis showing T3a, T3b and T4 spread.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - T3b = seminal vesicle; M1a = non-regional nodes.
> - EBRT + 2–3 years ADT is standard; EORTC 22863 and SPCG-7.
> - STAMPEDE: abiraterone for 2 years in very high-risk or N1 disease.
> - After RP: early salvage RT preferred to routine adjuvant RT.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Transrectal ultrasound study of prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3e

### Principle and technique
- **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound):** the prostate lies directly in front of the rectum. Probes: end-fire, side-fire or biplane, **6–10 MHz** (higher frequency: better resolution, less depth). Left lateral (or lithotomy for transperineal work), after DRE (digital rectal examination). Transverse and sagittal planes, base to apex.

### Normal sonographic anatomy
| Zone (McNeal) | Glandular share | TRUS appearance | Cancer share |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peripheral | ~70% | Homogeneous, echogenic (posterior) | ~70% |
| Central | ~25% | Echogenic, at base | ~5–10% |
| Transition | 5–10% (BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)) | Heterogeneous, anterior | ~20–25% |
| Anterior fibromuscular stroma | Non-glandular | Hypoechoic | – |

- Zones are not seen as such; TRUS separates echogenic posterior PZ (peripheral zone) from heterogeneous TZ (transition zone). **Corpora amylacea** mark the surgical capsule.
- Seminal vesicles 4.5–5.5 × 2 cm; absent seminal vesicle → ipsilateral renal agenesis in 79%.

### Measurements
- **Volume (ellipsoid) = 0.52 (π/6) × transverse × AP (anteroposterior) × length**; 1 mL ≈ 1 g; normal 20–25 g. **Planimetry** (serial sections) for brachytherapy.
- **PSAD (prostate-specific antigen density)** = PSA/volume; **>0.15** suggests cancer.

### Findings
- **Cancer:** classically a hypoechoic PZ lesion, but **39% are isoechoic**; false positives: BPH nodules, prostatitis, infarct.
- **Cysts:** Müllerian (midline), ejaculatory duct cyst (just off midline), seminal vesicle cyst (lateral, renal agenesis).
- Abscess, calculi, extraprostatic extension.
- After ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) or radiotherapy the gland shrinks and becomes hypoechoic.

### Indications
- **Guided biopsy** (12-core systematic ± MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-targeted fusion).
- Volume before BPH surgery, brachytherapy, cryotherapy; fiducial markers.
- **Azoospermia:** ejaculatory duct obstruction (seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide, midline cyst).
- Drainage of prostatic abscess or cysts.

### Biopsy notes
- Periprostatic block. Complications: bleeding, **sepsis** (fluoroquinolone-resistant *E. coli*); transperineal route lowers infection.

### Advances
- Colour/power Doppler, contrast-enhanced TRUS, **elastography**, multiparametric ultrasound.
- **Micro-ultrasound (29 MHz;** 70 µm resolution) with PRI-MUS scoring, comparable to MRI.
- MRI-first pathway: PROMIS (2017) mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS biopsy.

**Diagram to draw:** transverse and sagittal TRUS views labelling PZ, TZ, seminal vesicles and urethra.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 6–10 MHz; volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; PSAD >0.15.
> - 39% of cancers isoechoic; hypoechoic PZ lesion classic.
> - Seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide: ejaculatory duct obstruction.

## Robotic radical prostatectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- RARP (robot-assisted radical prostatectomy): Walsh's anatomical nerve-sparing principles done with **3D, 10× magnified vision and wristed instruments**. The robot removed the laparoscopic suturing barrier; da Vinci from 2000 (S, Si, Xi, SP).

### Indications and contraindications
- Localised cancer (cT1–T2, selected cT3); life expectancy over 10 years.
- **Absolute:** uncorrectable bleeding diathesis; unfit for general anaesthesia.
- **Relative:** morbid obesity, prostate >80 g, median lobe, prior TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), salvage surgery.

### Set-up
- Low lithotomy, arms tucked, **steep Trendelenburg (22–25°)**, padding; pressure 12–15 mmHg; camera port 15–17 cm above pubis, three robotic and two assistant ports.

### Steps (transperitoneal anterior approach)
1. Drop bladder; enter space of Retzius.
2. Incise endopelvic fascia; ligate DVC (dorsal venous complex).
3. Divide bladder neck (fat line, balloon traction); check for median lobe.
4. Seminal vesicles and vasa; incise Denonvilliers fascia.
5. Clip pedicles athermally; **interfascial nerve sparing** (extrafascial if high risk).
6. Apical dissection preserving urethral length.
7. Extended PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection) if indicated.
8. Posterior (Rocco) reconstruction; **Van Velthoven running anastomosis**.

### Postoperative care
- ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery); discharge within 24 h; catheter about 1 week (not before day 5: retention); pelvic floor training; penile rehabilitation with PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5) inhibitors.

### Outcomes
- **Yaxley 2016** randomised trial: equal function and early margins versus open; less blood loss (300 versus 1400 mL) and shorter stay.
- Margins about 15% (9% if pT2); 1-year continence mid-90s% in expert hands.

### Complications
- Transfusion 1–2%; bladder neck stricture <2% (open up to 16%); rectal injury; ureteric injury; lymphocele; positioning neuropathy, corneal abrasion; robot failure <0.5%.

### Recent advances
- **Retzius-sparing** (earlier continence, more positive margins); Hood technique; single-port robot; same-day discharge.

**Diagram to draw:** port layout and fascial planes (intra-, inter-, extrafascial).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Surgeon, not platform, decides outcome (Yaxley 2016).
> - Interfascial plane is the standard nerve-sparing plane.
> - Bladder neck stricture <2%; transfusion 1–2%.

## Prostate multiparametric MRI and endorectal coil MRI
**Asked as:**
- Multiparametric MRI : its components and interpretation for prostate cancer. — *Winter 2024 Paper I Q3d (SAQ)*
- Endorectal coil MRI — *Practice Set 7 Paper I Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Part A: Multiparametric MRI

#### Introduction
- **mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging):** anatomical plus functional sequences, usually at 3 T without endorectal coil. Reported by **PI-RADS v2.1 (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System, 2019)**.

#### Components
| Sequence | Principle | Cancer appearance |
|---|---|---|
| **T2W (T2-weighted)**, triplanar | Anatomy; normal PZ (peripheral zone) bright | Low-signal lesion; "erased charcoal" in TZ (transition zone); shows EPE (extraprostatic extension) and SVI (seminal vesicle invasion) |
| **DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging)** with **ADC (apparent diffusion coefficient)** map; high b-value (≥1400) | Cellular tumour restricts water movement | Bright on high-b DWI, **dark on ADC**; ADC falls as Gleason rises |
| **DCE (dynamic contrast-enhanced)** T1 with gadolinium | Angiogenesis: early enhancement and washout | Early focal enhancement (type 3 curve) |
- **Biparametric MRI (T2W + DWI):** no contrast, cheaper, similar detection; poorer staging.
- **PI-QUAL (Prostate Imaging Quality):** quality score (1–5) before reporting.

#### Interpretation: PI-RADS v2.1
| Score | Clinically significant cancer |
|---|---|
| 1 | Highly unlikely |
| 2 | Unlikely |
| 3 | Equivocal |
| 4 | Likely; lesion <1.5 cm |
| 5 | Highly likely; ≥1.5 cm or definite EPE |

| Zone | Dominant sequence | Tie-breaker |
|---|---|---|
| Peripheral | **DWI** | DWI 3 + DCE positive → 4 |
| Transition | **T2W** | T2W 3 + DWI 5 → 4; T2W 2 + DWI ≥4 → 3 |
- **DCE only upgrades PZ 3 to 4**; it never changes 4–5 or TZ scores.
- Cancer in about one-third of PI-RADS 3, over half of 4, 90% of 5.
- **PRECISE** score (1–5) tracks change during active surveillance.

#### Clinical use and evidence
- **PROMIS (2017):** sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) biopsy; 27% could avoid biopsy.
- **PRECISION (2018):** MRI pathway with targeted biopsy of PI-RADS ≥3 detected more significant cancer (38% vs 26%), 28% avoided biopsy.
- EAU (European Association of Urology) and AUA (American Urological Association): MRI **before biopsy**; also staging, active surveillance, focal therapy planning.
- Limitations: low specificity (Cochrane 0.37); reader-dependent.

**Diagram to draw:** prostate zones with a PZ lesion dark on ADC.

### Part B: Endorectal coil MRI

#### Principle
- **ERC (endorectal coil):** a receive-only surface coil inside an inflatable balloon placed in the rectum against the prostate, used with an external pelvic phased-array coil.
- **"The currency of MRI is signal."** A coil close to the prostate gives higher **SNR (signal-to-noise ratio)**, allowing **higher spatial resolution**.

#### Technique
- Antispasmodic to reduce rectal motion artefact; balloon filled with air or perfluorocarbon (fluid reduces susceptibility artefact).
- Sequences as for mpMRI (Part A): **T2W** triplanar for anatomy, **DWI with ADC** map, **DCE**; ERC was traditionally needed for **MRSI (MR spectroscopic imaging)**.

#### Uses
- **Local staging:** extraprostatic extension and seminal vesicle invasion (both low T2 signal) before radical prostatectomy.
- **Local recurrence** after prostatectomy: recurrence isointense on T1, slightly hyperintense on T2; DCE raises accuracy at low PSA (prostate-specific antigen).
- **Also know:** focal therapy planning (ERC recommended at 1.5 T); seminal vesicles; female urethral diverticulum.

#### ERC versus no ERC
| | With ERC | Without ERC (3 T, pelvic coil) |
|---|---|---|
| Signal and resolution | Higher | Adequate at 3 T |
| csPCa (clinically significant cancer) detection | GG (Grade Group) ≥3 equal; GG2 slightly better | GG ≥3 equal |
| Comfort, cost, time | Worse | Better |
| Gland | Deformed (fusion and radiotherapy planning harder) | Natural shape |

#### Disadvantages
- Artefacts: **near-field signal flare** next to the coil (needs intensity correction), air susceptibility at the rectal wall, malposition.
- **Contraindications:** absent rectum (abdominoperineal resection), recent rectal surgery, anorectal fistula or inflammatory bowel disease, severe haemorrhoids or stricture, latex allergy.

#### Current position
- At **3 T the ERC is largely historical**; PI-RADS v2.1 does not require it. **Consider it at 1.5 T** or with older scanners, or when body-coil quality is poor.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis with inflated endorectal coil against the posterior prostate.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - T2W anatomy, DWI/ADC cellularity, DCE vascularity.
> - PZ ruled by DWI, TZ by T2W; DCE only lifts PZ 3 to 4.
> - PRECISION and PROMIS: MRI before biopsy.
> - Endorectal coil close to prostate: higher SNR and resolution.
> - Useful at 1.5 T; largely unnecessary at 3 T (GG ≥3 detection equal).
> - Drawbacks: discomfort, gland deformation, near-field flare.

## Docetaxel
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 1, Q3b

### Introduction
- Semisynthetic **taxane** cytotoxic. Since **TAX 327 (2004)** it is the standard first-line chemotherapy for mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer).

### Mechanism of action
- Binds **β-tubulin** and **stabilises microtubules** (GTP [guanosine triphosphate]-independent polymerisation). Cells arrest in **G2/M**.
- Induces apoptosis independent of TP53, through **BCL2 phosphorylation** and p27 induction.
- Prostate-specific: blocks microtubule transport of ligand-bound **AR (androgen receptor)** into the nucleus.

### Dose and administration
- **75 mg/m² IV (intravenous) every 3 weeks with prednisone 5 mg twice daily**; 6 cycles in mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer). Steroid premedication limits fluid retention and hypersensitivity. Hepatic metabolism: caution in liver dysfunction.

### Uses in urology
| Setting | Evidence |
|---|---|
| **mCRPC** | **TAX 327:** overall survival (OS) 18.9 vs 16.4 months versus mitoxantrone (HR [hazard ratio] 0.76); better pain and PSA (prostate-specific antigen) response. SWOG 9916 (with estramustine) also positive |
| **mHSPC** | **CHAARTED:** OS 57.6 vs 44.0 months (HR 0.61), benefit in **high volume**. STAMPEDE: OS HR 0.78. GETUG-AFU 15 not significant |
| **Triplet** | ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) + docetaxel + abiraterone (**PEACE-1**) or darolutamide (**ARASENS**) improves OS |
| High-risk localised | RTOG 0521: added to RT (radiotherapy) + long-term ADT, OS HR 0.69 |
| Bladder | Second-line single agent (response about 13%); **intravesical gemcitabine–docetaxel** for BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin)-unresponsive or BCG-naive high-grade NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) |
| Penis | **TPF** (docetaxel, cisplatin, 5-fluorouracil) |

**CHAARTED high volume:** ≥4 bone metastases with ≥1 outside the axial skeleton, or visceral metastasis.

### Toxicity
- **Neutropenia** and febrile neutropenia (dose-limiting), fatigue, **fluid retention** (peripheral oedema), **peripheral neuropathy**, hyperlacrimation, **nail dystrophy**, alopecia, liver dysfunction, hypersensitivity.
- 3-weekly: more neutropenia; weekly: more fatigue, diarrhoea, tearing.

### Resistance and sequencing
- P-glycoprotein efflux pump mediates resistance. **Cabazitaxel** has poor P-glycoprotein affinity, so it works after docetaxel (**TROPIC**; **CARD** beat a second ARPI [androgen receptor pathway inhibitor]).
- **Also know:** failed combinations (bevacizumab, atrasentan).

**Diagram to draw:** microtubule stabilised by docetaxel causing G2/M arrest and blocking AR entry into the nucleus.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 75 mg/m² 3-weekly + prednisone; TAX 327 HR 0.76.
> - CHAARTED: benefit in high-volume mHSPC (HR 0.61).
> - Neutropenia, oedema, neuropathy, nail change; cabazitaxel after failure.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 17 · Prostate Cancer

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Describe staging of CA prostate and management of nonorgan confined CA prostate (LAQ)

- T3b = seminal vesicle; M1a = non-regional nodes.
- EBRT + 2–3 years ADT is standard; EORTC 22863 and SPCG-7.
- STAMPEDE: abiraterone for 2 years in very high-risk or N1 disease.
- After RP: early salvage RT preferred to routine adjuvant RT.

#### Transrectal ultrasound study of prostate (SAQ)

- 6–10 MHz; volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; PSAD >0.15.
- 39% of cancers isoechoic; hypoechoic PZ lesion classic.
- Seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide: ejaculatory duct obstruction.

#### Robotic radical prostatectomy (SAQ)

- Surgeon, not platform, decides outcome (Yaxley 2016).
- Interfascial plane is the standard nerve-sparing plane.
- Bladder neck stricture <2%; transfusion 1–2%.

#### Prostate multiparametric MRI and endorectal coil MRI (SAQ)

- T2W anatomy, DWI/ADC cellularity, DCE vascularity.
- PZ ruled by DWI, TZ by T2W; DCE only lifts PZ 3 to 4.
- PRECISION and PROMIS: MRI before biopsy.
- Endorectal coil close to prostate: higher SNR and resolution.
- Useful at 1.5 T; largely unnecessary at 3 T (GG ≥3 detection equal).
- Drawbacks: discomfort, gland deformation, near-field flare.

#### Docetaxel (SAQ)

- 75 mg/m² 3-weekly + prednisone; TAX 327 HR 0.76.
- CHAARTED: benefit in high-volume mHSPC (HR 0.61).
- Neutropenia, oedema, neuropathy, nail change; cabazitaxel after failure.
